The International Association for the Evaluation of Educational Achievement (IEA) is an in‐
dependent, international cooperative of national research institutions and governmental re‐
search agencies. It conducts large‐scale comparative studies of educational achievement and
other aspects of education, with the aim of gaining in‐depth understanding of the effects of
policies and practices within and across systems of education.
Cover design by Studio Lakmoes, Arnhem, The Netherlands.

Chapter 1:
Introduction
Julian Fraillon
1.1 Background
The International Association for the Evaluation of Educational Achievement’s (IEA’s) International
Computer and Information Literacy Study (ICILS) was established in response to the recognition of
the essential role that information and communication technology (ICT) has come to play in modern
society and the consequent need for young people to develop relevant capabilities to participate safely,
productively, and effectively in the digital world. The first cycle of ICILS was conducted in 2013, with
the second cycle in 2018. ICILS 2023 builds on the work of these two ICILS cycles.
Since 2013, ICILS has measured the achievement of grade 8 (or equivalent) students in computer and
information literacy (CIL) which is defined as an “individual’s ability to use computers to investigate,
create, and communicate in order to participate effectively at home, at school, in the workplace, and
in society” (Fraillon & Duckworth, 2024, p. 26).
Since 2018, participating countries could also choose for students to complete an optional additional
assessment of computational thinking (CT) which is defined as an “individual’s ability to recognize as‐
pects of real‐world problems that are appropriate for computational formulation and to evaluate and
develop algorithmic solutions to those problems so that the solutions could be operationalized with a
computer” (Duckworth & Fraillon, 2024, p. 38).
In ICILS 2023, students in 34 countries and one benchmarking participant completed the CIL assess‐
ment, and students from 24 countries and one benchmarking participant completed the additional CT
assessment.
In addition to measuring variations in CIL and CT among and within countries, ICILS collects and re‐
ports on extensive data representing the contexts in which student digital literacy capabilities are being
developed within and outside of school. These contextual data are collected from national study cen‐
ters, school principals, school ICT coordinators, teachers, and students in schools participating in the
study.
The ICILS international database (IDB) is publicly available for each of the ICILS study cycles, so that
ICILS data may be further analyzed and reported to contribute to our understanding of digital literacy
education and to inform policy and practice. The ICILS IDBs can be accessed from IEA’s data repository.
This report aims to provide comprehensive details of the implementation of ICILS 2023 including in‐
strument development and preparation processes, data collection procedures (including sampling and
field operations), and data management, analysis, and reporting procedures. Information in this report
supplements and is supplemented by information provided in:
• The ICILS 2023 assessment framework
• The ICILS 2023 international report
• The ICILS 2023 IDB and user guide
Some of the processes and procedures described in the chapters of this report are very similar to, or
even identical to those that were used in previous cycles of ICILS (see Fraillon et al., 2020; Fraillon
et al., 2014). Where this is the case, the descriptions of these processes and procedures from previous

2
ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
ICILS technical reports were used as the basis for the content in this report. These descriptions were
reviewed and, where necessary, revised. However, where it was deemed appropriate to do so, the text
from previous technical reports may have been included in this report without change. This text is not
cited, as it has been judged by the ICILS International Study Center (ISC), including the editors of this
report, to be an accurate description of what was completed in ICILS 2023.
1.2 Study overview
Study coordination and administration
International Study Center
ICILS 2023 was a large cross‐national research project in which a suite of instruments were first cen‐
trally developed to provide comparable data across countries, and then administered to representative
samples of students, teachers, ICT‐coordinators, and school principals within participating countries.
The corresponding data were then cleaned, coded, scored, analyzed, and reported.
The study administration was correspondingly established and managed with close coordination at the
international and national levels. The ICILS ISC was located at IEA, and national study centers were
located in each participating country.
The ISC comprises IEA staff, led by the ICILS International Study Director, with responsibility for the
development of the international instrument content used in the study, as well as oversight of the
operational procedures to be conducted within participating countries. The role of the ISC includes,
but is not limited to:
• Conceptual leadership of the study, including development of the ICILS assessment framework
and the ICILS international test and questionnaire instruments
• Operational leadership of the study, including international study coordination, and the devel‐
opment and oversight of operational procedures in countries including:
– Sampling of schools within countries and of participants within schools
– Instrument adaptation and translation
– Field operations including data collection within countries
– Quality assurance of the implementation of operational procedures within countries
– Scoring and coding selected student responses to test and questionnaire items
– Data management and adjudication
• Communication with, and support for participating countries, including development of interna‐
tional manuals and support materials, and provision of training
• Scaling and data analysis
• Dissemination of ICILS information, including reporting international results, their consequences,
and informing policy and practice with respect to the findings of ICILS
As ICILS is an IEA study, the study operations are based on and consistent with established IEA policies
and protocols. However, the ISC has the capacity, where appropriate, to tailor operational procedures
and practices to suit the specific needs of the study. The technical implementation of the study, includ‐
ing its adherence to IEA policies, protocols, and technical standards is overseen by the IEA Technical
Executive Group (TEG).
The ISC also operates in collaboration with external experts as required. Formally, one body, the Ques‐
tionnaire Development Group (QDG), was established to support development of the ICILS question‐
naire instruments (see Chapter 4).

INTRODUCTION
3
National study centers
Within each participating country, the national study center (hereafter referred to as national center)
is responsible for the coordination and implementation of the study. National centers are appointed
within each country according to their internal policies and procedures. This process is independent of
IEA and the ICILS ISC. The work of the national center in each country is led by the country‐appointed
national research coordinator1 (NRC). The NRC is the primary point of contact with the ISC and is
expected to manage ICILS‐related activities within the country.
National centers, under the leadership of NRCs and with the guidance and support of the ISC, com‐
plete the sampling, instrument preparation, and data collection activities listed above. NRCs, or their
delegates, are also invited to contribute to the development of the ICILS assessment framework and
instrument content in collaboration with the ISC. Many national centers also disseminate ICILS results
with a focus on their own national findings by authoring national reports and providing associated
advice relating to policy and practice in their own national contexts.
ICILS assessment framework
The contents and high‐level operational procedures of ICILS 2023 are explained and described in the
ICILS 2023 assessment framework (Fraillon & Rožman, 2024).
The framework includes:
• A detailed background and rationale for the study;
• The structure and content of the two ICILS achievement variables (CIL and CT);
• An explanation of the conceptual model underpinning the analytic relationship between contex‐
tual data collected in ICILS and student achievement;
• Details of the contextual data collected in ICILS; and
• Details of the student achievement test and all questionnaire instruments used in ICILS.
ICILS instruments
All ICILS instruments were delivered on computer. Three different delivery platforms were used, one
platform was used for the student test and questionnaire instruments; a second platform was used
to deliver the questionnaire instruments completed by staff in sampled schools; and a third platform
was used to deliver the questionnaire completed by staff in national centers (see Chapter 3 for more
details).
All ICILS instruments (excluding the national contexts survey—see below) were adapted and translated
into the target languages used in each participating country, with the express intention that the meaning
of each task was equivalent across all countries and languages. Each ICILS questionnaire (except the
national contexts survey) also included a small number of questions that were optional for national
centers to include or exclude in their questionnaires. National centers also had the opportunity to
include a small number of additional questions of particular national interest to their questionnaire
instruments. National centers were not permitted to add or remove tasks or task content from the
test instrument or instruments (CIL only or CIL and CT) used in their country. All decisions about
adaptations of student instrument content were completed in consultation with the ICILS ISC (see
Chapter 5 for more details about instrument adaptation and translation).
CIL test instrument
The CIL test instrument comprised seven 30‐minute test modules. Each student completed two mod‐
ules that were delivered in a fully balanced randomized design (see Chapter 2 for more details). Three
1 Countries may also appoint more than one person to the position of NRC.

4
ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
of the modules were newly developed for inclusion in ICILS 2023, two modules were first used in ICILS
2018 and two modules were first used in ICILS 2013 (and again used in ICILS 2018).
CT test instrument
The CT test instrument comprised four 25‐minute test modules. In countries where the CT interna‐
tional option was administered, each student completed two modules that were delivered in a fully
balanced randomized design (see Chapter 2 for more details). Two of the modules were newly devel‐
oped for inclusion in ICILS 2023 and two modules were first used in ICILS 2018.
International student questionnaire
All students in the sampled classes completed a 30‐minute international questionnaire.
International school staff questionnaires
Three separate questionnaires were developed to be completed by staff in sampled ICILS schools:
• A 30‐minute teacher questionnaire: This was designed to be completed by a random sample of
15 teachers of grade 8 students in each sampled school.
• A 20‐minute ICT coordinator questionnaire: This was designed to be completed by the desig‐
nated ICT coordinator in each sampled school.
• A 20‐minute principal questionnaire.
National contexts survey
ICILS national research coordinators (NRCs) oversaw completion of the national contexts survey (NCS)
within each participating country. This questionnaire included detailed information regarding the ed‐
ucation system, plans, and policies with respect to CIL and CT education within each country. It was
expected that NRCs would seek advice from relevant national experts or invite suitably qualified per‐
sonnel to provide answers for the questions in the NCS. The NCS was presented in English only and
any text responses were required to be provided in English.
ICILS participants
Education systems participating in 2023
The following 34 countries and one benchmarking participant took part in ICILS 2023.

Education systems participating in both 2023 and in previous cycles
Two countries, Germany and Korea (Rep. of), participated in all three cycles of ICILS, and met the nec‐
essary sample participation requirements within each cycle. In these two countries, valid comparisons
of students’ CIL achievement can be made across the three ICILS cycles.
Eight countries, Denmark, Finland, France, Italy, Kazakhstan, Luxembourg, Portugal, Uruguay, and the
benchmarking participant North Rhine‐Westphalia (Germany) participated in ICILS 2018 and 2023
and met the sample participation requirements within each cycle. In these eight countries and the
benchmarking participant, valid comparisons of students’ CIL achievement can be made between the
2018 and 2023 cycles.
Valid comparisons of student CT achievement between the 2018 and 2023 cycles can also be made
in all of these participants except Uruguay, where CT was not assessed in 2018.
A further five countries, Croatia, the Czech Republic, Norway, the Slovak Republic, and Slovenia par‐
ticipated in ICILS 2013 and 2023 and met the sample participation requirements within each cycle.
In these five countries, valid comparisons of students’ CIL achievement can be made between ICILS
2013 and ICILS 2023.
Population definitions and sampling
The samples were designed as two‐stage cluster samples. During the first stage of sampling, schools
with enrolled students at the target grade were randomly selected with a probability proportional to
size as measured by the number of students enrolled in a school. The numbers required in the sample
to achieve the necessary precision were estimated on the basis of national characteristics.
The ICILS student population was defined as students in grade 8 (typically about 14 years of age in
most countries), provided that the average age of students in this grade was at least 13.5 years at the
time of the assessment.

6
ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
Within each school, one class was randomly sampled from all the classes at the target grade. All
students within each sampled class were then invited to participate.
The ICILS teacher population was defined as consisting of all teachers teaching regular school subjects
to the students in the target grade at each sampled school. It included only those teachers who were
teaching the target grade during the testing period and who had been employed at the school since
the beginning of the school year.
In schools with more than 20 teachers at the target grade (grade 8 or equivalent), 15 teachers of these
teachers were to be selected at random to participate. In schools with 20 or fewer teachers at the
target grade, all teachers teaching the target grade were invited to participate.
The principal and the nominated ICT coordinator (either by title or designated by the school principal)
in each sampled school also were invited to participate.
ICILS 2023 collected data from 132,889 grade 8 (or equivalent) students from 5,299 schools across the
34 participating countries and one benchmarking participant. 132,652 students completed the CIL2
assessment and 85,240 completed the CT assessment. Data were collected from 60,835 teachers in
the sampled schools.
Complete details of the population definitions, sampling and participation are provided in Chapter 6
and Chapter 7.
Administration periods
The ICILS 2023 main survey data collection took place in the first half of 2023 in countries using a
Northern Hemisphere school calendar3 and in the second half of 2023 for participants using a Southern
Hemisphere school calendar.
1.3 Outline of this technical report
This introductory chapter is followed by 12 chapters. Chapters 2, 3, and 4, address the development
and content of the instruments and software systems that were used in the study. Chapter 2 focuses
on the tests of CIL and CT. Chapter 3 provides a high‐level description of the software systems used
across ICILS. Chapter 4 details the development of the questionnaires used in ICILS for gathering data
from students, teachers, principals, and school ICT coordinators. The chapter also provides an outline
of the development of the national contexts survey, completed by the national research coordinators.
Chapters 5 through 9 focus on the implementation of the survey in ICILS 2023. Chapter 5 describes
the translation procedures and national adaptations used in ICILS. Chapter 6 details the sampling de‐
sign and implementation processes. The outcomes of these processes, including the establishment
of sampling weights and the achieved participation rates are described in Chapter 7. Chapter 8 de‐
scribes the field operations procedures that underpinned data collection in ICILS 2023, and Chapter 9
describes the quality control procedures used to collect information and report on the success of the
implementation of field operations procedures within and across countries.
Chapters 10, 11, 12, and 13 address data management, analysis and reporting. Chapter 10 describes
the data‐management processes that resulted in the creation of the ICILS IDB. Chapter 11 details the
scaling procedures for the tests of CIL and CT and Chapter 12 describes the scaling procedures for the
questionnaire items (student, teacher, principal, and ICT coordinator questionnaires). The final chapter,
Chapter 13, presents an account of the analyses that underpinned the results reported in the ICILS
2023 international report (Fraillon, 2024).
2 Slightly fewer students completed the core CIL assessment than provided data because a small number of students
completed the CT assessment without having completed the CIL assessment.
3 Except Romania where data collection took place in the second half of 2023.

Chapter 2:
ICILS 2023 test development
Daniel Duckworth and Julian Fraillon
2.1 Introduction
This chapter provides a detailed description of the test development process and review procedures,
as well as the test design implemented for the ICILS 2023 field trial and main survey. The processes
are relevant for the tests of both computer and information literacy (CIL) and computational thinking
(CT) and are consequently described together. Where relevant, details of the content of each test are
described separately.
The development of new content for the ICILS 2023 test instruments occurred over a 24‐month
period, from January 2020 to December 2022. The majority of this work was conducted by the
International Study Center (ISC) at IEA, in collaboration with National Research Coordinators (NRCs)
and other research partners. All participating students completed the test of CIL. Additionally, the test
of CT was administered to students in countries that chose to participate in this optional assessment.
2.2 Terminology
This chapter refers to key terms related to the ICILS assessments of CIL and CT. The definitions and
relationships between these terms are provided below, with examples to illustrate their application.
• Modules: A module is a set of assessment tasks centered around an authentic theme, structured
in a linear narrative. For example, in the module Breathing, students managed files and collected
and evaluated information to create a presentation explaining the process of breathing to eight‐
or nine‐year‐old students.
• Tasks: A task refers to an individual assessment activity presented to students. Each task includes
stimulus material, instructions, and may include actions required to achieve an objective or a
question to answer. For example, in task two of the Breathing module, the instructions directed
students to save a new presentation as ‘Breathing,’ which required them to use the ‘Save as’
function (see Duckworth, 2024a, p. 301).
• Scoring variables: A scoring variable captures the codified student response to a task. The struc‐
ture of scoring variables varies by task type:
– Simple tasks (Duckworth & Fraillon, 2024b, p. 62) typically use a single scoring variable per
task, indicating whether the response is correct or incorrect.
– Complex tasks (Duckworth & Fraillon, 2024b, p. 66) often require multiple scoring vari‐
ables. For example, in the Breathing authoring task where students create a presentation,
scoring variables measure content accuracy, design quality, and organization separately
(see Appendix C).
– Composite variables: Some scoring variables are derived from multiple component vari‐
ables. For instance, in task four of the module School trip (Duckworth, 2024a, p. 312),
students were required to enter a specific value into a specific cell in a spreadsheet. One
component variable recorded whether the correct value was entered into any cell. A sec‐
ond component variable recorded whether any value was entered into the correct cell (see
Appendix C). These details were combined into a composite variable representing a single
score for the task (see Chapter 11). A full credit score of 2 was assigned if both compo‐
nent variables reflected the completed actions, a partial credit score of 1 was assigned if

10
ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
either one of the component variables reflected the completed actions, and a score of 0
was assigned if neither component variable reflected the completed actions.
• Score points: Score points define the possible values assigned to a scoring variable. The number
of score points varies by task type:
– Binary scoring (e.g., correct = 1, incorrect = 0) applies to simple tasks.
– Partial credit scoring applies to more complex tasks. For example, one of the scoring vari‐
ables associated with an authoring task may assign a score of 2 for a full credit response,
1 for a partial credit response, and 0 for an incorrect response.
• Items: An item represents a unit of measurement derived from scored student responses. Items
serve as the basis for constructing the CIL and CT achievement scales (see Chapter 11).
2.3 Test scope and format
ICILS assessment framework
The ICILS student tests of CIL and CT were developed with reference to the ICILS assessment frame‐
work.4 According to the framework, CIL is defined as an “individual’s ability to use computers to inves‐
tigate, create, and communicate in order to participate effectively at home, at school, in the workplace,
and in society” (Fraillon & Duckworth, 2024, p. 26). CT is defined as the “ability to recognize aspects
of real‐world problems which are appropriate for computational formulation and to evaluate and de‐
velop algorithmic solutions to those problems so that the solutions could be operationalized with a
computer” (Duckworth & Fraillon, 2024a, p. 38).
The framework describes CIL and CT as comprising strands, each of which is specified in terms of
several aspects. A strand refers to the overarching conceptual category for framing the skills and
knowledge addressed by the test instruments. An aspect refers to the specific content category within
a strand. This organizational structure was established to support users to access and understand
breadth and scope of the content of each construct. Establishment of the strands and aspects in each
of CIL and CT was based on the substantive content of each domain and did not presuppose that
either construct would comprise more than one measurement dimension. In ICILS 2013 and ICILS
2018, multidimensional IRT modeling was used to examine the potential dimensionality of CIL, with
ICILS 2018 also assessing the dimensionality of CT. In ICILS 2023, the dimensionality of CIL was re‐
evaluated, and additional analyses were conducted for CT (see Chapter 11). In each case the latent
correlations between potential sub‐dimensions were very high and each of CIL and CT were reported
in ICILS as unidimensional achievement constructs (Fraillon et al., 2015, 2020).
The following lists outline the strands and corresponding aspects of the CIL and CT constructs. Full
details of the constructs can be found in the ICILS 2023 assessment framework (Fraillon & Rožman,
2024).
The CIL construct
• Strand 1: Understanding computer use, comprising two aspects:
– Aspect 1.1: Foundations of computer use
– Aspect 1.2: Computer use conventions
• Strand 2: Gathering information, comprising two aspects:
– Aspect 2.1: Accessing and evaluating information
– Aspect 2.2: Managing information
• Strand 3: Producing information, comprising two aspects:
4 The framework can be downloaded from: https://www.springer.com/gp/book/9783030193881

ICILS 2023 TEST DEVELOPMENT
11
– Aspect 3.1: Transforming information
– Aspect 3.2: Creating information
• Strand 4: Digital communication, comprising two aspects:
– Aspect 4.1: Sharing information
– Aspect 4.2: Using information responsibly and safely
The CT construct
• Strand 1: Conceptualizing problems, comprising three aspects:
– Aspect 1.1: Knowing about and understanding digital systems
– Aspect 1.2: Formulating and analyzing problems
– Aspect 1.3: Collecting and representing relevant data
• Strand 2: Operationalizing solutions, comprising two aspects:
– Aspect 2.1: Planning and evaluating solutions
– Aspect 2.2: Developing algorithms, programs, and interfaces
The ICILS test instruments
The CIL test instrument
The ICILS main survey CIL test instrument comprised seven modules, each designed to be completed in
30 minutes. Each module has a series of small self‐contained tasks, designed to be able to be completed
quickly (usually in less than one minute), and a large authoring task designed to be completed in 10
to 15 minutes. A module presents the small tasks as a combination of skill execution and information
management activities that students must complete in preparation for the large task. Four of the
modules were secure trend modules, two from ICILS 2013 and two from ICILS 2018, which provided
a basis for reporting all CIL data collected in ICILS 2023 on the ICILS CIL achievement scale established
in 2013. The remaining three modules were newly developed specifically for ICILS 2023. Each student
completed a randomly assigned pair of CIL test modules out of the seven available modules in a fully
balanced rotational design.
At the start of each module, students were presented with an overview of the theme, the purpose of
the tasks, and a basic description of the large task they would complete. Students were required to
complete the tasks in the given sequence and could not revisit any tasks once completed. Table 2.1
provides a summary of the seven CIL test modules.

The CT test instrument
The ICILS main survey CT test instrument consisted of four modules, each designed to be completed
in 25 minutes. After completing the CIL test and the student questionnaire, each student completed
a randomly assigned pair of CT modules out of the four modules in a fully balanced rotational design.
Two of these modules were secure trend modules from ICILS 2018, while the remaining two were
newly developed for ICILS 2023.
Like the CIL assessment, each CT test module was organized in a linear narrative structure and re‐
flected a central theme. However, unlike the CIL modules, the CT modules did not culminate in a large
authoring task. Instead, each module consisted of a series of tasks focused on the processes of plan‐
ning and executing computer‐based solutions to real‐world problems. Table 2.2 provides a summary

of the four CT test modules.

The ICILS task types
CIL task types
The ICILS CIL test modules included three broad categories of tasks described below:
• Information‐based response tasks: These tasks make use of the “digital interface to deliver
pencil‐and‐paper style questions in a slightly richer format than traditional paper‐based meth‐
ods” (Duckworth & Fraillon, 2024b, p. 46). The response formats for these tasks can vary (e.g.,
multiple choice, short constructed response, drag and drop), the stimulus material for these
tasks is usually static or with minimal interactivity and the purpose of these tasks is to “capture
evidence of students’ knowledge and understanding of CIL independently of students using any‐
thing beyond the most basic skills required to record a response” (Duckworth & Fraillon, 2024b,
p. 62).
• Skills tasks: In these tasks students “interact with functional simulations of generic software or
universal applications to accomplish a task” (Duckworth & Fraillon, 2024b, p. 66). The number
of steps required to complete a skills task and the number of different correct methods for
executing a task varies across skills tasks. Linear skills tasks require students to execute one
or more commands in a given sequence (such as copy and paste) whereas nonlinear skills tasks
require students to execute a function involving more than one sub‐command without a single
given sequence (such as using the filter functions in an web‐based table to locate information).
The linear skills tasks test students’ point‐in‐time knowledge of how to execute a given simple
skill. Students receive feedback from the assessment system that they have completed an action,
without seeing the result of their action. In linear skills tasks, students have one chance to re‐
complete an action (without penalty) if they wish. The nonlinear skills tasks respond to students
actions as if they are operating in a live application environment. Students therefore must decide
when they believe they have completed each specified non‐linear skills task and prompt the
system to progress to the next task. Before students progress to the next task, they have the
opportunity to check and alter their response to a non‐linear skills task.

14
ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
• Authoring tasks: These tasks “require students to alter and create information products using
authentic computer software applications” (Duckworth & Fraillon, 2024b, p. 69). The complex‐
ity of authoring tasks varies according to the number of different applications students were
required to use, the degree of openness or constraint placed on the range of student responses
to the task, and the amount of information students were required to evaluate and make use of
when altering or creating information products. The authoring tasks were most commonly the
large task within each module and typically were scored by human scorers using multiple analytic
criteria.
CT task types
Like the CIL test modules, the CT test modules contain information‐based response tasks and skills
tasks. However, in addition to these, the CT test modules include task types that are unique to the
assessment of CT.
The three CT‐specific task types are described below:
• Nonlinear systems transfer tasks: These tasks require students to “interpret, transfer, and adapt
algorithmic information, so that the results of applying algorithmic instructions can be visually
displayed” (Duckworth & Fraillon, 2024b, p. 71). The response formats for these tasks can vary
but what they have in common is that students are required to make connections between a
visual representation of an algorithmic sequence and the steps of the sequence described as
text.
• Simulation tasks: These tasks “require students to set parameters, run a simulation to collect
data, and interpret the data to answer a research question” (Duckworth & Fraillon, 2024b, p. 72),
typically as part of developing an understanding of real‐world problems or to evaluate solutions
to problems.
• Block‐based coding tasks: These tasks require students to manipulate visual code blocks that
can be used to execute a range of actions depicted in a visual display. Students could also run
the code at any time and view the execution of code blocks and the corresponding visual display.
They could also separately reset both the visual display and the code blocks/work space to their
original states. There were two main forms of visual coding tasks:
– Algorithm construction tasks “require students to develop their own solution to a problem
by iteratively adding code blocks to the work space and executing the algorithm to see the
results” (Duckworth & Fraillon, 2024b, p. 74).
– Algorithm debugging tasks “require students to modify an existing algorithm (changing the
structure of the code and parameters of the code blocks in the work space) to solve the
problem presented by the task instructions” (Duckworth & Fraillon, 2024b, p. 74).
Further details of the ICILS CIL and CT test modules are presented in Chapter 5 of the ICILS 2023
assessment framework (Duckworth & Fraillon, 2024b) and detailed descriptions of tasks from released
CIL and CT modules are presented in Appendix E and Appendix F of the ICILS 2023 international
report (Duckworth, 2024a, 2024b).

2.4 Test‐development process
The test‐development process involved a series of stages that applied to both the CIL and CT test in‐
struments. While these stages were generally sequential, the iterative and collaborative nature of the
process meant that some materials were reviewed and revised multiple times within certain stages. In
summary, the ISC developed item materials, occasionally incorporating suggestions from NRCs, which
were then reviewed by NRCs and subsequently revised by the ISC. This process was sometimes re‐
peated. Table 2.3 provides an overview of the test development processes and timeline.
The NRC meetings and scorer training meetings were critical points in the test‐development process.
The first meeting of NRCs coincided with the early phases of the COVID‐19 pandemic. As a conse‐
quence of the pandemic, this meeting, as well as the second and third NRC meetings and the field trial
scorer training meetings were all conducted online rather than in‐person.

Activity
In ICILS, each task consists of several components: the stimulus materials presented to students, the
questions or instructions given, the programmed behavior of the computer delivery system in response
to students’ actions, and the scoring logic. Tasks can be scored by humans, according to the scoring
guides, or automatically, based on machine scoring specifications. The test development and review
process covered all these elements within the ICILS modules.
The following lists present the main review questions used to guide reviews of CIL and CT tasks at all
stages of the development process, from initial task development through to selection and finalization
of field trial and main survey task content.
• Content validity
– How does the material relate to the ICILS test specifications?
– Do the tasks test the construct (CIL or CT) described in the assessment framework?
– Do the tasks relate to content at the core of the aspects of the assessment framework or
focus on trivial side issues?
– How would the test content stand up to broader expert and public scrutiny?
• Clarity and fairness
– Are the tasks and stimulus material coherent, unambiguous, and clear?
– Are the modules and tasks interesting, worthwhile, and relevant?
– Do the tasks assume prior knowledge and, if so, was this assumed to be acceptable or part
of what the test intended to measure?
– Is the reading load as low as possible without compromising the real‐world relevance and
validity of the tasks?
– Are there idioms or syntactic structures that may prove difficult to translate into other
languages?

18
ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
– Do the constructed‐response items and the large‐task information provide clear guidance
to students about what was expected in response to the items and tasks?
• Test‐takers
– Does the content of the modules and tasks match the expected range of ability levels, age,
and maturity of the ICILS target population?
– Does the material appear to be cross‐culturally relevant and sensitive?
– Are specific items or tasks likely to be easier or harder for certain subgroups in the target
population for reasons other than differences in the ability measured by the test?
• Format and scoring
– Is the proposed format the most suitable for the framework content being assessed by each
task?
– Is the key5 clearly correct and representing the ‘best’ answer to the question as asked?
– Are the distractors6 plausible but also irrefutably and unequivocally incorrect?
– Do the scoring criteria for the large tasks assess the essential characteristics of task com‐
pletion?
– Are there different approaches to provide answers with the same score, and did they rep‐
resent equivalent or different levels of proficiency?
– Is the proposed scoring consistent with the underlying ability measured by the test, and
would test respondents with higher ability levels always score better than those with lower
ones?
– Are there other kinds of answers that have not been anticipated in the scoring guides (e.g.,
any that do not fall within the “correct” answer category description, but appear to be
equally correct)?
– Are the scoring criteria sufficient for scorers and do they clearly distinguish the different
levels of performance?
Drafting and review of preliminary module ideas
Before the first meeting of the ICILS 2023 NRCs, the ISC developed preliminary concepts for the
CIL and CT modules to be presented at the meeting. This meeting involved reflecting on the CIL
and CT tests from ICILS 2018, discussing preliminary module concepts for inclusion in ICILS 2023,
and considering the incorporation of process data into ICILS 2023. NRCs were invited to discuss and
propose new ideas for module themes. The feedback on the preliminary content and suggested themes
served as a foundation for subsequent module development.
Development of draft module storyboards
After the first NRC meeting, the ISC developed storyboards for 10 new modules (five for each of CIL
and CT). The storyboards were created as interactive Adobe XD prototypes of each task in sequence.
The tasks were presented in sequence so that those viewing the presentation could see the narrative
sequence of tasks in the module.
Each Adobe XD layout contained the stimulus material with key elements designed to be interactive,
together with the task instructions or question that students would be required to respond to. Each
storyboard was accompanied by a set of implementation notes for each task or question. The notes
elaborated the interactive elements and described the planned functionality/behavior of each task and
provided instructions on how the task was to be scored. Instructions were provided not only for the
human‐scored tasks but also for the tasks that would be scored automatically by the computer system.
5 The correct answer to a multiple‐choice question.
6 The incorrect options to a multiple‐choice question.

ICILS 2023 TEST DEVELOPMENT
19
Presentation of draft module storyboards
The second meeting of NRCs took place in November 2020 and was conducted online. As part of this
meeting, the ISC presented the storyboards and interactive prototypes for consideration. When pre‐
senting the storyboards, NRCs made recommendations relating to the modules. The NRCs’ feedback
informed further revision of the module storyboards.
Web‐based prioritization process
Following the second NRC meeting, a web‐based prioritization process was conducted to rank the
proposed CIL and CT modules. NRCs were provided with two forms, each listing five CIL and five
CT modules, and were asked to rank them in order of preference. The aggregated rankings from all
participating NRCs were used by the ISC to prioritize the most highly ranked modules for further
development. As a result, seven module storyboards, three CIL and four CT, were selected for further
development and refinement. Feedback from the second NRC meeting was incorporated into the
refinement of these storyboards.
Interim review of prioritized module storyboards
After refining the prioritized module storyboards based on feedback from the second NRC meeting,
the ISC held an online check‐in meeting in April 2021. During this meeting, revisions to the tasks for
the three CIL and four CT modules were presented.
Final review of proposed module storyboards
Following the online check‐in meeting, the revised draft storyboards were presented at the third NRC
meeting for final review. Feedback from this meeting was used to finalize the selection of modules
for implementation in the online test delivery system for a pilot study and field trial, resulting in the
decision to proceed with the three new CIL modules and four new CT modules.
Implementation of the final storyboards in field trial test delivery system and CT pilot study
The finalized storyboards were provided to RM Results for implementation into the online test delivery
system. This process served two main purposes: (1) it allowed for the review and refinement of the
draft modules based on their interactive features, and (2) it enabled the testing and enhancement of
the test delivery system. A small number of countries participated in a CT pilot study from 24 May
to 6 June, 2021. The pilot helped identify issues with the test delivery system and gathered student
feedback on the difficulty of the CT tasks. This feedback, along with observations from the ISC, was
presented to NRCs during the third NRC meeting.
Field‐trial scorer training
National center representatives attended an online international scorer training meeting7 held before
the field trial. These representatives subsequently trained the national center staff in charge of scoring
student responses in their respective countries. Feedback from the scorer training process led to
refinements to the scoring guides.
Field trial analysis review and selection of items for the main survey
Field trial data were analyzed to assess the measurement properties of the ICILS test items at both
the international and national levels. Based on this analysis, the ISC recommended which modules and
tasks should be included in the main survey instrument. These recommendations were discussed with
NRCs at the fourth ICILS NRC meeting, where minor refinements were suggested for several tasks.
The NRCs strongly recommended retaining all three new CIL test modules, and two of the four CT
modules from the field trial for the main survey.
7 Separate online sessions were offered for the Northern and Southern Hemisphere countries.

20
ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
Post‐field trial revision
Minor modifications were made to a small number of tasks and the functionality of all tasks was further
reviewed and refined. The main survey instruments, comprising seven CIL test modules (four trend
modules, three newly developed modules) and four CT test modules (two trend modules, two newly
developed modules), were then finalized.
Implementation of the final storyboards in the main survey online delivery system
Following the field trial, the module content was provided to RM Results to be authored into the main
survey test delivery system. Further review and refinement of the content and function of the modules
was conducted in this system.
Main survey scorer training
The main survey international scorer training meeting provided a final opportunity to reflect on the
experience of scoring the field trial responses and to further review the scoring guides. The meeting
was again attended by national center representatives who were responsible for training the national
center staff in charge of scoring student responses in their respective countries. Feedback from the
second international scorer training meeting along with student achievement data and the reported
experiences of scorers during the field trial prompted further refinements to the scoring guides.
2.5 Field trial test design and content
The ICILS 2023 field trial tests of CIL and CT included both newly developed material and trend material
which served two critical methodological purposes: equating new items with trend items to maintain
scale continuity and joint analysis of item fit for new and trend items.
One of the primary objectives of ICILS is to report trends over time in CIL and CT. To achieve this, new
test items introduced in ICILS 2023 must be linked to the existing CIL and CT scales used in previous
study cycles. By including trend items (items that appeared in previous cycles) alongside new items, it
is possible to estimate the difficulty of the new items relative to the established scale. This process,
known as item equating, ensures that the measurement of student proficiency remains comparable
across ICILS cycles.
Including trend material also allows for a more reliable psychometric evaluation of new items. In field
trials, item fit analysis assesses whether test items function as expected within the measurement model.
If only new items were analyzed, the results might be skewed due to uncertainty about the scale
properties of entirely new content. However, by evaluating new and trend items together, we can
assess how well the new items align with the CIL and CT constructs. Poorly fitting new items can then
be identified and revised or excluded from the main study, ensuring the validity and reliability of the
assessment.
Thus, trend items serve as anchors that enable both scale equating and robust psychometric analysis,
ensuring that ICILS 2023 maintains consistency with previous cycles while introducing new assessment
content effectively.
Test design
The CIL field trial test instrument included six test modules,8 yielding 160 score points from 112 scor‐
ing variables (see Table 2.7).9 Thirty‐two percent of the CIL scoring variables were associated with
information‐based response tasks, 17 percent with skills tasks, and 51 percent with authoring tasks
(see Table 2.4).
8 One trend module (School trip) was excluded from the field trial.
9 All authoring tasks and a small number of skills tasks were assessed using more than one criterion, with each criterion
constituting a scoring variable.

A balanced complete block design was used for each of the CIL and CT tests. Each student completed
a randomly assigned pair of CIL modules from the available six. In countries participating in the CT
international option, students also completed a randomly assigned pair of CT modules from the six CT
options. This design resulted in 30 possible combinations of CIL module pairs and 30 CT module pairs
(see Table 2.6).
In countries administering only the CIL assessment, students were assigned one of 30 possible CIL
module combinations, followed by one of two versions of the student questionnaire (see Chapter 4 for
a description of the questionnaire development process). In countries participating in the CT option,
students first completed one of the CIL permutations, then one of two questionnaire versions, and
finally one of the 30 CT permutations.
A total of 120 different test forms were created, consisting of 30 CIL permutations, 30 CT permu‐
tations, and two versions of the student questionnaire. Each CIL and CT module appeared an equal
number of times in the first and second module positions across the test forms, and each module was
paired equally with the others. However, while all CIL were fully administered across all field trial coun‐
tries, they were not fully balanced with the CT modules in terms of pairing frequency. This means that
each CIL module did not appear an equal number of times with each CT module. This partial balanc‐
ing was deemed sufficient because the tests of CIL and CT assessed different constructs, with both
completed by only a subset countries, and students who completed both tests completed the ques‐
tionnaire and had a break between the test of CIL and CT. The approach allowed for a manageable
number of test forms in cases where both CIL and CT were administered.

Field trial coverage of the CIL and CT constructs
All field trial tasks were designed and mapped according to the CIL and CT constructs. As stated in
the ICILS assessment framework, “The ICILS tasks are designed to collect information about specific
aspects of the relevant construct (CIL or CT), and each module typically includes content that addresses
most, if not all, aspects of the construct. However, the test design of ICILS does not require equal
representation of all aspects of the CIL and CT constructs” (Duckworth & Fraillon, 2024b, p. 76).
The CIL field trial instruments represented each strand of the CIL construct with varying levels of
emphasis: 14 percent of the scoring variables corresponded to CIL Strand 1, 27 percent to CIL Strand
2, 39 percent to CIL Strand 3, and 20 percent to CIL Strand 4 (see Table 2.7). These proportions
reflected the estimated time students were expected to spend on each CIL strand. CIL Aspects 2.1,
3.1, and 3.2 were assessed mainly through large tasks located at the end of each CIL module, with
students expected to spend about two‐thirds of their working time on these tasks. Overall, the test
design adequately represented the four CIL strands.

Selection of CIL and CT test content for main survey
As stated previously, the field trial data were used to investigate the measurement properties of the
ICILS test items, and ISC researchers made recommendations on which modules and tasks should be
included in the main survey instrument. Chapter 11 of this report describes the analysis procedures
used to review the measurement properties. Following the recommendation from NRCs, the ISC de‐
cided to retain the three new CIL test modules and two of the four CT test modules for use in the main
survey.
Also as mentioned previously, ISC researchers recommended refinements to a small number of tasks
and to the scoring of some tasks. A task was only refined when data provided clear evidence of a
problem with the task and if there was strong agreement that the refinement would very likely improve
the task’s measurement properties.
2.6 Main survey test design and content
Test design
The CIL main survey test instrument consisted of seven test modules (four trend modules and three
new modules) with a total of 55 (see Table 2.12) tasks, yielding data from 108 scoring variables (see Ta‐
ble 2.9). Overall, 25 percent of the scoring variables were associated with information‐based response
tasks, 20 percent with skills task, and 55 percent with authoring tasks.
Some of these tasks generated several score points based on the criteria that were applied to the large
tasks. The scoring variables shown in Table 2.9 are those that were used in the scaling and analysis of
the ICILS 2023 main survey CIL test data.
The CT main survey test instrument consisted of four test modules (two trend modules and two new
modules) with a total of 31 tasks (see Table 2.13), yielding data from 31 scoring variables (see Ta‐
ble 2.10). The CT test emphasized block‐based coding with 15 of the 31 scoring variables being
associated with block‐based coding tasks (see Table 2.10).
As was used in the field trial, the test modules were allocated using a complete balanced block design
for each of CIL and CT. Table 2.11 shows this design for the main survey. As described for the field
trial, the term test form refers to each combination of modules used in the main survey.

2.7 Released test modules
Two CIL (Breathing, School trip) and two CT (Automated bus, Farm drone) modules (see Table 2.1 and
Table 2.2), have been released since publication of the ICILS international report (Fraillon, 2024).
Online demonstration versions of each of these four modules can be accessed at: https://www.iea.nl/
icils2023‐assessment
Detailed descriptions of these tasks are provided in Appendix E and Appendix F of the ICILS 2023
international report (Duckworth, 2024a, 2024b).
Appendix C and Appendix D provide summary information about the human and machine scoring rules
used to score students responses for all released CIL and CT tasks. In addition to the scoring rules, an
explanation of the scoring methods used for the Farm drone module are provided in Appendix D.
See Appendix G for information about how the scoring variables were recoded during scaling and
analysis.

Chapter 3:
ICILS survey operations software
Sebastian Meyer and Daniel Duckworth
3.1 Introduction
This chapter outlines the key features of the survey operations software employed in ICILS 2023
and describes selected challenges associated with using the software in a cross‐national, large‐scale
assessment. The chapter begins by describing the individual survey operations software applications
and systems, as well as their integration with other technical systems used in survey operations. Further
details of specific procedures and related topics are discussed in the following chapters: assessment
and test design in Chapter 2, translation and adaptation in Chapter 5, field operations in Chapter 8,
data flow and integration in Chapter 10, and scaling and analysis of the test materials in Chapter 11.
3.2 Survey operations software applications and systems
IEA authored applications
IEA‐developed survey operations software applications and systems used in ICILS 2023 included the
following:
• Windows Within‐School Sampling Software (WinW3S): This application helps countries manage
within‐school sampling and test administration procedures.
• Online Survey System (OSS): This system supports the translation, adaptation, and delivery of
computer‐based questionnaire materials.
Both WinW3S and OSS were customized for use in ICILS 2023 survey operations.
Use of WinW3S was mandatory in all participating countries, primarily because the application ensures
adherence to the within‐school teacher and student sampling procedures stipulated by the study’s
design. All countries were required to import the school sample drawn by IEA into WinW3S and list
all eligible teachers and target grade classes. The software then randomly selected the teacher and
class samples within each school. Any modifications to the sample selected by the application were
strictly prohibited. However, exceptions were allowed in cases of listing errors that were identified
after school sample selection, but these had to be discussed with the IEA sampling team. All changes
were documented in the underlying database. After survey administration, countries were required to
enter participation data for all sampled teachers and students within selected classes. The participating
countries were required to provide the database containing all sampling and tracking information to
IEA after data collection. For more information on the within‐school sampling design, see Chapter 6.
Data at the school (principal and ICT coordinator) and teacher levels were collected using the OSS
across all participating countries. Designed as a comprehensive solution for survey instrument author‐
ing, data collection, and monitoring, the OSS is structured into three distinct components to accom‐
modate various usage scenarios. The Designer component facilitated the creation, deletion, disabling,
and editing of survey elements (e.g., questions and response categories) and their properties. It al‐
lowed for the translation of all text passages from the questionnaires, as well as additional system
texts, ensuring consistency across languages. The Web component provided respondents with access
to questionnaires in an HTML format, allowing completion directly within standard internet browsers.
Lastly, the Monitor component, a web‐based tool, enabled national centers to track participation in
real time, facilitating follow‐ups with schools when questionnaires were incomplete or missing.

32
ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
Commercially provided applications
In addition to the survey operations software provided by IEA, ICILS 2023 also employed the RM
Assessment Master (AM) software system. The AM software system facilitated the production and
delivery of the computer‐based assessment content, as well as the collection of student data and the
scoring of student test response data. The AM modules used in ICILS 2023 included the following:
• AM Manager: An administration module for managing countries, schools, students, and users;
• AM Designer: An item authoring, test form design and translation module;
• AM Examiner: The instrument delivery engine; and
• AM Marker: A scoring module.
3.3 Procedures and software user manuals
A comprehensive series of manuals, known as Survey Operations Procedures (SOP) manuals, guides
the work of national center staff during IEA studies. These SOP manuals, developed through close
collaboration between IEA and the International Study Center (ISC) team, contain detailed instructions
and guidelines for the use of essential software systems, including WinW3S and OSS. Each manual
is specifically tailored to meet the needs of the project, ensuring that national center staff can effi‐
ciently manage operations and maintain consistency across all participating countries (see Chapter 8
for details).
For ICILS 2023, the following software user manuals were incorporated into the SOP materials:
• IEA WinW3S Manual: This manual provided detailed guidance on using the IEA Windows Within‐
School Sampling Software. It outlined procedures for tracking student and teacher participation,
completing sampling forms, and coordinating test sessions at the school level.
• IEA OSS Manual: The IEA Online Survey System manual supported national centers in adapting
questionnaire materials, entering translations, and managing the online survey administration.
Detailed steps for checking translations, adaptation tracking, and finalizing questionnaires for
field operations were provided.
• Delivery Engine Module (AM Examiner): For ICILS 2023, the manuals related to the delivery engine
module (AM Examiner) were included within the instructions provided to school coordinators and
test administrators. These supplementary manuals provided step‐by‐step guidance on preparing
for the ICILS compatibility checks to be run in schools so that the computers intended to be used
for the IEA ICILS 2023 main survey were suitable and set up properly to run the student assess‐
ment, setting up computers for running the tests, administering the tests, and troubleshooting
issues during test sessions. They also included protocols for managing the different test delivery
methods (using the internet, USB, or a local server).
• RM AM Manual: The RM AM manual supported national centers in translating and reporting
layout issues for the test instruments, implementing feedback from translation and layout ver‐
ification, and preparing and administering the study instruments. A dedicated section in the
manual was provided for the translation module within the AM system. This section guided na‐
tional center staff through the translation and adaptation of student test items, ensuring that
the translated content was accurate and aligned with the international source version. The AM
Manager module enabled the management of student test sessions.
• Scoring Manual (AM Marker): This module enabled the assignment of scoring tasks and scoring
student responses.
These manuals were reviewed after the field trial data collection and updated to incorporate feedback
from national centers.

ICILS SURVEY OPERATIONS SOFTWARE
33
3.4 ICILS 2023 system architecture
ICILS 2023 employed a combination of survey operations software applications and systems to support
the study’s preparation, administration, and post‐processing phases. Table 3.1 provides an overview
of the applications and systems with their associated instrument development, delivery, and data col‐
lection processes. The table shows how these components interfaced and interacted.
