3.5 Instrument preparation
The student instruments (test and questionnaire) were authored, adapted and translated in the AM De‐
signer module. The teacher, ICT coordinator and principal questionnaires were authored, and adapted
and translated text was entered using the IEA OSS. In this section, the procedures used for each soft‐
ware system are described separately. The key for using two different systems is that questionnaires
focus on survey‐based self‐reported data collection, while student assessments require an environ‐
ment capable of delivering structured performance‐based tasks with automated scoring. Using OSS
for questionnaires and Assessment Master for student instruments allowed ICILS to leverage the most
suitable platform for each type of data collection, ensuring both data quality and international compa‐
rability.

34
ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
Authoring
Student instruments
The ICILS 2023 student questionnaire was authored directly in the RM AM system using the authoring
tools within the AM Designer module. No new development was required to support the questionnaire
development and delivery.
The development of the features within the test delivery platform specific to the ICILS 2023 student
test occurred in parallel with the test development process (see Chapter 2 for details on test devel‐
opment). The six trend test modules—two computer and information literacy (CIL) from ICILS 2013,
two CIL and two computational thinking (CT) modules from ICILS 2018—were already available and
largely ready to use. However, to accommodate changes in the delivery system, RM Results updated
certain backend functionalities in the AM Examiner module. These updates ensured that the software
continued to function effectively and did not alter the user experience of the trend modules compared
to previous cycles, thus ensuring consistency for participating students across cycles.
For ICILS 2023, three new CIL modules and two new CT modules were developed. After the initial
storyboards for these new modules were completed, they were sent to RM Results for implementation
into the delivery platform. This process involved multiple iterations to review, refine, and test the
functional aspects of each task to ensure that the intended tasks were fully operational.
Principal, ICT coordinator, and teacher questionnaires
The ICILS 2023 principal, ICT coordinator, and teacher questionnaires were authored directly in the IEA
OSS system using the authoring tools within the OSS Designer module. The OSS Designer provided
a flexible framework for constructing a variety of item types and questionnaire structures tailored to
the different respondent groups.
The international versions of the questionnaires were developed by the ICILS ISC and implemented into
the OSS platform without requiring additional software development. The system allowed for seamless
integration of the finalized questionnaire content into a survey environment suitable for previewing and
national adaptation.
Once the questionnaires were authored, IEA compiled an ICILS 2023‐specific OSS software package
containing the finalized international versions. This package was distributed to national centers, where
it could be installed on local systems. The installation included a fully functional local web server, en‐
abling national center staff to input verified translations and apply pre‐approved national adaptations.
This setup allowed users to preview the questionnaires in an environment closely resembling the actual
delivery system, supporting thorough review and quality assurance prior to final deployment.
Adaptation
Two types of adaptation were possible for the ICILS instruments.
Structural adaptations were possible for the questionnaires but not for the student test. Structural
adaptations include country‐specific alterations to the structure of a question. These could be, for ex‐
ample, the addition of some national items within an international question, the addition of an entirely
new question, or creation of additional response categories (that are sub‐categories of the original
international response category).
Language adaptations involved the replacement of specific words or phrases in an instrument with
locally equivalent terms. This differs from translation in that, in some instances, a direct translation of
a word or phrase in the source language may not convey the intended meaning in the target language.
Terms or phrases requiring language adaptation were shown in the instruments in square brackets [].
Once language adaptations requested by a country were approved by the ISC, they were included. The
national center team entered the text into the translated version of their national instrument using the
same method as entering all other translated text (see Chapter 5 for more details on the adaptation
procedures).

ICILS SURVEY OPERATIONS SOFTWARE
35
Student instruments
Structural adaptations to the national version of the student questionnaire had to be custom‐coded
into the instrument for each country by the ISC staff. Once a country’s requested adaptations were
approved by the ISC, the ISC team implemented them in AM Designer. For each adaptation, the original
questionnaire item was copied into a national variable, where the agreed‐upon structural changes were
applied. This approach clearly flagged the item as a nationally adapted version. The original item was
then hidden from the administered questionnaire. After implementation, national centers reviewed
and verified the adaptations to ensure accuracy and alignment with their national requirements.
Principal, ICT coordinator, and teacher questionnaires
Structural adaptations to the national versions of the principal, ICT coordinator, and teacher ques‐
tionnaires had to be implemented by national center staff in accordance with the guidelines outlined
in the IEA OSS manual. The system strictly prohibited structural modifications to any existing inter‐
national question or question part. To implement adaptations, users were required to create a copy
of the relevant question, apply the necessary structural changes, and ensure that the original inter‐
national version was hidden from the administered questionnaire. As with the student questionnaire,
this approach clearly flagged the item as a nationally adapted version. The web‐preview functionality
allowed users to verify that the structural adaptations were correctly implemented before finalizing the
questionnaire.
Translation
The translation processes used in AM Designer (for the student instruments) and OSS Designer (for
the principal, ICT coordinator, and teacher questionnaires) differed substantially. The main distinction
was that, for the student instruments, the entire translation and verification process was conducted
within the AM Designer system. In contrast, for the questionnaires for principals, ICT coordinators,
and teachers, translation and verification were completed outside the OSS system. As a result, the
following descriptions of the two environments—AM Designer and OSS Designer—differ accordingly.
Student instruments
In ICILS 2023, the translation system within the AM Designer module was used to translate the student
test and student questionnaire. Every text element in the student instruments required translation, with
the system providing an empty text field for users to enter the corresponding translation. A preview
functionality allowed users to verify whether all text elements had been translated correctly. In addition
to this core functionality, the translation system offered the following features:
• Some selective functionality relating to the role of the user (e.g., administrator, translator, trans‐
lation reviewer, translation verifier, verification reviewer);
• Opportunity to enter translated text for all screen elements;
• Ability for those countries where the test would be administered in more than one language to
select the target language;
• Ability to view the translation history of a text element;
• Ability to view the source text, the translated text, and any previous revisions of the translated
text, and to compare translated versions;
• A comments interface to allow translators, reviewers, and verifiers to enter comments linked to
elements of text;
• Opportunity to view “live” translated versions of the tasks at any point during the translation and
to simultaneously view (in a separate window) a live version of the source task;
• Ability to enter text as plain text, including editable HTML tags or to enter and use formatting
tools to format text;

36
ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
• Ability to view translated text elements as plain text or as rendered HTML;
• Ability to search for and/or selectively bulk‐replace text within tasks;
• Ability to bulk‐import the field trial translations so that they could be used as a starting point for
main survey translations;
• Opportunity for translators, reviewers, and verifiers to monitor the progress of task completion
(i.e., translation state); and
• Facility for designated users to add or remove questions and items for questionnaire instruments
(structural adaptations).
For more information on the translation procedures please refer to Chapter 5.
Principal, ICT‐coordinator, and teacher questionnaires
The translation of the principal, ICT coordinator, and teacher questionnaires was carried out by na‐
tional centers outside the OSS system. National centers received Microsoft Word files containing the
international source versions of the questionnaires for translation and verification (see Chapter 5 for
more details on the translation procedures). Once the translated content had been finalized and ver‐
ified, national centers manually entered the translations into the OSS Designer for each administered
language.
The OSS Designer offered several features to support this process:
• A dedicated text field for each translatable element in the international source version;
• The ability to input translations manually for each item and screen;
• A built‐in check functionality to verify that all required translations were entered;
• A web preview mode to confirm that translated text displayed correctly in the final questionnaire
format, reflecting how it would appear to respondents; and
• Support for multiple administered languages.
For more information on the translation procedures please refer to Chapter 5.
3.6 Student instrument delivery using AM Examiner
The student instrument delivery engine provided the following features:
• Delivery of multiple choice, constructed response, and drag and drop items;
• Delivery of linear and nonlinear skills tasks (based on software simulations with real‐world re‐
sponsiveness);
• Delivery of authoring tasks (live software applications with functionality to add, edit, and format
multimodal media);
• Display of closed web environments with the facility to display multiple pages and navigate
between and within pages with a simulated web browser;
• Delivery of nonlinear systems transfer and simulation tasks;
• Delivery of block‐based coding tasks;
• Capture of all student final responses to each task;
• Capture of time taken on each task;
• Capture of event data on each task;

ICILS SURVEY OPERATIONS SOFTWARE
37
• Facility to codify and machine score student responses to specified tasks;
• A progress display (number of tasks completed and to come per module) for students; and
• A countdown timer for students per module.
Regarding the data capture, several mechanisms were implemented to ensure detailed, structured,
and standardized output. Final responses were saved periodically and stored in the native RM system,
linked to unique identifiers for each student, module, and task. These responses were later transformed
into flat files—where each row represented a student and each column represented that student’s
response to a specific item—in CSV format for further processing. Time‐on‐task data were captured
in seconds, measuring the duration from task load to either proceeding to the next task or exiting the
module. For selected tasks, detailed event data were collected, including timestamped logs of user
interactions such as clicking named user interface elements, typing, and navigation actions.
In order to maximize participation, the ISC worked with RM results to ensure that the delivery could
be as flexible as possible, allowing for two main forms of delivery of the student instruments:
• Internet (browser‐based) delivery; and
• USB flash drive delivery for use with school computers running Windows operating systems.
The test delivery engine itself was web‐based. Offline solutions made use of a locally installed web
server (on USB or on the local server) through which the application could be accessed. Although
the USB‐based delivery engine was Windows‐based, it could be run using some Windows emulation
software on Mac OS X or Linux.
All offline solutions used a portable version of the Mozilla Firefox browser. All ICILS 2023 materials
were developed to be compatible with recent versions (not more than one year old) of the following
browsers:
• Mozilla Firefox
• Google Chrome
• Microsoft Internet Explorer 11 (IE11)
• Microsoft Edge
• Apple Safari
• Opera
AM Designer, AM Marker, and AM Manager functioned on recent versions of all major browsers.
As part of the field‐operation procedures, data collected using offline solutions (USB or local server)
needed to be uploaded to a central database as soon as practicable after each test session. Web‐based
delivery did not require any additional steps, as the data were immediately written to the AM database
during each test session.
Two backup delivery alternatives were also available to countries:
• Taking laptop computers with AM Examiner already installed into schools; or
• Connecting a single laptop computer into the school network to act as a web server that hosts the
student instruments (this was recommended for use with schools using networked computers
that did not use Windows operating systems such as Mac OS or Linux). This method is hereafter
referred to as the “local server method.”

38
ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
Recommended delivery methods
Internet delivery and USB delivery (in schools where internet delivery was not feasible) were the two
recommended delivery methods for use in ICILS 2023.
Internet (browser‐based) method
The student instrument delivery engine, AM Examiner, was web‐based, which meant that the instru‐
ments could technically be delivered over the internet and accessed using a web‐browser. In ICILS
2018, operational constraints led to the decision to primarily administer the instruments using USB
drives. These USB drives acted as local web servers, containing the test and questionnaire content,
and were used to administer the instruments on individual computers. Although the delivery engine
was designed to run on Windows, it could also operate on Mac OS X or Linux using certain forms of
Windows emulation. In ICILS 2023, however, internet‐based delivery was offered to all participating
countries, expanding the flexibility of the instrument administration. Schools with adequate infrastruc‐
ture could now deliver the instruments directly through an internet browser. Where this method was
used, student data were uploaded in real‐time to the central ICILS database.
USB flash drive method
The USB flash drive method remained available for schools with limited internet connectivity. The use
of a portable version of the Mozilla Firefox browser, hosted on each USB drive, enabled a consistent
experience across different systems. All ICILS 2023 materials were developed and tested using several
browsers to ensure compatibility across browsers.
Where this method was used, after completion of each test session, test administrators were required
to complete the additional steps of locating a computer with an internet connection and then uploading
the student data from each individual USB drive to the central ICILS database.
Alternative delivery methods
Providing computers to schools
In schools where the computers did not meet specifications, national centers were asked to consider
using the local network server method, or to provide computers to the school (such as a set of laptop
computers) that could then be configured for use with any of the three available delivery methods.
Local network server method
An alternative (but not recommended) delivery method was the use of a local network server. Under
the local network server method, the delivery engine was installed on a notebook computer, which
was then connected to the school’s local area network (LAN). Through this setup, students could ac‐
cess the test content locally using a modern web browser (e.g., IE11, Edge, Opera, Firefox, Chrome,
Safari), without requiring individual USB drives for each computer. This option was designed to sup‐
port schools where internet connectivity was unstable or where USB ports were disabled for security
purposes but a reliable internal network was available.
Where this method was used, after completion of each test session, test administrators were required
to complete the additional steps of connecting the local server computer to the internet and then
uploading the student data from the test session to the central ICILS database. However, the local
network server method proved to be problematic for countries that chose this option, which is dis‐
cussed in more detail in section 3.10.
Choosing a delivery method
In order to support national centers and school coordinators to choose the most suitable delivery
method within each school, and to maximize the chances of successful and smooth test delivery, school
coordinators were asked to complete a questionnaire describing the available instrument delivery in‐
frastructure (e.g., hardware, internet provision, and software security controls) and were provided with
specific minimum specifications for the computers used to administer the instruments for each method.

ICILS SURVEY OPERATIONS SOFTWARE
39
As part of this process, schools were also asked to run simple compatibility checks of the internet‐
and USB‐based methods. The internet‐based method check required the user to navigate and log in
to a website. They received immediate feedback of whether or not the access was successful and
whether or not the necessary configurations were in place to successfully run the testing software.
The USB‐method test was similar, except that the users were required to run the testing software
from a USB drive. In this test, the user was further asked to execute a simulated data upload, which
also determined whether data could be successfully written to the USB drive. NRCs, in collaboration
with school coordinators, were responsible for using data from the questionnaire and the results of the
compatibility tests to determine the best delivery method within schools. In many countries a single
delivery method was used (e.g., internet‐ or USB‐based) although many countries using the internet‐
based delivery method made provisions to use the USB‐delivery method as a backup.
Following are the minimum specifications provided to schools regarding the successful implementation
of each delivery method.
Internet (browser‐based) method
The internet (browser‐based) method required each student to have an internet connected and com‐
patible computer that accessed the ICILS Student Software from a cloud server via a web browser. This
method was more flexible than the USB method because it could be used on computers with any op‐
erating system (Windows, Mac, Linux) as long as they had a modern web browser (updated within the
past calendar year) and a stable internet connection. Typically, school computers required little or no
configuration under this method. In some cases security settings may have needed to be altered to
allow access to the ICILS testing URL.
School coordinators were provided with the following list of minimum specifications for computers to
run the ICILS student test using the internet (browser‐based) method.
• Screen resolution: A minimum resolution of 1280x800 pixels was required to ensure that all test
items displayed correctly.
• Display size: A minimum display size of 11‐inches was required to ensure rendered content was
large enough to see.
• Operating system: Any operating system that can run a modern web browser (e.g., IE11, Edge,
Opera, Firefox, Chrome, Safari).
• Peripherals: A mouse and external keyboard was required to ensure students could easily engage
with complex interactive assessment tasks.
• Power supply: Laptops were required to use a power supply to ensure an uninterrupted test
experience.
USB flash drive method
Under the USB flash drive method, the ICILS student instrument software was stored on individual USB
flash drives. The test was delivered to students by plugging one USB flash drive into each computer
used by a student and running the software from the USB flash drive. The computers could be local
school computers or external computers provided for the ICILS administration. This method was limited
to computers running Windows 8 or higher.
Features of the USB flash drive method were:
• The whole ICILS Student Software system was contained on the USB flash drive.
• No local network or internet connection was required to complete test sessions.
• Results were uploaded from the USB flash drive when connected to the internet.

40
ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
• A separate USB flash drive containing the ICILS Student Software was required for each individual
student in each test session. One USB flash drive could be used by multiple students across
different test sessions.
School coordinators were provided with the following list of minimum specifications for computers and
USB drives to run the ICILS student test using the USB flash drive method.
• Screen resolution: A minimum resolution of 1280x800 pixels was required to ensure that all test
items displayed correctly.
• Display size: A minimum display size of 11‐inches was required to ensure rendered content was
large enough to see.
• Operating system: Windows 8 (with the latest patches).
• Peripherals: A mouse and external keyboard was required to ensure students could easily engage
with complex interactive assessment tasks.
• Power supply: Laptops were required to use a power supply to ensure an uninterrupted test
experience.
• USB falsh drive brand: Recommended brands were Samsung, SanDisk, Kingston, PNY, Corsair
to ensure file integrity.
• USB capacity: A minimum storage capacity of 16GB was required to store the ICILS Student
Software.
Local network server method
The local server method was a hybrid of the USB and internet delivery methods. This approach was
used when internet access was unreliable but the school had a functioning internal network. The ICILS
student instrument software was loaded on a single server computer and then individual student (client)
computers connected to the computer through a local area network (LAN). The student experience
of the test was equivalent to that during internet delivery, and the client computers had the same
requirements as those using internet delivery. However, all student data in each test session were
saved locally on the server computer and subsequently had to be uploaded immediately after the test
session.
School coordinators were provided with the following list of minimum specifications for server com‐
puters to run the ICILS student test using the local server method.
• Operating system: Windows 8 (with the latest patches).
• Server CPU: 2.2 GHz processor.
• Memory capacity: 8 GB RAM.
• Storage capacity: 10 GB of available HDD storage.
• Privileges: Administrator level user privileges.
The minimum computer requirements for the computers allocated to students were the same as the
internet (browser‐based) method. If the server was using a WiFi hotspot, the allocated computers also
needed a WiFi device.
3.7 Principal, ICT coordinator, and teacher questionnaires delivery using OSS
web component
The OSS web delivery engine was used to administer the ICILS 2023 questionnaires for principals,
ICT coordinators, and teachers. Unlike the student instruments, the OSS component supported only a

41
single delivery mode—online browser‐based delivery—which allowed respondents to access and com‐
plete their questionnaires using an internet browser. Despite the streamlined setup, the OSS system
offered a robust and flexible platform for questionnaire administration.
The OSS delivery engine supported the following question formats:
• Question formats:
– Single (non‐categorical);
– Single (categorical);
– Single (check all that apply);
– List (non‐categorical);
– Matrix (categorical);
– Matrix (check all that apply); and
– Mix Matrix (combination of categorical and non‐categorical in a matrix).
In addition, the system provided the following features to support administration and data cap‐
ture:
• Features
– Capture of all final responses to each question;
– Capture of process data on the questionnaire, including timestamps for initial and final
logins; and
– A progress display (completion status of each question and question part).
Online‐only delivery
Questionnaires were delivered exclusively online and accessed via a secure web browser interface.
Respondents were provided with unique credentials to log in and complete their assigned question‐
naire. The system supported a responsive layout, ensuring accessibility across a wide range of screen
sizes and resolutions.
Given the relatively lightweight nature of the questionnaire interface, minimum hardware requirements
were modest. Computers were required to have a Pentium processor (1 GHz or higher), 512 MB of
RAM or more, and one of the following supported browsers:
• Google Chrome;
• Mozilla/Firefox 20.0 and above;
• Microsoft Edge;
• Microsoft Internet Explorer 7.0 and above;
• Apple Safari/WebKit 1.0 and above; or
• Opera 7.0 and above and Konqueror (all versions regardless of platform).
Operational procedures
Before the start of data collection, national centers created access credentials via WinW3S system.
Each questionnaire was activated by the ISC within the system according to the national data collec‐
tion and instrument production schedule. National centers were responsible for distributing access
credentials and providing basic technical support to respondents where needed.
To ensure smooth administration, national centers were advised to verify that respondents had access
to a computer that met the minimum requirements and could successfully access the questionnaire

ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
interface. In contrast to the student instruments, no compatibility check tools or offline alternatives
were required, significantly reducing the technical complexity of administration.
3.8 Student response scoring and coding
Scoring student test responses using AM Marker and AM Examiner
The ICILS student test included both human‐scored and automatically scored tasks. Automatically
scored tasks followed a set of machine scoring rules established by the ISC. RM integrated the scoring
logic into the AM Examiner module, while the ISC defined and evaluated multiple test cases for each
machine‐scored task to ensure the implementation aligned with expectations.
AM Marker
The ICILS 2023 scoring module, AM Marker, was adapted from the ICILS 2018 scoring module. It
provided the following key features:
• Role‐based access for different user types (e.g., administrator, team leader, scorer).
• Blind double‐scoring functionality to ensure marker reliability by requiring a subset of student
responses to be scored independently by multiple scorers.
• Early scoring capability, allowing scoring to begin before all student responses were uploaded,
without compromising the double‐scoring procedure.
Scoring workflow and user roles
To facilitate scoring, three primary user roles were established:
• Scorer – Engaged in training with pre‐scored student responses and scored student work.
• Scoring Team Leader – Had all scorer privileges plus full access to scoring and management
functions.
• Admin Scorer – Included all team leader and scorer functions, with additional permissions to
create scorer accounts and manage scoring, training, and administrative tasks in AM Marker.
Additionally, NRC users had all admin scorer permissions plus full access and control over the AM
Marker module.
Role‐specific permissions
Scorers could:
• View test tasks as presented to students, alongside student responses.
• Assign scores to student responses.
• Flag responses for review by senior staff.
• Navigate to previously scored responses and amend scores (except for double‐scored responses).
• View large tasks with full functionality.
• Enter a training mode to score pre‐scored responses and receive feedback on scoring accuracy.
Scoring team leaders could:
• Select, order, and annotate responses for scorer training.
• Review, check‐score, and amend scorers’ scores.

ICILS SURVEY OPERATIONS SOFTWARE
43
• Monitor and address flagged responses.
Scoring administrators could :
• Select, order, and annotate responses for scorer training.
• Assign scorers to teams.
• Generate and view reports on inter‐scorer reliability.
Coding student descriptions of parental occupations
Each student was presented two pairs of questions in the student questionnaire regarding the occu‐
pation of the student’s parents. In each pair the students were asked first to provide a job title for the
parent, and second to provide a brief description of the work completed by the parent in their job.10
The responses in each pair (job title and job description) were then coded according to the International
Standard Classification of Occupations (ISCO) framework (ILO, 2012).
The AM Marker system was not suitable for conducting the parental occupation coding. Students’
text responses to each pair of questions were first exported from the AM Manager system (a feature
available to ISC staff) and provided to national centers in a pre‐configured MS Excel workbook. The
MS Excel workbook included the facility for users to view and code each pair of responses. Once the
occupation coding had been completed within each country, the national center then returned the MS
Excel workbook to the ISC and the coded data were included in the international database.
3.9 Managing the online systems and data collection
Student instruments (AM Manager)
The ICILS 2023 administration module, AM Manager, was used by national center staff to manage
the delivery of the student instruments. It supported the monitoring of test session progress (i.e.,
submission status), the creation of user accounts, and the assignment of roles for users of related
modules (e.g., scoring and translation). It also provided functionality to export data for integration into
the IEA WinW3S system.
The AM Manager enabled users to:
• Create user accounts and allocate roles;
• Download the ICILS 2023 test software image (including the test delivery engine);
• View national test session details;
• Monitor the status of national test sessions; and
• Export test session data for import into IEA WinW3S.
Principal, ICT coordinator, and teacher questionnaires delivery (Monitor component)
The Monitor component of the OSS system allowed national centers to track the real‐time progress
and submission status of the online questionnaires for principals, ICT coordinators, and teachers. Once
the questionnaires had been activated for data collection, national centers received access credentials
and monitoring rights from the ISC.
The Monitor component enabled users to:
• View a list of respondents who had logged into the system at least once;
10 The questions presented to students are provided with the ICILS 2023 IDB and user guide.

44
ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
• See the most recent question viewed by each respondent;
• Track the overall submission status of each questionnaire; and
• Export participation data in Excel format for further processing.
The exported file included the IDs of principals, ICT coordinators, and teachers, provided that the
respondent had submitted at least one valid response.
3.10 Challenges with computer‐based delivery in ICILS
Successful collection of data in large‐scale CBAs rely on individual participants being able to provide
responses on computers in controlled standardized environments from which data can be recorded,
organized, and stored for later use. When the data being collected is assessment data, it is imperative
that all respondents experience the tasks in an identical manner.
In the CBA environment, variations in the delivery infrastructure can make it difficult to maintain stan‐
dardization of the test taking experience. This in turn can potentially influence respondents’ perfor‐
mance and these differences may be especially pronounced in complex tasks, such as those involving
(simulated) multimedia applications or other types of rich or interactive stimulus. These tasks place
greater demands on delivery methods than standard, flat, stimulus material, and multiple‐choice re‐
sponse options.
The AM test‐delivery architecture, including its USB‐delivery option, was designed to minimize the
potential for variation in students’ test‐taking experience. The on‐screen appearance of the ICILS
2023 instruments could be checked (as a feature of the translation module) throughout the translation
and verification processes and was formally verified during the layout verification process. Chapter 5
of this report describes these processes in detail.
The data collected during ICILS 2023 needed to be extracted, transformed, and then loaded into
systems for processing, weighting, and analysis. These tasks required the interaction of a complex set
of survey operations software applications and systems (as shown in Table 3.1). As is the case with
any system architecture comprising interconnected applications and systems with a range of specific
functions, the interfaces between each component are potential points of failure. To ensure that the
system worked successfully, study coordinators needed to monitor both the integrity of the functioning
of the individual components of the system (paying special attention to ensuring results data were being
stored) and the interfaces between the components.
It is not possible in this section of the report to look at the full range of sources and types of information
described above. Instead, the focus is on key findings relating to one particular type of information, that
is, the proportion and typology of cases that the NRCs and their staff classified as technical problems
during administration. Non‐response at the unit or item level, whether due to a lack of cooperation,
technical issues, or flawed administration, is one of the more serious factors influencing the robustness
and stability of inferences from sample studies. Those responsible for implementing a study nation‐
ally therefore regularly monitor response and participation rates, especially as these are used as key
indicators of success, quality, and fitness for use.
During ICILS 2023, the inspection of unweighted yet cleaned data from all 34 participating education
systems (147,637 students from a total of 6,696 sampled classes) yielded some interesting insights.
Test administrators were asked to code the participation status of each sampled student on a student
tracking form, and the coding scheme included codes for technical problems before, during, or after
the CBA sessions; participation in the test and questionnaire sessions were coded separately.
Table 3.2 presents aggregated percentages of these codings for all sampled students (so excluding
any non‐cooperating schools). Percentages are given across all countries given that the per‐country
proportions were similar.

Table 3.2 shows that test and questionnaire data were collected from 88.6 percent and 87.8 percent
of sampled students respectively. The slightly lower percentage of data collected for the questionnaire
is a result of two factors:
1. Students may have been given a break between the test and the questionnaire so some of the
students who completed the test may not have returned to complete the questionnaire.
2. In some countries parents gave permission for their children to complete the test but not the
questionnaire.
Approximately 9.3 percent of the sampled students had either permanently left school (0.9%) or were
absent on the day of testing (8.4%). The slightly higher percentage of students absent during the
questionnaire session reflects that some students, for various reasons, did not stay after the first test
session.
Please note that the above percentages are based on raw data from the database and consequently
may not match other figures relating to participation rates presented in previous chapters and the
ICILS 2023 international report. The presented proportions are from unweighted data and as initially
assigned by test administrators. We therefore subjected these data to further adjudication. We iden‐
tified some cases initially coded as a technical problem during the test or questionnaire session, which
had (at least partial) responses and should have been classified as participation by the students con‐
cerned. These cases, in which a technical problem had been flagged in the student tracking forms but
eventually been resolved (as evidenced by the presence of student data) corresponded to incorrect
flagging in the respective student tracking forms. It is also possible that a small number of technical
problems went unnoticed by the test administrators. Chapter 11 describes how data with some resid‐
ual technical failures (evidenced or assumed) were eventually treated during the calibration and scaling
of test data.
A rather severe issue occurred at the beginning of the data collection for two countries that opted to
collect student data via the local server method (see above), both of which met the minimum computer
requirements specified by the external software provider to the ISC. However, during the first testing
sessions in both countries, it quickly became apparent that the local network server method caused
significant delays in loading test content to an unsustainable extent, even though the requirements
were met. As a result, both countries successfully switched to the internet (browser‐based) method.
One country that administered the student instruments using the online method experienced issues
with inaccessible student instruments due to an error from the external provider on a day when a sig‐
nificant number of test sessions were scheduled. The issue was resolved by rescheduling the affected

46
ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
test sessions. Several other countries were also affected by this outage, but to a lesser extent. Most
were able to reschedule testing for the affected schools for another day.
A similar table to Table 3.2 is also available in the ICILS 2018 technical report, allowing for percent‐
age comparisons (Fraillon et al., 2020). Compared to 2018, technical problems before the assessment
appear to have increased from 0.1 percent to 1 percent in ICILS 2023, likely due to the previously men‐
tioned issues. However, issues encountered during and after the administration remained consistent.
Note that the online administration mode was not available in ICILS 2018.

Chapter 4:
ICILS questionnaire development
Mojca Rožman and Julian Fraillon
4.1 Introduction
This chapter describes the development process and the content of the ICILS 2023 field trial and
main survey questionnaires. The questionnaires collect information to support understanding of the
contexts in which students’ computer and information literacy (CIL) and computational thinking (CT)
learning develops, including the existing conditions, the surround learning, and the processes that con‐
tribute to learning. In addition, questionnaire data can contribute to our understanding of variation
in students’ CIL and CT achievement. Separate questionnaires were completed by students, teach‐
ers, principals, and information and communication technology (ICT) coordinators in sampled schools.
Another questionnaire, the National Contexts Survey, was designed to collect characteristics of edu‐
cation systems and was completed under the supervision of National Research Coordinators (NRCs),
who could obtain the necessary information from relevant national stakeholders or from national docu‐
ments. Each cycle, the questionnaires are updated to reflect changes in ICT use and new technological
developments and to focus on existing or new areas of research interest. In ICILS 2023, examples of
new areas of focus were school leadership and technology, approaches to teaching, and the use of
generative AI tools in schools. All ICILS questionnaires were administered on a computer.
4.2 The conceptual framework and questionnaire content
The contextual framework, which is part of the assessment framework of ICILS 2023 (Rožman et al.,
2024), identifies relevant topics related to the environment in which learning of CIL and CT takes place.
It assumes that young people develop their understandings of ICT through activities and experiences
that take place in schools and classrooms, as well as in their homes and other places outside school.
Since the first cycle in 2013, ICILS has used two main dimensions for the contextual framework. The
first dimension is described by the level of the environment in which learning takes place, ranging from
the wider community to the level of the individual student, and the second dimension relates to the
conditions (antecedents) and activities (processes) for learning (Rožman et al., 2024). The combination
of these two dimensions, form the basis of the ICILS context assessment categories. The categories
are shown in Figure 4.1.
The ICILS 2023 questionnaires cover all aspects across the different categories of the contextual frame‐
work. Information on the wider community is collected through the national context survey, principal,
ICT coordinator questionnaires, and other sources. The school‐level antecedents are collected through
the principal, teacher and ICT coordinator questionnaires, which also include information on processes
at school level. The student questionnaire also provides some additional information on school level
processes. The student and their home level antecedents and processes are collected through the
student questionnaire. Examples of specific topics included for each of the levels and their allocation
to the contextual framework categories are presented in Table 4.1.

48
ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
Figure 4.1: Contexts for ICILS 2023 CIL/CT learning outcomes
Notes: The double arrow between process‐related factors and outcomes emphasizes the possibility of a recip‐
rocal association between learning processes and learning outcomes. The single arrow between antecedents
and processes indicates the assumption within the ICILS contextual framework of a unidirectional influence
between these types of contextual factors.

ICILS QUESTIONNAIRE DEVELOPMENT
49
4.3 Development process
The International Study Center (ISC) at IEA coordinated the development of the ICILS 2023 context
questionnaires.
The development of the field trial questionnaires started with a review of the 2018 versions with par‐
ticular emphasis on the relevance of the preexisting content to the new cycle and the extent of use of
previous questionnaire material in reporting or additional analysis. Additional new topics considered
relevant to the study were also discussed within the ISC, with NRCs and with the questionnaire devel‐
opment group (QDG). For the field trial, the administered questionnaires included more content than
was expected to be used for the main survey. The purpose was to test some of the material and then
select the better performing version for the main survey. Two new topics of specific interest identified
for inclusion in ICILS 2023 were school leadership for technology and approaches to teaching with
and about ICT.
In ICILS, the international version of each questionnaire comprises a common set of questions and
items required to be included in the national questionnaires of all participating countries. In addition,
where it is deemed to be appropriate, the questionnaires may include a small number of questions
and/or items that national centers can choose to include or exclude from their national questionnaires.
These optional questions and items are hereafter referred to as international options. National centers
also have the opportunity to add a small number of questions or items of specific national interest or
to add or amend response categories to questions or items (hereafter referred to as national options).
The use of national options is subject to the approval of the ISC, primarily on the condition that their
inclusion does not compromise the collection of data to be included in the international database.
Communication between national centers and the ISC regarding national centers’ use of international
options and inclusion of national options is a key part of the instrument adaptation process presented
in detail in Chapter 5.
The development work included development of new material, extensive reviews, and discussions with
different stakeholders at different stages of the process. For the first time in ICILS, a QDG was formed
to accompany the process of questionnaire development in ICILS 2023. The members of the QDG
were: Frederik Aspling (NRC Sweden), Jeppe Bundsgaard (NRC Denmark), Sara Dexter (the United
States), Birgit Eikelmann (NRC Germany), Julian Fraillon (IEA), Mojca Rožman (IEA), Ronny Scherer
(Norway), Anabela Serrão (NRC Portugal), Meng Jung Tsai (NRC Chinese Taipei), and Gary Wong (Hong
Kong SAR). The group included experts on digital literacy education from a range of perspectives with
familiarity of different national contexts.
The development process for the student, teacher, and school‐level questionnaires followed a similar
timeline as the CIL and CT test development and can be described in three phases (a detailed timeline
is presented in Table 4.2):
• Phase 1 (March 2020 to September 2021): previous ICILS questionnaires were reviewed and
field trial material was developed with the content being guided by the assessment framework.
It also included various rounds of consultations and reviews by the QDG members and NRCs.
• Phase 2 (October 2021 to September 2022): Preparations for the field trial took place from
October 2021 to January 2022. From March to June 2022 the regular international field trial
was conducted in 28 participating ICILS countries. Subsequent analyses of field trial data took
place from July to September 2022 to inform the suitability of questionnaire material for the
main survey. The analysis reports included data from 28 countries for principals and teachers,
27 for ICT coordinators, and 25 for students.
• Phase 3 (October 2022 to November 2022): The final phase focused on the selection of items
for the main survey. Based on field trial analysis results the QDG suggested the materials for
the use in the main survey. These were discussed at the NRC meeting in September 2022.
Afterwards, based on the feedback from the QDG members and NRCs the final selection of
main survey material was prepared by the ISC.

50
ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
The NRC and QDG meetings were critical points in the test‐development process. The first meeting of
NRCs coincided with the early phases of the COVID‐19 pandemic. As a consequence of the pandemic,
this meeting, as well as the second and third NRC meetings were all conducted online rather than in‐
person. All QDG meetings were conducted online.

The procedures and criteria used to review the field trial material and results were the same for the
student, teacher, and school‐level questionnaires. During instrument development, particular attention
was paid to the appropriateness of questionnaire material for the large variety of national contexts and
target groups in participating countries as well as to existing differences between education systems

ICILS QUESTIONNAIRE DEVELOPMENT
51
and between schools within each participating education system. The following criteria informed the
selection of item material for the main survey:
• Relevance with regards to the ICILS 2023 assessment framework;
• Relevance for the national contexts of the participating countries; and
• Psychometric properties of single items and items designed to measure latent traits.
The analyses of the field trial data included inspections of descriptive statistics at the international and
national levels, with special attention to the response frequencies across response categories. Scale
statistics were computed for questions in which the items were intended to be used in scales. When
considering suggestions for revisions to items used in previous ICILS cycles, the relative merits of the
suggested revision to each item were compared to the impact of forgoing the use of data from any
trend analyses. These decisions were made on an item‐by‐item basis.
The National Contexts Survey is administered to the NRCs and does not follow the regular question‐
naire development procedures. Its development procedure is described in a separate section below.
Student questionnaire
The student questionnaire was developed to collect information about the students’ characteristics
and their home background as well as their use of and familiarity with ICT. The proposed content for
the ICILS 2023 field trial student questionnaire related to the information of students and their home
characteristics was, with a few exceptions, the same as in 2018.
Two changes related to collecting data about the quality of students’ access to ICT at home. One new
question asked students about the extent to which problems with the internet at home affected their
capacity to complete their schoolwork, and the second asked students whether or not they had access
to a computer at home to whenever they needed it to complete schoolwork. The Use of ICT section
was updated as well. The question on ICT use was expanded to distinguish between the use at school
for schoolwork, at school for other purposes, outside of school for schoolwork, and outside of school
for other purposes. In addition, the extent of use of ICT outside of school was differentiated between
school days and non‐school days. Two new questions on screen time (students’ reports of limits on
device use placed on them by their parents) were developed for the field trial: one about the screen
time limits and another about screen time enforcement. These two questions were optional at the
country level for inclusion in the questionnaire.
The question and items about learning ICT tasks were updated. New items were proposed to measure
learning about internet‐related tasks and software tasks, at school and outside of school. A new ques‐
tion on learning about responsible ICT use at school was added, and questions about academic‐media
multitasking and frequency of ICT use for school‐related purposes were added as well.
In order to collect data from a larger pool of items than could be reasonably completed by a student in
a single session, the field trial student questionnaire was administered in two different forms. The ICILS
field trial student questionnaire material included a total of 38 questions (including two international
options), with the total of 196 items (151 in form A and 149 in form B). In the field trial, data were
collected from 20,034 students from 706 schools in 27 countries.
The final main survey international student questionnaire consisted of 27 questions containing 135
items (including an international option) to be completed within the targeted time of 20 to 25 min‐
utes. Twenty‐eight of the items were designed to capture student background information (including
ICT experience, devices, and internet connection at home), and 107 (including two international op‐
tions) were designed to measure students’ use of and familiarity with ICT. The main survey student
questionnaire consisted of the following five sections:
• About you: this section included questions about the student’s age, gender, and expected edu‐

52
ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
cation.
• Your home and your family: included questions about the country of origin of the students and
their parents, the language spoken at home, their parents’ occupations and educational back‐
grounds, the number of books at home, the quality of the internet connection at home, the
number of ICT devices at home, and students’ access to ICT devices for schoolwork.
• Your general use of ICT: included questions about students’ experience with ICT, their ICT use in
different places and for different purposes, the question about the screen time limits (this was an
international option), their learning about various internet related and ICT tasks at and outside
of school, and their learning about responsible ICT use at school.
• Your experience of using ICT to do schoolwork: included questions about academic‐media multi‐
tasking, frequency of ICT use during lessons for different subjects, and software application use
in lessons.
• Your thoughts about using and learning about ICT: included questions about self‐efficacy, two ques‐
tions about attitudes towards ICT, a question about learning CT tasks at school, and one on
studying a ICT‐related subject in the current school year.
The student questionnaire was administered on a computer. The computer delivery system was con‐
figured to support the quality of data provided by students. The system could, for example, prevent
students from giving invalid responses (such as selecting multiple contradictory answers where only
one was required) and direct students to targeted questions on the basis of a given response. Further‐
more, when answering the questions on parental occupation, students were first asked if their parents
were currently in a paid job and were then directed to more specific questions about each parent’s
current occupation (if currently in a paid job) or previous occupation (if not currently in a paid job).
Principal questionnaire
The principal questionnaire was designed to gather information about the school context in general and
the schools’ systematic approaches to supporting the use of ICT in teaching and learning in particular.
The proposed principal questionnaire for the field trial included questions about school‐level and prin‐
cipal characteristics. These questions were identical to the content of the ICILS 2018 questionnaire. A
new section on school leadership was added, which included 12 newly developed questions. An ICILS
2018 question about procedures to monitor teachers use of ICT for specific learning outcomes was
replaced by items related to school leadership and ICT. ICILS 2018 items relating to responsibility for
decisions about ICT in the school were replaced by items evaluating distribution of ICT leadership. A
question about the principal’s age was added, as was a question about attitudes towards the usefulness
of ICT to support teachers’ work. The field trial questionnaire included 28 questions (including two
international options) with 147 items (including two international options). The data analyzed from the
field trial questionnaire were provided by 635 school principals from 28 countries.
As the ongoing and future relevance of COVID‐19 disruption was unclear at the time of the field trial
preparations, we decided to postpone the decision on whether or not to include some questions on
this topic in the school questionnaire. After the field trial, the ISC team, in consultation with NRCs
and the QDG, decided to include two additional questions associated with the impact of the COVID‐
19 pandemic on schools (one on the duration of the disruption to teaching and learning and one on
the impact of COVID‐19 on teaching and learning). In addition, a question on school support for the
effective use of ICT in teaching and learning was also added after the field trial.
The selected content of the principal questionnaire for the main survey resulted in a reduction in the
size of the principal questionnaire compared to the field trial. The principal questionnaire for the main
survey contained 24 questions (including two international options) with 143 items (including two
international options) in the following three sections:
• About your school: this section contained questions about school size by gender, grades taught

ICILS QUESTIONNAIRE DEVELOPMENT
53
at the school, number of teachers, community size, school management, one question about the
school socioeconomic background composition of students (this was an international option),
two questions COVID‐19, one about disrupted or online instruction because of COVID‐19 mea‐
sures, and one about COVID‐19 effects on different aspects of teaching and learning, questions
about distribution of ICT leadership, policies regarding aspects of ICT use in the school, impor‐
tance of ICT related outcomes of education, and expectations of teachers’ ICT related knowledge
and skills acquisition.
• Using ICT in teaching and learning at your school: contained questions about the school vision, a
question about peer monitoring in the use of ICT in teaching and learning (this was an interna‐
tional option), shared understanding of ICT in school, frequency of allocated times for teachers
to reflect on ICT and pedagogy, priority given to ways of facilitating the use of ICT in teaching
and learning, and teachers’ participation in professional development.
• You and your use of ICT: this section school principals reported their gender, age, and their ICT
use for different activities.
Optional generative artificial intelligence (ChatGPT) questions
At the end of 2022, when the ICILS questionnaires were finalized and made available for translation
in the ICILS countries, ChatGPT was launched. Based on the rapid and widespread recognition and
interest in ChatGPT, and the use of generative artificial intelligence (AI) in schools more broadly, the ISC,
in consultation with NRCs and the QDG, the ISC team decided to address the topic of generative AI
tools in ICILS 2023. The decision was made to create a set of additional questions as an international
option for the principal questionnaire. The decision to limit data collection to school principals was
taken primarily to minimize the operational burden on countries. The ChatGPT questionnaire was
developed rapidly and separately from the development schedule and processes used to develop the
ICILS 2023 questionnaires. The QDG contributed to the initial development of the questionnaire and
reviewed the final draft produced by the ISC. National research coordinators were also given a brief
period of time to provide feedback on the ChatGPT questions.
The ChatGPT questionnaire included nine questions and 53 items on frequency of ChatGPT use, exis‐
tence of school policy on ChatGPT use, existence of recommendations on ChatGPT use, whether they
had received information from the national authority on ChatGPT use, restrictions on ChatGPT use for
teachers and students, frequency of teacher and student use of ChatGPT, reference to ChatGPT use
in the curriculum, and three questions on beliefs about consequences of ChatGPT use for teachers
and students.
The content of the ChatGPT international option was made available to countries in mid‐2023, after
the main ICILS data collection had been completed in the Northern Hemisphere countries and before
it had started in the Southern Hemisphere countries. In countries choosing to collect data using the
international option, it was administered as a separate questionnaire to principals in Northern Hemi‐
sphere countries and as part of the questionnaire in Southern Hemisphere countries. The ChatGPT
international option was completed by principals in 12 ICILS countries.
ICT coordinator questionnaire
The ICT coordinator questionnaire was designed to collect information about the ICT resources in
schools and the support for the use of ICT in teaching and learning. The ICILS 2018 ICT coordinator
questionnaire was revised to include new questions: one about the role of the ICT coordinator in the
school, a new section on school leadership with eight questions, new questions about the provision of
software for smartboards by the relevant education authority, as well as three items about monitoring
software (these three were an international option). As the questions in the ICT questionnaire covered
many different topics, we also included a question about how many people needed to be consulted
to complete the questionnaire. The field trial questionnaire contained 24 questions (three of which
comprised the international option) with 141 items (three of which comprised an international option).
The data analyzed from the field trial were provided by 634 ICT coordinators from 27 countries.

54
ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
The final ICT coordinator questionnaire comprised 19 questions (including the three questions compris‐
ing the international option) with a total of 119 items (including five international options). It contained
the following four sections:
• About your position: this section included two questions about the ICT coordinators position at
school, their duties and their school’s experience with computers for teaching and learning.
• ICT technical resourcing in your school: included questions on the availability of technology infras‐
tructure, software, and hardware resources at school, number of device types, computers con‐
nected to the internet in the school, number of smartboards and provision of software for smart‐
boards, provision of ICT devices for teachers, percentage of students with access to portable
computers, location of ICT devices, and three questions about monitoring software, including its
use (these three were an international option).
• ICT support in your school: this section consisted of questions on the technical and pedagogical
support provided for ICT use at school and the extent to which a lack of resources was hindering
that use.
• Using ICT in teaching and learning at your school: contained questions about the school vision for
using ICT, availability of learning materials for ICT teaching and learning, shared understanding
of the use of ICT in the school, about the source to assess effectiveness of the professional
learning activities for ICT use in teaching and learning, and pedagogical resourcing hindrances
to the use of ICT in teaching.
Teacher questionnaire
The teacher questionnaire was designed to collect contextual information about the school and class‐
room contexts for ICT learning, the use of ICT for teaching and learning, teachers’ views on the ped‐
agogical use of ICT, and their confidence in using computers. The teacher questionnaire for the field
trial included questions on teacher characteristics, initial education, and professional learning activities.
With the exception of initial education, where items were heavily edited for clarification, this content
remained unchanged from 2018. Similar to the principal and ICT questionnaires, eight questions on
school leadership for ICT and a question on whether ICT hinders or helps in the work as a teacher were
added for inclusion in 2023. A question on the frequency of ICT‐based activities was added, as well
as two questions on teaching approaches: a question on the allocation of time to different teaching
activities, together with the extent of ICT use in these activities, and a question on teaching beliefs.
The question on the allocation of time for different classroom activities was tested in two different
versions, the first asking for the approximate percentage of time that students spent on different ac‐
tivities and the second asking teachers to choose from the response options given how often students
spent on different activities. The question about teaching beliefs was also trialed using two alternative
versions, one with the traditional Likert‐type response options and the other requiring teachers to rank
the degree to which statements reflected their beliefs.
As with the student questionnaire, our intention was to reduce the administration time for teachers,
and the teacher questionnaire was administered in two different forms. The teacher questionnaire for
the ICILS field trial contained a total of 30 questions and 286 items. Form A contained 22 questions
and 151 items and Form B contained 22 questions and 152 items. The analyzed data from the field
trial questionnaire included 8,323 teachers from 710 schools in 28 countries.
The final teacher questionnaire comprised 21 questions (including three optional questions) with a
total of 192 items (including 45 optional items). It contained the following five sections:
• About you: this section contained questions about the teacher’s gender, age, subjects taught,
and if they teach in the target grade.
• Your use of ICT: contained questions about experience with ICT for teaching purposes, general
use of ICT, and a question related to their self‐efficacy in ICT use.

ICILS QUESTIONNAIRE DEVELOPMENT
55
• Learning to use ICT in teaching: asked teachers about whether their initial teacher education
included learning to use ICT, whether they had participated in ICT‐related professional learning,
and their use of ICT in teaching and learning at school (a question about the use of software
tools in class, and a question about emphasis on computational‐thinking skills in teaching were
international options).
• Using ICT in teaching and learning at your school: included a question about the existence of a
school vision for using ICT, whether there is a shared understanding of the ICT use at school
among teachers, their perceptions on school ICT availability, and perceptions of positive and
negative outcomes of ICT for students.
• Teaching and learning with ICT: these questions asked teachers to select a reference class, provide
information about the subject taught in that class, how often they use various teaching activi‐
ties, and state whether they used ICT for teaching and learning activities in this class. Further
teachers had to indicate the proportion of lessons in which they use specific ICT based activi‐
ties, the frequency of their use of software tools, the emphasis they give to specific ICT based
and CT capabilities. The final question asked about their teaching beliefs (this question was an
international option).
National Contexts Survey
The development of students’ CIL and CT is embedded in their national contexts and should be in‐
terpreted with consideration of these national characteristics. The national contexts survey (NCS)
provides an opportunity to collect information at the national level on the general characteristics of
the education system as well as policies on, and the curricular background of, CIL and CT education.
The national center in each participating country was responsible for the completion of the NCS. Given
the broad nature of the content and the expectation that the responses provide defensible objective
data relating to each country, national center staff were encouraged to work, as necessary, with others
in their countries (e.g., ministries and departments of education, relevant non‐government organiza‐
tions, specialist organizations concerned with supporting the application of educational technologies,
and teachers’ associations) to complete the survey. The NCS in ICILS 2018 was influenced by the
experience and outcomes of studies such as the Second Information Technology in Education Study
2006 (Plomp et al., 2009), the U.S. Department of Education, Office of Educational Technology (2011)
study of educational technology, and ICILS 2013 (Fraillon et al., 2014). The NCS in the current ICILS
cycle was derived from the NCS developed for use in ICILS 2018.
The content to be included in the NCS was developed with consideration of the planned content and
structure of the ICILS 2023 international report chapter on National and school contexts for computer
and information literacy and computational thinking education (see Fraillon, 2024, Chapter 2). When
planning both the report chapter and the NCS, the ISC, together with NRCs and the QDG, reflected
on the content of the equivalent chapter in the ICILS 2018 international report. This chapter included
a brief profile of each country’s education system and approach to providing education for ICT. These
country profiles were collected separately from and after the 2018 NCS data collection. As the number
of participating countries increased significantly compared to the previous cycle, and this would also
increase the length of the corresponding chapter in the international report, the inclusion of country
profiles was discussed with NRCs and the QDG. There was general agreement among the NRCs, the
QDG, and the ISC staff that brief descriptions of each participating country would provide a useful
context for the results and should again be part of the international report. Specifications for the
content and length of the country profiles, as well as the facility for national centers to submit their
country profiles, were included as part of the NCS.
Further discussion included a close examination of the 2018 NCS content to determine the relevant
content for ICILS 2023. A focus was placed on the content reported in 2018, and items were revised
to include more information on the specifics of CT education. In addition, we focused on the rele‐
vance of the content in relation to the ICILS 2023 assessment framework, the relevance and added
value of collecting information about the wider community context of CIL and CT education, and the

56
ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
appropriateness of the question for the national contexts of the participating countries. The ISC team
and the QDG reviewed the content and presented a draft version, which was reviewed by the NRCs.
Once the final version was agreed, the questionnaire was implemented as an online questionnaire.
The final version of the NCS consisted of 19 questions with 122 items (some items were closed re‐
sponses, and others asked for free text responses); most items also had an option for respondents
to add additional comments if they felt they were warranted. The questions also addressed content
associated with key antecedents and processes in relation to CIL and CT education in each country.
The questions were grouped into two sections:
• The education system: this section started with the descriptive country profile and further in‐
cluded questions on some general characteristics of the country’s education system, questions
about how CIL and CT are taught at different International Standard Classification of Education
(ISCED) levels, and questions about how much autonomy schools have in various aspects related
to CIL and CT teaching and learning.
• Approaches to CIL/CT education in ICILS countries: questions in this section were related to general
policies and overall approaches to ICT in school education in the countries, assessment of CIL/CT,
development of teacher capacity to use ICT, teacher rather than specifically to the target grade
The online questionnaire delivery platform used for the NCS enabled national center staff to log on and
off in order to complete the questionnaire in sections over time rather than in one session. This enabled
national center staff to research responses to the questions before entering them into the system and
review and revise their responses before submitting the completed instrument. National centers were
encouraged to conduct internal reviews of the information they provided in the international report
and to seek advice from local experts where appropriate. The ISC used the outcomes of the national
contexts survey in conjunction with data from externally published sources to inform the descriptions
of the education systems participating in ICILS that were included in the ICILS 2023 international
report.

Chapter 5:
Preparation and verification of the ICILS 2023
study instruments
Marta Moreno Hidalgo and Tim Daniel
Introduction
This chapter describes the procedures for preparing national versions of the ICILS 2023 study instru‐
ments. The primary objective of the instrument preparation process is to maintain comparability of
the national instruments with the international versions, ensure equivalence across countries and lan‐
guages, guarantee the appropriateness of the instruments for each participant’s national context and
education system and ensure the consistency of translations across cycles for countries participating
in earlier ICILS cycles. To achieve the objective of cross‐national comparability and data validity, all
national ICILS 2023 study instruments underwent a thorough and standardized preparation process
as described in this chapter.
The chapter focuses on the following topics:
• Description of the ICILS 2023 materials that required translation and adaptation, the languages
administered, and the systems used for producing national instruments;
• Adaptation, translation, and layout review of national language versions of the international
source materials and associated support materials; and
• International adaptation, translation, and layout verification of the national instruments.
The ICILS 2023 International Study Center (ISC) at IEA developed the international versions of the
student assessment materials and context questionnaires (in English language) in close collaboration
with the national study centers.11
Upon the release of the international version of the instruments, all participating countries were re‐
quired to adapt and, if applicable, translate the international version of the instruments to their national
context and language(s). Guidelines for these procedures were provided in the Survey Operations Pro‐
cedures Unit 3, as well as in supplementary manuals for using the computer‐based administration
systems. After completing each step of national instrument preparation, the adaptations, translations,
and layout of the instruments underwent verification.
The ISC oversaw the instrument preparation and verification procedures. Figure 5.1 shows the overall
workflow for preparing the national study instruments. The process was repeated twice—once before
the field trial and then again prior to the main survey. To the extent possible, participating countries
were encouraged to use field trial translations as a basis for their translations of the main survey in‐
struments. The ISC also managed and coordinated the translation verification process in collaboration
with cApStAn Linguistic Quality Control, a leading provider for translation and translation verification
services based in Brussels, Belgium.
11 Please note that throughout this chapter we will use the term “countries” when referring to the 34 countries and one
benchmark that participated in the study.

were adapted, translated, finalized, and administered within RM Results’ Assessment Master (AM).

The principal, ICT coordinator, and teacher questionnaires were adapted and translated in MS Word
documents before being transferred to the IEA Online Survey System (OSS) for layout verification
and subsequent administration. For readability, this chapter refers to the student test modules and
questionnaire as “student instruments,” while the principal, ICT coordinator, and teacher questionnaires

PREPARATION AND VERIFICATION OF THE ICILS 2023 STUDY INSTRUMENTS
59
will be collectively referred to as “adult questionnaires.”
As for previous ICILS study cycles, all countries administered the computer and information literacy
(CIL) test. As in ICILS 2018, the computational thinking (CT) test was included as an optional compo‐
nent implemented by countries who decided to participate (for further details on the content of these
modules, see Chapter 2).
National study centers also had to adapt and translate procedural manuals and scoring guides for
constructed‐response test items. The ISC did not verify the adaptations and translations of these
procedural manuals. However, the translations of Test Administrator Manual and School Coordinator
Manual underwent a final quality control spot check by an independent quality observer (for further
details on the content of these modules, see Chapter 9).
Administered languages
In most countries, the ICILS 2023 instruments were adapted and translated into the language(s) of
instruction used in the national education system. A country’s decision to administer assessment in‐
struments and questionnaires in multiple languages depended on factors such as national requirements,
the sampling frame, and whether schools with instruction in a particular language were included in the
sample. Countries could also choose to administer only specific materials in a given language (e.g., only
administering the student assessment and student questionnaire and not the adult questionnaires).
For ICILS 2023 a total of 34 countries and one benchmarking participant prepared their national study
instruments in 50 languages (see Table 5.2). Of the participating entities, 10 administered the instru‐
ments in more than one language.
Translation system
The student instruments were adapted and translated via the Designer module in AM. The system en‐
abled users to edit translations, maintain a complete record of the editing history, and retrace changes
made by other users, ensuring transparency in the translation and verification process. Additionally,
the system displayed differences between previously saved translations and the current version in the
form of tracked changes. Users could preview the translations as they would appear to students, facili‐
tating the identification of potential layout issues. It also allowed for translation exports in CSV, XLIFF,
or JSON formats.
Adaptations requiring changes to the structure of the student questionnaire—such as adding national
questions, splitting or omitting answer options (see Subsection 5.2 for more information)—involved a
more complex workflow that could only be implemented by the ISC with assistance from RM.
The adaptation and translation of the adult questionnaires, along with their verification processes,
were conducted in MS Word files. The countries replaced the English version for their translations
and submitted these files for translation verification. After verification, and after implementing the
verifer’s feedback within the files, the NRCs transferred the final translations to the OSS and included
the approved structural adaptations within the platform. The ISC then verified the accuracy of these
adaptations as part of the final layout check before the materials were prepared for administration.
5.2 Adaptation, translation, and layout reviews
Adaptation process
NRCs were required to adapt and translate the international source version of the ICILS study in‐
struments in accordance with the guidelines outlined in Survey Operation Procedures Unit 3, which
detailed the procedures and necessary steps.
Adaptations were categorized into two types. Non‐structural adaptations referred to linguistic changes
that were either required and predefined by the ISC—such as the names of people, places, or terms
related to education systems within countries—or requested by the country to address specific na‐
