61
tional contexts. Structural adaptations involved modifications to the structure of the questionnaire or
individual questions, such as adding, splitting, or removing response categories; introducing national
dimensions; including national questions; or omitting questions or dimensions.
The types of adaptations that could be implemented by the NRCs are presented in Figure 5.2.
Figure 5.2: ICILS 2023 adaptation types
The international version of the study instruments indicated where non‐structural adaptations were
necessary by using square brackets and red font to note terms to be adapted. For example, [country of
test] was meant to be replaced with the name of the NRC’s country and [target grade] with the name
of the target grade (e.g., “Grade 8”).
To help participating countries to adapt their materials to the national context, the ISC provided them
with notes that included brief descriptions of the required adaptation clarifying the meaning of the
terms used (and thus enabling the translators to select the appropriate national term or expression
necessary to convey the intended meaning). In addition, general guidelines for translation and adap‐
tation were provided (listed in Subsection 5.2).
Both required and country‐specific adaptations had to be documented by each country in the National
Adaptation Form (NAF) and were subject to adaptation verification by the ISC.
National adaptation form
The NAF was an Excel document containing a comprehensive adaptation and verification history of
each set of national instruments. Two different NAFs were used for ICILS 2023, one for the student
instruments (test and questionnaire) and another for the adult questionnaires. This was to reflect that
the instruments were administered on two different software platforms, and that the preparation of
the national instruments followed different timelines.
The student instruments NAF consisted of individual worksheets for the CIL modules, the CT Modules
(where applicable) and the student questionnaire. The adult questionnaire NAF consisted of individual
worksheets for the adult questionnaires as well as a section to report layout verification comments.
Overall, documenting an adaptation in the NAFs required entering the proposed adaptation in the tar‐
get language, an English back translation of the adaptation, recoding instructions (if applicable and only
for structural adaptations to the adult questionnaires), and a justification for any further alterations. For
the ease of use and documentation of the different stages of verification, the NAF included designated
areas for each item, user, and instrument.
Both NAFs also contained separate worksheets for completion instructions and a style guide. The style
guide collected information about:

62
ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
• The language variant (e.g., British or American English, Maghrebi Arabic or Mashriqi Arabic);
• Language‐specific rules (e.g., capitalization rules for languages based on Latin or Cyrillic alphabet,
use of diacritic in Russian language);
• Use of anglicisms for technical terms (e.g., using Copy/Paste in English rather than the national
language);
• Form of address to the target audience (formal or informal);
• National conventions for numbers and time notations (e.g., use of dot, space, comma, or apos‐
trophe, for thousand separators);
• Grammar and measurement units used in the materials (e.g., use of symbols or full words); and
• An open section for countries to specify other aspects to be considered during the translation
work.
At specific points during the instrument preparation process, sections of the NAF were completed and
reviewed by NRCs and verifiers. The first version of the NAF was filled out by the NRC in collaboration
with national translator(s) and reviewer(s). This occurred while the materials were being adapted and
translated. This version was submitted to the ISC, for adaptation verification (i.e., review and approval).
The ISC comments were then returned to the NRC for review and to implement any suggested changes.
Once all adaptations were agreed upon by the NRC and the adaptation verifiers, the NAF was provided
to the international translation verifiers during translation verification so they could check the correct
implementation of the adaptations. When translation verification was complete, the NAF was again
returned along with the materials for NRCs to review and implement any final changes.
For the adult questionnaires NAF, an additional version was submitted to the ISC after translation
verification to document any layout issues identified following the transfer to the OSS. Once these
issues were corrected, the ISC returned the final version of the NAF with its comments to the country.
The NAF was an important record of each country’s final instrument versions, as it contained informa‐
tion used throughout the different stages of the instrument preparation process.
Translation process
Along with adaptation, another key part of the national instrument preparation process was the trans‐
lation of the materials into the target language(s).
Countries were strongly advised to use a double translation and reconciliation approach. This method
involved two independent translators working on the same materials, followed by a third individual
who reviewed and reconciled the translations to select the best final version. When time or financial
constraints made this approach unfeasible, a single translation with review method was recommended.
In this case, one individual translated the materials, and a second person reviewed the translations.
Regardless of the translation procedure used, the NRC was responsible for reviewing and finalizing the
translations of the ICILS 2023 instruments.
Essential qualifications for translators and reviewers included:
• Having the target language as mother tongue;
• Excellent knowledge of English;
• Good level of computer literacy;
• Familiarity with survey translations;
• Familiarity with the subject area; and
• Familiarity with education in the target grade.

PREPARATION AND VERIFICATION OF THE ICILS 2023 STUDY INSTRUMENTS
63
NRCs received specific guidelines for the translation of the student instruments. These guidelines
included:
• Minimizing cultural and contextual differences.
• Considering the use of anglicisms where appropriate for the target population, reflecting the
predominance of English‐language terminology in computing and internet culture.
• Maintaining the register and meaning between the source and target versions.
• Accurately translating idiomatic expressions, ensuring correct grammar, and ensuring appropri‐
ateness for the target population.
Student test instruments trend comparison
A total of six modules for the student test instruments (four CIL two CT) were carried over from the
previous ICILS cycle. These modules, known as “trend modules,” were administered across all countries
participating in ICILS 2023 (as applicable for the optional CT modules). They were used to measure
changes in achievement for those countries that participated in both ICILS 2018 and ICILS 2023. To
ensure the quality of comparisons over time, countries that participated in the 2018 cycle were asked
to maintain, to the extent possible, the translations they used earlier.
The list of countries and modules administered in ICILS 2018 are listed in Table 5.3. Please note that
some countries that participated in the CT module for 2023 did not participate in this option for the
2018 cycle.
A total of two countries participated in ICILS 2013 and ICILS 2023 but not in 2018 (Slovak Republic
and Slovenia). Please note that since these countries did not participate in the 2018 cycle, trend was
considered as lost for these cases.

ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
For countries that participated in ICILS 2018, the existing translations of the modules from the previous
cycle were made available within the AM Designer upon the release of the international version of the
materials. Countries were responsible for reviewing the trend translations and, only when absolutely
necessary, requesting and implementing changes to these (e.g., update of a national adaptation or
linguistic correction of a translation due to technical errors or mistranslations in 2018). Requested
changes to trend translations were required to be carefully documented in the Trend Verification Form
(TVF) and were reviewed by the ISC during translation verification and considered during the data
adjudication.
Trend verification form
The trend verification form (TVF) was an Excel file with a similar structure to the NAF. The TVF was
created to keep track of changes in the translations from the previous to the current study cycle for
the student test instruments (4 CIL modules and 2 CT modules from ICILS 2018) and therefore only
needed to be completed by countries that participated in ICILS 2018 for the CIL and the optional CT
modules.
The TVF consisted of individual worksheets for the CIL modules and CT modules (where applicable) and
a separate worksheet with completion instructions. NRCs were instructed to document any requested
changes to translations from the materials administered in 2018. To request a change, NRCs needed
to provide the translation as it was in 2018, the suggested new translation for ICILS 2023, the English
back translation of the proposed change, the location of the translation, the type of change (outdated
national adaptation, outright mistranslation, missing text, translation not being correctly copied over
or misspelling) and the justification of the change in separate columns of the TVF.
During translation verification, the international translation verifiers reviewed the TVF and provided
feedback on whether the proposed change to translation was necessary or not. In addition, the verifiers
documented any additional changes to the trend materials not listed in the TVF. Based on the verifier’s
feedback, the ISC advised the NRC on whether or not the suggested change should be implemented.
The final decision to keep or reject the change to trend translation was made by NRCs, considering
the comments provided by the ISC and verifiers during the translation verification process.
Layout preparations
The final step in the country’s preparation of national instruments was reviewing the layout of the
materials. NRCs were asked to review both the student instruments (within the AM Designer) and the
adult questionnaires (within OSS) to ensure that the content was accurate, the layout closely matched
the international version, and the functionality worked as intended.
For all instruments, NRCs were asked to check the following:
• The content was accurate and matched the translations entered.
• No adaptation brackets or HTML tags were visible.
• National extensions were displayed appropriately.
• All content was displayed correctly (e.g., text did not overflow outside boxes or pages).
• Functionality was working as expected.
For the review of the adult questionnaires, NRCs were asked to transfer their final translations to OSS
and document any layout related issues within the layout verification tab of the adult questionnaire
NAF. Upon completion of this step, NRCs were required to submit the completed NAF to the ISC
together with the final version of the Word and OSS files.
For the review of the student instruments, NRCs received access to a URL that contained all assembled
modules to support the layout review of the materials. Additionally, NRCs could make use of the full

PREPARATION AND VERIFICATION OF THE ICILS 2023 STUDY INSTRUMENTS
65
and live previews available within the AM Designer module.
While reviewing the layout of the student instruments, NRCs were required to flag, describe and cat‐
egorize within the AM Designer any layout verification issues by entering a comment linked to the
affected text element.
Countries used the following categories to report layout issues:
• Missing/Unreadable Text: Examples include elements completely or partly missing on the screen
or unreadable due to, for example, an overlap with other text elements.
• Text Out‐of‐Bounds: Examples include text not placed within boundaries.
• Misplaced Element: Such as text or images not appearing where they should, compared to the
international source version.
• Other: Used to report any other type of issue not covered by the previous categories or in case
of a combination of categories. For these cases provision of a detailed and clear description of
the issue was required from the country.
Once the comments were entered in the system, countries set the status for all student instrument
materials to “Ready for LV” status and informed the ISC that the materials were ready for review.
5.3 International verification processes
After the international instruments were adapted, translated, and internally reviewed by the countries,
the national versions of the instruments were submitted for international verification, which consisted
of a rigorous three‐part verification process: (1) adaptation verification, (2) translation verification, and
(3) layout verification.
Adaptation verification
NRCs were required to consult with ISC staff for a review of all proposed national adaptations. The ISC
particularly emphasized the need for NRCs to discuss and document any adaptation that might result
in a serious deviation from the international instruments, as well as providing recoding instructions
for structurally adapted questions. Properly documented and verified recoding instructions were of
utmost importance, particularly during data processing, where they were implemented to ensure in‐
ternational comparability of nationally adapted questions or question components. This step required
NRCs to map data collected in structurally adapted national items to the international items, with their
correspondent internationally comparable value schemes. This information was then used in analysis
and reporting.
National centers began completing the student instrument and adult questionnaire NAFs after review‐
ing the international version of the instruments. They then submitted the NAF for review by the ISC.
Following its review, the ISC provided the national centers with feedback on their requested adap‐
tations and, where appropriate, made suggestions for better alignment to the international version.
Discussions regarding the adaptation and final status were reported across different columns of the
NAF.
Common issues identified during review of adaptations included:
• Inconsistent use of adaptations within or across modules and questionnaires;
• Fictional names for software or technologies not considered to be equivalent to the source ver‐
sion; and
• Difficulties in establishing country‐appropriate adaptations for ISCED levels.
The student instrument and adult questionnaire NAFs were finalized only after the ISC and national

66
ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
study center reached an agreement on all adaptations. Once finalized, any updates were incorporated
into a new version of the NAF, which was subsequently provided for translation verification.
Translation verification
The national versions of the ICILS 2023 instruments underwent international translation verification.
During this step the ISC and cApStAn coordinated in the processing of the materials.
Whereas the ISC was responsible for the training of international translation verifiers, initial review of
the country translations, processing of the translation verifier’s feedback and returning the materials
back to the country, cApStAn was responsible for the selection, in depth training of the translation
verifiers, processing of the verifier materials (before being sent to the verifier or returned to the ISC),
and overall coordination and communication of the verification processes with the ISC.
The international translation verifiers were responsible for reviewing and documenting the quality and
comparability of the national instruments with the international source version of the instruments.
Verifiers were required to be professional translators or interpreters. In addition, they were expected
to meet the following qualifications:
• Native speakers of the target language;
• Fluency in English;
• Familiar with the subject area (if possible);
• Located in the target country or in close contact with it;
• A good level of computer literacy; and
• Trained by the ISC and cApStAn.
Verifiers’ were trained during an online webinar, where they received detailed information about the
study and study instruments, their tasks and role, the verification steps, the materials to be used during
verification (NAF, TVF, MS Word Files) as well as the use of the AM Designer for the review of the
student instruments. In addition to this, verifiers received access to relevant manuals and instruments,
as well as a comprehensive set of instructions and relevant examples for reviewing the national instru‐
ments and documenting deviations from the international source version. The ISC and cApStAn also
ensured all international translation verifiers received continuous support and provided the verifiers
with constructive ongoing feedback as needed.
There were three modes of translation verification in the ICILS 2023 main survey: full verification of
new translations, focused verification of translations which were already verified for the field trial or
done for English speaking countries, and trend verification of trend translations (only applicable to the
CIL/CT modules).
The international translation verification process included the following:
• Checking the accuracy, linguistic correctness, and comparability of the translations and adapta‐
tions of the student instruments and adult questionnaires. This included verifying the correct
implementation of national adaptations, ensuring the consistent use of words and terms listed in
the style guide, and confirming alignment with previously used translations (where applicable);
• Documenting any deviations between the national and international source versions, or between
new and previously used translations, such as additions, deletions, and mistranslations; and
• Proposing alternative translations or adaptations to enhance the accuracy and comparability of
the national instruments.

PREPARATION AND VERIFICATION OF THE ICILS 2023 STUDY INSTRUMENTS
67
Translation verifiers provided their feedback within the AM Designer (for student instruments), the MS
Word documents (adult questionnaires) and the NAFs.
For the adult questionnaires, verifiers were asked to correct the translations and/or to add comments
specifying errors using the edit and comment functions within the Word File. Changes were imple‐
mented in track changes to facilitate the NRC review.
For the student instruments, verifiers were asked to correct the translations and/or to add comments
specifying errors using the edit and comment functions within the AM Designer. Corrections or sug‐
gestions could be applied directly to the text elements in the online environment. Any changes made
by the verifier were displayed as track changes in the text element. Verifiers were required to docu‐
ment their corrections and suggestions by using the comment function for every affected text element
to describe the issue (see Figure 5.3).
Figure 5.3: Track changes after translation verifier edits
For the review of national adaptations and to report severe translation issues, verifiers were required
to enter comments in the NAF. Verifiers also used a style guide included in the NAF to ensure that
specific country linguistic requirements were correctly implemented throughout.
For student test instruments undergoing trend verification, verifiers focused on checking the identi‐
calness between 2018 and 2023 translations; checking the adequacy and correct implementation of
proposed changes to trend translations documented in the TVF; and recording any undocumented
differences in trend translations.
In order to document the comparability between the translated materials and the international version,
verifiers used a range of severity codes to indicate the nature of any issues that they may have found
as well as their severity. The verifiers used the following codes:
• Code 1: Major change or error. Examples include incorrect translations that change the meaning
or difficulty of the text, additional or omitted information, and undocumented changes to trend
translations. When in doubt over the severity of an error or when the verifier was unsure of
how to correct a possible error, Code 1? was used.
• Code 2: Minor change or error. Examples include spelling mistakes and punctuation errors that
do not affect comprehension.
• Code 3: Suggestion for an alternative. The translation may be adequate, but the verifier suggests
a different wording.
• Code OK: Acceptable adaptation. The adaptation (either required or country‐specific) was ac‐
ceptable and appropriate.
After translation verification, the ISC reviewed the verifiers’ feedback and returned the materials to the

68
ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
national centers, namely the updated versions of the NAF, TVF, and MS Word files (for the adult ques‐
tionnaires). Changes to the student instruments within the AM Designer were available to countries
immediately after translation verification.
NRCs were responsible for finalizing their instruments and thus had to review the feedback carefully
and consider the corrections and suggestions made by the verifier. The NRCs were required to review
and resolve every comment the verifier made within the AM Designer, MS Word files, the NAF, and
TVFs (where applicable) by accepting, modifying, or rejecting the verifier’s suggestion. For the latter
two options, NRCs had to give an explanation for why they changed or disagreed with the suggestion.
For issues involving adaptation implementation and trend translations, NRCs were further requested to
discuss any undocumented or unclear changes with the ISC. These discussions were recorded within
the final version of the NAF and/or TVF, which was then submitted for the final step of international
review, layout verification.
During translation verification, some of the most common errors identified by the verifiers included:
• Translations left in the source version (English)
• Mistranslations
• Omissions/additions of text
• Inconsistent translations within or across modules/questionnaires
• Grammatical errors
• Adaptations not implemented as documented by the country.
Due to some technical issues within the AM Designer, some translations were reverted to previous
translations or the English source version during the main survey. This occurred at different stages of
the instrument preparation process and affected countries’ translations. Some of these reversions re‐
quired additional checks, occasionally after the materials were verified and considered as final. In those
cases, additional checks were done to ensure that translations remained as originally implemented by
the country.
Countries experienced some difficulties in the adaptation and translation of some specific terms that
related to technologies within the context questionnaires and assessment materials. Examples of this
includes the familiarity of students with English/target language technological terms, the adaptation or
translation of specific software tools both for assessment instruments and context questionnaires (such
as Limnu, Stormboard, Google Jamboard, Microsoft Whiteboard) as well as the adaptation constructs
that related to the CT modules (such as commands or code blocks). Any adaptations reported in the
NAF were also reviewed by the verifiers, who were asked to comment on their adequacy.
Once translation verification was completed, NRCs were asked to implement the verifier’s feedback.
If there were any adaptations or translations that required final approval from the ISC, these were
discussed and reported in the final version of the NAF.
Layout verification
After finalizing the translations, NRCs were required to review the layout of the student instruments
and adult questionnaires before submitting them for layout verification. The primary purpose of this
verification step was to ensure that the materials were displayed correctly, maintained the correct
structure and functionality, and, most importantly, remained comparable to the international source.
For the student instruments, layout verification took place within the AM Designer. For the adult
questionnaires, countries were required to enter their final translations within OSS. In addition to this,
countries submitted an additional version of the NAF with comments on layout issues that needed to
be resolved with the support of the ISC.

PREPARATION AND VERIFICATION OF THE ICILS 2023 STUDY INSTRUMENTS
69
For the layout verification of the student instruments, the preview of the national materials in the AM
Designer was checked against the international version to ensure identical layout and functionality.
During this review, issues reported by the country in the NAF’s layout verification comments worksheet
were examined, and any additional errors identified by ISC verifiers were documented. Once layout
issues were compiled, they were either resolved by the verifiers when possible or forwarded to RM for
resolution. A response was added to the layout verification comments once the issue was addressed.
Countries were then asked to review the materials and confirm their readiness before the ISC created
a USB player or made the student instruments available online.
The most common layout issues that were identified in the student instruments were:
• Missing or additional line breaks
• Text not fitting within the pre‐defined spaces
• Display of HTML tags
• Issues specific to non‐Latin alphabet languages such as the need for using different sizes or fonts
to correctly display special characters or for better readability
• Incorrect display of left‐to‐right languages
• Extra spaces
• Issues with structural changes to national content in the questionnaires.
For the layout verification of the adult questionnaires, verifiers reviewed the layout of the national
online adult questionnaires by comparing the OSS version with the Word files (which contained the
final translations). During this review, the issues reported by the country within the NAF were ex‐
amined and resolved. Any other additional errors identified by the verifiers were corrected and also
documented within the NAF for countries’ information. This step also included verifying the correct
technical implementation of national structural adaptations in the OSS software. Countries were then
asked to review the final questionnaires online and confirm their readiness.


Chapter 6:
Sampling design and implementation
Sabine Tieck
6.1 Introduction
International comparative surveys require randomly drawn samples to ensure valid inferences from
the observed (sampled) data to the population characteristics under study. Random selection of study
participants is a key criterion for achieving international comparability and comparability between sub‐
national entities. ICILS follows an international study design that meets all Technical Standards for IEA
Studies (Martin et al., 1999) to ensure high sampling quality.
This chapter introduces the sampling design and implementation of the ICILS 2023 student, teacher,
and school surveys, which are largely consistent with previous cycles of ICILS. Therefore, this chapter
is largely based on Chapter 6 of the ICILS 2018 technical report (Tieck, 2020), which in turn was based
on Chapter 6 of the ICILS 2013 technical report (Meinck, 2015). However, an important modification
was made in the within‐school sampling procedure for the student survey: instead of selecting stu‐
dents directly from a comprehensive list of all target grade students, entire classes within the target
grade were sampled. In the first two cycles of ICILS, the ICILS International Study Center (ISC) was
concerned that school infrastructure (in particular the number of co‐located computers and the qual‐
ity of internet access) may prevent more than 20 students from simultaneously completing the ICILS
instruments in some schools. The change of within‐school sampling method for ICILS 2023 was made
possible by improvements in school‐level ICT resources over time, and reduced the administrative and
organizational level within schools. It is the same within‐school sampling approach used in other IEA
studies which over time, have transitioned form paper‐based to computer‐based delivery. It is beyond
the scope of the ICILS design to measure the exact impact of this change in the school‐level sampling
approach on the data. The impact of the change would vary across countries depending on how stu‐
dents are allocated to classes within countries. Given the overall sampling design in ICILS, it is likely that
the change in design would not affect estimates of scale means within countries (relative to previous
cycles). However, it may have a very small impact on the estimates of variance on the assumption that
the variation of achievement among students within classes in schools may be less than the variation
in achievement of students within grade levels in schools.
The ICILS 2023 sample design is considered a “complex” design because it incorporates multi‐stage
sampling, stratification, and cluster sampling. This chapter describes these features in detail, along
with information on target population definitions, design features, sample sizes, and achieved design
efficiency. While the focus is on the international standard sampling design, specific characteristics of
each national sampling plan are presented in B.
The samples of schools, students, and teachers in each ICILS country and education system were se‐
lected or verified by IEA Hamburg in collaboration with the National Research Coordinators (NRCs) of
each participating country or education system. NRCs received support for their sampling activities
through a series of manuals and the IEA Windows Within‐School Sampling Software (WinW3S). Addi‐
tionally, the sampling referee, Marc Joncas, provided guidance on sampling methodology and reviewed
and adjudicated all samples for this study.
6.2 Target population definitions
In cross‐country comparative surveys, it is essential to clearly define the target population(s) under
study. ICILS collected data from students, their teachers, and their schools, requiring precise definitions
for all three populations. These definitions enabled ICILS NRCs to accurately identify and list the

72
ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
targeted schools, students (i.e., target grade classes), and teachers from which samples were drawn.
Definition: Student Target Population
ICILS defined the target population of students as follows:
The student target population in ICILS consists of all students enrolled in the grade that represents
eight years of schooling, counting from the first year of ISCED level 1,a providing the mean age at
the time of testing is at least 13.5 years. Students older than 17 years are not part of the target
population.
a ISCED stands for International Standard Classification of Education (UNESCO Institute for Statistics, 2012).
For most countries, the target grade was grade 8 or its national equivalent. To ensure international
comparability, the ICILS NRCs had to specify their country’s legal school entry age, the name of the
target grade, and an estimate of the mean age of the students in that grade. Two countries devi‐
ated from the international target grade. Norway tested their grade 9 students, which is reflected by
including the tested grade in the country name for reporting, and Romania followed the international
population definition but decided to survey their grade 8 students (and their teachers) at the beginning
of the (next) school year. Due to this deviation, results were annotated accordingly in the international
report.
Hereafter, the term “students” is used to describe “students in the ICILS target population.”
Definition: Teacher Target Population
ICILS 2023 defined the target population of teachers as follows:
Teachers are defined as school staff members who provide student instruction through the delivery
of lessons to students. Teachers may work with students as a whole class in a classroom, in small
groups in resource rooms, or one‐to‐one inside or outside of classrooms.
The teacher target population in ICILS consists of all teachers teaching regular school subjects to
students of the target grade (regardless of the subject or the number of hours taught) during the
ICILS testing period and have been employed at school since the beginning of the school year, i.e.,
within the first four weeks of the school year.a
a Greece included also teachers, who joined within the first two months after the beginning of the school year as
this happens quite frequently.
School staff from the following categories were not regarded as part of the target teacher population
(i.e., were out of scope):
• Any school staff that attend to the needs of target grade students but do not teach any lessons
(e.g., psychological counselors, chaplains);
• Assistant teachers and parent‐helpers;
• Non‐staff teachers who teach (non‐compulsory) subjects that are not part in the curriculum (e.g.,
cases where religion is not a regular subject and taught by external persons);
• Teachers who have joined a school in the fifth week (or later) of the school year; and
• Teachers that are on a long‐term leave during the testing period (e.g., maternity or sabbatical
leave).
Hereafter the term “teachers” is used to describe “teachers in the ICILS target population.”

SAMPLING DESIGN AND IMPLEMENTATION
73
Definition: Schools
In ICILS 2023 schools were defined as follows:
A school is one whole unit with a defined number of teachers and students, which can include
different programs or tracks. The definition of ‘school’ should be based on the environment that is
shared by students, which is usually a shared faculty, set of buildings, social space and also often
includes a shared administration and charter.
Schools eligible for ICILS are those at which target grade students are enrolled.
In order to ensure international comparability, the definition of “school” should be equivalent in all
participating countries. In most cases, identifying schools for sampling purposes in ICILS was straight‐
forward. However, there were some cases where identification of schools for sampling purposes was
more difficult. National centers were provided with the following examples in order to help them
identify sampling units:
• A multi‐shift school that consists of two units that share the same building should be regarded
as two different schools as long as these shifts operate as different schools (with different staff)
and share only the same building (and administrative unit). Both units should be listed as schools
in the School Sampling Frame;
• A multi‐shift school in which students are assigned to different shifts but otherwise functions as
a single entity (in terms of staffing, enrollment, and administration) should be regarded as one
school and should be listed only once in the frame;
• The parts of a campus school (larger administrative schools with sub‐units scattered around cities
or regions) should be regarded as different schools for sampling. However, if a part of a campus
school is selected for ICILS, the principal and the ICT coordinator of the combined school will
be asked to fill out the school questionnaire and will need to be instructed to respond only with
respect to the sampled sub‐unit;
• Schools that consist of two administrative units but have shared staff shared buildings, and offer
some opportunities for the students to change from one school to the other should be regarded
as one combined school;
• The parts of a school with two or more different study programs (e.g., a vocational program and
an academic program) that have different teaching staff, are conducted in different buildings and
offer no or very little opportunity for students to change from one study program to the other,
should be regarded as two or more different schools for sampling. Those study programs should
be listed in the School Sampling Frame; and
• Schools that provide distance learning to students of the target population on a regular basis are
considered to be schools according to the above definition.
Schools that are not intended to be included in ICILS are considered out of scope. Examples include:
• Schools without students in the target grade; and
• Schools in which all students in the target grade do not fit the age definition for students in ICILS,
e.g., evening schools for adults.
6.3 Coverage and exclusions
The ISC encouraged participating countries to include all schools, students, and teachers defined in the
international target populations in the study in order to ensure full coverage of these target populations,
see figure 6.1. For ICILS 2023, in all but one country, the international desired target population equals
the national desired target population, in other words, all parts of the population were covered, see
below for details.

74
ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
Figure 6.1: Target Population ‐ Internationals desired to National defined
However, it was deemed appropriate to exclude some schools, students, and teachers from the respec‐
tive target population for practical reasons, such as difficult test conditions or prohibitive survey costs,
e.g., in remote regions. Some students and teachers were not surveyed due to the exclusion of their
entire school from the sampling frame (school‐level exclusions). Additionally, some classes or students
(but not teachers) were excluded from participating schools (within‐sample exclusions). The amount
of the overall exclusion rate for the student population is limited to five percent after rounding to the
nearest integer. Countries exceeding this limit are annotated in the respective tables of the interna‐
tional report. Fifteen countries had an overall exclusion rate between five percent and ten percent of
their respective national desired target population.
As in other large‐scale assessments, it should be emphasized that the ICILS 2023 sample data repre‐
sents the nationally defined target populations only, i.e., without the excluded members of the nation‐
ally desired target population.
Population coverage
If a significant portion of the international desired target population must be excluded, it is reported
as reduced coverage. This typically occurs when certain regions cannot be assessed (e.g., war zones)
or specific types of schools are not included (e.g., minority language schools). In ICILS 2023, all but
one country achieved full coverage of their nationally desired target populations. Bosnia and Herze‐
govina has a reduced coverage of 60.8 percent because students from the Republika Srpska did not
participate. The sample for Bosnia and Herzegovina includes students from the Federation of Bosnia
and Herzegovina and the Brčko District.
Exclusions of schools
In ICILS 2023 in most countries, very small schools and schools exclusively dedicated to students
with special needs students were excluded. Frequently, schools providing lessons in other language of
instructions or following a curriculum that differed from the mainstream curriculum were also not part
of the nationally defined target population.
Table 6.1 provides an overview of the typical school‐level exclusions applied in ICILS 2023, along
with their respective percentages relative to the desired national school target population in each
participating country. Schools identified for exclusion were removed from the school sampling frame
before the sampling process. The percentage of excluded schools (ERsch) were calculated as:
ERsch = Esch

SAMPLING DESIGN AND IMPLEMENTATION
75
where Esch denotes the number of schools excluded prior to sample selection, and TPsch is the total
number of schools belonging to the national desired target population. The respective figures were
provided by the NRCs.
Because school‐level data (collected via the principal and ICT coordinator questionnaires) in the ICILS
2023 survey were only used to complement the reporting of student‐ and teacher‐level data, no spe‐
cific thresholds were determined for exclusions at the school level. However, the percentages of stu‐
dents and teachers excluded due to the removal of entire schools were considered when determining
the overall proportions of students (see below).
School exclusions varied significantly across countries, which should be considered when interpreting
results based on school‐level data. Additionally, since school exclusions typically affect smaller schools,
the percentage of excluded schools tends to be higher than the corresponding percentages of excluded
students or teachers.

SAMPLING DESIGN AND IMPLEMENTATION
77
Please note that the school‐level exclusion rate for the student population is calculated as the propor‐
tion of students in the excluded schools. In addition, the proportion of students in very small schools
must not exceed two percent of the national desired target population. Therefore, the definition of a
very small school depends on the country’s data.
In rare cases, schools that should have been excluded were not marked as such in the school frame
and were therefore not removed prior to school sampling and were not included in the school‐level
exclusion rate. If such schools were sampled, they contributed to the within‐sample exclusion rate.
Student‐level exclusions
Students can be excluded by excluding schools (school‐level exclusion) and they can be excluded within
participating schools (within‐sample exclusion). However, within‐sample exclusion should be minimized
and is permitted according to the following international within‐school exclusion rules:
• Students with an intellectual disability – These are students who are considered, in the profes‐
sional opinion of the school principal or by other qualified staff members, to have an intellectual
disability or who have been tested psychologically and reported as having an intellectual disabil‐
ity. This includes students who are emotionally or mentally unable to follow even the general
instructions of the test. Students should not be excluded solely because of poor academic per‐
formance or normal disciplinary problems;
• Students with a functional disability – These are students who have a permanent physical dis‐
ability that prevents them from participating in the ICILS testing. Functionally disabled students
who are able to respond should be included in the testing, and
• Non‐native language speakers – These are students who are unable to read or speak the lan‐
guage(s) of the test and would be unable to overcome the language barrier of the test. Typically,
a student who has received less than one year of instruction in the language(s) of the test should
be excluded, but this definition may need to be adapted in different countries.
In the case where classes within sampled schools consist entirely of students who belong to one of
the exclusion categories, these classes can be excluded prior to class sampling.
Finally, the students’ overall exclusion rate is computed as the sum of the students’ school‐level ex‐
clusion rate and the weighted within‐sample exclusion rate. Table 6.1 and B of this report provide
details about the exclusion types for each country. Regarding the overall rate of excluded students,
each country was required to keep the overall rate (due to school‐level and within‐sample exclusions)
below five percent (after rounding) of the respective desired student target population.
Table 6.2 provides the percentages of coverage and exclusions for the ICILS 2023 student population
for each country participating in ICILS 2023. In 15 education systems participating in ICILS 2023,
the overall student exclusion rate was above five percent but less than ten percent, which resulted in
respective annotations in the ICILS 2023 international report (Fraillon, 2024). Why such a high number
of participating countries exceeded the allowed is not part of this chapter. One country, Bosnia and
Herzegovina, covered only 61 percent of their entire student population. Of this remaining student
population, another five percent of students were excluded.

*Within‐sample exclusion rates are estimates based on sample data. Some participating countries reported an increasing number

SAMPLING DESIGN AND IMPLEMENTATION
79
The student’s school‐level exclusions consisted of those students belonging to schools which were
excluded prior to the school sampling. The students’ school‐level exclusion rate ER1 was calculated
as:
ER1 = E1
TP ,
(6.2)
where E1 denotes the number of target grade students in excluded schools, and TP is the total number
of students belonging to the national desired target population. The respective figures were provided
by the NRCs.
The students within‐sample exclusions were based on information collected from the sample (i.e., after
the school sampling step). The within‐sample exclusions consisted of students (in regular or excluded
classes) with functional or intellectual disabilities or non‐native language speakers who who are unable
to read or speak the language(s) of the test. Complete classes with to‐be‐excluded students only could
be excluded prior to the within‐school sampling; single students could be excluded after the within‐
school sampling (class sampling) was performed.12 The percentage of student within‐sample exclusions
was calculated using the estimated number of students excluded within sampled schools and the total
number of students belonging to the national desired target population.
The students’ within‐sample exclusions ER2 were computed as:
ER2 =
W E2
W P + W E2 × (1 −ER1),
(6.3)
where W E2 is the sum of weights of excluded students identified in the sample and W P is the sum
of weights of participating students. Therefore, W P + W E2 denotes the (estimated) total number of
students belonging to the nationally desired target population. To make inferences not only to the
sample, but to the student population the students’ school‐level exclusion rate is taken into account
by multiplying the fraction by 1 −ER1.
The overall exclusion rate of students ERtot is the sum of the students’ school‐level exclusion rate and
the weighted within‐sample exclusion rate:
ERtot = ER1 + ER2.
(6.4)
Teacher‐level exclusions
Teachers working in excluded schools were not part of the nationally defined target population. Within
participating schools, all teachers who met the target population definition were eligible for participa‐
tion in the survey.
Each country was asked to provide information on the total number of teachers teaching in the target
grade, as well as the proportion of these teachers in excluded schools. For 19 countries, no statistics on
the number of eligible ICILS 2023 teachers were available, making it impossible to compute exclusion
rates. Teacher data were included in the ICILS 2023 international report (Fraillon, 2024) only if the
student school‐level exclusion rate exceeded five percent after rounding. In such cases, it is expected
that more than five percent of teachers were also excluded.
12 In some cases, these students were grouped in classes, which were then excluded as a group, or the sample schools
were found to have only enrolled students within the exclusion categories, which resulted in the corresponding school(s)
being excluded ex‐post.

ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
6.4 School sampling design
For all ICILS 2023 countries, a stratified two‐stage probability cluster sampling design was used to
conduct school sample selection. In the first stage, schools were systematically selected with probabil‐
ities proportional to their size (PPS), where ‘school size’ was measured by the total number of enrolled
target grade students. In the second stage, within participating schools, one or more classes from the
target grade were selected using a systematic simple random sampling approach. All students in the
selected classes were invited to participate.
The following subsections provide further details on the school sample design for ICILS 2023.
School sampling frame
In order to prepare the selection of school samples, national centers provided a comprehensive list of
schools, including the number of students enrolled in the target grade. This list is referred to as the
school sampling frame. To ensure that each ICILS 2023 school sampling frame provided complete cov‐
erage of the desired target population, the sampling team carefully checked and verified the plausibility
of the information by comparing it with official statistics.
The sampling team required the following information for each school in the sampling frame:
• Random school IDs,13 which are unique and used solely for the purpose of the sample selection—
for instance, a sequential number;
• School’s measure of size (MOS), which was usually the number of students enrolled in the target
grade or an adjacent grade;
• Values for each of the intended stratification variables; and
• A school exclusion variable, which indicates the schools to be excluded.
Stratification of schools
Stratification is part of many sampling designs and entails grouping sampling frame units by common
characteristics. Examples of such groups of units (schools in the case of ICILS 2023) would be perfor‐
mance level, administration type, geographic region, urbanization level, or source of funding.
ICILS 2023 applied two different methods of stratification. Implicit stratification means that the sam‐
pling frame has been sorted, prior to sampling, by implicit stratification variables, thus providing a simple
and straightforward method to achieve a fairly proportional sample allocation across all strata. With
explicit stratification, independent samples of schools are selected from each explicit stratum. The
sample sizes for each explicit stratum are assigned before the selection process in order to achieve the
desired sample precision overall and, where required, also for subpopulations.
In summary, IEA studies use stratification for the following reasons:
• To improve the efficiency of the sample design, i.e., reducing standard errors, thereby making
survey estimates more reliable. For this, national centers identify stratification variables expected
to be closely associated with students’ learning outcomes. This can be achieved using either
implicit or explicit stratification.
• To apply optimized sample designs, such as disproportionate sample allocations to specific groups
of schools (e.g., states or provinces). This is particularly important when subgroup estimates are
required by the national analysis plan. Explicit stratification is used for this purpose.
• To ensure a proportional representation of specific groups of interest (domains) of the target
population in the sample. This is achieved by applying implicit stratification.
13 Due to GDPR, national school IDs cannot be used anymore within Europe and shouldn’t be used in general.

SAMPLING DESIGN AND IMPLEMENTATION
81
The latter design feature was used if the country required estimates with higher precision levels for
specific subgroups of interest in the target population. For example, a country may wish to compare
public and private schools, but only 10 percent of the students in that country attend schools in the
latter category. In such a case, a proportional sample allocation would result in having too few private
schools in the sample to provide reliable estimates for this particular subpopulation, and even larger
differences between the two different school types might not appear as statistically significant.
To allow comparisons with sufficient statistical power without introducing any sample bias, an ap‐
propriately larger sample of private schools could be selected while keeping the original proportional
sample allocation for public schools the same. In the example above (with a distribution of 10 percent
of students in private and 90 percent in public schools), a proportional sample allocation for a selection
of 150 schools would lead to a sample of 15 private and 135 public schools. Through oversampling, it
would be possible to increase the number of private schools to a sufficiently large number (for example,
by selecting 50 private and 135 public schools).
Each country applied different stratification schemes following discussions with IEA sampling experts.
Table 6.3 provides details about the stratification variables used.

Note: Please note that the number of explicit strata in the third column is not automatically the product of the levels of the
stratification variables in column 2. For example, a country with two explicit stratification variables (region ‐ five level, and
urbanization ‐ two level) ends up with eight strata as one region has only school located in urban environment, while another
region has only schools in rural environment. For country details on the applied stratification, see Appendix B.
School sample selection
To select school samples for the ICILS 2023 main survey, the sampling team used stratified PPS sys‐
tematic sampling. This method is standard in most large‐scale educational surveys, including most IEA
surveys. Under ideal conditions—that is, in the absence of non‐response and disproportional sampling
of subpopulations—this method would lead to “self‐weighted” samples where all units sampled in the
last stage of sampling have a similar probability of selection. In reality, however, not a single ICILS
sample was self‐weighting. First, note that a sampling design can only be self‐weighting for one target
population, and ICILS is aimed at self‐weighted samples of students. In turn, and by design, the sam‐
ples of schools and teachers cannot be self‐weighting. Second, for most countries, the implemented
design actually guaranteed that the samples were not self‐weighting because explicit stratification was
used.14
General school sample selection involved the following steps:
1. Splitting the school sampling frame containing all eligible schools into separate frames according
to the defined, explicit strata unless no explicit stratification was used. In the latter, all schools
from the school sampling frame were kept in one explicit stratum. All of the following steps were
done independently within each explicit stratum.
2. Sorting the schools by implicit strata and by MOS (alternately sorted in increasing and decreasing
order).
3. Calculating a sampling interval (SI) by dividing the total MOS by the number of schools to be
sampled from that explicit stratum.
4. Identify the first sampled school by multiplying the sampling interval with a random number
(=random start).
5. For the nth school to be selected, calculate the value for selection by adding (n −1) ∗SI to the
random start. Whenever the cumulated MOS is equal to or above the value for selection, the
corresponding school was included in the sample.
14 When using explicit stratification, sampling allocation can hardly ever be exactly proportional for different explicit
strata. In addition, for really small explicit strata, a minimum number of schools needs to be sampled, which then leads to
the oversampling of such schools.

84
ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
Figure 6.2 visualizes the process of systematic PPS sampling within an explicit stratum. In this diagram,
the schools in the sampling frame are sorted descending by MOS, and the height of the cells reflects
the number of target grade students in each school. A random start determines the second school
in the list for selection, and a constant sampling interval determines the next sampled schools. Cells
with sampled schools are shown as shaded. Joncas and Foy (2012) provide a more comprehensive
description of the sampling process using an illustrative example.
Figure 6.2: Visualization of PPS systematic sampling.
In the following specific cases, the sampling design deviated from this general procedure:
• Small schools were selected with equal selection probabilities to avoid large variations of sam‐
pling weights due to expected changing size measures. A school was regarded as “small” if the
number of target grade students was less than the minimum cluster size15 in the respective
explicit stratum.
• Very large schools (i.e., schools with more students than the value of the sampling interval) were
temporarily removed from the explicit stratum and selected with certainty (i.e., all schools in this
category were included in the sample).
All countries conducted a field trial with a small sample of schools one year before the ICILS main
survey. Where possible it is preferable that any given school is not selected to participate in both the
the field trial and the main survey data collection. This is because selection in both the field trial and
main survey may reduce the likelihood of a school agreeing to participate and also because there may
be some information sharing within a school about the contents of the instruments that could influence
the data collected in the main survey. In ICILS, this was prevented by selecting the sample of schools
to participate in the field trial and the main survey simultaneously (as part of a single larger sampling
procedure) in each country. For example, if a country was planning to sample 25 schools in the field
trial and 150 schools in the main survey, then a single combined sample of 175 schools was selected
first. Then, from these schools, the main survey sample of 150 schools was subsampled, leaving the
remaining (25) schools for the field trial sample.
ICILS 2023 was conducted in the same year as the IEA Trends in International Mathematics and Science
15 The minimum cluster size is calculated as the average class size of all schools with more than one class.

SAMPLING DESIGN AND IMPLEMENTATION
85
Study (TIMSS) 2023. A lot of countries requested that schools be selected for one of these studies only
to reduce the administrative burden for the schools. The ICILS 2023 sampling team collaborated closely
with the sampling colleagues, implementing the sampling for TIMSS 2023 to prevent school sample
overlap whenever possible. The procedures used to prevent school overlap ensured randomness of
selection, known (correct) school selection probabilities, and consequently unbiased samples for all of
these studies. Where it was not possible to achieve distinct school samples for both studies, different
classes were selected randomly for both surveys where possible.
Once schools had been selected from the sampling frame, up to two (non‐sampled) replacement
schools were assigned for each originally sampled school. In order to reduce the risk of non‐response
bias due to replacement, the replacement schools were usually assigned as follows: The school that
appeared directly after a selected school in the sorted sampling frame was assigned as its first re‐
placement, while the preceding school of the sampled school was used as its second replacement.
This ensured that replacement schools shared similar characteristics with the corresponding sampled
schools to which they belonged, were of similar size, and belonged to the same stratum. Please note
that—as in all IEA studies—the use of replacement schools in ICILS 2023 was limited; see ICILS 2023
standards for sampling participation in 7.
6.5 Within‐school sampling design
Within‐school sampling constituted the second stage of the ICILS sampling process. The NRCs or
their appointed data managers carried out the selection of students and teachers. The use of the
WinW3S software, developed by IEA Hamburg, ensured the random selection of classes (and therefore
students) and teachers within the sampled schools. Replacement of non‐responding individuals was
not permitted in ICILS 2023.
Class sampling
WinW3S employed systematic stratified sampling with equal selection probabilities to select one or
more classes from comprehensive lists of classes with target grade students provided by the participat‐
ing schools. Explicit or implicit stratification, e.g., by track, could be applied. This was applied in close
collaboration with the IEA sampling team. In cases where the school was also selected for TIMSS 2023
and has two or more target grade classes, a different class was randomly selected for ICILS 2023.
Teacher sampling
WinW3S also employed systematic stratified sampling with equal selection probabilities to select teach‐
ers from comprehensive lists of in‐scope teachers provided by the participating schools. The procedure
also ensured a sample allocation among subgroups that was near to proportional. To increase sample
precision, teacher lists were implicitly stratified by sorting them according to gender, main subject
domain, and birth year.
6.6 Sample size requirements
The ISC, following standard practice in other IEA studies, set high standards for sampling precision
and aimed to achieve reasonably small standard errors for survey estimates. The student sample was
designed to ensure a specified level of precision for population estimates, defined by confidence in‐
tervals of ±0.1 standard deviation for means and ±5% for percentages. With respect to the main
outcome variable in ICILS 2023—that is, the students’ computer and information literacy (CIL) score
scale established in ICILS 2013 with a mean of 500 score points and a standard deviation of 100 for
equally weighted national samples from the first cycle—this requirement translated into standard er‐
rors that needed to be below five score points. IEA was responsible for determining sample sizes that
were expected to meet these requirements for each participating country. With the exception of three
participating countries (United States, Romania, and Azerbaijan), which failed to meet the IEA sample
participation standards, all participating countries and education systems achieved this requirement
(see Chapter 5 in Fraillon, 2024). The required precision levels of percentages were also met for the
vast majority of population estimates presented in the ICILS international report.

86
ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
There were also other considerations that needed to be taken into account when determining the
required number of sampled students and teachers:
• Some types of analysis, like multilevel modeling, require a minimum number of valid cases at
each sampling stage (see, for example, Mang et al., 2021);
• For the purpose of building scales and sub‐scales, a minimum number of valid entries per re‐
sponse item is required; and
• Reporting on subgroups (e.g., age or gender groups) requires a minimum sample size for each of
the subgroups of interest.
All these considerations were taken into account during the process of defining minimum sample sizes
for schools, students, and teachers.
School sample sizes
The minimum sample size for the ICILS main survey was 150 schools for each country. In some coun‐
tries, it was necessary to select more schools than the minimum sample size due to one or more of the
following reasons:
• Previous student surveys had shown a relatively large variation in student achievement between
schools in a country. In these cases, it was assumed that the IEA standards for sampling precision
could only be met by increasing the school sample size.
• The number of “small schools” in the target grade was relatively large, and it was not possible
to reach the student sample size requirements by selecting only 150 schools (see next section
below).
• The country requested oversampling of particular subgroups of schools to accommodate national
research interests.
Student sample sizes
In most sampled schools, one class was randomly selected from the full list of classes containing target
grade students. Each country was required to achieve a student sample size of approximately 3,000
tested students, which corresponds to around 3,350 to 3,400 sampled students. However, due to
factors such as non‐response, changes in school size, school closures, or other circumstances, some
countries did not reach this target. The ICILS 2023 sampling team did not consider this outcome
problematic as long as the country met the overall participation rate requirements.
Teacher sample sizes
Typically, 15 teachers of the target grade were randomly selected in each sampled school. In schools
with 20 or fewer teachers of the target grade, all teachers were selected. Countries with less than 150
schools were asked to increase the number of teachers to sample in each school.
In summary, the minimum sample size requirements per country for ICILS 2023 were as follows:
• Schools: 150 (or all schools if fewer than 150 exist in the country)
• Classes: at least one class per school
• Students: a total of 3,350 to 3,400 sampled students, aiming for at least 3,000 participating
students
• Teachers: 15 per school (or all, if 20 or fewer)

the achieved teacher sample sizes for each participating country. Note that schools may have been

minimum within‐school response rate requirements. This explains the differences in the number of
participating schools in the student and teacher survey across ICILS 2023 countries.

Chapter 7:
Sampling weights and participation rates
Sabine Tieck
7.1 Introduction
One major objective of ICILS 2023 was to make use of data collected from samples of students,
teachers, and schools within each country (or education system) to make accurate, precise, and in‐
ternationally comparable estimates of population characteristics. In order to achieve this objective, it
is important to consider the particular feature of the study’s complex sample design (see Chapter 6 for
details). Data from complex samples may provide biased population estimates if analysts disregard the
particular features of complex samples and treat data as if they were from a simple random sample.
One particularly important feature of a complex sample is that sampling units do not have equal se‐
lection probabilities. In ICILS 2023, this characteristic applied to the sampled students, teachers, and
schools. Furthermore, non‐participation can potentially lead to bias estimates, and differential pat‐
terns of non‐response increase this risk. To account for these complexities, sampling weights and
non‐response adjustments were computed within each participating country, leading to an estimation
(or “final”) weight for each sampled unit.16 All findings presented in ICILS 2023 reports are based on
weighted data.
This chapter is largely based on Chapter 7 of the ICILS 2018 technical report (Tieck, 2020), which in
turn was based on Chapter 7 of the ICILS 2013 technical report (Meinck & Cortes, 2015). Descrip‐
tions of how the several sets of weights and non‐response adjustments were computed follow. The
conditions under which students, teachers, and schools were judged to be “participants” are described
within the relevant sections describing the weights for each of these groups. Subsequent sections in
this chapter describe the computation of participation rates at each sampling stage, and the minimum
participation requirements. The ICILS 2023 ISC regarded response rates as an important indicator of
data quality and the achieved quality of sample implementation for each country is presented. Please
note that Chapter 13 covers the use of the jackknife replication method (needed for variance estima‐
tion).
7.2 Types of sampling weights
The ICILS 2023 final weights are the product of several weight components. Generally, it is possible to
discriminate between two different types of weight components:
• Base (or design) weights: these reflect selection probabilities of sampled units. They are computed
separately for each sampling stage and therefore account for multiple‐stage sampling designs
(see Chapter 6 for details). The base weight of a sampled unit is the inverse of the product of
the selection probabilities at every stage.
• Non‐response adjustments: these aim to compensate the potential for bias due to nonpartici‐
pation of sampled units. As with base weights, they are computed separately for each sam‐
pling stage. The main function of this weight component is that the (base) weight of the non‐
respondents within a specific adjustment cell is redistributed among the responding units in that
cell. Such an “adjustment cell” contains sampling units that share specific features. For example,
all private schools in a given region could comprise a stratum of schools, from which a sample of
16 For further reading on the topic, we recommend Franklin and Walker (2003), Groves et al. (2004), Meinck (2015), and
Rust (2014).

90
ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
schools was selected. If some of the sampled schools did not participate, then the remaining (par‐
ticipating) schools in this stratum would carry the (base) weight of the non‐participating schools.
This approach allows us to exploit the (usually) little information we have available about respon‐
dents and non‐respondents, and to assume that school non‐participation is associated with the
different strata (see also Lohr, 1999). The approach also assumes a non‐informative response
model, implying that non‐response occurs completely at random within the adjustment cell (i.e.,
in ICILS 2023 within a stratum).
7.3 Calculating weights for the student population
Final student weights were calculated for each eligible and as participating considered student in ICILS
2023. To be considered as “eligible,” the student needs to fit the defined target population, e.g., not
older than 17 years, and he or she must be a student of the sampled class and school. To be considered
as “participating,” the student worked on the assessment, and the class and the school —the student
belongs to— need to be considered as participating, e.g., at least 50 percent of the students in the class
participated. For details on participation requirements, see 7.7. The final student weight comprises of
six weighting components: three base weights and three non‐response adjustment weights.
School base weight (WGTFAC1)
The first sampling stage involved selecting schools in each country: the school base weight reflects the
selection probabilities of this sampling step. As each sampled school is selected for the student, the
teacher, and the school study, its selection probability, and therefore its school base weight, is the same
for the three ICILS 2023 target populations.
When explicit stratification was used, the school samples were selected independently within each
explicit stratum h, with h = 1, …, H. If no explicit strata were formed, the entire country was regarded
as being one explicit stratum.
Systematic random samples of schools were drawn in all countries, with a selection probability of school
i in stratum h proportional to its size (PPS sampling). The measure of school size Mhi was defined by
the number of students in the target grade or an adjacent grade. If schools were smaller than the
minimum cluster size17 (Mhi < MCS), the measure of size Mhi was redefined as the average size of
all small schools in that stratum. Let Mh the total number of students in stratum h and ns
h the number
of schools to select in that stratum.
The school base weight was defined as the inverse of the school’s selection probability. For school i in
stratum h, the school base weight was given by:
WGTFAC1hi =
Mh
ns
h × Mhi
.
(7.1)
In census countries or strata, all schools were selected. Here is WGTFAC1hi = 1 for all schools in
respective countries (Cyprus, Luxembourg, and Malta).
School non‐response adjustment (WGTADJ1S) ‐ student population
School base weights for participating schools needed to be adjusted to account for the loss in overall
sample size from schools that either refused to participate or had to be removed from the international
dataset due to low within‐class participation. Adjustments were calculated within non‐response groups
defined by the explicit strata. A school non‐response adjustment was calculated for each participating
school i within each explicit stratum h as:

anymore, or had only excluded students enrolled. Ineligible schools such as these were not taken into
account when calculating the school non‐response adjustment.
Class base weight (WGTFAC2S)
The class base weight was defined as the inverse of the selection probability of that class. IEA Within‐
School Sampling Software (WinW3S) was used to manage within‐school sampling during the second
sampling stage (see Chapter 6 and Chapter 8 for details) in order to conduct a systematic random
selection of classes from the target grade. The class base weight for a sampled class j in school i in
stratum h was calculated as:
WGTFAC2Shij = chi

Class non‐response adjustment (WGTADJ2S)
In most schools and countries, one class was selected and if this class was considered as not partic‐
ipating (i.e., less than 50% of the students were considered as participating), the respective school
would be considered as not‐participating and the loss would be covered with the school non‐response
adjustment. In this case WGTADJ2S would not be applied to other participating classes.
However, when two or more classes were selected in the second sampling stage and not all of these
classes were participating, but at least one, then participating classes of this school were kept and
the loss of the non‐participating classes was compensated by the class non‐response adjustment. To
minimize the potential bias the adjustment was done over all participating classes within the same
explicit stratum and within the same class group if any were defined. Thus WGTADJShij for each
participating class j in school i in stratum and class group h was calculated as:
WGTADJ2Shij = cs
h
cp
h
,
(7.4)
where cs
h is the total number of sampled classes in stratum and class group h, and cp
h is the number of
participating classes in stratum and class group h.
Student base weight (WGTFAC3S)
Since all eligible students in a sampled class were selected to participate, the student base weight was
WGTFAC3Shijk = 1 for each eligible and participating student.
Student non‐response adjustment (WGTADJ3S)
Not all selected, eligible students were willing or able to participate in ICILS 2023. Given the lack of
information about absentees, non‐participation has to be assumed, for weighting purposes, as being

92
ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
completely random within schools. This means that participating students represent both participating
and non‐participating students. The sampling weights of the participating students had to be adjusted
accordingly. The student non‐response adjustment for each participating student k in class j in school i
in stratum h was calculated as:
WGTADJ3Shijk = se
hij
sp
hij
,
(7.5)
with se

hij being the number of participating students in
the respective class.
In the context of student non‐response adjustment, students of the target population were regarded as
eligible if they had not been excluded due to disabilities or language problems.
Please note that sampled students who did not participate in the survey because they had left the
sampled school after class sampling were counted as absent in the sampled school. These students
were assumed to remain part of the target population (they moved to a different school but had a zero
chance of selection since within‐school sampling had already been completed at this point). Excluded
students within participating schools carried their weight (i.e., reflecting their proportion in the target
population) and this contributed to the overall estimates of exclusion.
Final student weight (TOTWGTS)
The final student weight of student k in class j in school i in stratum h is the product of the student‐
level‐weight components:
TOTWGTShijk =
WGTFAC1hi × WGTADJ1hi × WGTFAC2Shij × WGTADJ2Shij × WGTADJ3Shijk. (7.6)
Note as WGTFAC3S = 1 for all eligible participating students, this factor was removed from the
equation.
7.4 Calculating weights for the teacher population
Final teacher weights were calculated for each eligible and as participating considered teacher in ICILS
2023. To be considered as “eligible,” the teacher needs to fit the defined target population, e.g., he or
she teaches grade 8 students, and was selected for ICILS 2023. To be considered as “participating,” the
teacher worked on the questionnaire, and the school—the teacher belong to—need to be considered
as participating, e.g., at least 50 percent of the selected teachers participated, see 7.7. The final teacher
weight comprises of six weighting components: three base weights and three non‐response adjustment
weights, see below.
School base weight (WGTFAC1)
Because the same schools were sampled for the student survey and the teacher survey, the school
base weight of the teacher survey was identical to the school base weight of the student survey.
School non‐response adjustment (WGTADJ1T)
A school non‐response adjustment for the teacher survey was calculated in the same way as the student
non‐response adjustment. Given that schools could be regarded as participating in the student survey
but not in the teacher survey, and vice versa, the school non‐participation adjustment potentially dif‐
fered between student and teacher data from the same school. To account for non‐responding schools
in the sample, it was necessary to calculate a school weight adjustment for the teacher survey as follows
for school i:

Teacher base weight (WGTFAC2T)

teachers in each school. The teacher base weight for teacher l was calculated as:
WGTFAC2Thil = Thi

In schools with 20 or fewer target grade teachers, all eligible teachers were selected for participation.
In these cases this weight factor was equal to one.18
Teacher non‐response adjustment (WGTADJ2T)
Not all teachers were willing or able to participate in the study. Therefore, participating teachers
represented both participants and non‐participants. Again, the non‐response adjustment carried out
within a given school assumed, for weighting purposes, that there was a random process underlying
teachers’ participation.
The teacher non‐response adjustment was computed for each participating teacher l as:
WGTADJ2Thil = ts,e

data collection, were regarded as out of scope and their weights were not adjusted in these instances.
Teacher multiplicity factor (WGTFAC3T)
Some teachers in ICILS 2023 were teaching at the target grade in more than one school (based on
information from the teacher questionnaire) and therefore had a larger selection probability than those
teaching at the target grade in only one school. In order to account for this, a “teacher multiplicity
factor” was calculated as the inverse of the number of schools in which the teacher was teaching:
WGTFAC3Thil =
1
fhil
,
(7.10)
where, fhil is the number of schools where teacher l in school i in stratum h was teaching.
Final teacher weight (TOTWGTT)
The final teacher weight for teacher l in school i in stratum h is the product of the five teacher‐weight
components:
