TOTWGTThil =
WGTFAC1hi × WGTADJ1Thi × WGTFAC2Thil × WGTADJ2Thil × WGTFAC3Thil. (7.11)
7.5 Calculating weights for the school‐level survey
ICILS 2023 was designed as a survey of students and teachers but not as a school survey. However,
in order to collect context information at school level, a principal questionnaire and an information and
communication technology (ICT) coordinator questionnaire were administered to every participating
school. The final school weights were calculated and included in the international database in order to
allow for analyses of this school‐level data. However, results at the school level should be interpreted
with some caution as they may be subject to considerable sampling error. As for the student and
teacher survey, weights are only calculated for eligible and participating “schools.” A school is consid‐
ered as participating if at least one item was answered by the principal or by the ICT coordinator in
the respective questionnaire.
School base weight (WGTFAC1)
This weight component is identical to the school base weight of the student survey and the teacher
survey (see above).
School weight adjustment (WGTADJ1C)
Schools in which no items were completed in either the principal questionnaire or the ICT coordinator
questionnaire were regarded as non‐participants in the school survey. In order to account for these
non‐responding schools, a school weight adjustment component was calculated for each participating
school is as follows:
WGTADJ1Chi =
nh
np−sch
h
,
(7.12)
where nh represents the number of eligible sampled schools and np−sch
h
represents the number of
schools with completed questionnaires in stratum h (whether originally sampled or replacement schools).
Note that some schools may have been non‐participants in the school survey but participated in the
student and/or the teacher surveys. Consequently, some schools were regarded as participants in the
student and/or teacher survey but as non‐participants in the school survey. Some schools may also
have completed (at least one of) the school‐level questionnaires but were regarded as non‐participants
in the student and/or teacher surveys. It is very important to keep this in mind when undertaking
analysis with data from different data sets. For multivariate analyses using different data sources, the
proportion of missing values may accumulate with increasing numbers of variables. Those undertaking
secondary analyses should thoroughly monitor the potential loss of information due to missing data
across the different sampling units.
Final school weight
The final school weight of school i in stratum h is the product of the two weight components:
TOTWGTChil = WGTFAC1hi × WGTADJ1Chi.
(7.13)
7.6 Calculating participation rates
For ICILS 2023, weighted and unweighted participation rates were calculated at student and teacher
levels to facilitate the evaluation of data quality and reduce the risk of potential bias due to non‐
response. In contrast to the weight‐adjustments described earlier, participation rates were computed
first considering the originally sampled schools only and then considering originally sampled and re‐
placement schools.

SAMPLING WEIGHTS AND PARTICIPATION RATES
95
Unweighted participation rates in the student survey
Let op denote the set of originally sampled eligible and participating schools, fp the full set of eligi‐
ble participating schools, including replacement schools, and np the set of sampled eligible but non‐
participating schools in the student survey. Let nop, nfp, and nnp denote the numbers of schools in
each of the respective sets. The unweighted school participation rate in the student survey before
replacement UPRSschoolsBR is calculated as:
UPRSschoolsBR =
nop
nfp + nnp .
And the unweighted school participation rate in the student survey after replacement UPRSschoolsAR
is computed as:
UPRSschoolsAR =
nfp
nfp + nnp .
Similarly, let cop denote the set of eligible and participating classes in all participating schools (i.e.,
including replacement schools), cfp the full set of eligible classes in participating schools, and np the
set of eligible but non‐participating classes in participating schools. In addition, let ncop, ncfp and ncnp
denote the numbers of classes in each of the respective sets. The unweighted class participation rate
in the student survey was calculated as:
UPRSclasses =
ncop
ncfp + ncnp .
Let sfp represent the set of eligible and participating students in all participating schools, that is, in
the schools that constitute fp as the complete set of eligible participating schools. Let snp be the
set of eligible but non‐participating students in schools that constitute fp, and let nsfp and nsnp be
the number of students in these two respective groups. The unweighted student response rate is
computed as:
UPRSstudents =
nsfp
nsfp + nsnp .
Note that it was not deemed necessary to compute student response rates separately for originally
sampled and replacement schools because non‐response patterns did not vary between (participating)
originally sampled and replacement schools.
The unweighted overall participation rate in the student survey before replacement is then:
UPRSoverallBR = UPRSschoolsBR ∗UPRSclasses ∗UPRSstudents.
And the unweighted overall participation rate in the student survey after replacement is:
UPRSoverallAR = UPRSschoolsAR ∗UPRSclasses ∗UPRSstudents.

Overview of participation rates in the student survey
Table 7.1 and Table 7.2 display the unweighted and weighted participation rates of all countries in the
student survey. Small differences between the two tables indicate different response patterns among
strata with disproportional sample allocations. It should be noted that only those schools were treated
as participants in the student survey that had at least a participation rate of 50 percent among their
sampled students. A school that did not meet this requirement was regarded as a non‐participating
school for the student data collection. The non‐participation of this school had an effect on the school
participation rate; however, the students from this school were exempted from the calculation of the
student participation rate.

Unweighted participation rates in the teacher survey
The computation of participation rates in the teacher survey follows the same logic as applied in the
student survey.
Let op, fp, and np be defined as above, such that the participation status now refers to the teacher
survey instead of the student survey, and let nop, nfp, and nnp be defined correspondingly. The un‐
weighted school participation rate in the teacher survey before replacement is computed as:
UPRTschoolsBR =
nop
nfp + nnp .
The unweighted school participation rate in the teacher survey after replacement is calculated as:
UPRTschoolsAR =
nfp
nfp + nnp .
Let tfp be the set of eligible and participating teachers in schools that constitute fp, tnp be the set
of eligible but nonparticipating teachers in schools that constitute fp, and let ntfp and ntnp be the
number of teachers in the respective groups. The unweighted teacher response rate is defined as:
UPRTteachers =
ntfp
ntfp + ntnp .
Note that it was not deemed necessary to compute teacher response rates separately for (participating)
originally sampled and replacement schools because the non‐response patterns did not vary between
sample and replacement schools.
The unweighted overall participation rate in the teacher survey before replacement is computed as:
UPRToverallBR = UPRTschoolsBR × UPRTteachers.
The unweighted overall participation rate in the teacher survey after replacement is calculated as:
UPRToverallAR = UPRTschools[AR] × UPRTteachers.

Overview of participation rates in the teacher survey
Table 7.3 and Table 7.4 display the unweighted and weighted participation rates of all countries in

patterns between strata with disproportional sample size allocations.

ment was regarded as a non‐participating school in the teacher survey. The non‐participation of this
school had an effect on the school participation rate (for the teacher survey), but not on the teacher
participation rates as the teachers from this school were not included in the calculation of the teacher
participation rates.

7.7 ICILS 2023 standards for sampling participation
It is a significant challenge within countries to achieve full participation (i.e., 100%) in a large‐scale
assessment and the nature of these challenges vary across countries. Given that one essential purpose
of ICILS is to report data at the national level, it is necessary to adjudicate for each country whether
the achieved sample is sufficient to warrant scientifically defensible reporting of national estimates.
As is customary in IEA studies, ICILS 2023 established guidelines for reporting data for countries with
less than full participation. Adjudication of the data was done separately for each participating country
and each of the two different ICILS 2023 survey populations. This was carried out in accordance with
the recommendations of the sampling referee (Marc Joncas) and in agreement with all members of the
ICILS Joint Management Committee.
The first step of the adjudication process was to determine the minimum requirements for within‐
school participation.
Within‐school participation requirements
In general, decreasing response rates entail increasing the risk of getting biased population estimates.
Because very little information about non‐respondents is available, it was not possible to quantify
the risk or bias of estimates due to non‐participation in most countries. To overcome this, and in
addition to the overall participation rate requirements described below, ICILS 2023 established strict
standards for minimum within‐school participation: data from schools with a response rate of less than
half (50%) of sampled students or teachers, respectively, were discarded from the respective survey.
This constraint meant that not every student or teacher who completed a survey instrument was
automatically considered as participating and thereby contributing to the computation of population
estimates.
The within‐school response rate was computed separately for the student survey and the teacher
survey. Therefore, a school may count as participating in the student survey but not in the teacher
survey or vice versa.
Student survey participation requirements
Students were regarded as respondents if they replied to at least one task in the achievement test.
Please note, however, that the amount of partial non‐response (i.e., omitted items in questionnaires or
tasks that had not been attempted) differed for different items and tasks.
There is evidence that attendance and academic performance tend to be positively correlated (Balfanz
& Byrnes, 2012; Hancock et al., 2013). Consequently, the likelihood of biased results may increase as
within‐school response rates decrease.
Whenever there was evidence that the survey operation procedures in a school had not been con‐
ducted following the established ICILS 2023 standards, the corresponding school was regarded as a
non‐participant. For example, if a school failed to list all eligible students for the selection of a student
sample and thus causing a risk of bias due to insufficient coverage, the corresponding school’s student
data were not included in the final database.
Teacher survey participation requirements
Teachers were regarded as respondents if they replied to at least one item in the teacher questionnaire.
But again, as was the situation with respect to the students, the overall amount of partial non‐response
(i.e., omitted items in the questionnaires) was different for different tasks and items.
It is possible that specific groups of teachers tend to be less likely to participate in a survey. In order to
help reduce non‐response bias, a school was only regarded as a “participating school” in the teacher
survey if at least 50 percent of its sampled teachers participated. If the response rate was lower,
teacher data from this school were disregarded and the school was treated as non‐participating.

If a school failed to follow the survey operation procedures properly, it was classified as a non‐participating
school. For example, if a school failed to list all eligible teachers for the teacher sample selection, or if
the standard teacher selection procedures had not been followed, then teacher data from this partic‐
ular school were not included in the final database.
Participation requirement categories
Three categories for sampling participation were defined the same for the student and the teacher
population:
• Countries grouped in Category 1 met the ICILS 2023 sampling participation requirements.
• Countries in Category 2 met these requirements only after the inclusion of replacement schools.
• Countries in Category 3 failed to meet the ICILS 2023 sampling participation requirements.
The results from ICILS 2023 show that high response rates in the teacher survey were often harder
to achieve than in the student survey. However, there is no statistical justification to apply different
sampling participation standards to the two surveys. Since non‐response holds a high potential for bias
in both parts of the study, the participation requirements in the teacher survey were identical to those
in the student survey. No participation requirements were determined for the reporting of school‐level
data, however, the participation rate in the school survey was above 85 percent for all countries that
were placed in Category 1 and Category 2 for the student survey.
The three categories for sampling participation were defined according to the criteria presented in
Figure 7.1.

SAMPLING WEIGHTS AND PARTICIPATION RATES
105
Figure 7.1: Participation categories in ICILS
Category 1: Satisfactory sampling participation rate without the use of replacement schools.
In order to be placed in this category, a country has to have:
• An unweighted school response rate without replacement of at least 85 percent (after
rounding to the nearest whole percent) AND an unweighted overall student/teacher re‐
sponse rate (after rounding) of at least 85 percent
OR
• A weighted school response rate without replacement of at least 85 percent (after rounding
to the nearest whole percent) AND a weighted overall student/teacher response rate (after
rounding) of at least 85 percent OR
• The product of the (unrounded) weighted school response rate without replacement and
the (unrounded) weighted overall student/teacher response rate of at least 75 percent (after
rounding to the nearest whole percent).
Category 2: Satisfactory sampling participation rate only when replacement schools were in‐
cluded.
A country is placed in this category if: It fails to meet the requirements for Category 1 but has
either an unweighted or weighted school response rate without replacement of at least 50 percent
(after rounding to the nearest percent) AND HAS EITHER
• An unweighted school response rate with replacement of at least 85 percent (after rounding
to the nearest whole percent) AND an unweighted overall student/teacher response rate
(after rounding) of at least 85 percent
OR
• A weighted school response rate with replacement of at least 85 percent (after rounding to
nearest whole percent) AND a weighted overall student/teacher response rate (after round‐
ing) of at least 85 percent
OR
• The product of the (unrounded) weighted school response rate with replacement and the
(unrounded) weighted overall student/teacher response rate of at least 75 percent (after
rounding to the nearest whole percent).
Category 3: Unacceptable sampling response rate even when replacement schools are included.
Countries that can provide documentation to show that they complied with ICILS sampling pro‐
cedures, but do not meet the requirements for Category 1 or Category 2 are placed in Category
3.
7.8 Reporting data
The ICILS 2023 research team considered it necessary to make readers of the international reports
aware of the increased potential for bias, regardless of whether such a bias was actually introduced.
Based on their respective sample participation categories, national survey results were reported on as
follows:
• Category 1: Countries in this category appear in the tables and figures in the international reports
without annotation.
• Category 2: Countries in this category are annotated in the tables and figures in the international
reports.
• Category 3: Countries in this category appear in a separate section of the tables and figures.

106
ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
Table 7.5 shows the used annotation symbols and their meaning for the student and the teacher survey.
Table 7.6 lists for each country its reporting category and the corresponding annotation for the student
survey, while Table 7.7 lists the same for the teacher survey.
For the student survey, 26 countries and one benchmarking participant were reported as meeting
sampling participation requirements in Category 1. Of these, 13 countries got an annotation due to
exceeding five percent exclusion limit. Five countries were in Category 2, thereof two with additional
annotations due to exclusion rates larger than five percent. One country was reported as Category
3; and two countries were not reported: Chile conducted an additional national data collection, com‐
pleting the ICILS 2023 data and requested to be not reported, while the participation rates from the
Netherlands were too low to be reported. Compared to earlier cycles, the number of countries ex‐
cluding between five percent and ten percent of their student population has increased.
For the teacher survey, 14 countries and one benchmarking participant had high participation rated as
Category 1. Three countries countries were reported in Category 2, and 12 countries were reported
as a Category 3 country. Five countries were not reported due to low participation rates. Please note
that additional annotations were needed for not following the sampling procedures, and for excluding
more than five percent of students on school‐level. For the latter case, it is to be expected that also
more than five percent of the teacher would then not be covered, see Table 7.7.

Chapter 8:
ICILS field operations
Sebastian Meyer
8.1 Introduction
Successful administration of ICILS 2023 depended heavily on the contributions of the study’s national
research coordinators (NRCs) and national center staff. To assist the national centers and promote con‐
sistency in the study administration across countries, the ICILS International Study Center (ISC) at IEA
developed internationally standardized field operations procedures. The ICILS operations procedures
were designed to be flexible enough to meet the needs of individual participants whilst maintaining
IEA’s high quality standards.
The ICILS operations procedures were developed with reference to those used in ICILS 2018 and in
other IEA studies and were tailored to suit the specific requirements of ICILS 2023. All national centers
received guidelines on survey operations procedures for each stage of the study. These guidelines
provided information about contacting schools, listing and sampling students, preparing materials for
data collection, administering the assessment, scoring and coding the assessment, creating the data
files, and implementing quality control procedures at various stages.
8.2 Field operations personnel
The role of the national research coordinators and their centers
Appointing an NRC was one of the first steps for all countries or education systems participating in
ICILS 2023. The NRC acted as the main coordinator and point of contact for ICILS 2023 at both the
national and international level. They were in charge of the overall implementation of the study at
the national level. They also, where necessary, implemented and adapted internationally agreed‐upon
procedures for the national context under the guidance of the ISC and national experts.
The role of school coordinators and test administrators
Two roles were essential to facilitate the successful national administration of ICILS 2023 within coun‐
tries: the school coordinator and the test administrator. Their work involved preparing for the test
administration in schools and carrying out the data collection in a standardized way.
National centers identified and trained school coordinators for all participating schools, most often in
coordination with school principals. The school coordinator could be a teacher or other staff member
in the school. In some cases, national centers appointed external individuals as school coordinators.
The coordinators’ responsibilities included the following major tasks:
• Listing target grade classes and identifying teachers belonging to the target population to allow
the national center to perform within‐school sampling;
• Arranging the date(s) and modalities of the test administration, in particular the delivery method
of the student test, with the national center;
• Distributing related materials needed for test administration and making sure they were kept in
a secure place and confidential at all times;
• Working with the school principal, the test administrator, and the affected teachers to plan and
administer the student test;
• Confirming with the national center the status and eligibility of students qualified to use some
form of accommodation to complete the ICILS instruments; and

114
ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
• Ensuring that the test administrators return all testing materials after the testing session.
The test administrators were mainly responsible for running the assessment sessions in schools where
students completed the online test and questionnaire. Test administrators were employed either by
the national center or directly by the schools, but could not to be a teacher of any of the sampled
students. A training session was run by the national center centrally or by the schools to make sure
that the test administrators were adequately prepared to run the assessment sessions. In some cases,
the school coordinators also served as the test administrators.
8.3 Field operations resources
Manuals and documentation
The international study team released the ICILS 2023 Survey Operations Procedures (SOP) manuals
to the NRCs in five units. Each unit was accompanied by additional materials such as manuals for use
in schools, software manuals and packages, and informational webinars. The materials were organized
and distributed chronologically according to the stages of the study.
The five units were as follows:
• Unit 1: Sampling Schools specified the actions and procedures required to develop a national
sampling plan in compliance with the international ICILS 2023 sample design.
• Unit 2: Working with Schools contained information about how to work with schools to plan
the successful administration of the ICILS 2023 instruments.
Accompanying manuals:
– The Compatibility Check and School Computer Resources Survey: Instructions for NRCs de‐
scribed the tasks for testing computers for the computer‐based ICILS assessment admin‐
istration, including some technical troubleshooting scenarios.
– The Preparing Computers for ICILS Manual described the tasks for preparing computers for
the computer‐based ICILS assessment administration.
• Unit 3: Preparing National Study Instruments described the processes involved in preparing
the ICILS 2023 instruments for production and use in countries.
Accompanying manuals:
– The Assessment Master Manual provided technical information on how to use the RM Aus‐
tralia Assessment Master throughout the process of preparing national instruments (trans‐
lation and translation verification).
– The IEA Online SurveySystem Manual provided technical information on how to use the IEA
Online SurveySystem for setting up the online adult questionnaires.
• Unit 4: Data Collection and Quality Monitoring Procedures dealt with the processes involved
in preparing for, supporting, and monitoring ICILS 2023 data collection in schools.
Accompanying manuals:
– The School Coordinator Manual, described the role and responsibilities of the school coor‐
dinator, the main contact person within each participating school.
– The Test Administrator Manual, described the role and responsibilities of the test adminis‐
trator, whose work included administration of the student assessment.
• Unit 5.1: Monitoring Participation and Data Submission provided guidelines on tracking the
data collection progress and described the data uploading of student assessment data (when the
USB delivery method was used).

ICILS FIELD OPERATIONS
115
• Unit 5.2: Scoring and Parental Occupation Coding provided guidelines on scoring student re‐
sponses, and coding parental occupations.
Accompanying manuals:
– The Scoring Guides, provided detailed and explicit guidelines on how to score each constructed‐
response item.
The international versions of some of the manuals could be adapted to account for the national context.
In addition, manuals distributed to national staff could be translated to the national language(s) within
the country.
Software
To digitally deliver the student tests and questionnaire, ICILS 2023 made use of the Assessment Master
(AM), an assessment platform owned by RM Results (see Chapter 3 for further details). Prior to data
collection, the ISC provided countries with compatibility check software for checking the suitability
of computer devices for ICILS 2023 for the intended delivery mode. During data collection, national
center staff could use AM’s Manager module to monitor the data collection progress.
In addition to the computer‐based delivery software and consistent with the goal of automating and
streamlining procedures wherever possible, the ISC provided NRCs with custom‐built software pack‐
ages to assist with sampling and data collection:
• IEA Windows Within‐School Sampling Software (WinW3S): This enabled the ICILS 2023 national
centers to randomly select classes and teachers in each sampled school in agreement with sample
design specifications and mandatory sampling algorithms. National centers used the software
to track school, teacher, and student information, prepare the survey tracking forms, and assign
test instruments to students.
• IEA Online Survey System (OSS): was used as a delivery system for the online adult question‐
naires (for teachers, ICT‐coordinators and school principals). It included a designer module for
entering verified translations and implementing agreed upon national adaptations to the inter‐
national version of the questionnaires and data monitoring module that allowed the tracking of
principal, ICT coordinator, and teacher responses flowing into the system.
In addition to providing software and manuals, the ISC conducted field operations training seminars
prior to both the field trial and the main survey. These seminars were designed to train national center
staff on all procedures and to familiarize them with the supporting software, namely IEA WinW3S and
IEA OSS. The ISC also offered a webinar immediately after the release of the field trial and main survey
instruments to explain translation procedures within the AM Designer module.
8.4 Field operations processes
Linking students and teachers to schools
The ISC established a system for assigning hierarchical identification codes (IDs) to uniquely identify
and track sampled schools, teachers, and students. Table 8.1 presents the hierarchical identification
system.
Each sampled student was assigned an eight‐digit ID number unique within their country. This ID
consisted of a four‐digit school identifier, followed by a two‐digit class identifier within the school, and
a two‐digit student identifier within the class.
Similarly, each sampled teacher within a sampled school was assigned a teacher ID number, consisting
of the four‐digit school identifier followed by a two‐digit teacher identifier unique within the school.

Activities for working with schools
In ICILS 2023, the within‐school sampling process and the assessment administration required close
cooperation between the national centers and representatives from the schools (i.e., the school coor‐
dinators and test administrators as described previously). Figure 8.1 presents the major activities the
national centers conducted when working with schools to list and sample students and teachers, track
respondents, prepare for test administration, and carry out the assessment.
Contacting schools and within‐school sampling procedures
Once national centers had obtained a list of the schools sampled for ICILS 2023, it was important for
the success of the study to establish good working relationships with the selected schools (for more
information on sampling procedures, please refer to Chapter 6 of this report). The national centers
were responsible for contacting the schools and encouraging them to participate in the assessment,
a process that often involved obtaining support from national or regional educational authorities or
other stakeholders, depending on the national context.
As described earlier, national centers identified and trained school coordinators for all participating
schools. School coordinators were responsible for providing all required information about their re‐
spective schools and coordinating the date, time, and place of the student assessment. School co‐
ordinators were also responsible for arranging modalities of the test administration with the national
center, for example, regarding the use of school or externally provided computers. This work required
them to complete the school computer resources survey, run compatibility checks locally, and send
results to the national study center.
National centers sent a class listing form to each school coordinator and asked them to provide infor‐
mation on all target‐grade classes in the school, including the number of students in each class. The
national centers used this information to sample classes within the schools.
For each sampled class, the national centers created a student listing form which was sent to the
schools coordinator. School coordinators were required to list all students within the sampled class
along with selected details about these students, such as their names (if country regulations allowed
names to be provided to the national centers), birth month and year, gender, exclusion status, the
assessment language of the student (in case the national center provided different language versions
of the student instruments), and if any special accommodations for the student test sessions were
necessary.
National centers also sent a teacher listing form to each school coordinator and asked them to provide
information on all the eligible target‐grade teachers within the school. The school coordinators listed
the eligible target‐grade teachers and provided details about these teachers, such as their names (if
country regulations allowed names to be provided to the national center), birth month and year, and
gender. The national centers used the collected information to sample teachers within the schools.

ICILS FIELD OPERATIONS
117
School coordinators were also responsible for obtaining parental permission as necessary, liaising with
the test administrator to coordinate the test session, distributing information about teacher, school,
and ICT coordinator questionnaires, and coordinating completion of the student tracking forms and
teacher tracking forms. School coordinators also ensured that assessment materials were received,
kept secure at all times, and returned to the national center after the administration.
The following Figure 8.1 illustrates the exchange of information between the national centers and the
participating schools.

118
ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
Figure 8.1: Procedures for working with schools to prepare for test administration

ICILS FIELD OPERATIONS
119
Preparing the computer‐based test delivery at schools
Because ICILS 2023 was a computer‐based assessment, it was necessary to test the computer re‐
sources available at participating schools to ascertain whether the school computer resources could
be used to deliver the assessment. Two delivery methods were recommended for the student test:
• Internet (browser‐based) method; and
• USB flash drive method.
Countries were asked to select both a primary and a backup delivery method, with no obligation to
use only one method exclusively, although doing so was recommended for organizational purposes.
Countries were also given the possibility to arrange for portable computers to be transported to schools
that use USB flash drives to deliver the ICILS Student Software. See Chapter 3 for details of the system
requirements for the different delivery methods.
The compatibility check and school computer resources survey were administered in order to answer
three questions: (i) if school computers could be used for the testing or if schools would need to be
provided with computers able to do this task; (ii) if, in those cases where the school computers were
intended to be used via online delivery, the school provided sufficient bandwidth to deliver the testing
through web browser; and (iii) if, in those cases where the school computers were intended to be used
for USB flash drive delivery, special arrangements would be needed (e.g., altering the configuration of
computers) for the student test to run correctly.
Schools were requested to run the compatibility check and complete the computer resources survey
far enough in advance of the ICILS testing to allow for any problem solving and modifications to com‐
puter configurations in preparation for the ICILS testing but sufficiently close to the ICILS testing day
to minimize the risk of computer configurations changing greatly between the time that they were
checked and the ICILS testing date.
In schools where the student test was planned to be delivered using the internet method, school
coordinators were asked to access the ICILS browser compatibility check website to verify whether
the browser and screen resolution were suitable on each testing computer. They were also required
to use the internet latency check website to measure the response time between at least one testing
computer and the Amazon Web Services (AWS) regions.
In schools where the student test was planned to be delivered using the USB method, school coor‐
dinators were instructed to run a program from a USB flash drive to confirm whether the minimum
computer requirements were met. This program needed to be executed on all computers designated
for testing.

120
ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
The school resources survey specifically requested the results of the compatibility checks. The com‐
pleted surveys were required to be sent back to the national center, where they were evaluated to
assess the specific situation in each participating school. If the evaluation identified any risks that
could compromise the feasibility of the planned delivery method, the national center could implement
targeted measures to mitigate those risks. National centers in non‐English‐speaking countries were
also asked to translate the school computer resources survey questions.
Once the technical requirements were met, the national centers sent the following items to each school:
tracking forms, labels, manuals, and, where applicable, USB sticks for each student listed on the student
tracking form, along with three extra sticks.
Administering the assessment at schools
Preparing assessment materials and distributing them to the participating schools required careful or‐
ganization and planning by national centers. The assessment materials were provided to the school
coordinators prior to testing, providing ample time for the school coordinators to confirm the receipt
and correctness of the materials.
For all survey instruments, individual login information was provided to ensure confidentiality. For the
principal, ICT coordinator, and teacher questionnaires, the national centers sent the login information,
along with general information on how to access the online questionnaire, to the school coordinator
in the form of “cover letters.” The school coordinator delivered the principal, ICT coordinator, and
teacher cover letters to the designated individuals. Student login information was provided to the
school coordinator with the student tracking forms and kept in a secure room until they were provided
to the test administrators.
In accordance with the international guidelines and requirements as well as local conditions, national
centers assigned a test administrator to each school as described earlier. Their responsibilities included:
running a pretest administration on the day of testing in order to confirm that the student computers
were prepared for the test; distributing materials to the appropriate students; logging in and initializing
the test on the computers (either via the USB sticks provided by the national centers or the internet
method); leading students through the assessment; and, accurately timing the sessions.
For each sampled student, the student tracking forms indicated the assigned student instrument which
consisted of either two or three parts depending on whether or not the computational thinking (CT)
modules were administered within the country. Each sampled student was assigned two out of seven
computer and information literacy (CIL) modules from a completely balanced design so that each mod‐
ule was assigned to an approximately equal number of students. Each student also was asked to
complete a questionnaire. If a country opted for administering the CT modules, each student was also
asked to complete two out of four additional CT modules which also were assigned from a balanced
design.
During the administration of the assessment, administrators were required to provide a range of in‐
structions to students. For some parts of the assessment, test administrators were asked to read
instructions to the students as provided to them in the Test Administrator Manual. Administrators had
to read the text to the students exactly as it appeared in the script. In some other parts of the as‐
sessment test, administrators were required to read instructions from a script but had the option of
modifying or adapting it to best suit a given situation.
In these instances, it was essential that the exact contents and meaning of each of the scripts was
conveyed to each set of students. The only instances in which test administrators could use their own
words was when the Test Administrator Manual did not include a script for the instructions, for example,
when the manual explicitly advised administrators that they could answer any questions or points of
clarification. Please see Chapter 9 for data on how well test administrators adhered to the script.

ICILS FIELD OPERATIONS
121
The time allotted for each part of the student testing and questionnaire administration was standard‐
ized across countries. In all countries, target‐grade students were allowed 30 minutes to complete
each of the two modules (60 minutes in total). Students who completed the assessment before the
allotted time was over were allowed to review answers or read quietly, but were not allowed to leave
the session. Students were given around 25 minutes to complete the student questionnaire and were
allowed to continue if they needed additional time. Test administrators were required to document
the starting and ending time of each part of the assessment administration on the test administration
form.
In countries administering the two CT modules, after the regular CIL test and student questionnaire
sessions, the test session was extended by an additional 50 minutes (25 minutes for each of the two
CT test modules). Table 8.2 details the time allotted to the different parts of the student assessment.
Once the administration was completed, the school coordinators were responsible for collecting and
returning all materials to their respective national center.
Table 8.2: Timing of the ICILS assessment

Online data collection of school principal, ICT coordinator, and teacher questionnaires
In ICILS 2023, it was mandatory to deliver all adult questionnaires online. After the principal, ICT
coordinator, and teacher questionnaires had gone through the instrument preparation and verifica‐
tion processes, they were prepared for delivery online using the IEA Online Survey System (IEA OSS)
software. These procedures and software are described in more detail in Chapter 5 of this report.
The electronic versions of the ICILS 2023 principal, ICT coordinator, and teacher questionnaires could
only be completed via the internet. Accordingly, the design ensured that respondents needed only an
internet connection and a standard internet browser. No additional software or particular operating
system was required. Respondents were not allowed to use other delivery options, such as sending
PDF documents via email or printing out the online questionnaires and mailing them to the national
center.
During the administration period, respondents could log in and out as many times as they needed and
could resume answering the questionnaire at the question they had last responded to in their previous

122
ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
session. Answers were automatically saved whenever respondents moved to another question, and
respondents could change any answer at any time before completing the questionnaire. During the
administration, the national center was available for support; the center, in turn, could contact the ISC
if unable to solve a problem locally.
Responses to the online questionnaires were not made mandatory, evaluated, or enforced in detail
(e.g., using hard validations). Instead, some questions used soft validation, such as respondents being
asked to give numerical responses to questions that had a minimum and maximum value, for example,
the total number of students enrolled in a school. In some instances, respondents’ answers to this type
of question led to the response being updated according to the individual respondent’s entries, even
if that response was outside the minimum or maximum value, but with the caveat that the response
still needed to be within the specified width.
To reduce response burden and complexity, the online survey automatically skipped questions not
applicable to the respondent. Rather than presenting multiple questions per page, the online ques‐
tionnaire proceeded question by question. While vertical scrolling was required for a few questions,
particularly the longer questions with multiple “yes/no” or Likert‐type items, horizontal scrolling was
not. Because respondents could easily estimate through visual cues the length and burden of a paper
questionnaire, the online questionnaires attempted to offer this feature through progress counters and
a “table of contents” that listed each question and its response status. Multiple‐choice questions were
implemented with standard HTML radio buttons.
Because the national centers were able to monitor the responses to the online questionnaires in real‐
time, they could send reminders to those schools where people had not responded in the expected
period of time. Typically, in these cases, the centers asked the school coordinators to follow up with
those individuals who had not responded. It was also possible to provide school coordinators with
access to the monitoring tool. In that case national centers were asked to send a list including the
number of school coordinators and the school IDs each school coordinator was overseeing to IEA.
That way, school coordinators were only seeing the progress of the schools they are responsible for.
Scoring the assessment
The success of assessments containing constructed‐response items and large tasks depends on the
degree to which student responses are scored reliably. Nineteen of the ICILS 2023 CIL assessment
tasks were constructed‐response tasks, and seven large tasks were scored against a total of 60 criteria.
One of the large‐task criteria was automatically scored by the Assessment Master Examiner module.
Human scorers reviewed the automatically generated suggested score and could either accept or mod‐
ify the score. Of the 55 ICILS 2023 CIL tasks, 26 were scored by human scorers. There were 31 CT
tasks for ICILS 2023. Three of these tasks involved human scoring. All human scoring was conducted
within the Assessment Master Marker module. It was critical to the quality of the ICILS 2023 results
that these tasks were scored in a reliable manner. Reliability was accomplished by providing national
centers with explicit scoring guides, extensive training of scoring staff, and continuous monitoring of
the quality of the work during scoring procedures.
During the scoring training, which was conducted at the international level, national center staff mem‐
bers learned how to score the constructed‐response items and to use the scoring criteria for the large‐
task items in the ICILS 2023 assessment. Scoring training took place before both the field trial and
the main survey. The training that took place prior to the field trial provided the participants with their
first opportunity to give extensive feedback on the scoring guides, which were then revised on the
basis of this feedback. The training conducted before the main survey enabled national center staff
to give additional feedback on the scoring guides, with that feedback based on their experiences of
scoring the field‐trial items. The content of the scoring guides for the five ICILS trend modules (four
CIL and one CT module containing constructed response items) were not revised and were identical
to the content presented in ICILS 2018. Further details of the development and revision of the ICILS
2023 main survey scoring guide for open‐ended response items are provided in Chapter 2.

ICILS FIELD OPERATIONS
123
The main survey scorer training employed a sample set of student responses collected during the field
trial in English‐speaking ICILS 2023 countries. The example responses used during scorer training
were a mixture of those that clearly represented the scoring categories and those that were relatively
difficult to score because they were partially ambiguous, unusually expressed, or on the “borderlines”
of scoring categories. The scores that national center staff gave to these example responses were
shared with the group, with discussion focusing on discrepancies in particular. The scoring guides and
practice responses were refined following the scoring training to clarify areas of uncertainty identified
during the scorer training.
Once training had been completed, the ISC provided national centers with a final set of scored sample
responses as well as the final version of the scoring guide. The scored sample responses were accessible
electronically through the web‐based Assessment Master Marker module and were available only in
English. National centers used this information, as they saw fit, to train their scoring staff on how to
apply the scoring guides to the constructed‐response items and large tasks. In some cases, national
centers created their own sets of example responses from the student responses collected in their
country.
To prepare for this task, the ISC provided national centers not only with suggestions on how to organize
staff, but also with materials, procedures, and details on the scoring process. The ISC encouraged the
national centers to hire scorers who were attentive to detail, familiar with education, and who, to the
greatest extent possible, had a background in CIL. The ISC also provided guidelines on how to train
scorers to accurately and reliably score the items and tasks.
Documenting scoring reliability
Documenting the reliability of the scoring process within countries was a highly important aspect of
monitoring and maintaining the quality of the ICILS 2023 scored data. Scoring reliability within each
country required two different scorers to independently score a random sample of approximately 200
responses for each constructed‐response item and each large task.
The selection of responses to be double‐scored and the allocation of these responses to scorers were
random and managed by the web‐based scoring software. The software was set up to ensure that a
random selection of approximately 250 responses of all responses were double‐scored, and that scor‐
ing could begin before all student responses had been uploaded to the system (thus allowing for late
returns of data from some schools). The software set‐up also allowed these tasks to be accomplished
without compromising the selection probability of each piece of work for double scoring.
The degree of agreement between the scores, as assigned by the two scorers, provided a measure
of the reliability of the scoring process. The web‐based scoring system was able to provide real‐time
inter‐rater reliability reports to scoring leaders who were encouraged (but not required) to use this
information to help them monitor the quality of the scoring. Scoring leaders could, for example, use
the information to monitor the agreement of each scorer with their colleagues (and identify scorers
whose agreement was low relative to others), or identify items or tasks with relatively low inter‐rater
reliability that might need to be rescored or to have scorers provided with some additional training to
improve the quality of their scoring.
Items with relatively low inter‐rater reliability within a given country were not used in the estimation
of student achievement for that country. Chapter 11 outlines the adjudication process relating to
inter‐rater reliability.
Online data collection for survey activities questionnaires
In order to collect feedback about survey operations from NRCs, the international project team set
up a survey activities questionnaire online. The questionnaire was prepared and administered using
the IEA OSS. The survey activities questionnaire, unlike the other ICILS 2023 questionnaires, did not
require national adaptations and was completed in English.

124
ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
The purpose of the survey activities questionnaire was to gather opinions and information about the
strengths and weaknesses of the ICILS 2023 assessment materials (e.g., test instruments, manuals,
scoring guides, and software) as well as countries’ experiences with the ICILS 2023 survey operations
procedures. NRCs were asked to complete these questionnaires with the assistance of their data
managers and the rest of the national center staff. The information was used to evaluate survey
operations. It is also being used to improve the quality of survey activities and materials used in future
ICILS cycles. Results of the survey activities questionnaire are reported in Chapter 9.
IEA sent the NRCs individual login information and internet links for accessing the online question‐
naires. Before submitting the responses to IEA, NRCs could go back and change their answers if
necessary.
8.5 Field trial procedures
The ICILS 2023 field trial was a smaller administration of the ICILS 2023 assessment; on average,
approximately 1,000 students were tested in each participating country.
The international field trial was conducted from May to June 2022.
The field trial was crucial to the development of the ICILS 2023 assessment instruments and also
served the purpose of testing the ICILS 2023 survey operations procedures in order to avoid any
possible problems during the ICILS 2023 data collection.
The operational resources and procedures described in this chapter were used during the field trial
under conditions approximating, as closely as possible, those of the main survey data collection. This
process also allowed the NRCs and their staff to acquaint themselves with the activities, refine their
national operations, and provide feedback that could be used to improve the data collection proce‐
dures. As a result of the field trial, several modifications were made to survey operations, including
enhanced procedures for collecting detailed information on the types of accommodations used for
student instruments, where applicable. These adjustments supported the effective implementation of
ICILS 2023.

Chapter 9:
Quality assurance in the ICILS data collection
Kateřina Hartmanová, Lauren Musu, and Marta Moreno Hidalgo
Introduction
To allow for ICILS 2023 data of the highest standard and comparability across countries, a significant
effort has been put into ensuring standardized materials and survey operations procedures. For this
purpose, quality control and assurance procedures were an integral part of ICILS 2023 and encom‐
passed internal mechanisms built into each stage of the ICILS 2023 implementation.
The aim of this chapter is to provide an overview and present results of the quality assurance activities
that occurred during the data collection for ICILS 2023. There are two primary activities described in
this chapter. The first activity, the quality control program, consisted of two independent components:
An international quality control program, managed by IEA, and a national quality control program,
managed by each national center, based on the international guidelines and manuals provided.
The second activity that collected information on the overall quality of the ICILS 2023 administration
was the Survey Activities Questionnaire (SAQ). Through this questionnaire, National Research Coordi‐
nators (NRCs) provided information about the implementation of the ICILS procedures, and the extent
to which procedures were followed according to international standards.
More details on the quality assurance procedures employed during other phases of the ICILS 2023
study cycle—such as instrument development and preparation, scaling, and data analysis—can be found
in the other chapters within this report.
9.1 International quality control program
This section provides an overview of the international quality control program and the data collected
for the program.
The international quality control program was implemented by independent international quality con‐
trol monitors (IQCMs) appointed and trained by IEA for each participating country. The major task
of the IQCMs was to conduct on‐site visits during the main survey data collection. In each country,
the IQCM and the IQCM’s assistant(s) visited a sample of testing sessions at participating schools. A
total of 15 school visits were required unless specified otherwise. Other essential responsibilities of
the IQCMs included visiting the National Study Center (NSC) for their country, meeting with the Na‐
tional Research Coordinator (NRC), interviewing the school coordinator and test administrator in each
visited school, and reviewing selected sections of the School Coordinator and Test Administrator Man‐
uals. Together, these tasks served to document data collection activities and determine whether the
ICILS assessment was administered in compliance with the standardized procedures in their respective
countries.
It is important to note that IQCMs visited only a sample of the schools that administered the ICILS
survey in each country. In addition, IQCMs reported on any issues that may have been observed, even
if only one student was affected. Results presented in this chapter should therefore be considered
with this information in mind.
Selecting and training international quality control monitors (IQCMs)
The ICILS 2023 IQCMs were independent individuals experienced within school environments and
(ideally) the information and communication technology (ICT) context, who resided in the participating
country and had no affiliation with the NSCs. If no previous IQCM was available to serve again for

126
ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
the country, IEA asked NRCs to nominate two candidates to serve as IQCMs for ICILS 2023 in their
respective countries, which included sending the candidates’ CVs and a short explanation why they
thought the nominees would be suitable for the position at IEA. Potential candidates needed to be
fluent in English as well as the administered language(s) in the target country. IQCMs were also re‐
quired to not have any professional or personal affiliation with the NSC. Potential candidates were, for
instance, school inspectors, relevant ministry officials, retired school teachers, or graduate students.
Out of the IQCMs ultimately selected for ICILS 2023, nearly 60 percent previously served as an IQCM
for another IEA study and the rest were newly nominated by NRCs.
Prior to beginning their assignments, the IQCMs were required to attend an online training session
conducted by IEA. The online training session was held over two days in early February 2023. During
the training, IQCMs were introduced to the ICILS operation procedures, study instruments, and tasks
associated with their role. IQCMs were also provided with a manual detailing their responsibilities and
the necessary materials for completing their tasks. After the training seminar and during the IQCMs’
fieldwork, IEA remained in close contact with them and provided further assistance and instructions if
required, especially in case of unforeseen circumstances.
For ICILS 2023, a total of 34 IQCMs were trained across 34 countries and one benchmarking par‐
ticipant.19 The benchmarking entity shared the same IQCM with their respective country. In turn,
the IQCMs trained 90 assistants. Altogether, IQCMs and their assistants observed 509 administration
sessions.
IQCM’s tasks and responsibilities
The IQCMs were required to:
• Work with the NRC to select schools to visit to observe testing sessions and interview the school
coordinator and test administrator
• Collect selected materials being used in their country to conduct the testing, including the School
Coordinator and Test Administrator Manuals
• Compare selected sections of the national version(s) of the School Coordinator and Test Adminis‐
trator Manuals against the international versions and document any substantial differences
• Submit electronic versions of the national version(s) of the above manuals and the results of the
comparison to IEA
• Observe the ICILS 2023 testing sessions
• Verify the completeness and accuracy of the lists of participating classes, teachers, and students
for each visited school
• Interview the school coordinator about ICILS 2023 activities leading up to and including the test
administration
• Interview the test administrator about the actual administration of ICILS 2023
• Record the observations and the interview responses on the Classroom Observation Records
(CORs) and submit these to IEA
Observing the ICILS administration sessions
IQCMs were required to document their observations of testing sessions using standardized CORs.
The observation records were organized into four sections:
19 Please note that throughout this chapter we will use the term 35 countries when referring to the 34 countries and
one benchmark participant.

QUALITY ASSURANCE IN THE ICILS DATA COLLECTION
127
• Section A: Preliminary activities;
• Section B: Administration process;
• Section C: Summary observations; and
• Section D: Interview with the school coordinator and the test administrator.
In Section A of the COR, IQCMs registered their observations of the condition of the assessment
materials and testing equipment. Section B addressed different aspects related to the administration
of the test and questionnaire, including how accurately the test administrator followed the prescribed
administration procedures and read the administration scripts. Section C collected IQCMs’ general
impressions of the ICILS 2023 assessment and Section D was dedicated to the interview(s) with the
school coordinator and test administrator, to get their evaluation of the ICILS 2023 assessment, gather
suggestions for improvement, and obtain additional background information.
While IQCMs could decide for themselves how they recorded their findings during the school visits,
they were required to enter the results of their observations and their notes in an online version of the
COR after every school visit. The online version of the COR was accessible through an online platform
and all information needed to be entered in English. IEA received documentation of the international
quality control program from all 35 countries in ICILS 2023. The results of the IQCM observations are
reported in the following sections. The tables and bulleted text throughout this chapter presents the
IQCM responses to the questions in the COR.
9.2 Findings from the ICILS 2023 administration observations
Preliminary activities and condition of materials for the assessment
In the first section of the COR, IQCMs noted their observations of the condition of the assessment
materials, the testing equipment, and the test administrator’s level of technical preparation.
In line with Table 9.1, the survey materials were stored safely and securely in all cases where applicable
(i.e., where physical materials such as USB sticks were used). Besides the test administrator, school
coordinators and IT support staff often had access to the assessment room prior to the testing session.
In addition, other individuals such as (vice) principals, the NRC, teachers of the class, handymen, or
education department representatives could also access the room in some cases.

In terms of equipment, desktop computers were most commonly used for the assessment (58%), fol‐
lowed by portable computers connected to a power plug (49%), and portable computers not connected
to a power plug (22%). A combination of equipment types was used in some administrations. Despite
the fact that some computers were not connected to a power plug, no power failure issues were
reported.
The workstations were set up by the test administrator in a way that the welcome screen was displayed
prior to the students’ arrival in 82 percent of the observed sessions. In the rest, the computers either
had yet to be turned on when the students arrived (especially when personal laptops were used for the
testing) or were set up by a designated IT support staff rather than the test administrator themselves.
Student IDs were displayed on the login screens in 90 percent of the cases, with student names used
in the remaining 10 percent.
Each testing session started with the administration of computer and information literacy (CIL) mod‐
ules, followed by the administration of the student questionnaire. Countries participating in the com‐
putational thinking (CT) module implemented this after the student questionnaire administration. For
the purpose of this chapter, IQCMs’ observations from the CT modules administration are reported
last, as not all countries administered this module for ICILS 2023.
The ICILS student assessment modules were self‐timed (30 minutes each for CIL/25 minutes each
for CT), with recommended breaks of maximum 5 minutes between each CIL module and the student
questionnaire (administration of which was estimated at approximately 25 minutes). Typically, a longer
break of 15–45 minutes followed before the administration of the CT modules (where applicable).
While the testing session was in progress, the test administrator used a device to keep track of time in

QUALITY ASSURANCE IN THE ICILS DATA COLLECTION
129
the majority of the observed sessions (98%). In the remaining two percent of the observed sessions,
the timer on the screens or a clock in the classroom were used, in other instances the time keeping
task was delegated to somebody else (e.g., an assistant or the IQCM themselves). The modules were
also self‐timed, so that students could track their own progress.
Administration of the CIL module
Table 9.2 shows that IQCMs reported that the assessments were conducted in accordance with the
international procedures. In very rare instances, the IQCMs observed and carefully documented sig‐
nificant deviations from the administration procedures which will be noted throughout this section.
In the majority (94%) of observed sessions, the test administrator ensured that each student sat in
front of the computer specially prepared for him or her, sometimes with the help of the class teacher,
by providing and checking the names/IDs written down on or in front of each computer or sending
them directly to their allocated seats (in case they were familiar with the students). Alternatively, ID‐
s/login details were handed out after the students took their seats. Each student’s participation status
was recorded by the test administrator on the student tracking form in 97 percent of the observed
administration sessions.

One of the most important methods of standardizing the assessment administration was to have all test
administrators follow the script in the Test Administrator Manual. IQCMs reported that in about three‐
quarters (72%) of the observations, the test administrators exactly followed the script when preparing
the students, distributing the materials, and giving directions and examples. In the cases where the test
administrators deviated from the script (28% —141 cases total), most modifications were reported as
“minor” (105 cases) and characterized most frequently as omissions (65 cases), followed by revisions (63
cases) and additions (61 cases). Note that these categories were not mutually exclusive and selecting
multiple of them to characterize the changes (additions, revisions, omissions) was possible.
When IQCMs were asked to report on how many students did not have enough time to complete the
CIL test module for each of the observer sessions:
• “None” of them did not have sufficient time to complete the CIL test modules in 54 percent of
the observed sessions.
• “Some” of them did not have sufficient time to complete the CIL test modules in 43 percent of
the observed sessions.
• “Many” or “all or almost all” of the students did not have sufficient time to complete the CIL test
modules in two percent and one percent of the observed sessions.

130
ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
Similarly, there were at least some students who finished the CIL test early (before the allotted time
was up) in most (93%) of the observed testing sessions. These students were instructed to not disturb
others, remain seated and keep quiet, and read or engage in other silent activities.
As for the CIL test modules content, IQCMs reported they observed that some (43%), many (2%),
or all or almost all (1%) of the students found it difficult or confusing to answer the questions in the
observed testing sessions, while none of the students appeared to have difficulty in the remaining 54
percent. Other than that, IQCMs stated that students may have experienced minor issues in about
36 percent of the observed sessions. These consisted mostly of technical issues such as weak or
slow network connection, network breakdowns causing login problems, or interruptions during the
test and computer or device malfunctions (e.g., USB issues, devices freezing or crashing, keyboard or
mouse not working properly). In the majority of observed sessions, this impacted one or two students
and the issues could be resolved during the administration such that the student could continue with
the assessment. IQCMs also reported that some students experienced problems understanding and
navigating the test according to the instructions (e.g., task skipping, understanding specific tasks), for a
few of the students this was related to the language of the assessment not being their native language.
Additionally, in some instances, students displayed a lack of motivation or did not seem to take the test
seriously.

In accordance with the ICILS procedures, test administrators were asked to secure the test devices and
USB sticks (if applicable) at the end of the testing session (see Table 9.3). The IQCMs reported that this
occurred in 76 percent of the observed CIL administration sessions. In the remaining sessions, there
was a general emphasis across the IQCM responses that at no point were the computers or testing
materials left unattended, as there was always some form of supervision in place—the test administra‐
tors, students, or any additional personnel often remained in the room during breaks, meaning there
was no perceived need to secure the materials. In addition, many responses indicated that breaks were
either very short or non‐existent. In cases where breaks were given (81% of the observed cases), they
were often too brief to require securing the materials. Several responses also noted that the test was
conducted online, which reduced concerns about the physical security of materials such as USB sticks
or paper tests. The IQCM responses collectively highlighted a consistent approach to ensuring test
security through supervision and procedural measures, rather than securing materials in the traditional
sense.

for the StQ on the student tracking form in 95 percent of the observed sessions. For the remaining
five percent, issues with the participation status were resolved at a later stage by IEA together with
the NRC.

about three‐quarters of the observations (78%), the test administrators followed the script exactly. In
the cases where the test administrators deviated from the script (21%—109 cases total), most modifi‐

Administration of the CT module
Table 9.6 illustrates IQCMs’ observations of the CT module administration. Only the 355 observed
sessions that included CT administration were included in the analysis. IQCMs reported that the test
administrator ensured that all students were sitting at the correct computers in 97 percent of the
observed sessions. In the two percent of the observed sessions where this procedure was reportedly
not followed, the most commonly given reason was that this was not necessary as students remained
seated during the break or there was no break altogether. In a few other cases, the test administrator
trusted the students to come back to their assigned seats.
According to IQCMs’ reports, the test administrators followed the CT administration script exactly in
82 percent of the observed sessions. In the cases where the test administrators deviated from the
script (17%—60 cases total), most modifications were reported as “minor” (42 cases) and characterized
most frequently as omissions (38 cases), followed by revisions (28 cases) and additions (10 cases).
Note that these categories were not mutually exclusive and selecting multiple of them to characterize
the changes (additions, revisions, omissions) was possible. Most frequently, the changes mentioned
in IQCM reports were adaptations or simplifications made by the test administrator to fit the target
group and classroom situation, omissions of details about the different modules, types of questions or
procedural specifics like the timer, and rephrasing and summarizing of the instructions using the test
administrator’s own words.

The IQCMs reported that all students had time to complete the CT test modules in nearly two‐thirds
(59%) of observed testing sessions. In the rest of the observed testing sessions, in less than one

modules and some of them did not have enough time in 38 percent of the sessions. There were at

and keep quiet, read or engage in other silent activities. In few cases, students were also allowed to

As for the CT test modules content, IQCMs reported that some of the students found it difficult or
confusing to answer the questions in 47 percent of the observed testing sessions, many of them in six
percent of the sessions, all or almost all in one percent of the observed sessions, and none in 44 percent
of the sessions. Other than that, IQCMs reported that students experienced minor problems with the
testing in about 21 percent of the observed sessions. These problems usually affected only one to
two students per observed session and consisted mostly of problems with login, technical issues with
the hardware, software or internet connection, problems with concentration or motivation (sometimes
also exacerbated by a noisy environment created by other students who finished early) and problems
with understanding specific task instructions.

133
Summary observations of the ICILS 2023 administration
Section C of the COR included questions about IQCMs general impressions of how overall the ad‐
ministration was conducted and how well the test administrator monitored students (Table 9.7), the
students’ cooperation (Table 9.8), and any special circumstances or technical issues (Tables 9.9 and
9.10) that arose during the session. The below tables report the IQCM responses on these.
Administration and test administrator monitoring
IQCMs perceived that most test administrators (96%) were familiar with the test administration script.
In addition, a majority of the observed administration sessions took place under favorable room con‐
ditions that were suitable for students to work without distraction (91%), as indicated by the IQCMs.
The most commonly reported inconveniences were noise distractions from outside the classroom,
temperature issues (i.e., the classroom being either too hot or too cold), poor lighting or ventilation, at
times lack of space between the individual desks (affecting 11% of the observed sessions, as reported
in Table 9.7) or disruptions by the school personnel entering the room or talking among themselves.
The test administrator walked around the room to be sure students were completing the test or the
questionnaires and/or behaving properly in nearly all (98%) of the observed sessions. In the rest of the
sessions (2%), the test administrator sat at their desk and monitored the students from there. Accord‐
ing to the IQCMs’ opinions, the test administrator addressed students’ questions appropriately in 95
percent of the observed sessions. In the remaining cases, the test administrator struggled to answer
the students’ questions, gave answers that were too specific, or simply referred students to the task
instructions.

Students’ cooperation
Student refusal to take the assessment was rare and only occurred in six percent of observed as‐
sessment sessions (see Table 9.8), most often (in 3% of the observed sessions) only a single student
within the classroom refused. There were a few problems observed in relation to test administrators
accurately following the instructions for excusing the student who refused to take the test, which con‐
sisted either of the student not being responsive or the test administrator not making a record of this
anywhere.
There were no late students in the majority (86%) of observed sessions. Out of the students who
did arrive late (14% of observed sessions), most were still admitted into the room (10%), either before
(6%) or after (4%) the beginning of the administration. Across all the observed sessions, there were 19

134
ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
reported cases (4%) of late students not admitted to the room. In 17 percent of the observed adminis‐
tration sessions, students left the room for an “emergency” during the test administration (examples of
“emergencies” included students needing to use the toilet, or feeling unwell at the time of the testing).
In 83 percent of such cases, IQCMs reported that the test administrator addressed the situation appro‐
priately. In the 15 percent of the cases where IQCMs did not perceive this was handled appropriately,
the IQCMs reported that the test administrator did not take any action or that the computer was not
secured during this time. However, IQCMs often clarified that it did not seem necessary to secure the
computer.

Did you see any evidence of students attempting to cheat on the

While in most observed testing sessions (11%) students complied with the instructions to store ev‐
erything away, in a few cases (7 reported cases—1% of observed sessions) this instruction was never
given and students kept their cell phones on them. In some cases, students were allowed to use their
phones or other devices after they had finished the test to keep them from disturbing others. In addi‐
tion, the IQCMs noted that test administrators and teachers faced difficulties in consistently enforcing
these rules. Repeated interventions by the test administrators were documented in a few cases, with
some teachers resorting to a proactive approach of collecting the phones from students. In addition
to cell phones, some students also reportedly had tablets, e‐readers, or laptops on their desks.
In eight percent of the observed sessions, IQCMs reported seeing evidence of students attempting to
cheat, such as glancing at fellow students’ screens, discussing among themselves or using their phones
when the test administrator wasn’t looking. IQCMs reported that test administrators intervened in ap‐
propriate ways whenever they noticed this. However, the IQCMs also pointed out that the assessment
was different for individual students, which made it unlikely for them to effectively cheat.
Overall, IQCMs described the students as:
• “Extremely orderly and cooperative” in 60 percent of the observed sessions
• “Moderately orderly and cooperative” in 31 percent of the observed sessions
• “Somewhat orderly and cooperative” in eight percent of the observed sessions
• And “Disorderly and hardly cooperative” at all in the remaining two percent of the observed
sessions

QUALITY ASSURANCE IN THE ICILS DATA COLLECTION
135
The additional comments on student behavior during the testing reflect the above data and reveal
a mix of positive and less positive observations. While many students were described as extremely
orderly, cooperative, and well‐behaved, other students were described as restless, unmotivated, and
slightly disruptive. In many cases, the combination of fatigue, technical difficulties, and early finishers
led to some level of disorder.
Special circumstances
There were students using special accommodations (additional support) to complete the instruments
in 12 percent of the observed sessions (Table 9.9). Several students were reportedly granted additional
time, with specifics including 10 extra minutes (7 cases), 20 extra minutes (1 case), and multiple un‐
specified instances. For other students, the timer was disabled altogether, which in a few cases turned
out to be unnecessary as they completed the test before the allotted time ran out. Multiple students
completed their tests in a separate room, often with an additional test administrator or teacher present
to help, while others received help from a teacher or assistant in reading instructions and/or writing
their responses in the classroom. In addition, a tablet was used to translate test content into another
language for some students, and special information or translation assistance was provided for lan‐
guage reasons in a few cases. There were also reported cases of students with a social‐emotional
support assistant in the room to manage behavior or students using headphones during the test.
