Where accommodations were provided, these were reported to be consistent with the agreed accom‐
modations listed on the student tracking form in 73 percent of the observed cases. In the remaining
22 percent of reported cases, the inconsistencies mentioned were either cases where students were
listed to require special accommodations but were effectively not using them, or not listed but using
accommodations regardless. The need for accommodations was often only identified on the day of
the assessment, contrary to the student tracking form which listed that no accommodation was nec‐
essary. In one case, it was reported that a test administrator attempted to provide the accommodation
(disabling the timer), but was not successful.
Technical challenges
There were technical problems reported with using computers in 27 percent of the observed ses‐
sions, using USB sticks in six percent of the observed sessions, logging into the assessment player
in 12 percent of the observed sessions, timer function in two percent of the observed sessions and
other malfunctions when using the assessment player in 23 percent of the observed sessions (see
Table 9.10). Most commonly mentioned were login and connectivity issues, applications or computers
freezing or crashing, language or display issues, hardware malfunctions, and timer or other assessment
player issues. Within the observed sessions where technical problems occurred (47% of the observed
sessions), the IQCMs reported that the test administrator solved those problems effectively in 85 per‐
cent of the relevant cases. However, in the remaining 15 percent of the relevant cases (7% of all
observed sessions), test administrators were not always able to solve the problems effectively or the
solution took longer than expected. There were cases (although these were the exceptions) where
despite repeated attempts and contacting IT support the issue could not be resolved. A member of
IT staff was available to provide support throughout the ICILS administration process in 71 percent of

136

the observed cases.
As in other instances, IQCMs also reported that technical issues arose only with one to two students
per observed session. In addition to that, IQCMs also mentioned that overall when these issues arose,
test administrators and IT support, were able to quickly address these, minimizing the overall impact
on the testing.

In over three quarters (78%) of the relevant observed sessions, the IQCMs reported that the test

sent to them instead.
General impressions
When IQCMs were asked to report on the overall quality of the ICILS administration:
• Forty‐five percent of the IQCMs described the quality as “Excellent”
• Thirty‐five percent of the IQCMs described the session as “Very Good”
• Thirteen percent of the IQCMs described the session as “Good”
• Only four percent and three percent of the IQCMs described the session as “Fair” or “Poor”
The additional IQCM verbal feedback on the ICILS 2023 testing sessions highlighted that the admin‐
istration process was generally deemed well‐organized, with many praising the professionalism and
preparedness of the test administrators and school coordinators. Students were described as mostly
cooperative and engaged, although there were instances where the students displayed challenging be‐
havior or rushed through the tests. In this line, IQCMs also reported that some students showed some
signs of fatigue due to the length of the testing sessions. In some other instances, some technical

Interview with the school coordinator and test administrator
The IQCMs conducted interviews with the school coordinators and test administrators according to the
guidelines included in Section D of the COR. The purpose of this activity was to solicit an evaluation
about the ICILS administration from the responsible persons, to collect suggestions for improvement,
and to obtain additional background information on survey‐related activities such as:
• Shipment of assessment materials
• Arrangements for ICILS administration
• Responsiveness of the national center to queries
• Organization of classes in the school (to validate within‐school sampling procedure)
School coordinators
When school coordinators were asked about their position at that school/school district, it was re‐
ported that:
• Thirty‐two percent worked as assistants or deputies to the principal/school head.
• Twenty‐one percent worked as a teacher from that school, (although not as a teacher of the
class assessed).
• Twenty percent acted as principals or school heads.
• Eleven percent worked as the teachers of the assessed class.
• Fifteen percent held other positions at the school or school district.

138
ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
• One percent acted as counselors for that particular school.
The school coordinators rated the attitude of the school staff members towards ICILS 2023 as positive
in 62 percent of cases, neutral in 31 percent, and negative in the remaining seven percent of the cases.
The related comments showed that the majority of staff members were positive and supportive of
the assessment, appreciated its significance and were willing to contribute in most cases. Especially
schools with a focus on digital literacy viewed the assessment as relevant and were curious about the
results, and staff members valued the professionalism and the experience gained from participating in
an international assessment. In addition, some staff members were already familiar with such tests,
finding no major issues with participation. Nonetheless, a portion of staff often viewed the assessment
as an additional burden due to its timing and the preexisting workload. The timing of the assessment
was commonly criticized as it coincided with national exams and the end of the school year, leading to
some stress and inconvenience. Furthermore, some teachers felt that the assessment affected regular
teaching schedules and added pressure during busy periods. Some other points of concern were
the lack of (immediate) feedback from the assessment, logistical challenges in organizing the test, or
doubts about the necessity and benefits of the assessment. Overall, while there was some resistance
and fatigue due to the additional workload, the prevailing attitude reported was cooperative with an
understanding and acknowledgment of the test’s importance.
As for school coordinator preparations prior to the assessment day, there were a small number of
cases where some components from the shipments of test materials were not received by the school
coordinator, as Table 9.12 shows. In many instances, USB sticks were brought by test administrators on
test day, and schools often did not receive return envelopes or boxes. Common explanations for this
were that the process was managed electronically, reducing the need for physical materials, or that the
test administrators (rather than school coordinators) were responsible for receiving and managing test
materials, including manuals and USB sticks. In a few cases, school coordinators received the shipment
from the NSC but did not open or inspect them due to time constraints or perceived irrelevance, while
other school coordinators were still waiting to receive (some of) the materials at the time of reporting.
Many responses also indicated that external organizations, such as the NSC or contracted companies,
handled significant parts of the process, including material distribution and collection. In one country,
the School Coordinator Manual was not used as such, instead, a curated website designed by the national
center was shared with school coordinators via email.
School coordinators reported that the NSC was responsive to questions and concerns in 89 percent
of the cases. In most other cases, the school coordinators mentioned delays and fluctuations in com‐
munication, depending on the time period. In line with technical preparations and procedures, 79
percent of the school coordinators reported they had run the ICILS Compatibility Check Program on
at least some of the computers prior to the assessment. In the cases this was not done (21%), the
reasons provided included reliance on external support for this (from the test administrator, NSC, or
IT specialists), lack of information or awareness of its necessity, logistical constraints (e.g., lack of time
or access to the devices prior to the assessment), assumptions of pre‐checked equipment, and either
having conducted other (partial) checks or deeming the compatibility check unnecessary (e.g., due to
the use of online testing platforms).

Overall, a vast majority (95%) of school coordinators were satisfied with the setup they were able to ar‐
range for the administration of the assessment and questionnaire. The reasons for dissatisfaction (5%)
included technical issues (e.g., unreliable computers, software issues, confusion about compatibility
checks), logistical challenges (e.g., changes in test dates, tight schedule, time and administrative con‐
straints), coordination or communication issues, insufficient room preparation or control over students,
and concerns about inclusivity.
As for incentives, teachers, ICT coordinators and/or the school principal were reported to receive
incentives for participating in ICILS 2023 in 13 percent of the visited schools. The most common
incentives for school staff included monetary compensation, gift cards, and snacks or meals during
breaks. Certificates and small gifts were also frequently mentioned, alongside professional credits and
benefits (e.g., free time, access to webinars). While many incentives were provided, there were several
instances where promised incentives had not yet been received at the time of the interview.
When school coordinators were asked to report on some of the forms used during the test admin‐
istration operations (see more information about this in Chapter 8), 88 percent of the coordinators
provided the list of classes in the tested grade to the NSC and 97 percent of the IQCMs reported that
the information provided by them matched the information provided on the listing form (see Table
9.13). The only deviation mentioned was a case of the student listing form not matching the identi‐
fication of students excluded with Special Educational Needs on the registration form. To the best of
school coordinators knowledge, there were no students in this grade level enrolled in more than one of
the grade 8 classes that they listed in 99 percent of the cases. The reported exceptions were students
enrolled in special programs to support them due to their academic difficulties. All students participat‐
ing in the ICILS assessment were listed on the student listing form in 90 percent of the reported cases.
The students not listed on the listing forms were predominantly due to absences (sickness, personal
reasons), new arrivals or transfers, exclusions by parents or due to behavioral issues, and tracking form
discrepancies.

The teacher listing form included all eligible teachers for the target grade in 94 percent of the reported
cases. The primary reasons for teachers not being listed on the forms included administrative over‐
sights, misunderstandings, sampling processes, teacher availability, and lack of clarity or time to include
all teachers. In a few cases, the school coordinator had not compiled the list yet or did not know why
certain teachers were not included.
Only a bit over third (35%) of the school coordinators were reportedly involved in the selection and
training of test administrators at their school (see Table 9.14). All (100%) of the relevant school coordi‐
nators reported this went well at their school and nearly all (98%) reported the Test Administrator Manual
was useful for the purpose. Out of the relevant school coordinators, 84 percent were given time by
the principal to do the training. Some school coordinators expressed a need for more efficient train‐
ing provided by the NSC due to time constraints, especially for those with additional responsibilities.
Both school coordinators and test administrators often had to self‐train using the provided manuals.
Eighty‐six percent of school coordinators expressed that they felt the ICILS 2023 School Coordinator
Manual worked well. Suggestions for improvement included more efficient organization of the man‐
uals, better scripting, and more distinctive font usage to enhance readability. In addition, making the
manual shorter, simpler, with clearer instructions would have been appreciated. Enhancing the train‐
ing and communication, addressing specific instructional gaps, and tailoring content to country‐specific
needs were also highlighted as important improvements to the process. Overall, while there were some
challenges related to time constraints, material clarity, and role overlap, school coordinators generally
managed to prepare and select test administrators effectively through collaboration, self‐training, and
external support.

Altogether, and concerning the overall quality of the ICILS 23 administration:
• Seventy‐six percent of the school coordinators reported that the administration in their school
went “overall very well”
• Twenty‐two percent of the school coordinators reported that the administration went “satisfac‐
torily with few problems”
• Only two percent of the school coordinators indicated that the administration went “unsatisfac‐
torily and with many problems”
In line with the coordinators’ positive impressions about the study administration, the vast majority
(90%) reported that they would be willing to serve in this role again.
Additional feedback provided by the coordinators reflected a mix of positive and critical insights about
the ICILS testing process. While many appreciated the opportunity for international experience and
praised the organization and support from the NSC, there were also some worries about the time and
effort required for preparation and administration, which at times impacted other school activities and
caused some stress for both students and staff. Issues with the technology, the timing of the tests,
and the complexity of questionnaires were common themes. School coordinators suggested the need
for better timing in the administration of the study, ideally not overlapping with busy periods such as
exams or holidays, the provision of more streamlined and accessible training and materials, improved
technical support, and more flexibility to accommodate specific school contexts circumstances.
Test administrators
IQCMs also interviewed the test administrators—in 17 percent of the cases this was the same person as
the school coordinator. About 22 percent of test administrators were teachers from a different class,
nine percent teachers from the assessed class, four percent assistants or deputy principals/school
heads, two percent principals/school heads, one percent school counselors and the rest (63%) fell
into the “other” category—mostly external individuals not affiliated with the schools being assessed.
They were hired by various external entities such as the NSC or consulting agencies. Several test
administrators held positions as educational methodologists (or were associated with methodological
centers), other educational professionals (e.g., psychologists, retired educators, researchers) or student
assistants.

142
ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
Regarding test administrator preparations prior to the assessment day, there was a small number of
cases where some materials were not received by the test administrator, as shown in Table 9.16. Nearly
83 percent of test administrators felt the ICILS 2023 Test Administrator Manual worked well, while the
remaining test administrators indicated the manual needs improvement. The suggested improvements
included for the manual to be simplified and made more concise, using language that is easy for students
to understand and eliminating redundant information. Test administrators also suggested structure
improvements such as providing separate guides for different tests, boxing read‐aloud instructions,
highlighting key information or providing other visual aids. The test administrators further expressed
a need for clear timing instructions and strategies for managing student behavior, especially for early
finishers and those requiring extra time. Additionally, it was mentioned that tips on addressing technical
issues would improve usability. Providing supplementary materials like PowerPoint instructions and an
FAQ section was also suggested to further support test administrators.
As for the setup of the administration of the assessment and questionnaire, 93 percent of the test
administrators who were different from the school coordinator expressed their satisfaction with this
(77% of test administrators overall). The remaining seven percent pointed out that the preparation and
setup phase faced challenges due to technical issues, inadequate IT support, and cumbersome logistics
(e.g., managing insufficient school staff and the timing of the questionnaire), which often fell solely on
the test administrators’ shoulders. Additionally, environmental factors and room arrangements hin‐
dered effective student monitoring and contributed to a sub‐optimal testing environment. Behavioral
issues and lack of student motivation further complicated the testing process.

Over one‐third (35%) of test administrators reported there were no problems encountered during the
ICILS administration. Out of the remaining 65 percent of test administrators, 36 percent reported
any problems encountered were listed on the test administrator forms while the rest (29%) did not
list those. The issues described in test administrator comments included technical problems such as
browser crashes, internet connectivity issues, computer malfunctions, software freezes, and USB stick
failures. There were also disruptions caused by late arrivals, traffic delays, and unexpected entries by
teachers and students. In addition, some students faced difficulties with logging in, completing tasks,
and understanding instructions due to some discrepancies in the manual and the tutorial. Some behav‐
ioral issues also arose, such as students not taking the survey seriously, logging out prematurely, or not
finishing modules. Few of the reported problems were specific to task functionality (e.g., non‐working
backspace keys and unresponsive maps). Despite these challenges, most issues were reportedly ad‐
dressed on the spot by IT support or solved by restarting the systems.
Overall, 67 percent of the test administrators who were different from the school coordinator said

QUALITY ASSURANCE IN THE ICILS DATA COLLECTION
143
the assessment administration went very well with no problems, 28 percent said it went satisfactorily
with few problems and only five percent said it went unsatisfactorily with many problems, reflecting
the issues mentioned above. Despite the challenges, 92 percent of the test administrators expressed
they would be willing to serve as a test administrator again. Open feedback from test administrators
highlighted several areas for improvement, as well as some positive aspects of the process. Many test
administrators noted that the test duration was too long and suggested that longer breaks between
modules or splitting the test into two parts could enhance focus and performance. There were con‐
cerns about the complexity and length of the instructions and manuals, which could be streamlined
for clarity. Technical issues with equipment and logistical challenges underscored the need for better
technical support and preparation. Test administrators also expressed a wish for more comprehensive
pre‐test training and a demo version of the test to better prepare themselves for student questions.
On a positive note, the cooperation from NRCs and the support from IEA were appreciated, and the
standardized approach was seen as beneficial. Overall, while the process was generally well‐organized,
adjustments in timing, training, and technical support were suggested to improve the testing experi‐
ence.
9.3 Findings from the national materials review
As part of their tasks, IQCMs reviewed selected sections of the national versions of the School Co‐
ordinator and Test Administrator Manuals, to ensure the content was comparable to the international
versions.
For the School Coordinator Manual, the selected section was one outlining the exclusion and accom‐
modation rules that school coordinators should follow.
Twenty‐five out of the 35 participating countries made some adaptations or omissions to the reviewed
text. A majority of these were very minor and/or necessary to reflect the respective country’s context,
most commonly affecting the passages and codes detailing the specific accommodations made for
students requiring additional support, often reflecting the fact that a type of support/accommodation
was not available in the given country due to lack of resources or other organizational limitations. In
five countries, special accommodations for students requiring additional support were not available at
all. In addition, some omissions were made related to some parts not being applicable, e.g., related
to a specific mode of administration (i.e., USB) in a given country. Another common omission was
related to a paragraph describing how to fill in the column of a student listing form related to the
language of the test, which was optional for school coordinators to fill in. It is also interesting to note
that in one country the School Coordinator Manual as such was not used and was replaced by another
resource (a curated website) providing the same information in what was considered a more engaging
and digestible format.
For the Test Administrator Manual, IQCMs were asked to review a section including the script describing
the assessment procedures to students, particularly the test introduction. There were adaptations or
omissions to the reviewed text reported in 21 of the 35 countries. Omissions of full sections were
very rare, only reported in two countries and both related to the visual coding tutorial. Adaptations
to the test administrator script were more common and mostly related to the technical/procedural
instructions, such as “You must click on the arrow to move to the next task” or “You cannot return to
previous tasks.” In these cases, the text was often rephrased or reworded (while the overall meaning
remained), for example, replacing “must” with “need to” or simply the “click on” instruction. As reported
by one of the IQCMs, “The main difference is the choice of words used to convey the same idea.” In
addition, specific instructions from the tutorial messages (such as “Go back” or “Try again”) have been
correctly adjusted to reflect the adaptations/translations used in the national materials.
Some additional fillers or omissions for test instructions reported more than once were leaving out a
mention about the displayed websites being safe for students to use (which was already implied by the
previous sentences), leaving out information on the breaks between modules and what to do once a
student completed the assignment, leaving out the reminder to complete the modules independently

144
ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
and not disturbing others, naming few examples of tasks students might encounter (e.g., visual coding,
programming), or adding some motivational sentences such as “Please try to do your best in the tests.
Please try to think of working on them as a kind of international competition in which you and pupils
from other schools represent [name of the country]” and advising the test administrator “One way to
do this is to start with a short talk about the importance of the work they [the students] are about to
undertake.” Finally, some adjustments were made in a few countries related to how the timing of the
test and tutorial was described, while still preserving the important information (such as changing “no
more than 30 mins” to “exactly 30 mins,” or not specifically mentioning the 5 minutes inbuilt into the
timer for the tutorial but highlighting that this timer is only there as an indication in this case and that
the time for the tutorial is in fact not limited).
Some additions were also made to the StQ instructions, such as reminders that students did not have
to answer all the questions if they did not wish to, that the results would not affect students’ grades,
that it is not possible to trace a response back to the individual students, or that students should not
eat or drink at the computers. Differences were also reported in the specific terms used to refer to the
“parents/guardians” in the instructions, presumably also in line with the country‐specific adaptations
made to the national instruments.
In general, any modifications reported by the IQCMs to the national versions of the School Coordinator
and Test Administrator Manual were minor and did not compromise the quality of the collected data.
9.4 Findings from the survey activities questionnaire
As another valuable source of information on the overall quality of the ICILS administration, the SAQ
gathered information from the NRCs about whether the implementation of the ICILS procedures ac‐
corded with the standards outlined in the Survey Operations Procedures (SOP) manuals. The SAQ also
collected feedback on the strengths and weaknesses of the approaches and materials used. It covered
various topics including:
• sampling
• contacting schools
• recruiting school coordinators
• translating and preparing the survey instruments
• administering the assessment
• implementing the national quality monitoring program
• scoring open‐ended response items
• entering and coding occupation data
• submitting data
• time required for survey activities
• other experiences
One major purpose of the SAQ is to gather information to improve the quality of future IEA studies.
Data were collected online from each NRC personally, with data manager and/or other national center
staff assisting this process where necessary. The results presented in this section reflect the quality of
the ICILS procedures and materials in all of the participating countries.
Sampling related procedures
The first part of the SAQ collected information regarding sampling related procedures. Table 9.17 pro‐
vides the NRCs’ responses to questions about sample selection and other related matters. Out of the

QUALITY ASSURANCE IN THE ICILS DATA COLLECTION
145
35 respondents, 32 (all those who answered the question) indicated they felt well supported by the IEA
sampling team. Concerning the Survey Operations Procedures Unit describing the sampling process,
32 NRCs felt it sufficiently described the process for defining and identifying the target populations
of the survey, as well as developing national sampling plans. As for the creation of a restricted school
frame and of randomized school IDs, 33 NRCs felt this was sufficiently described in the SOP.
According to the NRCs, there were some conditions or organizational constraints that required devia‐
tions from the standard ICILS 2023 main survey within‐school sampling design in seven cases. These
included the exclusion of private schools or special classes, differences between laws in different parts
of a country or the overlap with a Trends in International Mathematics and Science Study (TIMSS) 2023
sample. Problems with participating in TIMSS 2023 grade 8 with the same schools were reported by
six NRCs, resulting in the same students being tested in both surveys in some countries, while the
overlap was intentionally avoided in others.

Contacting schools and recruiting school coordinators
The following section of the SAQ collected information regarding school recruitment. Table 9.19 pro‐

of means to contact schools and different materials to request school participation in the main sur‐

relevant ministries. Schools’ participation in ICILS 2023 was compulsory in 15 of the participating
countries and benchmarking participants (for all schools in 10 countries and in some schools for the
remaining five). Relatedly, the NRCs reported difficulties in convincing schools to participate in 21 of
the countries. This was mostly due to the busy workload during the time of the year and limited staff
capacity.

Administering the student instruments and adult questionnaires
The section of the SAQ inquiring about main survey administration addressed some important aspects
of the data collection; selected results are presented in Tables 9.27, 9.28, and 9.29. In line with the
data collected by the test administrator interviews, NRCs confirmed that at least some of their test
administrators were drawn from national center staff or contracted externally, but it was more common
to recruit test administrators from among the school coordinators or teachers, either of the sampled
schools or from other schools. test administrators were mostly trained through formal training sessions
(29 cases), through telephone, email or video‐link (16), or with written instructions (19). Other means
mentioned were trainings through an external provider, helpdesk, or weekly memos.

Regarding participation (Table 9.28), there were some difficulties to achieve high participation rates of
teachers (in 24 countries), students (in 13 countries), ICT coordinators (in 11 countries), or principals
(in 10 countries). According to the NRC responses, the participation rates were negatively impacted
by several factors including the voluntary nature of the study, technical issues, and logistical chal‐
lenges. Teachers were at times less motivated due to the time‐consuming nature of the survey, lack
of remuneration, and skepticism about the assessments. Competing international and national exams,
teacher shortages, heavy workloads, and end‐of‐year schedules further reduced participation. Efforts
like constant communication and individualized follow‐ups were necessary but not always effective in
overcoming these barriers.

Finally, most (28) NRCs noted that more than 75 percent of student tests and questionnaires were

percent in seven countries.
National quality monitoring program
IEA provided materials to assist national centers in conducting their own national quality control pro‐
gram. The ICILS 2023 Survey Operations Procedures Unit 4 provided guidelines for selecting the
national quality control monitors (NQCMs), and basic information about their duties. In addition, the
ICILS 2023 National Quality Control Monitor Manual was developed for use in training NQCMs. The
NQCMs’ responsibilities were similar to those of the IQCMs, and involved visiting selected schools to
observe and document the data collection sessions and interview school coordinators. Twenty‐eight
NRCs confirmed that they had implemented a national quality control program (Table 9.30), the number
of NQCMs appointed ranging from one to 30 and the number of school visits across the participat‐
ing countries being 664. In the countries where this was not implemented, it was often mentioned
that test administrators were contracted from the NSC (thus deeming additional control monitoring
unnecessary) or that there was a lack of time and resources.

NQCMs were trained mostly through formal training sessions (in 22 countries), written instructions (in
12 countries) or through telephone, email, or video link (in 10 countries). The NQCM Manual was used
in all 28 countries which conducted the National Quality Monitoring Program and the COR in nearly
all (27) of them. Adaptations to the NQCM Manual were made in six countries.
In line with the IQCM reports, the NQCMs confirmed the quality of the survey process was gener‐
ally good. In nine countries NQCMs documented some major problems or special circumstances that
occurred frequently during the assessment administration. The problems reported included test ad‐
ministrators not duly following the script or other administration procedures, students skipping through
the assessment without making any effort, unsuitable rooms being used for the assessment or techni‐
cal difficulties. In six countries, actions were undertaken by the NRC as a follow‐up to these problems.
In addition to NQCMs, the NRCs also collaborated with IQCMs. Only one NRC reported experiencing
issues in working with the IQCM, explaining that preparing materials needed for the IQCM was cum‐
bersome and time‐consuming. Nonetheless, no other complaints were made by the NRCs regarding
the appointed IQCMs.
Scoring open‐ended response items and coding occupation data
NRCs were also asked to comment on the persons responsible for scoring open‐ended response items,
the procedures used to verify scoring reliability, and the coding of occupation data (see Table 9.31). The
participating countries used scorers with a variety of different backgrounds, including national center
staff, teachers/professional educators, university students or externals with degrees in relevant areas.
In one country, this was done by the test administrators. The number of scorers employed in countries
ranged from four to 53 and the number of days for their training, between one and 10 days.
When asked about difficulties using the web‐based administration and scoring system to create user
accounts for scorers and to allocate them to teams, eight NRCs reported experiencing issues with
this. These difficulties were mostly related to user role permissions in the system and problems with
assigning scorers to multiple teams. Five NRCs also reported difficulties using the web‐based scoring
system to select, score and allocate example pieces of work to be used in the scorer training. As for
the scorers themselves, they experienced difficulties using the scoring system for training and then for
scoring student work in 12 countries, as reported by NRCs. These included software malfunctions, such
as scores not being saved or the system crashing, or the inability to assign a predetermined number

154
ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
of responses to score to each user or double score additional items. However, the most frequent
complaint by far was the system being slow. Only one NRC reported difficulties with the reports for
reliability scoring, related to the reports being updated too late.

As shown in Table 9.32, the countries mostly relied on staff from the national centers to code occupa‐

The coders had experience in this area in about 60 percent (21) of the countries. In addition, NRCs had
access to expert advice on occupational coding in their country in 14 countries. Countries used any‐
where between two and 20 coders to code the occupation data and used a job classification scheme
other than the ISCO‐08 for coding occupations in three cases, in which national classification schemes
were used. There were no difficulties reported with converting national coding scheme to ISCO‐08
(which was available in the national language in most (26) countries), but there were some difficulties
with coding student responses (7 cases) and the occupation coding sheets in Excel (6 cases).

Chapter 10:
Data management and creation of the ICILS
database
Tim Daniel
10.1 Introduction
This chapter describes the procedures for checking the ICILS 2023 data and database creation that
were implemented by IEA and the national study centers of the participating countries. For readability,
this chapter refers to the principal, ICT coordinator, and teacher questionnaires as “adult question‐
naires.”
Preparing the ICILS 2023 international database (IDB) and ensuring its integrity was a complex en‐
deavor requiring extensive collaboration between the international study center (ISC) at IEA and the
national study centers. The IDB was one of the key products released at the end of the study cycle
as it contained all data from all participating countries. These data were the ones used by the ISC to
produce the international report and all accompanying release material. Once the countries had col‐
lected the data files and submitted them to the ISC, data cleaning began. Data cleaning is an extensive
process of checking data for inconsistencies and formatting the data to create a standardized output.
The main goals of the data cleaning process were to ensure:
• All information in the database conformed to the internationally defined data structure;
• The content of all metadata and documentation appropriately reflected national adaptations to
questionnaires;
• All variables used for international comparisons were comparable across countries (after harmo‐
nization where necessary); and
• All stakeholders involved in this process applied quality control measures throughout, to assure
the quality and accuracy of the ICILS 2023 data.
10.2 Data sources
Computer‐based student assessment
As a computer‐based assessment, ICILS 2023 exclusively generated electronic data from the student
assessment along with paper or electronic tracking information. One version of the student assessment
software existed per country and administered language. Overall, 50 language versions across 32
countries were created. It included all test modules and the context questionnaire, and supported
all assessment languages. The student assessment was conducted using the RM Assessment Master
Examiner (see Chapter 3).
Each student was assigned an instrument version that contained two of the seven computer and in‐
formation literacy (CIL) test modules as well as the student questionnaire. In those countries where
the computational thinking (CT) modules were administered, students were assigned two additional
CT assessment modules after the questionnaire session. All three components were administered as
timed tests (see Chapter 1) and were to be done in one go by students. Short breaks in between each
components were given. Administration modes offered were:
• Internet (browser‐based) delivery;
• USB flash drive delivery (for use with school computers running Windows operating systems);

158
ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
and
• Server mode delivery (where one school laptop hosted the software and student computers
connected to this local server then).
As the default administration methods for ICILS 2023, the national study centers provided schools
with one USB stick per student (plus spare sticks in case of technical failure) or the web address on
which the online version of the assessment was hosted. If the computers at a school were deemed
unsuitable for administering the student assessment on USB or internet connectivity was not given, a
laptop computer could be used to host the application and connect school computers with it (server
mode). Some countries also carried‐in laptops from the national study center to overcome issues with
school computers. The national study centers also provided schools with a student tracking form
that notified the test administrators of the students who were to be assigned the assessment and
questionnaire. The form also allowed administrators to indicate student participation for subsequent
entry and verification of it in WinW3S.
In schools where one of the default administration methods was utilized, data from the student as‐
sessment were either stored on the individual USB sticks used to deliver the assessment or directly
saved on an international server if the assessment was taken online. If the server method was utilized,
then the student data were stored on the server laptop that was used to administer the assessment.
After the assessment, the national study centers uploaded the data from USB sticks or server laptop
to a central international server. For assessments conducted using the online method, an upload was
obsolete.
The data were transposed from long format to a wide format so that they could be transformed for
processing and analysis. This process resulted in a predefined table structure, containing one record
per student as well as all variables that were to be used for data processing, analysis, and reporting.
During this step, a set of calculations needed to take place. Examples include automatic scoring of
some of the complex or constructed‐response items and large tasks or aggregating time spent on an
item across multiple visits to it.
Online data collection of adult questionnaires
ICILS 2023 was offered as an online‐only data collection for the adult questionnaire. All participating
countries used the OSS software to administer the adult questionnaires.
To ensure confidentiality, national study centers provided every respondent with a letter that contained
individual login information along with information on how to access the online questionnaire. This
login information corresponded to the respondent identification (ID) and password provided from the
WinW3S software, meaning that the identity validation step occurred when the respondents’ logged‐in
to the survey.
As respondents completed their online questionnaires, their data were automatically stored on a cen‐
tral international server. Respondents could log in multiple times until they actively submitted their
responses and, with this, indicated that they finalized it. Once that occurred, the questionnaire was
locked for this respondent from then on. Data for each country‐language combination were stored in
separate tables on the server. The different language versions within countries were then merged (at
the ISC) with the data collected as part of the within‐school sampling process in WinW3S (for details,
please refer to Chapter 8.
Data verification at the national study centers
Before sending the data to the ISC for further processing, national study centers were to carry out
mandatory validation and verification steps on all data and apply corrections as necessary. The cor‐
responding routines were included in the WinW3S software, which automatically and systematically
checked ID codes present in the OSS and AM Examiner databases to correspond to the participa‐
tion indicator variables and entries on the tracking forms and as entered in WinW3S. The ID codes

DATA MANAGEMENT AND CREATION OF THE ICILS DATABASE
159
were imported into WinW3S from Excel files formerly created from the OSS and AM systems for this
purpose.
In addition to submitting the data files described above, national study centers provided the ISC with
detailed data documentation, including hard copies or electronic scans of all original, filled student
and teacher tracking forms. The ISC already had access, as part of the layout verification process, to
electronic copies of the national versions of all questionnaires and the final national adaptation forms.
While the questionnaire data were collected, each national study center used the information from
the student and teacher tracking forms to verify the completeness of the materials. Participation
information (e.g., whether the concerned teacher or student had left the school permanently between
the time of sampling and the time of administration) was entered via the WinW3S software.
This process was also supported by the option in WinW3S to generate an inconsistency report. This
report listed all discrepancies between variables recorded during the within‐school sampling and test
administration process and so made it possible to cross check these data against the actual availability
of data entered in the online questionnaires, and the uploaded student data on the central interna‐
tional server hosted by RM. Data managers were requested to resolve these problems before final
data submission to the ISC. If inconsistencies had to remain or the national study center could not
solve them, the ISC asked the center to provide documentation on these problems. The ISC used this
documentation when processing the data at a later stage.
10.3 Confirming the integrity of the national databases
Overview
As described earlier in this chapter, national study centers in each participating country were responsi‐
ble for verifying their national ICILS 2023 data and submitting all accompanying documentation as well
as a finalized WinW3S database including final participation information to the ISC. Furthermore, the
data from the adult online questionnaires were automatically stored on a central international server
hosted by IEA, and the results from the student assessment on a central server hosted by RM. Hence,
the ISC already had the adult questionnaire data available on its server and downloaded the student
data from RM hosted servers once a country announced completion of data collection. Data from both
data sources were then merged together with the participation information received via the WinW3S
database and fed into a comprehensive processing system for checking and editing. To facilitate the
data cleaning process, the ISC asked the national study centers to provide them with detailed docu‐
mentation of their data together with their national data files. The data documentation included copies
of all original survey tracking forms, as well as any additional information from field operations within
a country provided by the national study center. They also submitted their final national adaptation
forms to provide and confirm complete documentation on all national adaptations. In addition, national
study centers were asked to provide documentation on all changes or edits applied to the data prior to
submission, as well as any issues remaining in the data which were already verified and deemed that
they could remain.
Ensuring the integrity of the international database required close cooperation between the interna‐
tional and national institutions involved in ICILS 2023. After each country had submitted its data
and required documentation, the ISC, in collaboration with the national research coordinators (NRCs),
conducted several cleaning procedures upon these:
1. Checking documentation, import, and structure;
2. Cleaning identification (ID) variables;
3. Checking linkages; and
4. Resolving inconsistencies in questionnaire data.
The cleaning process was an iterative process. Numerous iterations of the cleaning procedure were

160
ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
completed on each national data set. This repetition ensured that all data were properly cleaned
and that any new errors that could have been introduced during the data cleaning were rectified.
The cleaning process was repeated as many times as necessary until all data were consistent and
comparable. Any inconsistencies detected during the cleaning process were resolved in collaboration
with national study centers, and all corrections made during the cleaning process were documented in
a cleaning report produced for each country.
Having completed this work, the ISC staff sent queries to the national study centers where inconsisten‐
cies were found. These required the centers to either confirm the ISC’s proposed data‐editing actions
or provide additional information to resolve inconsistencies. After all modifications had been applied,
the ISC rechecked all datasets. This process of editing the data, checking the reports, and implementing
corrections was repeated as many times as necessary to help ensure that data were consistent within
and comparable across countries.
After the national data had been checked, the ISC provided national study centers with univariate
statistics. This material enabled national study centers to compare their national data with the inter‐
national results as they were included in the draft international report and related data and documen‐
tation.
This step was one of the most important quality measures implemented, because it helped to ensure to
increase validity of the data. For example, a particular statistic that might have seemed plausible within
a national context could have appeared as an outlier when the national results were compared with
the international results. The outlier could hint to an error in translation, data capture, coding, etc. The
team at the ISC reviewed all such instances and, where necessary, addressed them, for example, by
recoding the corresponding variables in appropriate ways or, if errors could not be corrected, removing
them from the international database.
Once the national databases had been verified and formatted according to the international file format,
the ISC produced data files containing information on the participation status of students, teachers,
principals, and ICT coordinators in each country’s sample. The ISC then used this information to cal‐
culate sampling weights, population coverage, and school, teacher, and student participation rates.
Chapter 7 of this report provides details about the weighting procedures.
In a subsequent step, the IEA analysis team estimated CIL performance and CT scores as well as ques‐
tionnaire indices for students, teachers, and school‐level survey data (see Chapter 11 and Chapter 12
for scaling methods and procedures). On completing their verification of the sampling weights and
scale scores, the the ISC included these derived variables in the international database and for distri‐
bution to the national study centers.
Data cleaning quality control
Because ICILS 2023 was a large and highly complex study with high standards for data quality, main‐
taining these standards required an extensive set of interrelated data checking and data cleaning proce‐
dures. To ensure all procedures were conducted in the correct sequence, that no special requirements
were overlooked, and that the cleaning process was implemented independently of the persons in
charge, the data quality control included the following steps:
• Thorough testing of all data cleaning programs: before using the programs to process real datasets,
the ISC used mocked simulation datasets containing all possible problems and inconsistencies to
check the accurate set up and work of the cleaning programs.
• Registering all incoming data and documents in a specific database: the ISC recorded the date
of arrival as well as specific issues requiring attention.
• Carrying out data cleaning according to strict rules: deviations from the cleaning sequence were
not possible, and the scope for involuntary changes to the cleaning procedures was minimal.

DATA MANAGEMENT AND CREATION OF THE ICILS DATABASE
161
• Documenting all systematic data recodings that applied to all countries: the ISC recorded these
in the ICILS 2023 Main Study General Cleaning Documentation.
• Documenting every case‐level and country‐specific correction to a country’s data files in a re‐
coding script: logging these changes, which only occurred occasionally, allowed the ISC to undo
changes or to redo the whole manual cleaning process at any later stage of the data‐cleaning
process.
• Repeating, anytime and especially after completion of data cleaning for a country, all cleaning
steps from the beginning: this step allowed the ISC to detect any problems that might have been
inadvertently introduced during the data‐cleaning process.
• Working closely with national study centers at various steps of the cleaning process: the ISC pro‐
vided national study centers with the processed data files and accompanying documentation and
statistics so that center staff could thoroughly review and correct any identified inconsistencies.
Preparing national data files for analysis
The main objective of the data cleaning process was to ensure that the data adhered to international
formats, that student, teacher, and school‐level survey data could be linked across different survey
data files, and that the data reflected the information collected within each country in an accurate and
consistent manner.
The program‐based data cleaning consisted of the activities summarized in Figure 10.1 and explained
in the following subsections. The ISC carried out all these activities in close communication with the
national study centers.

162
ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
Figure 10.1: Overview of data processing at the ISC
Checking documentation, import, and structure
For each country, data cleaning began with an exploratory review of its data file structures and data
documentation (i.e., national adaptation forms, student tracking forms, teacher tracking forms, survey
activities questionnaire).
The ISC began data cleaning by combining the tracking information and sampling information captured
in the WinW3S database with the student‐level database containing the corresponding student survey
instrument data. During this step, the ISC staff also retrieved the data from the principal, ICT coordina‐
tor, and teacher questionnaires. This step also saw data from the different sources being transformed
and imported into one structured query language (SQL) database so that this information would be
available during further data processing stages.

DATA MANAGEMENT AND CREATION OF THE ICILS DATABASE
163
The first checks identified differences between the international and national file structures. Some
countries made adaptations (such as adding national variables or omitting or modifying international
variables) to their questionnaires. The extent and nature of such changes differed across countries:
some countries administered the questionnaires without any modifications (apart from translations and
necessary adaptations relating to culture or language‐specific terms), whereas other countries inserted
response categories within existing international variables or added national variables.
To keep track of adaptations, the ISC asked the national study centers to complete national adaptation
forms. Where necessary, the ISC modified the structure and values of the national data files to ensure
that the resulting data remained comparable across countries. Details about country‐specific adapta‐
tions to the international instruments can be found in Supplement 2 of the ICILS 2023 user guide for
the international database (Meyer et al., 2025).
At this stage, the ISC discarded variables created purely for verification purposes during administration
and made supplement provision for new variables necessary for analysis and reporting. The latter
included reporting variables, derived variables, sampling weights, and scale scores.
The ISC referred problems that could not be rectified automatically to the responsible NRC so that the
national study centers could check the original data collection instruments and tracking forms to trace
the source of these errors. Wherever possible, the ISC suggested a remedy and asked the national
study centers to either accept or propose an alternative. If a national study center could not resolve
problems through verification of the instruments or forms, the ISC applied a general cleaning rule to
the files to rectify the error. When all automatic updates had been applied, the ISC ran individual
recodings in the data to directly apply any remaining corrections to the data files.
Cleaning identification (ID) variables
Each record in a data file needs to have a unique ID number. The existence of records with duplicate
ID numbers in a file implies an error of some kind. If two records in an ICILS database shared the same
ID number and contained exactly the same data, the ISC deleted one of the records and kept the other
one in the database. If both records contained different data and the ISC found it impossible to identify
which record contained the “true data,” they removed both records from the database. The ISC tried
to keep such losses to a minimum; actual deletions were very rare.
Although the ID cleaning covered all data from all instruments, it focused mainly on the student file
given ID mix‐ups or duplicate use was way more likely here. This step in data cleaning included the
preparation of the student test records. Due to the administration of the student test on USB sticks,
data uploaded after test sessions often contained several student records within a country with the
same student ID. In most of the cases, such records were duplicates. In few cases, multiple students
used the same student ID and the correct ID had to be evaluated and assigned after the fact.
The possible sources of multiple records were tracked back to the test administration procedures at
schools or technical constraints of the student test delivery software. Depending on the nature of
a multiple session, the records were used for processing, deleted, assigned a different/corrected ID,
or merged. In addition to checking the unique student ID number, it was crucial to check variables
pertaining to student participation and exclusion status, as well as students’ dates of birth and dates
of testing to calculate student age at the time of testing. The student tracking forms provided an
important tool for resolving anomalies in the database. The records were cleaned by the ISC with
confirmation from national study centers for individual records.
After national study centers had confirmed the cleaned ID variables, the clean databases with infor‐
mation about student participation and exclusion were used by the IEA sampling team to calculate
students’ participation rates, exclusion rates, adjudication flags, and student sampling weights (see
Chapter 7 for details).

164
ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
Checking linkages
In ICILS 2023, student, teacher, and school‐level survey data appeared in different data files at the
respective levels. Correctly linking these records to provide meaningful data for analysis and reporting
was therefore vital. Linkage was implemented through a hierarchical ID numbering system as described
in Chapter 8 of this report. Student ID numbers in the main student data file had to be matched
correctly with those in the reliability scoring file. Ensuring that teacher and student records linked to
their corresponding schools was also important.
Resolving inconsistencies within questionnaire data
The amount of inconsistent and implausible responses in questionnaire data files (examples, see in
section below) varied considerably among countries. The ISC determined the treatment of inconsistent
responses on a question‐by‐question basis, using all available documentation to make an informed
decision. The ISC also checked all questionnaire data for consistency across the responses given.
For example, Question 1 in the principal questionnaire asked for the total school enrollment (number
of boys and number of girls, respectively) in all grades, while Question 2 asked for the enrollment in
the target grade only. Clearly, the number given as a response to Question 2 could not exceed the
number provided in Question 1. Similarly, it was not possible for the sum of all full‐time teachers and
part‐time teachers, as asked in Question 4, to equal zero. In another example, the ICT coordinator
questionnaire Question 7a asked for the total number of ICT devices in the school, and the number of
ICT devices available to students. The total number of ICT devices in the school could not be smaller
than the number available to students.
The ISC flagged inconsistencies of this kind and then asked the national study centers to review. The
ISC recoded those cases that could not be corrected or where the response provided was not usable
for analysis as “omitted.”
Filter questions, which appeared in some questionnaires, directed respondents to a particular sub‐
question or further section of the questionnaire. The ISC applied the following cleaning rule to these
filter questions and the dependent questions that followed: If the answer to the filter question is “no” or
“not applicable,” any responses to the dependent questions were recoded as “logically not applicable.”
The ISC also applied what is known as a split variable check to questions where the answer was coded
into several variables. For example, Question 2 in the ICT coordinator questionnaire asked: “Which
of the following teaching duties do you have?” ICT coordinators responses were captured in a set of
four variables, each one coded as “Yes” if the corresponding “Yes” option was checked and “No” if the
“No” option was filled in. Occasionally, ICT coordinators checked the “Yes” boxes but left the “No”
boxes unchecked. Because, in these cases, it was clear that the unchecked boxes actually meant “No,”
these responses were recoded accordingly, provided that the ICT coordinators had given affirmative
responses in the other categories.
Resolving inconsistencies between questionnaire and tracking data
Two different sets of ICILS 2023 data indicated age and gender for both teachers and students. The
first set was tracking information provided by the school coordinator or test administrator throughout
the within‐school sampling and test/questionnaire administration process. The second set comprised
of actual responses given by individuals in the contextual questionnaires. In some cases, data across
these two sets did not match and resolution was needed.
If the information on gender or birth year and month was missing in the student questionnaire but the
student participated, then this data was copied over from the tracking information to the questionnaire,
if available.
The teacher questionnaire did not ask teachers to provide birth year and month but rather to list

DATA MANAGEMENT AND CREATION OF THE ICILS DATABASE
165
their age. Year of birth, which was indicated in the tracking forms, was then cross checked against
the age indicated by the questionnaire responses. If gender and/or age information was missing from
the teacher questionnaire but the teacher participated, this data was copied over from the tracking
information to the questionnaire.
If discrepancies were found between tracking and questionnaire gender and age data, the questionnaire
information (for both teachers and students) replaced the tracking information.
Note for countries who adapted questions about gender Several countries opted to adapt questions on
gender and added a third category for selection. For cases that selected the third category, the informa‐
tion from tracking forms were not considered as described above. Here, the data for the international
variables were eventually set to missing for these cases rather. For information on which countries
adapted the gender questions, please refer to Supplement 2 of the ICILS 2023 user guide for the
international database (Meyer et al., 2025).
Handling of missing data
Two types of data existed: valid data values and missing data values. Missing data can be assigned a
value of “omitted or invalid,” or “not administered.” The ISC applied additional missing codes to the data
to facilitate further analyses. This process led to four distinct types of missing data in the international
database:
• Presented but not answered, Omitted: The respondent had a chance to answer the question but
did not do so, leaving the corresponding item or question blank. Alternatively, the response was
non‐interpretable or out of range.
• Not administered: This signified that the item or question was not administered to the respondent,
which meant that the respondent could not read and answer the question. The not administered
missing code was used for those student test variables that were not in the sets of modules
administered to a student either deliberately (due to the rotation of modules) or, in very few
cases, due to technical failure or incorrect translations. The missing code was also used for those
records that were included in the international database but did not contain a single response to
one of the assigned questionnaires. This situation applied to students who participated in the
student test but did not answer the student questionnaire. It also applied to school‐level data
where only one of the principal or ICT coordinator questionnaires was returned with responses.
In addition, the not administered code was also used for individual questionnaire items that were
not administered in a national context because the country removed the corresponding question
from the questionnaire or because the translation was incorrect.
• Logically not applicable: The respondent answered a preceding filter question in a way that made
the following dependent questions not applicable.
• Not reached (applied only to individual items of the student test): This code indicated those items
where it was believed that the students did not reach because of a lack of time. This items were
to be used different in scaling procedures than omitted codes then. “Not reached” codes were
derived as follows: First, the last answer given by a student in a session was identified. Following,
the first omitted response after this last answer is coded as ‘omitted,’ but all following responses
to items in the session are then coded as ‘not reached.’ For example, the response pattern ‘1 9
4 2 9 9 9 9 9 9’ (where ‘9’ represents ‘omitted’) is recoded to ‘1 9 4 2 9 7 7 7 7 7’ (where ‘7’
represents ‘not reached’). When recoding ‘missing’ items to ‘not reached,’ all items, which were
coded to ‘not administered’ have been ignored. For example, the pattern ‘3 1 5 2 9 9 9 8 9 9’ is
recoded to ‘3 1 5 2 9 7 7 8 7 7.’
Checking the interim data products
Building the international database was an iterative process. Once the ISC completed each major data

166
ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
processing step, it sent a new version of the data files to the national study centers so that they could
review their data and run their own separate checks to validate the new data file versions. This process
implied that national study centers received several versions of their data, and their data only, before
release of the draft and final versions of the international databases.
The ISC sent the first version of data and accompanying documentation to national study centers on
3 November, 2023. At this time, data for all countries that administered ICILS in the first half of 2023
were sent out. The data for countries who administered ICILS in the second half of 2023 received
their data on a rolling basis, i.e., when the data was fully processed and cleaned, starting 20 December,
2023. This first version of each country dataset included the following data and documentation:
• Student‐, teacher, and school‐level SPSS and SAS data files;
• Univariate descriptive statistics for all variables in the MS Excel files;
• A cleaning report that included a list of structural and case‐level findings;
• A recoding documentation for country‐specific data edits applied by the ISC;
• Cleaning documentation describing the initial cleaning procedures undertaken at the ISC and
describing the data files and statistics provided; and
• A codebook containing variable specifications for all provided variables.
During this stage of the data processing procedure, national study centers were asked to review the
documentation on national adaptations to their instruments and the related edits applied to the data
files. Thus, the response data were finalized prior to further analyses, namely calculating sampling
weights and scaling.
The ISC provided the IEA analysis team with subsequent versions of the data and related documen‐
tation as soon as it had implemented feedback from national study centers. These additional versions
of the data files were accompanied with the sampling weights and international achievement scores
as soon as these became available. During this stage of the data processing process, the ISC asked
national study centers to review the documentation on adaptations to the national versions of their
instruments and the related edits applied to the data files.
In April 2024, all national study centers received the data from all other ICILS 2023 countries. This
data version is called the draft international database since it roughly reflects the structure of the
released databases already but certain changes are still possible and expected. Most prominently and
compared to earlier data send‐outs this version included sampling weights and scales. All persons
within the national study centers needed to sign a confidentiality agreement assuring no sharing of
any ICILS 2023 data products or information with respect to the results.
During the fifth NRC meeting in Taipei, Taiwan in June 2024, NRCs had the opportunity to raise any
further issues concerning their data that had not yet been raised. In July 2024, the ISC provided
NRCs with an updated version of the draft international database. This version was necessary due to
minor edits resulting from internal quality control procedures. The ISC used this version of the data to
produce the updated, final tables for the international report.
10.4 The ICILS 2023 international database
The ICILS 2023 international database incorporated all national data files from participating countries.
The data processing and validation at the international level helped to ensure that:
• Information coded in each variable was internationally comparable;
• National adaptations were reflected appropriately in all variables;
• Questions that were not internationally comparable were removed from the database;

DATA MANAGEMENT AND CREATION OF THE ICILS DATABASE
167
• All entries in the database could be linked to the appropriate respondent—student, teacher, prin‐
cipal, or ICT coordinator;
• Only those records considered as participating (following adjudication) remained in the interna‐
tional database files;
• All variables derived by the ISC from result data were correctly added;
• Sampling weights and student achievement scores were available for international comparisons;
and
• Indirect identification of individuals was prevented by applying confidentiality measures, such as
scrambling ID variables or removing some of the personal data variables that were needed only
during field operations and data processing.
More information about the ICILS 2023 international database is provided in the ICILS 2023 user guide
for the international database (Meyer et al., 2025).
10.5 Summary
To achieve a high‐quality database, ICILS 2023 implemented a series of data management procedures
that included checks to ensure the consistency of national database structures, proper documenta‐
tion of all national adaptations, and ensure the comparability of international variables across national
datasets. All national databases were reviewed in cooperation with national study centers and the
larger international team. The review process followed a series of thorough checking procedures,
which led to the creation of the final ICILS 2023 database. The final data products included the fol‐
lowing data and materials:
• The international database, i.e., student, teacher, and school‐level data files for all countries in
SPSS, SAS, and R format;
• A user guide on how to work with the international database using the IEA IDB Analyzer soft‐
ware;
• Item almanacs including statistics and information on all achievement items;
• A codebook describing all variables contained in the international database and their attributes
in Excel format; and
• Information from the national context survey in SPSS format and an accompanying codebook
in Excel.

Meyer, S., Daniel, T., & Tieck, S. (2025). ICILS 2023 user guide for the international database. Interna‐

Chapter 11:
Scaling procedures for ICILS 2023 test items
Yuan‐Ling Liaw, Mojca Rožman, Julian Fraillon, and Eugenio Gonzalez
11.1 Introduction
This chapter describes the procedures used to scale the ICILS 2023 test items that were designed
to measure students’ computer and information literacy (CIL) and computational thinking (CT). The
chapter begins with an overview of the scaling methodology as used in ICILS. The scaling models used
for ICILS 2023 data are described, focusing on the Rasch model and its assumptions. Further it details
the use of population modeling, multiple imputation or “plausible values” methodology, which enables
proficiency generation optimal for group reporting of results. The overview concludes with a short
description on equating that is used to report ICILS 2023 achievement scores on the existing ICILS
achievement scales.
The chapter then explains the implementation of the scaling procedures in three sections:
• Test and item adjudication includes information about test targeting, dimensionality and test reli‐
ability, local dependence, treatment of item‐level non response, item statistic summaries, scorer
agreement, and ends with reporting the item adjudication outcomes.
• Item calibration includes a description of the procedures for calibrating the test items.
• Population modeling to estimate students’ scores contains details on the principal component anal‐
ysis, calculating the latent regression model, drawing plausible values, and transforming them for
placement on ICILS scales as well as estimating the linking error.
The chapter concludes with a description of the process used to develop the the CIL and CT described
proficiency levels.
In this chapter data from the computer‐based assessment in 34 countries and the one German bench‐
marking participant are used for the purpose of main survey scaling analyses. The ICILS 2023 test
instruments comprised seven CIL and four optional CT modules.
The modules are paired into a test form in a fully balanced rotation design. Each student responds
to a test form containing either two CIL modules or two CIL and two CT modules, if the country
opted for CT. This approach allows ICILS to assess a broad range of computer and information literacy
and computational thinking skills, while minimizing the response burden on individual students (see
Chapter 2 for more details on the test instruments).
Because students are only administered a subset of the test instrument content, direct comparisons
of group estimates using conventional statistics, such as total scores, is not appropriate. To produce
achievement estimates, ICILS employs Item Response Theory (IRT), a psychometric scaling method that
combines student responses, and “plausible value” methodology, which involves multiple imputations.
ICILS assigns proficiency scores to each student based on both item parameters from the IRT model and
parameters from latent regression models based on questionnaire data. This approach allows for the
description of student performance across various groups or subgroups and estimating relationships
between achievement and context variables.

170
ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
11.2 Overview of scaling methodology
Scaling model and assumptions
One parameter (Rasch) item response theory model
The scaling of ICILS achievement data relies on item response theory (IRT) methods. IRT is particularly
useful for developing comparable achievement estimates across students, even if they respond to
different sets of items, as was the case in ICILS 2023. Each student completed two of seven available
CIL modules and, where applicable, two of four available CT modules. In each case, the modules
were allocated to students using fully balanced assessment designs that enable unbiased comparisons
of achievement estimates based on different forms across groups (see Chapter 2 for details of the
assessment design).
As in ICILS 2013 and ICILS 2018, both the Rasch model (Rasch, 1960) and, its extension to accommo‐
date partial‐credit items, the Rasch partial credit model (PCM) (Masters & Wright, 1997) were used.
This latent variable model captures the relationship between a student’s proficiency and their likelihood
of correctly answering each item (or achieving each score on a partial‐credit item).
For dichotomous items, the Rasch model estimates the probability of a student correctly answering
item i and receiving a score of 1 rather than 0 as follows:
Pi(θn) =
exp (θn −δi)
1 + exp (θn −δi),
(11.1)
where Pi(θn) represents the probability that person n scores 1 on item i, θn is the estimated latent
trait of person n (i.e., ability), and δi is the estimated location of item i on the latent dimension (i.e.,
difficulty). For each item, the responses are modeled as a function of the latent trait θn.
For items with more than two response categories (e.g., items scored 0, 1, or 2), this model can be
extended to the Rasch partial credit model, which is expressed as:
Pxi(θn) =
exp (Px
k=0(θn −δi + τk))
Pmi
k=0 exp
Pk
k=0(θn −δi + τk)
,
x = 0, 1, . . . , mi,
(11.2)
where Pxi(θn) denotes the probability that person n scores x on item i out of the mi possible scores.
θn represents the person’s latent trait, δi is the item parameter indicating the location of item i on the
latent continuum, and τk is the step parameter for each category, with P τk = 0.
Assumptions in using the Rasch model and its characteristics
As with the application of any mathematical model, the use of IRT makes assumptions about the data
to which the model is applied. The test, and item evaluation processes described later in this chapter
are conducted in order to test the degree to which the assumptions are met by the ICILS data.
Unidimensionality and local independence
The unidimensionality assumption posits all the items in an instrument measure a single and common
latent trait (or measurement construct). In the case of the ICILS tests, there are two constructs that are
measured, CIL and CT. The Rasch IRT manifestation of unidimensionality is that a single latent variable
(θ) accounts for the probability of a test taker’s responses to the CIL or CT items, as the two constructs
were scaled separately.
The local independence assumption states that item response probabilities are not influenced by other
factors, but solely depend on the latent variable and not on responses to other items within the same
module or test. These assumptions were tested in previous ICILS cycles (see (Gebhardt & Schulz,
2015; Ockwell et al., 2020)), and item parameters for the CIL and CT domains were estimated using a

SCALING PROCEDURES FOR ICILS 2023 TEST ITEMS
171
unidimensional IRT model.
Measurement equivalence across groups
Measurement equivalence is necessary to ensure that derived scores are comparable across groups. It
is achieved when the probability of an item response depends only on the person’s measured latent
trait, and not on their group membership. Differential item functioning (DIF) indicates that a specific
item of interest is not equivalent across groups. Measurement equivalence in ICILS 2023 (as in previous
cycles) was tested by gender (for the pooled international data across countries) and by country.
Monotonicity
Monotonicity, a central concept in IRT, is the assumption that when scores on the latent trait (typically
referred to as ability in achievement tests) increase the probability of a correct response to any given
item increases as well. This also reflects the intuitive idea that, when measuring an achievement con‐
struct (CIL and CT in ICILS) test‐takers with higher ability are more likely to succeed when responding
to the test items. When testing the assumptions of monotonicity at the item‐level, we used the IRT‐
based infit measure of fit (Bond & Fox, 2015; Linacre, 2017), the classical test theory discrimination
measure (point biserial correlation was used in ICILS), and the item characteristic curve (ICC). An ICC
plots the probability of a correct response to an item as a function of the latent trait. For binary IRT
model, the ICC is assumed to have an S shape. An example of the ICC is presented in Figure 11.1.
Figure 11.1: Item characteristic curve

0.2
0.4

0.8
1.0

-2.0
