Ability (q)
Probability of Correct Response
Equal discrimination
In the Rasch IRT model, all items are assumed to discriminate equally among individuals of different
abilities implying that every item is equally effective at distinguishing between test‐takers with higher
or lower abilities. This contrasts with other IRT models (like the 2‐ and 3‐parameter models) that allow
discrimination to vary across items. When evaluating the fit of the items to the Rasch model, we
compare the model ICC for the item (noting that the shape of the model curve is the same for all items,
and the horizontal position of the curve varies according to the difficulty of the item) with a plot of the
probabilities based on the data for that item.

172
ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
Population modeling
Balanced block instrument designs, in which individual students are systematically allocated modules
and respond to a subset of items from the main item pool, is widely used in assessments like ICILS.
However, reducing the test length and administering subsets of items to individual students introduces
a considerable degree of uncertainty in the measurement of the latent trait at the individual level.
Aggregating student ability in this way can lead to biased population estimates. ICILS uses a population
model to estimate distributions of ability based on item parameters and latent regression of ability
on contextual data. This provides more accurate population and subgroup estimates (Mislevy, 1991;
Mislevy & Sheehan, 1987; von Davier et al., 2009).
In the latent regression model, also known as the population or conditioning model, the posterior
distribution of the proficiency variable (θ) is modeled as dependent on both cognitive item responses
(X) and several predictors (Y ) derived from the context questionnaires. Generating plausible values re‐
quires both the item parameters from the IRT scaling stage and the estimates from the latent regression
analysis.
The regression uses the context questionnaire variables to predict the proficiency variable θ (see Sub‐
section 11.5 for a description of how the the variables are transformed and selected for the regression
model). It is assumed that:
θ ∼N(yΓ, Σ).
(11.3)
The latent regression parameters Γ and Σ are estimated conditional on the previously determined item
parameter estimates. Γ is the matrix of regression coefficients, and Σ is a common residual variance–
covariance matrix.
The latent regression model of Θ on Y with Γ = (γsj, s = 1, . . . , S; l = 0, . . . , L), Y = (1, y1, . . . , yL)t,
and Θ = (θ1, . . . , θS)t can be written as follows:
θj = γs0 + γs1y1 + · · · + γsLyL + εs,
(11.4)
where ϵs is an error term.
The residual variance–covariance matrix is given by the following equation:
Σ = ΘΘt −Γ
 Y Y t
Γt.
(11.5)
The conditional distribution from which plausible values for each respondent j are drawn can be written
as follows:
P (θj|xj, yj, Γ, Σ) .
(11.6)
Using standard rules of probability, this posterior probability of proficiency can be represented as fol‐
lows:
P(θj | xj, yj, Γ, Σ) ∝P(xj | θj, yj, Γ, Σ) P(θj | yj, Γ, Σ) = P(xj | θj) P(θj | yj, Γ, Σ)
(11.7)
where θj is a vector of scale values (these values correspond to the performance on CIL and CT),
P(xj|θj) is the product over the scales of the independent likelihoods induced by responses to items

SCALING PROCEDURES FOR ICILS 2023 TEST ITEMS
173
within each scale, and P(θj|yj, Γ, Σ) is the multivariate joint density of proficiencies of the scales, con‐
ditional on the observed value yj of context questionnaire responses and latent regression parameters
Γ and Σ. As described above, the item parameters obtained from the IRT scaling are treated as fixed
values in the estimation.
An expectation–maximization (EM) algorithm is used for estimating Γ and Σ; the basic method for the
single scale case is described in Mislevy (1985) and Mislevy (1991). The EM algorithm requires the
computation of the mean and variance of the posterior distribution in the equation (11.7).
Reporting on the existing ICILS achievement scales
The metrics for the CIL and CT scales were originally established in ICILS 2013 (Gebhardt & Schulz,
2015) and ICILS 2018 (Ockwell et al., 2020), respectively. These metrics were set by normalizing the
international average scores for all participating countries meeting the sampling requirements (exclud‐
ing benchmarking participants) to a mean of 500 and a standard deviation of 100. So that scores
can be compared across cycles, a large subset of items is reused in the next cycle and achievement
data from subsequent ICILS assessment is transformed and aligned with these original metrics. This
alignment is achieved using a concurrent calibration approach, which combines data from the previous
cycle alongside current assessment data. Common items and common countries contribute to linking
or equating the achievement scores to the established metric.
11.3 Implementation of scaling procedures: test and item adjudication
To evaluate the psychometric quality of ICILS 2023 achievement tests and items, we calculated a range
of statistics for the tests and for each item in each country. These statistics were carefully reviewed as
part of the ICILS quality assurance process to identify any problems with the measurement properties
of the tests and items within tests. All test and item analysis as well as item calibration and plausible
value imputation relied on the open‐source package TAM (Robitzsch et al., 2024), available in the R
statistical programming language (R Core Team, 2023).
Test targeting
As a prelude to the main IRT scaling, the preliminary Rasch analysis was conducted and the distribu‐
tions of CIL and CT were estimated, respectively, along with the location of the corresponding item
thresholds (with a response probability, rp = 0.520; see Figure 11.2 and Figure 11.3). In each figure, the
left panel displays a histogram of student ability estimates, arranged from the most able students at the
top to the least able at the bottom. The middle section shows the distribution of items on the same
latent scale, with the most difficult items positioned at the top and the least difficult at the bottom.
The right panel presents country‐level ability estimates, where each country is denoted by the letter
C. For dichotomous items, the item thresholds were equal to the item difficulties. In the case of partial
credit items, a difficulty threshold was estimated for each score level.21
In both the CIL and CT assessments, the range of item difficulties generally aligned with the abilities of
students in the population. However, the match between test item difficulty and student ability varied
significantly across countries, reflecting differences in student achievement distributions within each
ICILS 2023 country.
20 This means that a respondent with the same score on the latent continuum as the item location parameter has a 50
percent probability of giving a correct response.
21 This “Thurstonian” threshold for a score category indicates the point on the continuum where a respondent has a 50
percent probability of obtaining this item score or higher.

Dimensionality and test reliability
CIL
In ICILS 2023, three new CIL test modules were added to the CIL test instrument. These new modules
followed the general structure and integrated the assessment content in ways that were very similar to
the CIL test modules from previous cycles. The overall test reliability for CIL following the removal of
items with non‐satisfactory psychometric properties, based on the pooled dataset and obtained from
the scaling model, was 0.86. Given the stability of the test content over time, the similarity of the new
test content to existing test content, and the high test reliability, we did not complete further tests of
dimensionality for the CIL assessment.

176
ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
CT
Two new modules were added to the CT assessment. In ICILS 2018, one CT module focused on
conceptualization of problems and the second module focused on operationalizing solutions. These
two modules were used again in ICILS 2023. The two new CT modules developed for ICILS 2023
integrated tasks that address both conceptualization and operationalization, and included a stronger
emphasis on evaluating solutions as part of the iterative problem‐solving process.
The differences between the integration of conceptualization and operationalization tasks in the two
new CT modules and their greater separation in the two trend CT modules prompted us to carefully
evaluate potential dimensionality in the CT data. We fitted a range of different two‐dimensional models
to the data.
Firstly, we checked each module (dimension one) against the rest (dimension two). Secondly, we split
the items based on their categorization, either they contain visual coding (dimension one) or not (di‐
mension two). Finally, we also tested the unidimensional model.
The results suggested that students’ CT was well described by using a single dimension given that
latent correlations between potential sub‐dimensions were very high. The reliabilities based on two
dimensional models were mostly lower (between 0.51 and 0.65, with the exception of one module
where the reliability was 0.79) than for the one‐dimensional model (0.76). With the addition of new
modules, the assumption of unidimensionality for CT was revisited and re‐examined, with results con‐
firming that the unidimensionality assumption remains valid. The overall test reliability for CT following
the removal of items with non‐satisfactory psychometric properties, based on the pooled dataset and
obtained from the scaling model, was 0.83.
Local dependence
The tasks within each CIL and CT test module are thematically related. In addition, in the ICILS CIL
modules, the large tasks are evaluated using sets of analytic scoring criteria. These criteria focus on
either the substantive content, or the use of software features associated with the presentation of the
information products students produce. Across all cycles of ICILS we have therefore been conscious
of the potential for local dependence to affect the data across tasks within each module, and among
assessment criteria within each large task. We have consequently checked for any evidence for local
dependence within modules and among the large task criteria (Gebhardt & Schulz, 2015; Ockwell et al.,
2020).
In ICILS 2023, an analysis of local dependence was conducted by inspecting residual correlations be‐
tween pairs of items within trend and new modules.
• If justified by the item content, a new scoring rule was developed and used to combine the
responses from both items into a single item.
• Alternatively, if necessary, one of the two items was removed from the scale to maintain mea‐
surement integrity.
Treatment of item‐level non response
In ICILS, students responded to tasks, and the scored data from these tasks (sometimes combined or
recoded before scoring) are stored as items for inclusion in the scaling. In this chapter, the term item
refers to the item‐level data included in the scaling process. Non‐response occurs when a student does
not provide a response to a task. This can happen for several reasons: the task was not administered by
design, the task was administered but the student did not provide an answer, or the task was intended
to be administered but was not reached by the student. Tasks contributing to a scored item generally
share the same non‐response code.
Item response categories included several types of non‐response and item score categories. There are
three possible types of missing responses:

SCALING PROCEDURES FOR ICILS 2023 TEST ITEMS
177
• “not‐administered”: this is used when the student was not administered the task by design, given
the matrix‐sampling design used by ICILS, where each student is administered only a sample of
the assessment tasks (two CIL modules and two CT modules). Additionally, tasks may be recoded
as “not‐administered” at the international and national levels due to technical failures, incorrect
translations, or scaling issues, as previously described.
• “omitted”: this code is used when a student did not respond to an item, based on one or more
associated tasks, but did respond to one or more subsequent items within the same module.
• “not‐reached”: this code is used when a student did not respond to a item and the item imme‐
diately preceding it, and no subsequent items in the module were completed. In such cases, the
first unanswered item after the last completed response is coded as “omitted,” and all remaining
unanswered items in the module are coded as “not‐reached.”
Details of the codes used in the ICILS international database (IDB) to represent item‐level non re‐
sponses are provided in the ICILS 2023 user guide.
In ICILS 2023, items corresponding to tasks that were not reached by students were treated differently
when estimating item parameters than when generating student proficiency scores. For item parameter
estimation, not‐reached responses were ignored and treated as not administered. This approach avoids
confounding item difficulty with speededness. In contrast, not‐reached items were treated as incorrect
when generating student proficiency scores. Omitted items were consistently treated as incorrect
during both the calibration and scaling stages.
Item‐ and task‐level non response
CIL
Table 11.1 presents the international percentages of omitted and not‐reached CIL items. These are
grouped by test module within the table. The modules can be differentiated by the first letter of the
item code and the numeral within each item code represents the ordinal position of the related task
within the module. Each CIL module contains a large task that is assessed using more than one crite‐
ria.22 Non‐response codes can either be allocated to all items corresponding to a large task (indicating
that the student did not respond to the task) or to none of the items corresponding to the large task
(where the student did respond to the task). As a consequence, in Table 11.1 for each large task, all
corresponding items show equal percentages of omitted responses. Similarly the items corresponding
to each large task show equal percentages of not‐reached.
Table 11.2 presents the average percentages of non‐response values by item type and for the items
within each module for CIL. The omitted and not reached percentages were highest for the large tasks.
This is to be expected both because the large tasks are open response format (which typically have
higher non‐response rates in tests when they are mixed with closed‐format items) and also because
the large tasks always appear at the end the modules (non‐response rates typically tend to increase
with ordinal item position in any given test). On average, about eight percent of students omitted an
item, while roughly one percent did not reach an item.
The large tasks have between seven and nine corresponding items which may result in inaccurate
representations of the extent of task‐level non‐response within modules. Therefore, Table 11.3 has
been included to show the percentages of omitted and not reached task per module.
When comparing omitted and not‐reached items and tasks across CIL modules, the “Paper books vs
e‐books” module had the highest percentage of omitted responses, while the “Internet safety” module
had the lowest. The “Recycling” module, which also had the largest number of tasks, showed slightly
higher percentages of not‐reached responses compared to the other modules.
22 In Table 11.1 the items associated with the large task in each module have the same numeral in their item codes
because each item refers to the same task.

ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
CT
Table 11.4 presents the average percentages of missing values by item type and for the items within
each CT module. Overall, these percentages were slightly higher than for CIL. Omissions were below
10 percent for approximately two‐thirds of the items. In three of the remaining items omissions were
above 20 percent. Two of these items were constructed response and the third was an abstraction
item (including a set of data recording and comparing actions).
Table 11.5 presents the average percentages of omitted and not reached responses by CT module.
Omitted response averages ranged from approximately eight percent to 12 percent. Not reached
responses varied from approximately two percent to five percent. As each CT task corresponded to a
single item, we have not separately reported omitted and not reached percentages for tasks by module
(these data are the same as reported in the bottom section of Table 11.5).

Item statistic summaries
To evaluate the psychometric quality of ICILS 2023 achievement items, we calculated a range of coun‐
try level statistics for each item. These statistics informed the degree to which the data for each
item were consistent with the assumptions of the Rasch IRT model described at the beginning of this
chapter.
Item statistics were computed for both pooled international data and separately for each of the partic‐
ipating countries. Examples of summary visual displays provided to countries and used by the ISC to
support item adjudication are shown for item R02Z in Figure 11.4 and Figure 11.5. The items statistics
were based on ICILS 2023 data and include preliminary Rasch analysis statistics, such as Rasch item
difficulty, the standard error of this estimate, the Rasch goodness‐of‐fit index (i.e., infit), gender DIF,
and item‐by‐country interaction.
For human‐scored items, the display presents scorer agreement, along with the observed item char‐
acteristic curve by category and gender DIF. The second part provides statistics calculated separately
for each country.
To assist the reviewing process, the item analysis summary includes a series of ‘flags’ to highlight con‐
ditions that may suggest an issue with an item. While these flags do not always indicate a problem,
they help identify potential areas of concern, where a consultation with NRCs might be needed. The
following conditions were flagged:
• For human‐scored items, within‐country scoring reliability data showed an agreement of less
than 70 percent.
• National item difficulties deviated significantly (by more than 1.3 logits) from the international
item difficulty.
• Item discrimination (the point‐biserial for the correct answer) was less than 0.2.
• The point‐biserial correlation for one or more distractors was greater than zero.
• The point‐biserial correlation for the correct response was less than zero.
• Performance or ability estimate was not ordered by score for items with multiple score levels.

SCALING PROCEDURES FOR ICILS 2023 TEST ITEMS
185
• The Rasch goodness‐of‐fit index was less than 0.88 or greater than 1.12.
As illustrated in Figure 11.4 and Figure 11.5, for each item, the first section presents statistics for the
pooled international data, which include the number of student responses, the percentage of students
selecting each option or score level, including the percentages of students who omitted or did not
reach the item, the percentage of students answering correctly, and the discrimination index (the point‐
biserial correlation between success on the item and the total score).
Figure 11.4: Item statistics for item R02Z

ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
Figure 11.5: Item statistics for item R02Z (cont’d)
Item fit
The Rasch model assumes that all items are equally discriminating by assuming a slope parameter
of 1 so that relatively higher or less discriminating items than the average discrimination across the
test show a lack of item fit. Item discrimination refers to the way in which student ability relates to
their likelihood of giving correct responses to any given test item. Overall, for any given test item,
students of higher ability should be more likely to respond correctly than students of lower ability. In
highly discriminating items, the shift between low probability of correct response and high probability

SCALING PROCEDURES FOR ICILS 2023 TEST ITEMS
187
of correct response, takes place over a small range of student abilities. In poorly discriminating items,
this shift of probability takes place over a broad range of student abilities. Very low item discrimination
occurs when student ability is not or weakly associated with the probability of a correct response on
an item. Clearly, low discrimination is not a desirable property for a test item.
In order to judge the measurement properties of test items, it is important to examine the level of dis‐
crimination of the items, to flag those for which discrimination is low (because they do not effectively
measure the latent trait of CIL or CT), and to consider how well the overall assumption of uniform dis‐
crimination is being met by the remaining set of items. Three indicators were used to identify items that
violate the equal discrimination assumption: the point‐biserial correlation, the weighted mean square
statistic (infit), and ICC. The point‐biserial correlation is used to measure the strength and direction
of the association that exists between a continuous and a dichotomous variable. It represents the
correlation of each response category with the test score. We calculated the point‐biserial correlation
coefficient also for distractors in multiple choice items. The weighted mean square statistic should be
close to 1 when an item fits the Rasch model; items with fit statistics lower than 1 discriminate more
than expected, whereas those with a fit index greater than 1 discriminate less than expected. While
the point‐biserial correlation coefficient is affected by ceiling and floor effects (the discrimination can
be low if almost all students respond correctly or incorrectly), the weighted mean square statistic is
independent of the item difficulty.
In addition, we checked the observed ICC by response categories of the items. The ICC shows the
probability of students of different abilities selecting each of the different response options for an
item. Ideally, the probability of a correct response should increase, and the probability of an incorrect
response should decrease as the ability of student increases (to the right on the horizontal axis). In
multiple choice items students with lower ability should be more likely to select options other than
the key, but as ability increases the probability of students selecting options other than the key should
decrease, while the probability of selecting the key should increase.
Measurement equivalence – Gender DIF
DIF occurs when students with the same ability (represented by their scaled achievement scores) have
different probabilities of answering an item correctly. For instance, if boys with the same ability as
girls have a higher probability of correctly answering a given item, that item shows DIF with regard
to gender. This suggests a violation of the model’s assumptions, that the probability is exclusively a
function of ability and not of any other characteristics of the respondents.
To estimate gender DIF, interaction terms were included in the item response model. For dichotomous
items, gender DIF was modeled as:
Pi(θn) =
exp(θn −(δi −ηn + λig))
1 + exp(θn −(δi −ηn + λig)).
(11.8)
In this equation, θn represents the estimated ability of person n, and δi is the estimated difficulty of
item i. The term λig accounts for the gender effect on the item, and ηg represents the overall gender
effect. To obtain accurate estimates, both the item‐by‐gender interaction (λig) and the overall gender
effect (ηg) were constrained so that their sum equals zero.
For items with more than two categories, gender DIF was modeled using a partial credit model:
Pxi(θn) =
exp
Pxi
j=0(θn −(δi −ηn + λig + τj))

Pmi
h=0 exp
Ph
j=0(θn −(δi −ηn + λig + τj))
,
xi = 0, 1, . . . , mi,
(11.9)
where θn represents the person’s ability, δi is the item difficulty on the latent continuum, τj is the step
parameter, λig is the item‐by‐gender interaction effect, and ηg is the overall gender effect.

188
ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
We used a DIF value of 0.3 (approximately about one third of a standard deviation) as a criterion for
identification of gender DIF. No items showed significant DIF that would warrant their removal from
the scaling process in either scale.
Measurement equivalence – Item‐by‐country interaction
As ICILS is a cross‐national study it is important that the test items function similarly across participating
countries. When considering the difficulty of test items, the overarching assumption is that the relative
difficulty of the test items to each other will be consistent across countries. Item‐by‐country interaction
is evident when, within a given country, a given item is relatively either considerably easier or more
difficult in comparison to the other test items than it is, on average, across the remaining countries.
Item‐by‐country interaction is consequently an indicator that the probability of students completing
a given item correctly in a given country has a different relationship to student ability in that country
than it does across the remaining countries. There can be many causes of item‐by‐country interaction.
In some cases it can be explained by a simple error (such as an error in translation that causes an item
to become easier or more difficult in a given country), a problem with scoring (if, for example the item
is scored more harshly or leniently in a given country than in other countries), a result of differences in
student learning across countries (if, for example, the specific content of an item is a specific strength or
weakness of students within a given country) or other unknown factors. One of the aims of the rigorous
item development and review process (see Chapter 2) is to minimize the potential for item‐by‐country
interaction to affect items within countries, however, some degree of item‐by‐country interaction is
inevitable in international large scale assessment (ILSA) and this likelihood increases with the number
of countries participating in a study.
Items with a large item‐by‐country interaction are not suitable to be used in international studies.
The magnitude of item‐by‐country interaction was estimated by scaling the items separately for each
country and then comparing the standardized item difficulty parameter for each item to the corre‐
sponding standardized item difficulty parameter calibrated from the pooled data (including all coun‐
tries meeting the sampling participation requirements). In these displays, the difference between the
item difficulty for each country and the international average Rasch item difficulty is represented along
with a 95 percent confidence interval. This confidence interval includes a Bonferroni correction23 to
account for multiple comparisons across participating countries. The confidence interval limits were
computed as follows:
Upper Limit = RDIFFik −RDIFF¯k + SE(RDIFFik) · Zb
(11.10)
Lower Limit = RDIFFik −RDIFF¯k −SE(RDIFFik) · Zb,
(11.11)
where RDIFFik is the Rasch difficulty of item i in country k, RDIFF¯k is the international average
Rasch difficulty of item i, SE(RDIFFik) is the standard error of the Rasch difficulty of item i in country
k, and Zb is the 95 percent critical value from the Z distribution, adjusted for multiple comparisons using
the Bonferroni correction.
In addition to graphical displays of item‐by‐country interaction, as in ICILS 2013 and 2018, country DIF
was identified when national item difficulties deviated from the international item difficulty by more
than 1.3 logits. These items were excluded from calibrations and scaling at the national level. A list of
items that were excluded is available in Appendix F.
23 When multiple statistical tests are performed such as comparing the item difficulty for each country with the interna‐
tional average Rasch item difficulty, the probability of finding a false positive (rejecting a true null hypothesis) increases.
The Bonferroni correction helps counteract this by adjusting the significance level (α) downward.

SCALING PROCEDURES FOR ICILS 2023 TEST ITEMS
189
An example of the graphical representation of item‐by‐country interaction (Rasch difficulty) for item
R02Z is shown in Figure 11.5. This figure illustrates how an item’s difficulty varies across countries.
The y‐axis represents the difference between a country’s item difficulty and the international average,
while a horizontal line at zero serves as a reference, indicating no deviation from the international
difficulty level. The bars represent the standardized national item difficulties, each accompanied by a
confidence interval that accounts for statistical uncertainty and is adjusted using a Bonferroni correc‐
tion for multiple comparisons. If a country’s confidence interval does not include zero, it indicates that
the item is significantly easier or harder in that country than expected.
Scorer agreement
The ICILS constructed‐response items and large tasks were human‐scored according to the speci‐
fications of the ICILS 2023 scoring guides (see Chapter 8). New scoring guides were developed for
items introduced in ICILS 2023, which were refined after the international field trial. Scoring guides for
items already used in ICILS 2018 were kept consistent to ensure that scores retained the same meaning
across cycles. In each country, approximately 200 student responses to each task were scored twice.
This was done by randomly assigning responses to different scorers through a controlled online scoring
system (see Chapter 3). This double‐scoring process allowed for calculation of scorer agreement.
In Table 11.6 and Table 11.7 we present the percentages of scorer agreement for CIL and CT items,
based on data from 33 countries24 and one benchmarking entity for CIL, and 23 countries and one
benchmarking entity for CT items. The tables show the number of student responses that were double‐
scored, the international average scorer agreement, and the minimum and maximum agreement values
across countries. As has been practice in other IEA studies and previous ICILS cycles, only items that
reached a certain threshold of agreement were considered to be used in scaling. The average scorer
agreement across countries ranged from 79 for item P06E to 97 percent for items R11A, R11C, and
S08B among the 77 constructed response CIL items and 85 for item T3A08Z to 90 percent for item
TS07Z among the three CT items. The within country percentage of agreement between scorers on
double‐scored items across countries ranged from 35 to 100 percent for CIL and 71 to 100 percent
for CT items.
The scorer agreement was monitored through the AM Marker system (see Chapter 3), which was
accessible to the lead scorer. In some cases, the scorer agreement displayed in the AM Marker interface
was inconsistent with the values calculated by the ISC after scoring was completed. For selected
items where the ISC‐calculated scorer agreement in a given country was below 70 percent, the scorer
agreement shown in the AM Marker system was used instead. All scorer agreement statistics were
reviewed and confirmed at the national level. After careful review and discussion, items with scorer
agreement below 60 percent were excluded from the scaling process when drawing plausible values
for the corresponding national data, although these items remain included in the public database. The
list of affected items is available in Appendix F.
24 Chile conducted an additional data collection and thus was excluded from the presentation of scorer agreement.

Test and item evaluation outcomes
Item statistics were reviewed based on the described criteria.
In total, four CIL items (H04A, S05Z, S07Z, C01Z) showed unsatisfactory item statistics, leading to
their exclusion from further analysis. Items H04A, S05Z, and S07Z had already been excluded from
scaling in 2018 at different stages for calibration and scoring but were readministered in 2023. Item
C01Z was newly developed for ICILS 2023. Additionally, due to local dependence, two CIL items,
I07A and I07B, were combined into I07AB.
No CT items were removed from scaling in the international pooled data. Items TS06E and TS06F
were combined into TS06Z.
Appendix G provides details about items that were excluded from scaling or removed from the database.
11.4 Implementation of scaling procedures: item calibration
After the test and item adjudication are completed, the assessments are linked for trend scaling. This
involves estimating the item parameters for the CIL and CT items in the current assessment by jointly
calibrating data from both the current and previous assessments. In 2023, the ICILS concurrent cal‐
ibration combined achievement data from the 2018 and 2023 assessments. Similar procedures are
used in TIMSS, PIRLS, and ICCS (Foy & Yin, 2016, 2017; Ockwell et al., 2020).
The ICILS 2023 data included 30 countries for the CIL assessment, and 21 for the CT assessment,
excluding one benchmarking participant and countries that did not meet sampling requirements. The
ICILS 2018 calibration sample included 11 countries for CIL (excluding two benchmarking entities and
the United States, which did not meet sampling requirements), with seven of these countries also
participating in CT. Consequently, concurrent calibration for CIL included student responses from 30
countries in 2023 and 11 from 2018, while the CT calibration incorporated data from 21 countries in
2023 and seven in 2018. Countries were equally weighted within each ICILS cycle for the CIL and CT
calibration and all items were included (except for items that were removed nationally or internationally
following the adjudication process).
Calibrating the test items
More than half of the CIL items and over one‐third of the CT items from the 2018 assessment were
reused in the 2023 cycle. To enhance the information available for proficiency estimation and to reduce
measurement error, all items from both cycles were included in the calibration process.
Before conducting the concurrent calibration, a separate analysis was carried out to evaluate the quality
of the link between the two cycles. Separate calibrations were conducted using all items administered
in each respective cycle. Item statistics for the common items were then extracted, reviewed, and com‐
pared between 2018 and 2023, both at the international level and separately for each trend country.
A total of 64 CIL items and nine CT items were common between the two cycles. This comparison
included 10 countries for CIL, Germany, Denmark, Finland, France, Italy, Kazakhstan, Korea (Rep. of),
Luxembourg, Portugal, and Uruguay, and seven countries for CT, with Italy, Kazakhstan, and Uruguay
opting not to participate in CT.

SCALING PROCEDURES FOR ICILS 2023 TEST ITEMS
193
The left panel of Figure 11.6 presents the item difficulties for CIL common items across the 2018 and
2023 assessment cycles, while the right panel displays the corresponding item difficulties for CT com‐
mon items. By comparing item difficulties across cycles, this figure provides insights into the stability
of item parameters over time and the potential impact of contextual or cohort differences on item
functioning. After careful review of the analysis results, eight CIL items (S04B, G03Z, G06Z, G07Z,
G09G, R07Z, R10Z, R12J) were retained in the joint calibration dataset but treated as separate items
across cycles. Consequently, in the CIL domain, a total of 63 common items were included in the
calibration process, of which 55 were used as link items to ensure comparability across cycles, while
the remaining eight items were treated differently due to variations in their administration or response
patterns.
In CT, all of 11 common items were identified and used as link items to establish score continuity across
cycles.
With the linking items defined, item parameters were estimated using multiple‐group concurrent cal‐
ibration based on the Rasch model, with country‐by‐cycle as the grouping variable. The final sets of
CIL and CT item parameters are presented in Table 11.8 and Table 11.9.

11.5 Implementation of scaling procedures: population modeling to estimate
students’ scores
Principal component analysis
To select and prepare the variables to be used in the latent regression model needed to generate
plausible values (PVs), some pre‐processing is needed. The pre‐processing varied for different variable
types:
• Categorical variables with fewer than eight response categories were dummy‐coded, including
categories representing missing data. For a variable with N categories, N‐1 binary variables were
created, with one category omitted as the reference to avoid multicollinearity. For example, a
variable indicating school type (public versus private) was transformed into a binary indicator,
with 1 representing public schools and 0 otherwise. If data were missing, an additional binary
variable flagged these cases (1 for missing, 0 otherwise).
• Continuous variables were retained in their original form, with missing values imputed using an
appropriate estimate (e.g., student age), and a dummy code was added to indicate those cases
with missing values in the original variable.
Given the large number of variables, principal component analysis was employed to reduce the number
of variables needed by identifying a smaller number of orthogonal components that account for most of
the variation in the context variables. Principal components that explained 90 percent of the variance
in all context variables were retained as conditioning variables. To prevent overfitting, the number of
retained components was also limited to no more than five percent of a country’s student sample size.
Therefore, principal components were selected that either retained 90 percent of common variance
or five percent of the sample size, whichever led to fewer principal components. Because principal
component analysis was conducted separately for each country, the number of components retained
varied accordingly.
Additionally, conditioning variables included some direct regressors, specifically student gender, explicit
stratum indicators, and the adjusted school mean achievement (the average of weighted likelihood
ability estimates (WLEs) for all other students in the same school).
Note that the principal component analysis was conducted separately for each country based on inter‐
national variables (collected by every participating country) as well as variables with country‐specific
response categories, e.g., country of birth. Table 11.10 provide details of the conditioning variables
used for proficiency estimation. Appendix E provides a comprehensive list of the student variables
used for conditioning, with their respective values.

SCALING PROCEDURES FOR ICILS 2023 TEST ITEMS
199
Latent regression analysis
Latent regression analysis with IRT item parameter estimates (X) treated as fixed values and the prin‐
cipal components of the context questionnaire variables as predictors (Y ) for estimating the latent
regression parameters Γ (regression coefficients) and P (residual variance–covariance matrix). Note
that latent regression models are estimated separately for each country to allow for differences in
associations between the context characteristics and the cognitive skills.
The TAM package used for analysis supports two‐dimensional latent regression models by using item
responses across the CIL and CT scales and the correlation between these scales, thereby enhancing
the reliability of each scale. For countries administering both CIL and CT, a two‐dimensional model
was applied; for those administering only CIL, a one‐dimensional model was used.
The inputs include students’ responses to administered items, item parameters estimated during con‐
current calibration, and selected conditioning variables. The output includes estimated regression ef‐
fects, the residual variance‐covariance matrix, and posterior distribution of each student’s latent ability
(CIL and, where relevant, CT) (see, for example, (Mislevy, 1991; von Davier & Sinharay, 2013; von
Davier et al., 2006)). The posterior distribution is a probability distribution of a students’ true ability
and is based on their responses to the test and questionnaire items.
Drawing plausible values to estimate students’ scores
Plausible values are randomly drawn from the resulting posterior distribution for each of the partici‐
pating students. Additionally, the same procedure were applied to the 2018 data for trend countries
using parameters from concurrent calibration, allowing the generation of plausible values known as
“ICILS 2018 data from the ICILS 2023 calibration.”
When the estimation of the regression parameters (Γ and Σ) is complete, plausible values are randomly
drawn from the joint distribution of the values of Γ for all sampled respondents in a three‐step process:
1. First, a value of Γ is drawn from a normal approximation to P(Γ, Σ | xj, yj) that fixes Σ at the
value ˆΣ.
2. Second, conditional on the generated value of Γ (and the fixed value of Σ = ˆΣ), the mean and
variance of the posterior distribution of θ are computed using the same methods applied in the
EM algorithm.
3. In the third step, the θ is drawn independently from a multivariate normal distribution with mean
and variance.
These three steps are repeated five times, resulting in five independent plausible values (PVs) of θ given
x and y for each sampled student in CIL and CT who participated in both assessments, or five PVs of
CIL for students who only participated in CIL.
Transforming proficiency scores to measure trends
For comparability with previous ICILS assessments, the 2023 CIL and CT scale scores were transformed
to the metric used in 2018 as part of the concurrent calibration approach. Linear transformations
constants needed for placing the 2023 scores to the trend metric were based on the subset of countries
that met sampling participation requirements and participated in both ICILS cycles, 2018 and 2023.
Finally, 10 countries contributed to the CIL transformation constants (Germany, Denmark, Finland,
France, Italy, Kazakhstan, Korea (Rep. of), Luxembourg, Portugal, and Uruguay), and seven to the
CT ones (Germany, Denmark, Finland, France, Korea (Rep. of), Luxembourg, and Portugal). Linear
transformations matched the means and standard deviations of ICILS 2018 data from the ICILS 2023
and 2018 calibrations and ensured alignment for trend reporting. This resulted in directly comparable
CIL and CT achievement scores across the 2018 and 2023 assessments.

200
ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
These linear transformations were given by:
PV ∗
ik = Ak + Bk × PVik,
(11.12)
where
• PVik is the ICILS 2018 plausible value i of scale k prior to transformation;
• PV ∗
ik is the ICILS 2018 plausible value i of scale k after transformation; and
• Ak and Bk are the linear transformation constants.
The linear transformation constants were obtained by first computing the international means and
standard deviations of the plausible values for the CIL and CT scales using the plausible values produced
in 2018 based on the 2018 item calibrations for the trend countries that met sampling requirement,
resulting in 10 CIL countries and seven CT countries. These were the plausible values published in
2018.
Next, the same calculations were done using the plausible values from “ICILS 2018 data from the ICILS
2023 calibration” for the same set of trend countries. From these calculations, the linear transformation
constants were obtained as:
Bk = σk
σ∗
k
(11.13)
Ak = µk −Bk · µ∗
k,
(11.14)
where
• µk is the international mean of scale k averaged across the five plausible values published in
2018;
• µ∗
k is the international mean of scale k averaged across the five plausible values from the 2018
assessment based on the 2023 concurrent calibration;
• σk is the international standard deviation of scale k averaged across the five plausible values i
published in 2018;
• σ∗
k is the international standard deviation of scale k averaged across the five plausible values
from the 2018 assessment based on the 2023 concurrent calibration.
Table 11.11 shows the international mean and standard deviation of plausible value published in 2018
as well as the plausible values from the 2018 assessment based on the 2023 concurrent calibration.
Table 11.12 summarizes the ICILS 2023 transformation constants for both scales.

Estimating linking error
The transformation of proficiency scores allows us to place the scores of ICILS 2023 to the trend
scale established in earlier ICILS cycles. One way to evaluate the transformation is to calculate the
difference between linking item parameters obtained from 2023 with those reported in ICILS 2018.
The sample of items chosen as link items might influence the transformation. This means that the
resulting transformation would have been slightly different if we had chosen an alternative set of link
items. Uncertainty in the transformation thus relates to the sampling of the link items, in the same way
that uncertainty in values such as country averages is an outcome of the particular sample of students
that is used.
The uncertainty resulting from link‐item sampling is referred to as linking error, and it is an error that
analysts have to take into account when comparing the results arising out of different data collections
(see (Monseur & Berezner, 2007)). As is the situation with the error that is introduced through the
process of sampling students, the exact magnitude of this linking error cannot be determined. We
can, however, estimate the likely range of magnitudes for this error and take it into account when
interpreting results. As with sampling errors, the likely range of magnitude for the errors is represented
as a standard error.
To calculate linking error between the 2023 and 2018 CIL and CT, we first estimated the Rasch difficulty
parameters for the 2023 assessments. The common item parameters were then centered by setting
the mean to 0. The Rasch difficulty parameters for the common items in the 2018 administration
were also centered by setting the mean to 0. The difference between the two item parameters was
calculated and averaged across all items, thus representing the difference in relative difficulty of the
common items across cycles. Finally, linking error was computed as the standard error of the mean
difference using the traditional formula for calculating the standard error of a mean. This approach
assumes that the common items are a random sample of all possible common items.
It is evident that linking error in ICILS is a function of item drift (differences in item difficulty from one
administration to the next), the clustering of items within units, the weighting of maximum points for
polytomous items, and the number of common items across cycles.
Suppose we have a total of L score points in the link items in K modules. Use i to index items in a
unit and j to index units so that δy
ij is the estimated difficulty of item i in unit j for year y, and let:

202
ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT

Table 11.13 and Table 11.14 present the differences between countries’ published and re‐estimated
2018 results. These results compare the published 2018 scores with the re‐estimated 2018 scores
based on the concurrent calibration conducted in 2023, alongside the ICILS 2023 results. Overall,
there was good agreement between the published and re‐estimated results. Although small differences
were observed at the country level, most differences in CIL were within one point, and no standard
errors exceeded 0.6 points. In CT, absolute differences reached up to 3 points, with no standard errors
exceeding 1.6 points. These minor differences are expected, given the larger number of participating
countries in 2023, the numbers of principal components used for conditioning, and changes in the
software used.

11.6 Proficiency levels for CIL and CT
One of the objectives of ICILS is to establish clearly defined CIL and CT scales that serve as reference
points for future international assessments in this learning area. Defining proficiency levels provides
a meaningful way to describe student performance across countries and sets benchmarks for future
surveys.
The ICILS reporting scales for CIL and CT were established in 2013 and 2018 respectively. Each of
these scales were established with a mean of 500 and a standard deviation of 100 for equally weighted
national samples for countries that had met the IEA sample participation requirements (Fraillon et al.,
2014, 2020).
The described proficiency levels for CIL were established as part of ICILS 2013. The level boundaries
have remained the same since that time. The level descriptions, and examples of student achievement
at each level are reviewed and, where appropriate, revised with as part of each ICILS cycle to ensure
that the content of the scale reflects the new content included in each new cycle.
In ICILS 2018, we established descriptions of three regions of achievement on the CT scale. Because
these descriptions were based on item content collected from two modules and seven countries only,
the descriptions were reported as preliminary, and with a view to them being revised and consolidated
in ICILS 2023 (Fraillon, 2024). This work was completed as part of ICILS 2023. The CT described
proficiency levels are presented and discussed in detail in the ICILS 2023 international report (Fraillon,
2024), and, similar to the CIL described levels, will be reviewed and revised with each new ICILS cycle.
The same processes was used to establish the CIL and CT described levels of proficiency.
Two parameters affecting decisions about the level widths and descriptions were first established.
1. The level widths would be equal. While it is feasible to establish described levels with unequal
widths on the scale, this would lead to inconsistent interpretations of what it means for a student
to “be at a level.”
2. The response probability (rp) for reporting item parameters was set at rp = 0.62. In practice, this
means that a student with an estimated ability equal to the difficulty of a given item would have
a 62 percent probability of correctly responding to the item. This reflects a higher and more
appropriate level of “mastery” than rp = 0.5.
With these parameters established, the process of establishing the level widths and descriptions began.
This included the following iterative processes:
• Reviewing the content and scaled difficulty of the test items to identify themes, content and
concepts that contribute to their difficulty
• Characterizing common difficulty‐related characteristics of items within difficulty ranges on the

204
ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
scale
• Establishing equally‐spaced boundaries on the scale and review the characteristics of the items
within each “level”
• Continuing to adjust the boundaries until “the content of each level showed distinct character‐
istics and the differences between each level demonstrated a clear progression from lower to
higher achievement” (Fraillon, 2024, p. 120)
While in theory, according to the above decisions and processes the level widths could be very small
or very large, one final requirement was used to inform the level width.
This relates to what can be inferred about a student whose score places them within a given level.
Ideally, when a student’s score places them within a level, one can infer that the student can correctly
respond to a reasonable proportion of test content within that level. Students with scores near the
top of a level could be expected to complete a larger proportion of content than students with scores
near the bottom. However, if a student with a score near the bottom of a level is to be described as
being “within the level” then it is reasonable to assume that this student has mastery of some of the
content within that level.
For the purpose of evaluating the viability of level widths, it is possible to estimate the expected suc‐
cess of students with scores at different points within a level of a given width, on a hypothetical test
containing items with difficulties distributed evenly across the level.
Ultimately, following iterative processes to determine level content and widths, both the CIL and CT
levels were established to have widths of 1.00 logits on the ICILS CIL and CT scales.
Using these parameters, we were able to infer the following about students’ CIL and CT in relation to
their respective proficiency levels:25
• Students whose scores placed them at the lowest possible point of a proficiency level were likely
to correctly answer (on average) approximately 50 percent of the items (on a hypothetical test
made up of items with locations spread uniformly across the level).
• Students whose scores placed them at the top of a proficiency level were likely to correctly
answer (on average) approximately 72 percent of the items (on a hypothetical test made up of
items with locations spread uniformly across the level).
When reporting released CIL items and mapping them against proficiency levels, we had to transform
location parameters of these items to a value that reflected a response probability of 62 percent (rp
= 0.62). This is achieved by adding the natural log of the odds of a 62 percent chance to the original
log odds and transforming the result to the international metric by applying the same transformation
as for the (original) student scores. The standardized item difficulty δ′
i for each item was obtained as
follows:

For CIL, the final level boundaries established were set at 407, 492, 576, and 661 scale score points
on the CIL scale, with a corresponding level width of 85 scale score points for each level with an upper
and lower boundary. The levels are: Below Level 1 (407 scale score points and below), Level 1 (above
407 to 492 scale score points), Level 2 (above 492 to 576 scale score points), Level 3 (above 576 to
661 scale score points), and Level 4 (above 661 scale score points).
For CT, the final level boundaries were set at 330, 440, 550, and 660 scale score points on the CT
scale, with a corresponding level width of 110 scale score points for each level with an upper and lower
boundary. The levels are: Below Level 1 (330 scale score points and below), Level 1 (above 330 to
440 scale score points), Level 2 (above 440 to 550 scale score points), Level 3 (above 550 to 660 scale
score points), and Level 4 (above 660 scale score points).

Chapter 12:
Scaling procedures for ICILS 2023
questionnaire items
Minge Chen, Andrés Christiansen, Mojca Rožman, and Rolf Strietholt
This chapter describes the procedures for the scaling of the ICILS 2023 context questionnaire data
(collected from students, teachers, school principals, and ICT coordinators) and the indices that were
derived from these data. Three types of indices were computed with questionnaire data for ICILS
2023:
• Simple indices were constructed through arithmetical transformation or recoding, for example an
index of immigration background based on information about the country of birth of students
and their parents;
• Students’ socioeconomic background indices were constructed via principal component analysis
(PCA) of three simple indices;
• Scale indices were derived through item response theory (IRT) modeling.
This chapter is divided into four sections. The first section describes the construction of simple in‐
dices; the second section explains the procedure for the calculation of the students’ socioeconomic
background indices; the third section includes the theoretical framework underpinning, and the proce‐
dure for scaling and scoring scale indices; and the last section lists the scaling results of questionnaire
scales.
Questions from the questionnaires are referred to throughout this chapter. For brevity, the following
abbreviations are used to denote the questionnaires: student questionnaire (StQ), teacher question‐
naire (TQ), principal questionnaire (PQ) and ICT coordinator questionnaire (CQ). Questions within each
questionnaire are referred by their number only, for example, Question 5 in the student questionnaire
is referred to as StQ 5, or Question 10 in the teacher questionnaire as TQ 10.
12.1 Simple indices
Student questionnaire
Student age
Student age (S_AGE) was calculated as the difference between the year and month of the testing and
the year and month of a student’s birth. Information from StQ 1 was used to derive age, except for
students where this information was missing. In these cases information from student tracking forms
provided data for the calculation of this index.
The formula for computing S_AGE was:
S_AGE = (Ty −Sy) + (Tm −Sm)
12
,
where Ty and Sy are, respectively, the year of the test and the year of birth of the tested student, in
four‐digit format (e.g., 2023 or 2010), and where Tm and Sm are respectively the month of the test
and the month of the student’s birth. The result was rounded to two decimal places.
Sex of student
The sex of students (S_SEX) was obtained from StQ 2, where the students were asked about their
gender. Girl was coded as 1 while boy was coded as 0. For students with omitted data for this question,

210
ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
the gender from the tracking form was included. Countries could adapt the responses to include one
or more additional categories for students to self‐identify as neither male nor female. Few students
responded using the additional categories in countries where the national option was implemented,
during data processing data in any additional categories were recoded to missing.
Students’ expected education
Students’ expected education (S_ISCED) was obtained from StQ 3, where the students were asked
about their expected highest level of educational attainment. The responses were classified using the
International Standard Classification of Education (ISCED) framework (UNESCO Institute for Statistics,
2012) and had the following categories (in parentheses the code for each category in the dataset is
presented):
(0) No completion of ISCED level 2;
(1) Completion of ISCED level 2 (lower‐secondary);
(2) Completion of ISCED level 3 (upper‐secondary);
(3) ISCED level 4 (non‐tertiary post‐secondary) or ISCED level 5 (vocational tertiary); and
(4) ISCED level 6 (bachelor’s level tertiary) or ISCED level 7 (master’s level tertiary) or ISCED level
8 (doctorate level tertiary).
Immigrant background
Immigrant background (S_IMMIG) was created using the three responses to StQ 4, which collected
information on the country of birth of the students and their parents. National research coordinators
(NRCs) were asked to adapt the terms [Parent or guardian 1] and [Parent or guardian 2] to an appropri‐
ate term for parents in their national context. Instructions were provided to students on who to select
as a parent for each corresponding option (including how to respond if they spend time with more
than one set of parents and if they only spend time with one parent). For each student (S_SBORN),
their first parent (S_P1BORN), and their second parent (S_P2BORN), a code of 1 was given if they
were born in the country of the assessment while a score of 2 was assigned if they were born outside
the country of assessment. The index S_IMMIG had the following three categories (in parentheses the
code for each category in the dataset is presented):
(0) Students and/or at least one parent born in country of test;
(1) Student born in country of test but both/only parent(s) born abroad; and
(2) Student and both/only parent(s) born abroad.
We assigned missing values to students with missing responses for either their own place of birth, or
that of all parents, or for all three questions. The analyses of the relationship of immigrant background
with computer and information literacy (CIL) and computational thinking (CT) were based on a dichoto‐
mous indicator variable that distinguished between students with immigrant background (coded 1) and
students students without immigrant background (coded 0) called S_IMMBGR.
Home language
Home language (S_TLANG) was obtained from StQ 5, where the students were asked if the language
spoken at home most of the time was the language of assessment or another language;26 this index
had the following two categories:
(0) The language spoken at home most of the time differed from the language of assessment; and
26 Most countries collected more detailed information on language use. This information is not included in the interna‐
tional database.

SCALING PROCEDURES FOR ICILS 2023 QUESTIONNAIRE ITEMS
211
(1) The language spoken at home most of the time was the language of assessment.
Parents’ or guardians’ occupational status
Occupational status of the first parent or guardian (S_P1ISEI), occupational status of the second par‐
ent or guardian (S_P2ISEI), and the highest occupational status of both parents or guardians (S_HISEI),
are three indices obtained from StQ 6 to StQ 12, where StQ 6 and 10 ask students whether their
parents are in paid work or not while StQ 7/8 and 11/12 ask students to provide details about their
parents’ jobs, using pairs of questions with open text‐response formats. The paid work status of the
students’ parents was classified into S_P1WORK (parent or guardian 1) and S_P2WORK (parent or
guardian 2) (0 indicating that the parent is not in paid work, and 1 indicating that the parent is in paid
work). The students’ text responses were coded by national centers into four digit codes using the
International Standard Classification of Occupations (ISCO‐08) framework (International Labour Orga‐
nization, 2007). These codes are contained in the indices S_P1ISCO and S_P2ISCO for the students’
parents or guardians. We then mapped these codes to the international socioeconomic index of oc‐
cupational status (S_P1ISEI and S_P2ISEI) (Ganzeboom et al., 1992), where higher ISEI scores indicate
higher levels of occupational status.
Level of parental education
Highest educational level of parental education (S_HISCED) was created from StQ 9 and 13, which
asked students about the highest parental education attainment of their parents, and provided the
data for measuring another important family background variable. Levels of parental education were
classified according to the ISCED levels.
Recoding educational qualifications into the following categories provided indices of highest parental
educational attainment:
(0) Did not complete ISCED level 2;
(1) ISCED level 2 (lower‐secondary);
(2) ISCED level 3 (upper‐secondary);
(3) ISCED level 4 (non‐tertiary post‐secondary) or ISCED level 5 (vocational tertiary); and
(4) ISCED level 6 (bachelor’s level tertiary) or ISCED level 7 (master’s level tertiary) or ISCED level
8 (doctorate level tertiary).
Indices with these categories are available for each student’s first parent or guardian (S_P1ISCED)
(Question 9) and second parent or guardian (S_P2ISCED) (Question 13). S_HISCED corresponds to
the higher ISCED level of either parent.
Literacy resources at home
Students’ home literacy resources (S_HOMLIT) was created from StQ 14, which asked students how
many books they had in their homes. This index had the following categories:
(0) 0 to 10 books;
(1) 11 to 25 books;
(2) 26 to 100 books;
(3) 101 to 200 books; and
(4) More than 200 books.
Internet connection at home
Internet connection at home (S_INTNET) was derived from the two items included in StQ 15 where
students were asked about the quality of their internet connection at home. The first item was about

212
ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
how often the internet in their home drops out and the second about how often the internet connection
in their home is so slow that it made it difficult for them to complete their schoolwork. One response
option for both items was “there is no internet connection in my home.” In case students gave this
response for both items this was coded as 0. Any other combination of responses was coded as 1.
Computer experience in years
Computer experience in years (S_EXCOMP), includes desktop, laptop, notebook/netbook, and tablet
devices, was created from StQ 17, which asked students about the number of years of experience in
using computers. The index has the following categories:
(0) Never or less than one year;
(1) At least one year but less than three years;
(2) At least three years but less than five years;
(3) At least five years but less than seven years; and
(4) Seven years or more.
ICT studies in current school year
ICT studies in current school year (S_ICTSTUD) was created from StQ 27 which asked students whether
they studied a computing, computer science, information technology, informatics, or similar during the
current school year where “Yes” was coded as 1 and “No” as 0.
Teacher questionnaire
Sex of teacher
Sex of teacher (T_SEX) was computed from the data captured from TQ 1. Female teaches were coded
as 1, male teachers were coded as 0. As for students, countries could adapt the response categories
to include one or more additional categories for teachers to self‐identify as neither male nor female.
Few teachers responded using the additional categories in countries where the national option was
implemented, during data processing data in any additional categories were recoded to missing.
Teacher age
Teachers were asked to report their age in TQ2. The index T_AGE was created from TQ2. In case of
missing information the data from the tracking form was used to calculate the age of the teacher.
Teaching in target grade schools in the current year
Teaching in target grade schools in the current year (T_WGT) was created from TQ 4 which asked
teachers to indicate the number of schools in which they teach the target grade population for the
current school year. The coding of the response categories are shown as below:
(1.00) Only in the sampled school;
(0.50) In the sampled school and another school;
(0.33) In the sampled school and in two other schools; and
(0.25) In the sampled school and in three or more other schools.
ICT experience with ICT use
ICT experience with ICT use during lessons (T_EXLES) and ICT experience with ICT use for preparing
lessons (T_EXPREP) were created from Question 5 which asked teachers to indicate how long they
have been using computers for preparing (TQ5a) or during lessons (TQ5b). These indices were coded
as:

SCALING PROCEDURES FOR ICILS 2023 QUESTIONNAIRE ITEMS
213
(0) Never;
(1) Less than two years;
(2) At least two years but less than five years;
(3) At least five years but less than ten years; and
(4) Ten years or more.
School‐level questionnaires
Sex of principal
Sex of principal (P_SEX) was created from PQ 22, which asked whether respondents were male or
female where female principals were coded as 1, and male principals coded as 0. As for students and
teachers, countries could adapt the response categories to include one or more additional categories
for principals to self‐identify as neither male nor female. Few principals responded using the additional
categories in countries where the national option was implemented, during data processing data in any
additional categories were recoded to missing.
Number of students in school and grade
Number of students in the entire school (P_NUMSTD) and the Number of students in the target grade
(P_NUMTAR) were created from PQ 1 and PQ 2, which asked the principals about the number of
female and male students in the entire school and also at the target grade. Categorical versions of the
two indices were created. P_NUMSTD_CAT has the following values:
(1) 1–300;
(2) 301–600;
(3) 601–900; and
(4) More than 900.
Index P_NUMTAR_CAT has the following values:
(1) 1–100;
(2) 101–100; and
(3) More than 200.
Number of grades in school
Number of grades in school (P_NGRADE) was created from PQ 3, which asked principals to report the
lowest (youngest) grade and the highest (oldest) grade taught in their school. The difference between
these two grades was calculated as the number of grades in school.
Number of teachers
Number of teachers (P_NUMTCH) was created from PQ 4, which asked the principals about the num‐
ber of teachers in the school. This index was calculated by summing the total number of full time
teachers with the total number of part‐time teachers weighted at 50 percent. Further, the index was
categorized and P_NUMTCH_CAT was created which has following categories:
(1) 1–25;
(2) 26–50;
(3) 51–75; and
(4) More than 75.

214
ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
Ratio of school size and teachers
Ratio of school size and teachers (P_RATTCH) was calculated by dividing the number of teachers
(P_NUMTCH) by the number of students (P_NUMSTD) in a school.
Private school indicator
Private school indicator (P_PRIV) was created from PQ 6a which collected information about whether
the school was a public school or private school where public schools are coded as 0 and private
schools coded as 1.
School composition by student background
School composition by student background (P_COMP) was created from PQ 6b which asked principals
to estimate the percentage of students at their school coming from economically affluent homes and
those from economically disadvantaged homes (“0–10%,” “11–25%,” “26–50%,” and “more than 50%”
for each home type). The index was coded as:
(1) schools with more students from socio‐economically affluent than disadvantaged homes;
(2) schools with students from neither more socio‐economically affluent nor more disadvantaged
homes; and
(3) schools with more students from socio‐economically disadvantaged than affluent homes.
ICT experience in years in the school
ICT experience in years in the school (C_EXP) was created from CQ 3, which asked coordinators to
indicate the number of years that the school has been using ICT for teaching and learning. This index
was coded as:
(0) Never, we do not use ICT;
(1) Fewer than 5 years;
(2) At least 5 but fewer than 10 years; and
(3) 10 years or more.
Number of devices at the school
Question 7a of the ICT coordinator questionnaire collected data on the number of desktop computers,
the number of laptops (including notebooks and netbooks), and the number of tablet devices in the
school. The sum of ICT devices (C_ICTDEV) was derived by adding these numbers regardless if a
response for all of them was provided. Respondents were also asked to indicate the number of these
different types of devices that were available for student use. These were summed to derive an index of
sum of ICT devices available for student use (C_ICTSTD), again not requiring a response to all of them.
The Question 7b asked respondents to indicate the number of school‐provided interactive whiteboards
available in the school. In conjunction with the number of students at school (P_NUMSTD) these data
also provided the following ratios:
• Ratio of school size and number of ICT devices (C_RATDEV) = Number of students in the school
(P_NUMSTD) / number of ICT devices in the school altogether (C_ICTDEV).
• Ratio of school size and number of devices available for students (C_RATSTD) = Number of
students in the school (P_NUMSTD) / number of ICT devices in the school that are available to
students (C_ICTSTD).
• Ratio of school size and smart boards (C_RATSMB) = Number of students in the school (P_NUMSTD)
/ number of smart boards or interactive white boards available (II3G07B).
In ICILS 2018 the calculation of these indices was slightly different. This is why the indices are cal‐

SCALING PROCEDURES FOR ICILS 2023 QUESTIONNAIRE ITEMS
215
culated also with the procedure equivalent to 2018 and this is indicated in the index name. Indices
C_ICTDEV_2018 and C_ICTSTD_2018 were calculated as the sum of all devices only, if all three re‐
sponses were given. Following, the indices C_RATDEV_2018, C_RATSTD_2018 had to be calculated
as well as described above.
12.2 Students’ socioeconomic background indices
In ICILS 2023, two students’ socioeconomic background indices were constructed: the national index
of students’ socioeconomic background (NISB) and the international index of students’ socioeconomic
background (IISB). Both indices were constructed using the same procedure. Nevertheless, the NISB
was constructed individually for each country, only taking into account that country’s data and the IISB
was constructed using data from all countries that met participation requirements.
It is important to note that neither index should be used in absolute terms, their values do not indicate
if a student has or has not a high or low socioeconomic background. Both indices only indicate the
relative socioeconomic position of each student within their country, for the NISB, and within the
total sample, for the IISB. Therefore, both indices can only be used within analyses that evaluate the
relation between two or more variables, like correlation analysis and regression analysis. Moreover,
when dealing with a single country analysis the NISB should be used, because their parameters are
adjusted to the specific country, and when dealing with multiple countries, the IISB should be used
because the NISB is not comparable between countries.
Both the NISB and the IISB were derived from the following three indices: highest occupational status
of parents (S_HISEI), highest educational level of parents (S_HISCED), and the number of books at
home (S_HOMLIT).
With these three indices, we constructed a three‐by‐three correlation matrix using the appropriate cor‐
relation coefficient for each index combination: Spearman’s correlation coefficient between S_HISEI
and S_HISCED, and between S_HISEI and S_HOMLIT, and a polychoric correlation coefficient between
S_HISCED and S_HOMLIT. Later on, we conducted a principal component analysis (PCA) on this cor‐
relation matrix.
PCA was used to reduce the dimensionality of the original dataset while preserving as much variance as
possible (Hastie et al., 2009; Jolliffe, 2002). The results of the first principal component, that explains
the largest portion of the variance, were used as an indication of dimensionality. We expected that
more than 50 percent of variance is explained by the first component in a unidimensional construct,
like the students’ socioeconomic background. As shown in Table 12.1 this is the case for all countries.
