12.3 Scaling procedures
Many of the ICILS questionnaire questions were created with sets of items that were developed specif‐
ically with the purpose and assumption that the data from the items within each set could be used to
establish measurement scales of a latent trait underpinning the item sets. These were developed with
reference to the ICILS 2023 assessment framework (see Fraillon & Rožman, 2024). The questionnaire
item development process is described in Chapter 4.
In this section, we explain the processes used to evaluate the viability of establishing the planned scales
from sets of items. These processes evaluate both the degree to which individual items within a set
contributed to the scale measures and the measurement properties of the derived scales. The majority
of proposed scales were to be derived from sets of items with four Likert‐type response options.
Review of item statistics
Item reviews began with an evaluation of item‐level non‐response. In the ICILS 2023 questionnaires,
there are three types of item level‐non response.
• Omitted: The respondent had a chance to answer the item but did not do so, leaving the corre‐
sponding item without a response.
• Not administered: This signified that the item or question was not administered to the respondent,
meaning that the respondent could not read and answer the question. This could happen for
example in the case where a country chose not to administer an item or question that was an
international option.
• Logically not applicable: This missing code was assigned to questionnaire items that depended
on a filter question. If a respondent’s answer to the filter question indicated that subsequent
questions were not relevant, those follow‐up items were coded as ‘logically not applicable.’
Omitted codes were considered only when presenting the percentage distributions and did not con‐
tribute to the calculation of scale statistics. Not administered and logically not applicable codes did not
contribute to the calculation of the item statistics or scale construction.
We then examined Cronbach’s alpha measure of the internal consistency or reliability of each scale.
When reviewing Cronbach’s alpha, we regarded values above 0.7 as indicating satisfactory reliability
and those above 0.8 as showing high degrees of reliability (see, for example, Nunnally & Bernstein,
1994, pp. 264–265). The reliabilities of all ICILS 2023 questionnaire scale indices are presented in H.

218
ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
Scale check
After reviewing item statistics to select the proposed item content for each scale, we completed further
checks to determine whether the item data were suitable for generating IRT scales.
Only proposed scales comprising data from three or more items were considered for further scale
construction (using IRT scaling) and scoring.
In addition, the following two criteria were applied to the data for item sets in the proposed scales to
determine their fitness for further scaling analyses. These criteria were first applied to the item sets
using the pooled international data.
• There must be at least 200 valid cases (for a case to be considered valid, the respondent has to
have provided responses to at least two items in the proposed scale).
• There must be variation shown in the responses.
For proposed item sets that met the above criteria using the pooled international data, the same criteria
were then applied separately for data within each country. Where the data within a country did not
meet one or more criteria for a proposed scale, the data from this country were not included into
multi‐group IRT analysis for that scale.
It is noted that even though some countries were not included into by country analysis due to failure to
meet all criteria, all countries received scale scores for valid cases, using the international parameters.
We checked as well, if Cronbach’s alpha measure of the internal consistency or reliability of each scale
was low based on criteria described above. All ICILS 2023 questionnaire scaled indices presented
satisfactory reliability, further supporting the internal consistency of each scale.
Scale dimensionality
Since IRT models often assume unidimensionality (Embretson & Reise, 2000; Lord, 1980), PCA was
conducted first to evaluate the dimensionality of the data for each proposed scale, and how much
variance the first principal component accounted for. The steps to determine the dimensionality of
the original dataset are shown as below (Jolliffe, 2002):
1. Calculate the polychoric correlation matrix with all scale items;
2. Calculate the Eigenvectors and Eigenvalues from the polychoric correlation matrix;
3. Sort the Eigenvalues associated with each principal component in descending order;
4. Determine the number of principal components.
A scree plot is used to choose the number of principal components (Cattell, 1966). It is a visual repre‐
sentation of how much variance each principal component explains with X axis indicates the principal
components in descending order and Y axis shows the Eigenvalues or the percent of variance explained.
The point where the curve starts to flatten (the so called “elbow point” or “inflection point”) is used to
select the number of principal components. Components beyond this point explain significantly less
variance within the data.
Item response modeling
For ICILS 2023, the majority of the context questionnaire items are polytomous, where each item
has multiple ordered response categories (e.g., rating scales), then Partial Credit Modeling (PCM), an
extension of Rasch model was applied (Masters & Wright, 1997). The mathematical formulations of
both Rasch model and PCM model are shown as below:
Pi(θn) =
exp(θn −δi)
1 + exp(θn −δi),

SCALING PROCEDURES FOR ICILS 2023 QUESTIONNAIRE ITEMS
219
where Pi(θn) is the probability of person n scoring 1 on item i, θn is the estimated latent trait of person
n, and δi is the estimated location of item i on this dimension. For each item, item responses are
modeled as a function of the latent trait θn:
PXi(θn) =

where PXi(θn) denotes the probability of person n scoring x on item i, θn denotes the person’s latent
trait, the item parameter δi describes the location of the item on the latent continuum, and τij provides
an additional step parameter.
International parameter calibration
The international item parameters were derived from two PCM models shown below using equally
weighted national datasets:
A) International Pooled PCM Model.
The first PCM model was constructed with international pooled dataset with senate weight so
that each country contributes equally to the final calibration of the item parameters. Data were
included only from countries meeting the sampling participation requirements relevant to the
specific questionnaire.27
B) Multiple Group PCM Model.
The item parameters obtained from the pooled model were then fixed in each country’s multiple
group PCM model. It is noted that countries that did meet the previously described criteria
(insufficient valid cases or no variation for all items) were not included in the country analysis.
Then, the item parameters obtained from the pooled multiple group PCM were used to build
PCM models for each country, including those countries that were excluded from the multiple
group PCM so that all country participants could have scale scores except for invalid cases.
Model evaluation and scoring
Once the PCM models were constructed, the following statistics were used to evaluate the perfor‐
mance of the models:
• Inlier‐sensitive or Information‐weighted Fit (infit): This is an item statistic to measure the dis‐
crepancy between the observed data for the item and the IRT model implied item value. As a
general rule of thumb, 1 indicates a perfect fit; values less than 1 suggest overfit, values bigger
than 1 suggest underfit, meaning that there is more variation in the item than the model predicts,
excluding outliers (Wright & Masters, 1982).
• Outlier‐sensitive Fit (outfit): This is a similar item statistic as infit to measure the discrepancy
between the observed data for the item and the IRT model implied value. It also uses 1 to indicate
the perfect fit; but unlike infit, outfit considers all responses, including the outliers (Wright &
Masters, 1982).
• Weighted Root Mean Square Deviation (WRMSD): This metric assesses the weighted average
of the squared differences between observed and expected responses. The weighting usually
takes into account the standard errors of the observed scores, making it more sensitive to areas
27 Romania met the sampling participation requirements, but due to late testing their data were not included in the final
calibration.

220
ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
with higher measurement precision. A lower WRMSD value indicates a better fit between the
model predictions and the observed data.
– WRMSD < 0.1: Excellent fit;
– 0.1 ≤WRMSD < 0.2: Good fit;
– 0.2 ≤WRMSD < 0.3: Moderate fit;
– WRMSD ≥0.3: Poor fit, suggesting the model may not be adequately describing the data.
• Root Mean Square Deviation (RMSD): This is the square root of the average of the squared
differences between observed and expected responses. It provides a measure of the overall fit
of the model. Similar to WRMSD, a lower RMSD value indicates a better fit. While specific
cutoffs are less common, an RMSD cutoff value of 0.3 has shown to be effective in simulation
studies (Buchholz & Hartig, 2019) and was applied in other ILSAs (Yamamoto et al., 2013). In
this report, we use 0.25 as the cutoff point.
Following the estimation of international item parameter from the calibration sample, we computed
weighted likelihood estimation to obtain individual student, teacher, or school scores. Weighted likeli‐
hood estimations are computed by minimizing the equation:

for each case n, where rx is the sum score obtained from a set of k items with j steps between adjacent
categories. This can be achieved by applying the Newton‐Raphson method. The term Jn/2In (with In
being the information function for student n and Jn being its derivative with respect to q) is used as a
weight function to account for the bias inherent in maximum likelihood estimation (Warm, 1989). We
used the R library TAM (Robitzsch et al., 2024) for the pre‐calibration of item parameters in order to
subsequently derive scale scores.
For all questionnaire scales in ICILS 2023 the transformation of weighted likelihood estimates to an
international metric resulted in reporting scales with an ICILS 2023 average of 50 and a standard devia‐
tion of 10 for equally weighted datasets from the countries that met sample participation requirements.
This is achieved by applying the following formula:
θ′

where θ′

logits, and µθ is the international mean of logit scores with equally weighted country subsamples, and
σθ is the corresponding international standard deviation of the original weighted likelihood estimates.
12.4 Scaling results
In this section we present the results for ICILS 2023 questionnaire scales. For each scale, we present a
short description, its items, their response categories, and their item parameters. This section is divided
into: student scales, teacher scales, principal scales, and ICT coordinator scales.
Student Scales
Learning about internet related tasks at school (S_LRNINTS)
In Question 20a.i, students were asked to indicate the extent to which they had learned various
internet‐related tasks at school. For each task, they selected from the following response options:

SCALING PROCEDURES FOR ICILS 2023 QUESTIONNAIRE ITEMS
221
“To a large extent,” “To a moderate extent,” “To a small extent,” or “Not at all.” The responses to these
items were used to derive a scale reflecting students’ learning about internet‐related tasks at school
(S_LRNINTS). This scale includes items related to students’ ability to search for information online, re‐
fine search results, evaluate the reliability of information, include accurate references, and recognize
potential online scams or deceptive messages.
Higher values on this scale indicate greater self‐reported learning of internet‐related skills through
formal school instruction. The scale is composed of 6 items, item wording is presented in Table 12.2.
The IRT results are good (Table 12.3). Both outfits and infits are all close to 1. WRMSD values are all
less than .1, suggesting an excellent fit, and RMSD values for all items and all countries are all below
.25, indicating that this scale is invariant across countries.

Learning about internet related tasks outside of school (S_LRNINTO)
In Question 20a.ii, students were asked to indicate the extent to which they had learned various
internet‐related tasks outside of school. For each task, they selected from the following response
options: “To a large extent,” “To a moderate extent,” “To a small extent,” or “Not at all.” The responses to
these items were used to derive a scale reflecting students’ learning about internet‐related tasks outside
of school (S_LRNINTO). This scale includes items related to students’ ability to search for information
online, refine search results, evaluate the reliability of information, include accurate references, and
recognize potential online scams or deceptive messages.
Higher values on this scale indicate greater self‐reported learning of internet‐related skills through
informal means outside of school settings. The scale is composed of 6 items, item wording is presented

222
ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
in Table 12.4. The IRT results are good (Table 12.5). Both outfits and infits are all close to 1. WRMSD
values are all less than .1, suggesting an excellent fit, and RMSD values for all items and all countries
are all below .25, indicating that this scale is invariant across countries.

Learning about safe and responsible ICT use at school (S_LRNSAFE)
In Question 20c, students were asked to indicate the extent to which they had learned about various
aspects of safe and responsible ICT use at school. For each topic, they selected from the following
response options: “To a large extent,” “To a moderate extent,” “To a small extent,” or “Not at all.” The
responses to these items were used to derive a scale reflecting students’ learning about safe and
responsible ICT use at school (S_LRNSAFE). This scale includes items related to students’ knowledge
of responsible and respectful use of social media, recognizing cyberbullying, understanding the physical
health implications of ICT use, and awareness of psychological well‐being related to ICT use.
Higher values on this scale indicate greater self‐reported learning about these topics through school
instruction. The scale is composed of 4 items, item wording is presented in Table 12.6. The IRT results
are very good (Table 12.7). Both outfits and infits are all close to 1. WRMSD values are all less than .1,
suggesting an excellent fit, and RMSD values for all items and all countries are all below .25, indicating
that this scale is invariant across countries.

Academic‐media multitasking (S_ACMULT)
Question 21 of the student questionnaire asked students to indicate the frequency with which they use
ICT for various activities outside of school. The response options included: “Never,” “Almost never,”
“Sometimes,” “Often,” and “Very often.” A subset of items from this question was used to derive a
scale reflecting students’ engagement in academic‐media multitasking. The S_ACMULT scale measures
the extent to which students engage in concurrent academic and media‐related activities using ICT.
It includes items such as text chatting, checking social media for updates, using the internet to find
information, and watching online videos.
Higher values on this scale indicate more frequent engagement in multitasking behaviors while using
ICT. The scale is composed of 6 items, item wording is presented in Table 12.8. The IRT results are
good (Table 12.9). Both outfits and infits are mostly close to 1. WRMSD values are all less than .1,
suggesting an excellent fit, and RMSD values for all items and all countries are all below .25, indicating
that this scale is invariant across countries.

General classroom applications (S_GENCLASS)
Two scales were constructed from the statements in Question 23. Students were asked to indicate
how often they used different types of ICT applications during their lessons throughout the school year.
For each application, they selected from the following response options: “Never,” “In some lessons,”
“In most lessons,” or “In every lesson.” The responses to these items were used to derive a scale
reflecting students’ use of general ICT applications in the classroom (S_GENCLASS). This scale includes
items related to students’ use of word‐processing software, presentation software, spreadsheets, and
computer‐based information resources (e.g., websites and online encyclopedias) in their schoolwork.
Higher values on this scale indicate more frequent use of general ICT applications during classroom
activities. The scale is composed of 4 items, item wording is presented in Table 12.10. The IRT results
are very good (Table 12.11). Both outfits and infits are all close to 1. WRMSD values are all less than .1,
suggesting an excellent fit, and RMSD values for all items and all countries are all below .25, indicating
that this scale is invariant across countries.

lessons?

almost every lesson

how often they used different types of ICT applications during their lessons throughout the school year.

reflecting students’ use of specialist ICT applications in the classroom (S_SPECLASS). This scale includes
items related to students’ use of multimedia production tools (e.g., video editing software), concept

General ICT self‐efficacy (S_GENEFF)
Two scales were constructed from the statements in Question 24. Students were asked to indicate
how well they could perform various ICT‐related tasks. For each task, they selected from the following
response options: “Very well,” “Moderately well,” “I have never done this, but I could work out how
to do it,” or “I do not think I could do this.” The responses to these items were used to derive a

226
ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
scale reflecting students’ general ICT self‐efficacy (S_GENEFF). This scale includes items related to
students’ confidence in performing ICT tasks such as editing digital photographs, writing or editing
text for a school assignment, searching for relevant information, changing device settings, and creating
multimedia presentations.
Higher values on this scale indicate higher self‐efficacy in using ICT for various tasks. The scale is
composed of 10 items, item wording is presented in Table 12.14. The IRT results are acceptable (Table
12.15). Both outfits and infits are mostly close to 1. WRMSD values are all less than .1, suggesting an
excellent fit, and RMSD values for all items and all countries are all below .25, indicating that this scale
is invariant across countries.

their level of agreement with various statements regarding the role of ICT in their future studies and
careers. For each statement, they selected from the following response options: “Strongly agree,”
“Agree,” “Disagree,” or “Strongly disagree.” The responses to these items were used to derive a scale
reflecting students’ expectations for ICT use in the future (S_ICTFUT). This scale includes items related
to students’ aspirations to study ICT‐related subjects, use ICT as an important part of their future work,
engage in programming, and recognize the value of ICT skills for career prospects.
Higher values on this scale indicate stronger expectations for using ICT in future academic or profes‐
sional settings. The scale is composed of 5 items, item wording is presented in Table 12.22. The IRT
results are good (Table 12.23). Both outfits and infits are mostly close to 1. WRMSD values are all less
than .1, suggesting an excellent fit, and RMSD values for all items and all countries are all below .25,
indicating that this scale is invariant across countries.

Attitudes toward ICT ‐ positive outcomes (S_ICTPOSS)
Another scale was constructed from the items in Question 25b. Students were asked to indicate their

230

the benefits of ICT for learning (S_ICTPOSS). This scale includes items related to ICT making learning
more enjoyable, the importance of learning how to use ICT, the significance of learning programming,
and the need for students to stay updated with ICT advancements.
Higher values on this scale indicate stronger positive attitudes toward the role of ICT in education and
its relevance for students’ learning and future opportunities. The scale is composed of 4 items, item

Learning about CT in class (S_CTCLS)
Question 26 of the student questionnaire asked students to indicate the extent to which they had
learned specific computational thinking (CT) skills in class. The response options for each item were:
“To a large extent,” “To a moderate extent,” “To a small extent,” or “Not at all.” A set of items from this
question was used to derive a scale measuring students’ learning of CT in class (S_CTCLS). The scale
reflects the extent to which students report having learned various CT‐related skills, such as using a
solution for one problem to help solve another, breaking down complex problems into smaller steps,
making diagrams to explain concepts, planning tasks in a structured manner, and detecting patterns in
data.
Higher scores on this scale indicate that students perceive they have learned CT skills to a greater
extent in their classes. The scale is composed of 9 items, item wording is presented in Table 12.26.
The IRT results are very good (Table 12.27). Both outfits and infits are all close to 1. WRMSD values
are all less than .1, suggesting an excellent fit, and RMSD values for all items and all countries are all
below .25, indicating that this scale is invariant across countries.

Teacher Scales
Teachers’ ICT self‐efficacy (T_ICTEFF)
In Question 7, teachers were asked to indicate how well they could perform various school‐related
tasks using ICT. For each task, they selected from the following response options: “I can do this very
well,” “I can do this moderately well,” “I haven’t done this, but I could find out how,” or “I do not think
I could do this.” The responses to these items were used to derive a scale reflecting teachers’ ICT
self‐efficacy (T_ICTEFF). This scale includes items related to evaluating the quality of online teaching
resources, contributing to discussion forums, creating presentations, conducting online transactions,
and preparing ICT‐integrated lessons.
Higher values on this scale indicate greater confidence in using ICT for teaching‐related tasks. The
scale is composed of 12 items, item wording is presented in Table 12.28. The IRT results are acceptable
(Table 12.29). Both outfits and infits are mostly close to 1. WRMSD values for the majority of items
are less than .1 and for all items are less than .2, suggesting a general good fit. RMSD values for all
items and all countries are all below .25, indicating that this scale is invariant across countries.

Emphasis on ICT use in initial teacher education (T_EMPITE)
In Question 8, teachers were asked to indicate whether their initial teacher education (ITE) included
training in various aspects of using ICT for teaching and learning. For each item, they selected from
the following response options: “Yes,” “No” or “I cannot remember.” The responses to these items
were used to derive a scale reflecting teachers’ reports on the emphasis of ICT use in their initial
teacher education (T_EMPITE). This scale includes items related to general ICT approaches for teaching,
subject‐specific ICT use, using ICT for teacher collaboration, using ICT for student assessment, and
supporting students’ ability to evaluate digital information.

SCALING PROCEDURES FOR ICILS 2023 QUESTIONNAIRE ITEMS
233
Higher values on this scale indicate greater exposure to ICT training in initial teacher education pro‐
grams. The scale is composed of 8 items, item wording is presented in Table 12.30. The IRT results
are good (Table 12.31). Both outfits and infits appear smaller and are not close to 1 compared to other
scales, which is because binary items have less variability. WRMSD values are all less than .1, suggest‐
ing an excellent fit, and RMSD values for all items and all countries are all below .25, indicating that
this scale is invariant across countries.

less than .1 except for item IT3G10F (0.101), suggesting a good fit. RMSD values for all items and all

Availability of computer resources at school (T_RESRC)
In Question 13, teachers were asked to indicate the extent to which they agreed with various state‐
ments regarding the availability and functionality of ICT resources in their school. For each statement,
they selected from the following response options: “Strongly agree,” “Agree,” “Disagree,” or “Strongly
disagree.” The responses to these items were used to derive a scale reflecting teachers’ perceptions
of ICT resource availability at school (T_RESRC). This scale includes items related to the adequacy of
ICT equipment, the up‐to‐dateness of ICT resources, the reliability of ICT equipment, the sufficiency
of technical support, and the quality of internet connectivity.
Higher values on this scale indicate more positive perceptions of ICT resource availability and func‐
tionality in schools. The scale is composed of 6 items, item wording is presented in Table 12.38. The
IRT results are acceptable (Table 12.39). Both outfits and infits are mostly close to 1, WRMSD values
are all less than .1 except for item IT3G13H (0.102), suggesting a good fit. RMSD values for all items
and all countries are all below .25, indicating that this scale is invariant across countries.

Negative views on using ICT in teaching and learning (T_VWNEG)
Two scales were constructed from the statements in Question 14. Teachers were asked to indicate the
extent to which they agreed with various statements regarding potential negative consequences of ICT
use in education. For each statement, they selected from the following response options: “Strongly
agree,” “Agree,” “Disagree,” or “Strongly disagree.” The responses to these items were used to derive a
scale reflecting teachers’ negative views on using ICT for teaching and learning (T_VWNEG). This scale
includes items related to concerns that ICT makes it difficult for students to develop deep thinking, leads
to increased copying of material from the internet, distracts students from learning, results in poorer
written expression, and negatively affects calculation and estimation skills.
Higher values on this scale indicate stronger concerns among teachers about the negative impacts of
ICT use on student learning. The scale is composed of 8 items, item wording is presented in Table
12.40. The IRT results are acceptable (Table 12.41). Both outfits and infits are mostly close to 1.
WRMSD values are all less than .1 except for item IT3G14F (0.106) , suggesting a general good fit.
RMSD values for all items and all countries are all below .25, indicating that this scale is invariant across
countries.

Positive views on using ICT in teaching and learning (T_VWPOS)
Another scale was constructed from the statements in Question 14. Teachers were asked to indicate
the extent to which they agreed with various statements regarding the potential benefits of ICT use in
education. For each statement, they selected from the following response options: “Strongly agree,”
“Agree,” “Disagree,” or “Strongly disagree.” The responses to these items were used to derive a scale re‐
flecting teachers’ positive views on using ICT for teaching and learning (T_VWPOS). This scale includes
items related to beliefs that ICT helps students develop a greater interest in learning, supports differ‐
entiated instruction by allowing students to work at an appropriate level, enhances problem‐solving
skills, facilitates student collaboration, and improves students’ ability to plan and organize their work.
Higher values on this scale indicate stronger positive perceptions of ICT’s role in teaching and learning.
The scale is composed of 7 items, item wording is presented in Table 12.42. The IRT results are very
good (Table 12.43). Both outfits and infits are all close to 1. WRMSD values are all less than .1,
suggesting an excellent fit. RMSD values for all items and all countries are all below .25, indicating that
this scale is invariant across countries.

Teacher centered teaching approach (T_TEAPTC)
Three scales were constructed from the statements in Question 16.i. Teachers were asked to indicate
how often students in their reference class engaged in various teacher‐directed learning activities.
For each activity, they selected from the following response options: “Never,” “In some lessons,” “In
most lessons,” or “In every or almost every lesson.” The responses to these items were used to derive a
scale reflecting teachers’ use of teacher‐centered teaching approaches with ICT (T_TEAPTC). This scale
includes items related to presenting information to the whole class, conducting whole‐class discussions,
leading whole‐class checking of schoolwork, having students work individually on assignments, and
administering teacher‐provided tests.
Higher values on this scale indicate more frequent use of teacher‐directed instructional strategies in
the classroom. The scale is composed of 5 items, item wording is presented in Table 12.44. The
IRT results are good (Table 12.45). Both outfits and infits are all close to 1. WRMSD values are all
less than .1 except for items IT3G16AB (0.106) and IT3G16AE (0.112), suggesting a general good fit.
RMSD values for all items and all countries are all below .25, indicating that this scale is invariant across
countries.

some lessons,” “In most lessons,” or “In every or almost every lesson.” The responses to these items
were used to derive a scale reflecting teachers’ use of inquiry‐based teaching approaches with ICT
(T_TEAPIN). This scale includes items related to helping students plan research or inquiries, guiding
them in conducting academic or subject‐related research, supporting students’ inquiry‐based investi‐
gations, providing feedback on research activities, and facilitating student presentations of research
findings using ICT.
Higher values on this scale indicate more frequent use of ICT to support inquiry‐based learning strate‐
gies. The scale is composed of 5 items, item wording is presented in Table 12.46. The IRT results are
good (Table 12.47). Both outfits and infits are mostly close to 1. WRMSD values are all less than .1,
suggesting an excellent fit. RMSD values for all items and all countries are all below .25, indicating that
this scale is invariant across countries.

responses to these items were used to derive a scale reflecting teachers’ use of ICT for multimedia and
collaborative classroom activities (T_ICTCLASB). This scale includes items related to recording audio
or video (e.g., discussions, presentations), using chat or video apps for collaboration, editing digital
images or videos, manually collecting and entering data, and creating or editing digital documents or
presentations.
Higher values on this scale indicate more frequent use of ICT for multimedia and collaborative activities
in the classroom. The scale is composed of 5 items, item wording is presented in Table 12.50. The IRT
results are very good (Table 12.51). Both outfits and infits are all close to 1. WRMSD values are all
less than .1, suggesting an excellent fit. RMSD values for all items and all countries are all below .25,
indicating that this scale is invariant across countries.

or almost every lesson.” The responses to these items were used to derive a scale reflecting teach‐
ers’ use of general utility software (T_USEUTIL). This scale includes items related to word‐processing
software, presentation software, spreadsheets, video and photo editing software, and computer‐based
information resources such as websites and online encyclopedias.
Higher values on this scale indicate more frequent use of these general utility tools in classroom in‐
struction. The scale is composed of 6 items, item wording is presented in Table 12.56. The IRT results
are good (Table 12.57). Both outfits and infits are mostly close to 1, WRMSD values are all less than
.1 except for item IT3G18L (0.108), suggesting a general good fit. RMSD values for all items and all
countries are below .25 except for item IT3G18L for Malta (0.259) , indicating that this scale is invariant
across countries except for Malta.

Emphasis on ICT capabilities in class (T_ICTEMP)
In Question 19, teachers were asked to indicate the emphasis they had given to developing different
ICT‐based skills among students in their reference class. For each skill, they selected from the follow‐
ing response options: “Strong emphasis,” “Some emphasis,” “Little emphasis,” or “No emphasis.” The
responses to these items were used to derive a scale reflecting teachers’ emphasis on developing ICT
capabilities in class (T_ICTEMP). This scale includes items related to displaying information for a spe‐
cific audience, evaluating the credibility of digital information, sharing digital content, using software
to construct digital representations, and providing digital feedback on others’ work.
Higher values on this scale indicate greater emphasis on developing students’ ICT‐related skills in the
classroom. The scale is composed of 12 items, item wording is presented in Table 12.58. The IRT
results are good (Table 12.59). Both outfits and infits are mostly close to 1, WRMSD values are all
less than .1, suggesting an excellent fit. RMSD values for all items and all countries are all below .25,
indicating that this scale is invariant across countries.

Teacher epistemological beliefs‐Embodied cognition (T_EPIEMC)
Three scales were constructed from the statements in Question 21. Teachers were asked to indicate
the extent to which they agreed with various statements about knowledge and learning. For each
statement, they selected from the following response options: “Not at all,” “To a very small extent,” “To a
small extent,” “To a moderate extent,” “To a large extent,” or “Completely.” The responses to these items
were used to derive a scale reflecting teachers’ epistemological beliefs aligned with embodied cognition
(T_EPIEMC). This scale includes items related to understanding abstract phenomena by relating them
to real‐world experiences, the role of whole‐body experiences in cognition, learning as an active and
social process, and the idea that deep understanding emerges from unsystematic exploration.
Higher values on this scale indicate stronger alignment with embodied cognition perspectives on learn‐
ing and knowledge development. The scale is composed of 5 items, item wording is presented in Table
12.62. The IRT results are good (Table 12.63). Both outfits and infits are mostly close to 1. WRMSD
values are all less than .1, suggesting an excellent fit. RMSD values for all items and all countries are
all below .25, indicating that this scale is invariant across countries.
Table 12.62: Item wording for the scale Teacher epistemological beliefs‐Embodied cognition (T_EPIEMC)
Scale or item
Question or item wording
T_EPIEMC
To what extent does each of the following statements reflect your beliefs about knowledge,
learning, and cognition?
Recoded response categories for scaling: 0=Not at all or to a very small extent;1=To a small
extent;2=To a moderate extent;3=To a large extent;4=Completely
IT3G21B
People understand abstract phenomena by relating them to physical experiences
IT3G21C
Learners construct their own understanding by internalizing their experiences
IT3G21E
Cognition depends on an individual’s whole‐body response to the physical world
IT3G21H
Knowledge is best created and maintained through personal action and reflection
IT3G21P
Deep understanding of concepts happens unsystematically and depends on the experiences
made available to the learner

Teacher epistemological beliefs‐Cognitivism (T_EPICOG)
Another scale was constructed from the statements in Question 21. Teachers were asked to indicate
the extent to which they agreed with various statements about knowledge and learning. For each
statement, they selected from the following response options: “Not at all,” “To a very small extent,” “To
a small extent,” “To a moderate extent,” “To a large extent,” or “Completely.” The responses to these
items were used to derive a scale reflecting teachers’ epistemological beliefs aligned with cognitivism
(T_EPICOG). This scale includes items related to systematic engagement in knowledge construction,
conceptual knowledge being based on fundamental truths, learning complex concepts through mas‐
tery of simpler ones, the importance of guidance before independent learning, and the necessity of
maintaining knowledge through repeated practice.
Higher values on this scale indicate stronger alignment with cognitivist perspectives on learning and
knowledge development. The scale is composed of 5 items, item wording is presented in Table 12.64.
The IRT results are very good (Table 12.65). Both outfits and infits are all close to 1. WRMSD values
are all less than .1, suggesting an excellent fit. RMSD values for all items and all countries are all below
.25, indicating that this scale is invariant across countries.
Table 12.64: Item wording for the scale Teacher epistemological beliefs‐Cognitivism (T_EPICOG)
Scale or item
Question or item wording
T_EPICOG
To what extent does each of the following statements reflect your beliefs about knowledge,
learning, and cognition?
Recoded response categories for scaling: 0=Not at all or to a very small extent;1=To a small
extent;2=To a moderate extent;3=To a large extent;4=Completely
IT3G21A
Knowledge is developed through systematic engagement with basic facts that leads to deeper
understanding in the future
IT3G21D
Conceptual knowledge is based on fundamental truths and the relationships between these
truths
IT3G21F
Complex concepts are mastered by first mastering the underlying basic concepts
IT3G21I
The best learning happens when preceded by a general overview of the learning content
IT3G21L
Knowledge dissipates if it is not maintained through repetition and practice

Principal Scales
Principals’ views on using ICT for educational outcomes (P_VWICT)

SCALING PROCEDURES FOR ICILS 2023 QUESTIONNAIRE ITEMS
251
In Question 11, school principals were asked to indicate how important they believed various ICT‐
related outcomes were for education in their school. For each outcome, they selected from the follow‐
ing response options: “Very important,” “Quite important,” “Somewhat important,” or “Not important.”
The responses to these items were used to derive a scale reflecting principals’ views on using ICT
for educational outcomes (P_VWICT). This scale includes items related to the perceived importance
of developing students’ basic computer skills, enhancing students’ ability to use ICT for communica‐
tion, fostering responsible ICT use, improving learning through ICT, and promoting students’ critical
understanding of digital information.
Higher values on this scale indicate greater importance assigned by school principals to ICT‐related
skills as an outcome of education. The scale is composed of 10 items, item wording is presented in
Table 12.68. The IRT results are good (Table 12.69). Infits are mostly close to 1, suggesting a general
good fit. Due to small sample size, there is no by country analysis for principal scales, therefore no
measurement invariance results are available across countries.

Principals’ reports on expectations of ICT use by teachers (P_EXPLRN)

“Expected but not required,” or “Not expected.” The responses to these items were used to derive
a scale reflecting principals’ reports on expectations of ICT use by teachers (P_EXPLRN). This scale
includes items related to expectations that teachers will integrate web‐based learning into instruction,
use ICT for student assessment and monitoring progress, incorporate subject‐specific digital learning
resources, and integrate ICT into teaching and learning.
Higher values on this scale indicate greater expectations among school principals regarding teachers’
use of ICT in their instructional practices. The scale is composed of 10 items, item wording is presented
in Table 12.70. The IRT results are good (Table 12.71). Both outfits and infits are mostly close to 1,
suggesting a good fit. Due to small sample size, there is no by country analysis for principal scales,
therefore no measurement invariance results are available across countries.

Principals’ reports on expectations for teacher collaboration using ICT (P_EXPTCH)
Another scale was constructed from the statements in Question 12. School principals were asked to
indicate whether teachers in their school were expected to acquire knowledge and skills in various
ICT‐related activities. For each activity, they selected from the following response options: “Expected
and required,” “Expected but not required,” or “Not expected.” The responses to these items were
used to derive a scale reflecting principals’ reports on expectations for teacher collaboration using ICT
(P_EXPTCH). This scale includes items related to expectations that teachers will collaborate with other
teachers via ICT, communicate with parents via ICT, and communicate with students via ICT.
Higher values on this scale indicate greater expectations among school principals regarding teachers’
use of ICT for collaboration and communication. The scale is composed of 3 items, item wording is
presented in Table 12.72. The IRT results are acceptable (Table 12.73). Both outfits and infits are
mostly close to 1, suggesting a good fit. Due to small sample size, there is no by country analysis for

Principals’ reports on priorities for facilitating use of ICT (P_PRIORS)
In Question 20, school principals were asked to indicate the priority given to different strategies for
facilitating the use of ICT in teaching and learning. For each strategy, they selected from the following
response options: “The school has no influence over this way of facilitating the use of ICT in teaching
and learning,” “High priority,” “Medium priority,” “Low priority,” or “Not a priority.” The responses to
these items were used to derive a scale reflecting principals’ reports on priorities for facilitating ICT
use in teaching and learning (P_PRIORS). This scale includes items related to increasing the variety of
digital learning resources, enhancing online learning systems, supporting professional development in
ICT, expanding technical support availability, and providing incentives for teachers to integrate ICT into
instruction.
Higher values on this scale indicate higher levels of priority assigned to these ICT support and imple‐
mentation strategies in schools. The scale is composed of 10 items, item wording is presented in Table
12.74. The IRT results are good (Table 12.75). Both outfits and infits are mostly close to 1, suggesting
a good fit. Due to small sample size, there is no by country analysis for principal scales, therefore no
measurement invariance results are available across countries.

Principals’ use of ICT for general school‐related activities (P_ICTUSE)
Two scales were constructed from Question 24. School principals were asked to indicate how often
they used ICT for various general school‐related activities. For each activity, they selected from the
following response options: “Never,” “Less than once a month,” “At least once a month but not every
week,” “At least once a week but not every day,” or “Every day.” The responses to these items were
used to derive a scale reflecting principals’ use of ICT for general school‐related activities (P_ICTUSE).
This scale includes items related to the frequency with which school principals provide information
on educational issues, maintain and analyze data using ICT, prepare presentations, work with learning
management systems, and manage staff through ICT‐based tools.
Higher values on this scale indicate more frequent use of ICT for general administrative and managerial
tasks in school leadership. The scale is composed of 7 items, item wording is presented in Table 12.76.
The IRT results are acceptable (Table 12.77). Both outfits and infits are mostly close to 1, suggesting

measurement invariance results are available across countries.

Principals’ use of ICT for school‐related communication activities (P_ICTCOM)
Another scale was constructed from the statements in Question 24. School principals were asked
to indicate how often they used ICT for various school‐related communication activities. For each
activity, they selected from the following response options: “Never,” “Less than once a month,” “At
least once a month but not every week,” “At least once a week but not every day,” or “Every day.” The
responses to these items were used to derive a scale reflecting principals’ use of ICT for school‐related
communication activities (P_ICTCOM). This scale includes items related to the frequency with which
school principals communicate with teachers, education authorities, principals and senior staff in other
schools, and parents using ICT.
Higher values on this scale indicate more frequent use of ICT for communication purposes in school
administration and leadership. The scale is composed of 4 items, item wording is presented in Table
12.78. The IRT results are acceptable (Table 12.79). Both outfits and infits are relatively bigger com‐
pared with other principal scales but still close to 1, suggesting a general good fit. Due to small sample
size, there is no by country analysis for principal scales, therefore no measurement invariance results
are available across countries.

The following five scales were constructed from the optional generative artificial intelligence (ChatGPT
or similar) questions of the principal questionnaire.
Principals’ views on negative impact of ChatGPT on students’ learning (P_CIMNEG)
Two scales were constructed from Question 8 of the optional questionnaire. School principals were
asked to indicate how likely they believed AI‐based tools (e.g., ChatGPT or similar technologies) would
lead to various negative consequences for student learning. For each potential impact, they selected
from the following response options: “Very likely,” “Somewhat likely,” “Somewhat unlikely,” or “Not likely
at all.” The responses to these items were used to derive a scale reflecting principals’ views on the neg‐
ative impact of AI‐based tools (P_CIMNEG). This scale includes items related to concerns that AI tools
may hinder students’ ability to develop independent thinking, encourage academic dishonesty, provide
misleading or biased information, create anxiety about AI influence, and foster student dependence
on AI for learning.
Higher values on this scale indicate stronger concerns among school principals about the potential
negative effects of AI‐based tools on student learning. The scale is composed of 5 items, item wording
is presented in Table 12.80. The IRT results are good (Table 12.81). Both outfits and infits are mostly
close to 1, suggesting a good fit. Due to small sample size, there is no by country analysis for principal
scales, therefore no measurement invariance results are available across countries.

Principals’ views on positive impact of ChatGPT on students’ learning (P_CIMPOS)
Another scale was constructed from Question 8 of the optional questionnaire. School principals were
asked to indicate how likely they believed AI‐based tools (e.g., ChatGPT or similar technologies) would
contribute to various positive outcomes for student learning. For each potential impact, they selected
from the following response options: “Very likely,” “Somewhat likely,” “Somewhat unlikely,” or “Not
likely at all.” The responses to these items were used to derive a scale reflecting principals’ views on
the positive impact of AI‐based tools (P_CIMPOS). This scale includes items related to the perceived
benefits of AI tools in enhancing students’ interest in learning, improving logical sequencing in writing,
supporting creativity by generating ideas, enhancing the quality of student work, and strengthening
students’ capacity to critically evaluate information.
Higher values on this scale indicate stronger positive perceptions among school principals regarding
the potential benefits of AI‐based tools for student learning. The scale is composed of 6 items, item
wording is presented in Table 12.82. The IRT results are good (Table 12.83). Both outfits and infits
are all close to 1, suggesting a good fit. Due to small sample size, there is no by country analysis for
principal scales, therefore no measurement invariance results are available across countries.

Principals’ views on increased workload as a consequence of ChatGPT (P_CWORK)
Three scales were constructed from the statements in Question 9 of the optional questionnaire. School
principals were asked to indicate how likely they believed AI‐based tools (e.g., ChatGPT or similar
technologies) would lead to increased workload for teachers and students. For each potential impact,
they selected from the following response options: “Very likely,” “Somewhat likely,” “Somewhat unlikely,”
or “Not likely at all.” The responses to these items were used to derive a scale reflecting principals’
views on increased workload due to AI‐based tools (P_CWORK). This scale includes items related to
expectations that teachers will need specific professional learning on AI, students will need to learn
how to use AI responsibly, teachers will need to monitor students’ use of AI, and students will need to
understand AI’s limitations and potential biases.
Higher values on this scale indicate perceived increased likelihood among school principals about the
additional responsibilities and workload AI may introduce for both teachers and students. The scale is
composed of 7 items, item wording is presented in Table 12.84. The IRT results are acceptable (Table
12.85). Both outfits and infits are mostly close to 1 except for IA3G09Q, suggesting a general good fit.
Due to small sample size, there is no by country analysis for principal scales, therefore no measurement
invariance results are available across countries.

ICT coordinators’ reports on the availability of ICT infrastructure and resources (C_ICTRES). This scale
includes items related to internet access through the school network, school network storage space,
instant messaging services for school‐related use, video conferencing systems, and school intranet
applications and workspaces.
Higher values on this scale indicate greater availability of ICT infrastructure and digital resources in

ICT coordinators’ reports on availability of ICT resources at school ‐ tools (C_RESTOOL)
Two scales were constructed from Question 5. ICT coordinators were asked to indicate the availability
of various software and digital learning tools in their schools. For each resource, they selected from
the following response options: “Made available to teachers and students,” “Made available to teachers
only,” “Made available to students only” or “Not made available.” The responses to these items were
used to derive a scale reflecting ICT coordinators’ reports on the availability of ICT‐based learning tools
(C_RESTOOL). This scale includes items related to the availability of practice programs or apps where
teachers determine content, single‐user digital learning games, concept mapping software, data logging
and monitoring tools, and simulations and modeling software.
Higher values on this scale indicate greater availability of digital learning tools to support teaching and
student learning in schools. The scale is composed of 9 items, item wording is presented in Table 12.92.
The IRT results are acceptable (Table 12.93). The majority of outfits and infits are close to 1 except for
items II3G05I, II3G05J, II3G05K, suggesting an acceptable fit. Due to small sample size, there is no by
country analysis for principal scales, therefore no measurement invariance results are available across
countries.

Chapter 13:
Reporting the ICILS 2023 results
Andrés Strello, Mojca Rožman, and Julian Fraillon
Introduction
This chapter describes the procedures for obtaining the population estimates presented in the ICILS
2023 international report. The chapter is divided into three sections. In the first section, we explain
how the sampling design affects the estimation of population parameters. We first consider the use
of weights to attain unbiased estimations of these parameters and then discuss how to quantify the
uncertainty in the estimation arising from the complex sampling design with which the data was gen‐
erated. In the second section, we describe the imputation variance that quantifies the uncertainty
arising from the test design. This uncertainty arises from using balanced incomplete block designs for
the computer and information literacy (CIL) and computational thinking (CT) tests, where each student
completes only a subset of items within the total item pool. In the subsequent section, we describe
how the relationship between CIL and CT scores was calculated. Finally, we describe the procedures
for conducting significance tests for differences between averages, country, and subgroup means or
percentages, and between results from ICILS 2023, 2018, and 2013 for the countries that participated
in more than one cycle.
13.1 Estimation of sampling variance
Sampling, weights, and non‐response model
In order to select schools, teachers, and students, ICILS 2023 used a stratified two‐stage probabil‐
ity sampling design. In order to facilitate the estimation of population parameters accounting for the
probabilistic nature of the sampling design and the non‐response model, the ICILS 2023 databases
provide a set of total sampling weights. These variables are the product of the varying probabilities
of inclusion across participating units and the different non‐response adjustment factors. For more
details on the non‐response adjustments and the construction of the sampling weights, see Chapter 6
and Chapter 7, respectively. Each unit in the ICILS 2023 databases is accompanied by a total sampling
weight variable: TOTWGTC for the school‐level, TOTWGTS for students, and TOTWGTT for teach‐
ers. Weighted estimation using these variables is necessary to obtain unbiased population parameter
estimates.
Jackknife repeated replication
The complex sampling design requires the estimation of variance to account for the following three
features. The first is explicit stratification, which follows that an estimated parameter’s sampling vari‐
ance is the sum of the stratum‐level sampling variances. The second is that the total sampling weights
may vary across units. Finally, the two‐stage process of the sampling design implies that student and
teacher characteristics or traits are not independent and identically distributed because they are clus‐
tered within schools. To account for these features in the estimation, ICILS 2023 (as this was the case
in previous ICILS cycles and in other IEA studies) uses the jackknife repeated replication (JRR) variance
estimator. Replication (re‐sampling) techniques are useful tools to approximate the sampling variance
of population estimates (Gonzalez & Foy, 2000; Quenouille, 1949; Tukey, 1958; Wolter, 1985). ICILS
2023 uses the JRR variance estimator when reporting standard errors for all estimated population
parameters, such as means, percentages, correlation coefficients, and regression parameters.
During sample selection, schools are sorted within each explicit stratum by a measure of their size and,
where appropriate and feasible, other observable characteristics so that adjacent schools are often
more similar to each other. In the JRR estimation procedure, pairs of adjacent primary sampling units

270
ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
(PSUs)—typically sampled schools—are allocated to pseudo strata. These pseudo‐strata are referred
to as jackknife zones or variance strata. In the publicly available datasets, these are captured by the
variable JKZONE, with an additional character identifying the relevant population.28 Some specific
rules apply when there is an odd number of PSUs or if the number of PSUs exceeds 150. Please
refer to Chapter 6 for more details about this. Each country participating in ICILS 2023 has up to 75
jackknife zones available for each population (see Table 13.1).
Within each jackknife zone, the PSUs were assigned to two different groups. This assignment is docu‐
mented in the jackknife replicate code variable JKREP, where the groups are assigned either the value
1 or 0. Based on the jackknife zone and jackknife replicate code, a total of R different replication
weights were created (RWGT). Each replication weight is derived by manipulating the total sampling
weights of all sampling units in a respective jackknife zone. That is, to create the r = 1, ..., R replicate
weight, the total sampling weight of all units is either doubled (in case PSUs within jackknife zone r
have a jackknife replicate code of one) or set to zero (in case of a jackknife replicate code of zero). The
total sampling weights of all units falling outside the jackknife zone r are kept unmodified. A total of R
replicate weights are computed by iterating across all R jackknife zones. If fewer than 75 zones were
in a dataset, the remaining replicate weights were set to the original sampling weight. Therefore, they
do not contribute to the sampling variable estimate.
Table 13.2 shows an example of the variables included in a dataset that are needed to calculate the
sampling variance.29 There are a total of five jackknife zones in this example. In the first column, we
show the school ID; in the second and third columns, the jackknife zone and jackknife zone replicate
code, respectively; in the fourth, the total sampling weight (TOTWGTC). The replicate weights 1‐5
follow in the remaining of the columns. The respondents within schools with ID 1 or ID 2 are part of
the jackknife zone 1, with a sampling weight of 48.96 and 25.77, respectively. The first school has a
replicate code of zero, and the second has a replicate code of one. Therefore, the weight of the first
school will be zeroed, and the weight of the second school will be doubled when generating the first
replicate weight. The total weight remains unchanged for all schools outside the first jackknife zone.
The same procedure is applied for the schools in zone 2 for the second replicate variable. The value
for replicate weight 2 for the school with a code zero will be zeroed, and the value for the other school
will be doubled, whereas the rest of the schools will retain their original total weight. This procedure
continues until the last jackknife zone.
The last row in Table 13.2 presents the weighted means of the variable C_ICTSTD (sum of ICT de‐
vices available for student use) using different sets of weights: total sampling weight (which would
correspond to the reported mean in the ICILS 2023 international report), and for each of the five
replicate weights (i.e., replicated means). The sampling variance of the estimated mean is, therefore,
approximated by measuring the variation between the reported mean and the replicated means.
28 JKZONES represent sampling zones for students, JKZONEC for schools, and JKZONET for teachers. The same logic
applies to JKREP and RWGT mentioned below.
29 Please note that Table 13.2 uses fictional school IDs and jackknife zones, so these numbers do not correspond to the
available datasets.

where ˆθ corresponds to the reported estimate of θ (i.e., estimated using total sampling weights) and ˆθr
corresponds to the estimate of the parameter using the replicate weight r. As above, R corresponds
to the total number of jackknife zones included in the dataset, which is 75 in ICILS 2023.
The standard error SEˆθ corresponds to the square root of the sampling variance SVˆθ:
SEˆθ =
q
SVˆθ.
(13.2)
In the example presented in Table 13.2, that includes the weighted means of the variable C_ICTSTD,
the standard error would be calculated as follows. The mean in the column of the total weight (67.45)
is representing ˆθ, while ˆθr corresponds to each of the means in the columns of replicate weights.
Following this, the sampling variance would correspond to 201.62, which leads to a standard error of
14.20.
Sampling variance estimation using JRR can be used when estimating parameters, such as means,
percentages, standard deviations, correlations, and differences in means, for data that do not include
student achievement scores. In ICILS 2023, these relate to data collected using the questionnaires.
When working with estimates of CIL or CT scores, imputation variance must also be included. In
the next section, we explain how the imputation variance is incorporated into the standard error of
estimated parameters involving CIL and CT achievement scores.
13.2 Estimation of imputation variance for CIL and CT scores
ICILS 2023 includes two tests of student achievement—one for CIL and one for CT. Each test instru‐
ment contains a pool of items delivered using a balanced incomplete block design (see Chapter 2 for
details of the test development and test design) in which students completed a subset of CIL and
(where relevant) CT test items. Having students respond to a subset of items introduces additional

REPORTING THE ICILS 2023 RESULTS
273
uncertainty to the measures of student achievement. In order to accommodate this uncertainty, for
ICILS 2023, each student’s score (CIL and—where applicable CT) was represented by five random
draws from an individual’s posterior distribution—plausible values—which represent the uncertainty in
the measurement of student achievement (for more details on plausible values see Chapter 11). When
reporting estimates of parameters characterizing the distribution of CIL and CT scores in a population,
the variation among these values must be considered (von Davier et al., 2009). Population estimates
and their standard errors for ICILS 2023 CIL and CT scores should always be estimated using all five
plausible values This procedure follows the principles developed by Rubin (1987), where the estima‐
tion is performed once per each plausible value and the results are aggregated (Mislevy et al., 1992).
For more information about plausible values and their use in secondary analyses, see, for example,
Jewsbury et al. (2024).
To estimate a parameter θ that can be used to characterize the distribution of CIL or CT scores in a
(sub)population, the estimate of interest ˆθ can be computed as the average across the estimates using
each plausible value:
ˆθ = 1
P
P
X
p=1
ˆθp,
(13.3)
where ˆθp corresponds to the estimate of θ based on plausible value p = 1, ..., P and P = 5, corre‐
sponding to the five plausible values of CIL and CT that are drawn in ICILS.
The total variance of ˆθ is a composite of the sampling and the imputation variance. The sampling
variance SVˆθ is calculated as the average of the sampling variances across the plausible values:
SVˆθ = 1
P
P
X
p=1
SV p
ˆθ ,
(13.4)
where SV p
ˆθ corresponds to the sampling variance of ˆθ based on the pth plausible value, similar to the
one documented in 13.1.
The imputation variance IVˆθ consists of the variation across ˆθp relative to its mean; that is,
IVˆθ =
1
P −1
P
X

(ˆθp −ˆθ)2.
(13.5)
The total variance TVˆθ is then the sum of the sampling and the imputation variances:
TVˆθ = SVˆθ +

IVˆθ.
(13.6)
Finally, the reported standard error of ˆθ is given by:
SEˆθ =

(13.7)
The same approach was followed whenever CIL or CT scores were used in the analyses. For example,
to calculate means, mean differences, correlation, or regression coefficients with the corresponding
standard errors, among others. The sampling and imputation components of the variance for national
CIL and CT scores, together with the national sample size, are presented in Table 13.3 and Table 13.4.

13.3 Reporting relationships between CIL and CT scores
There are two examples in the ICILS 2023 international report that require some additional clarification
on how to be computed. The first is the correlation between CIL and CT, i.e., where the two sets of
plausible values are correlated. The other relates to the estimation of student CT mean achievement at
each of the CIL proficiency levels30 (both included in Table 5.8 in the ICILS 2023 international report).
Reporting correlation between CIL and CT scores
To understand how plausible values from different domains can be used in the same analysis, it is useful
to know how they are generated. For countries using both domains, plausible values for CIL and CT
are drawn simultaneously. We start by drawing the first CIL and CT plausible values, then the second
for both domains and so on (for more details, see Chapter 11). Therefore, the correlation between
the first plausible values of two domains provides an unbiased estimate of the relationship between
the domains (Jewsbury et al., 2024). To obtain the estimates of the correlation coefficient between
CIL and CT that were reported in Table 5.8 in Chapter 5 of the ICILS 2023 international report, the
correlation coefficient between CIL and CT for each country was estimated P times (which is in our
case five times), each time using CIL and CT plausible value p. The reported estimate and its standard
error are then estimated using Equations (13.3) and (13.6).
30 ICILS established a described scale, where certain cut‐off points are used to define CIL and CT proficiency levels (for
more details on proficiency levels, see Chapter 11).

276
ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
Reporting student CT mean achievement by CIL proficiency levels
To estimate the mean CT achievement by CIL proficiency levels (see Table 5.8 in the international
report), each student must be categorized into a proficiency level according to their CIL score on each
plausible value. Therefore, it may be that a student is categorized as reaching proficiency level A
when using one plausible value while categorized as reaching proficiency level B when considering a
different plausible value. This student will contribute to the estimate of mean CT achievement among
those students reaching proficiency level A of CIL when using one plausible value. In contrast, they will
not contribute to the same estimate when considering the next plausible value. In summary, the mean
CT of students reaching proficiency level A in CIL is estimated at P times. Only students reaching
proficiency level A when using the CIL plausible value p will contribute with their CT plausible value p
to the estimate of the mean CT score for this CIL proficiency level.
In a second step, the reported estimate of mean CT achievement among students reaching CIL profi‐
ciency level A and its standard error are estimated as described in the previous sections (see Equations
(13.3) and (13.6)).
13.4 Reporting of differences
In this section, we explain how we calculated the standard error of group differences in the ICILS 2023
international report. Four types of group differences were used: differences between countries (e.g.,
difference in CIL between two countries), differences between subgroups of students within countries
(e.g., gender difference in CIL within countries), differences between countries and an international
average (e.g., difference between a country CIL and the ICILS 2023 CIL average), and differences
between cycles within a country (e.g., the difference in CIL in a country between years 2023 and
2018).
The estimate of these differences ˆθ(a−b) can be generalized as:
ˆθ(a−b) = ˆθa −ˆθb,
(13.8)
that is, a simple difference between ˆθa and ˆθb. The t‐statistic associated to this estimated difference is
then given by:
t(a−b) =
ˆθ(a−b)
SEˆθ(a−b)
,
(13.9)
where SEˆθ(a−b) corresponds to the standard error of the estimated difference. Recall, that as a general
rule,
V arˆθ(a−b) = V arˆθa + V arˆθb −2 ∗Cov(ˆθa, ˆθb),
(13.10)
therefore, the estimation of SEˆθ(a−b) depends on whether or not, by design, the sampling covariance
of both estimates exists. If the data from which both estimates are derived are generated with the
same random process, then we say there is a sampling covariance between the estimates. This would
be the case, for example, of boys and girls: in most countries, boys and girls attend the same schools,
therefore, the subsample of boys and subsample of girls are based on the same sample of schools,
i.e., both subsamples are not independent. We typically refer to this by saying that the samples are
dependent. However, if each estimate is derived using data generated with an independent random
process, then, by design, the sampling covariance between both estimates is zero. As example, the
sample of different countries are drawn separately and do not depend on each other. We typically refer
to this by saying that the samples are independent. Below, we discuss different scenarios for estimating
SEˆθ(a−b), emphasizing whether or not the estimate of sampling covariance should be included.
In addition, it is worth noting that in the ICILS 2023 international report, we highlight differences that
are statistically different from zero with a confidence level of 95 percent. To do so, we compare t(a−b)
with the critical values of the t‐distribution. Since we have large samples, this distribution is equivalent
to the standard normal distribution.

REPORTING THE ICILS 2023 RESULTS
277
Differences in population estimates between countries
Appendix C of the international report includes countries’ differences in mean CIL and CT scores. Since
for each country (or explicit stratum within a country), an independent random process selects students
to participate in ICILS, it follows that there is no sampling covariance between the estimates of two
different countries. Therefore, the variance of the difference between the estimates of country c and
d is defined as the sum of the variance of the estimate of each country. The standard error of the
estimated difference in this context can be expressed as:
SE(c−d) =
q
SE2c + SE2
d.
(13.11)
Differences between subgroups within countries
In most cases, when we estimated differences between subgroups of the target population within a
country, we had to consider that the sampling covariance between those estimates is larger than zero.
That is, the samples contributing to each of the estimates are dependent. An exception to this is when
the subgroups are in different explicit strata, in which case the sampling covariance is zero (for the
description of the national samples and the national explicit strata, please see Appendix B). In this
latter case, the samples are independent, and the methodology used to estimate the standard error of
the difference follows the methodology used when we estimate differences between two countries
(see below).
Following, we describe how we estimated the standard error of an estimated difference when sampling
covariance between the two estimates exists. As explained in Section 13.1 and Section 13.2, the
computation of the standard error depends on whether the estimate involves plausible values (i.e., CIL
or CT) or not.
Standard error of differences between estimates based on dependent samples not involving
plausible values
The standard error of differences between estimates of dependent samples must consider the sampling
covariance of the two estimates. Considering (13.2), and assuming that the two groups are named as
a and b, the standard error of the difference estimate SE(a−b) is computed as:
SE(a−b) =

(13.12)
where ar and br are the estimates of subgroups a and b respectively per each replication weight r,
while a0 and b0 are the estimates of subgroups a and b using the total sampling weight. In other words,
the sampling variance consists of the variation of the difference between both groups among jackknife
replications.
Standard error of differences between estimates based on dependent samples involving
plausible values
As explained in Section 13.2, any parameter estimated involving the variables CIL or CT needs to
consider the uncertainty arising from the implemented assessment design. That is, any estimate should
consider the variation in the estimate arising from using the different plausible values. In the context
of CIL and CT, the sampling variance of the difference, SV(a−b), can be formulated as:
SV(a−b) = 1

Differences between a country and the ICILS 2023 average
In the ICILS 2023 international report, most of the analyses compare a country estimate (e.g., mean,

because they collected their data later than the other participants. This means that in most cases where
an international average was estimated using CIL, C corresponded to 30 countries, while in analysis
involving CT, C corresponded to 21 countries. However, this number could have varied, as in some
specific tables, some countries were excluded for other reasons (e.g., a low number of valid cases per
subgroup or the question not being applied in that country).

difference between country averages (for more details on linking error see Chapter 11). The equating
