To estimate the standard error of the percentage at or above the cut‐off point that defines the threshold
between Levels 1 and 2 (492), within each participating country a number of n replicate cut‐off points
were generated by computing the observed threshold plus a random error component with a mean
of 0 and a standard deviation equal to the estimated equating error (see Table 13.5). We added this
component to the CIL/CT scores, replicating this procedure N times (N = 5000). If EqErrρ,c is the
equating error for proportion ρ in country c, it can be calculated as:

REPORTING THE ICILS 2023 RESULTS
281
EqErrρ,c =
sPN
i=1(ρi,c −ρ0,c)2
N
,
(13.23)
where ρi,c corresponds to the proportion of students that reached Level 2 in the replication i in country
c, while ρ0,c corresponds to the proportion of students that reached Level 2 with the original score.
To calculate the difference in the proportion of students that reached Level 2 between 2023 and 2018,
the equating error computed in (13.23) is added to the standard error of the estimated proportion of
students that reached Level 2 in 2023 and 2018, analog to (13.21):
SE(ρ,c2023−ρ,c2018) =
q
SE2ρ,c2023 + SE2ρ,c2018 + EqErr2
(ρ,c2023−ρ,c2018).
(13.24)
To estimate the difference in the proportion of students that reached Level 2 between 2023 and 2013,
the equating error of 2018–2013 is added to the equating error of 2023–2018, analog to (13.22):
SE(ρ,c2023−ρ,c2013) =
q
SE2ρ,c2023 + SE2ρ,c2013 + EqErr2
(ρ,c2023−ρ,c2018) + EqErr2
(ρ,c2018−ρ,c2013).
(13.25)
There are some countries that participated in 2023 and 2013, but did not participate in 2018: Croatia,
Czech Republic, Norway (Grade 9), Slovak Republic, and Slovenia. Since in these countries it is not
possible to estimate the equating error from 2018 with 2013, their equating errors are imputed based
on the pooled sample of ICILS 2018. The estimated equating errors per country and pooled sample
are available in Table 13.6. Please note that the indicated equating errors are valid only to calculate
the difference in the proportion of students reaching Level 2.
13.5 ICILS 2023 reporting policy
The context in which the data was generated plays an important role in understanding and interpreting
the estimates reported in the ICILS 2023 international report. For this reason, in reporting ICILS 2023
country results, the tables are accompanied by the known information considered relevant for their
interpretation. This ensures transparency and may highlight any limitations in their interpretation.
A first set of annotations related to a whole country. In terms of sampling, the relationship between
the original sample and the realized sample (as documented by the participation rate) is relevant for
interpreting and generalizing national results, as are exclusions from the target population. Countries
that did not meet sampling participation requirements (combined participation rate lower than 75%),
with combined school and student (or teacher) exclusion rates higher than five percent, were annotated
accordingly. Other annotations included a deviation in the timing of ICILS administration and lower
coverage of the nationally defined population. These annotations described important characteristics
of the samples and were included in each table. A detailed description of these notes is included in
Chapter 7.
Further, special attention was given to item response rates. In the ICILS 2023 international report,
estimates based on a reduced weighted item response rate were annotated as follows:
• r indicates data are available for at least 70 percent but less than 85 percent of the <population>,
• s indicates data are available for at least 50 percent but less than 70 percent of the <population>,
• x indicates data are available for at least 40 percent but less than 50 percent of the <population>
– to be interpreted with caution,
• y indicates data are available for less than 40 percent of the <population>, and the estimate is
not reported.

282
ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
Specific rules were applied for reporting or suppressing population estimates. Estimates were sup‐
pressed if participants from five or fewer jackknife zones contributed to them. In addition, when
estimating the mean of a variable of interest for a subgroup, the subgroup had to consist of at least 30
participants or the estimate was suppressed.
Problems with the data collection for the ICILS main survey in the Netherlands resulted in low partic‐
ipation rates for all populations. As a consequence, data collected from schools and students in the
Netherlands are not included in the main tables of the ICILS 2023 international report. Selected data
from the Netherlands are included as an appendix and should be interpreted cautiously.
Due to problems with the data collection for the ICILS 2023 main survey in Chile, data from Chilean
schools and students are not included in the ICILS 2023 international report, except data from the
national context survey in Chapter 2.
13.6 Notes about software
Different software can be used to estimate population parameters with ICILS 2023 data. For the
international report, we used custom codes in R in combination with the IEA IDB Analyzer for R, which
is publicly available on the IEA website.32 The analysis results were verified using the IEA IDB Analyzer
for SPSS to ensure consistency with previous cycles.
The IDB Analyzer is a tool for analyzing data from IEA studies. It is freely available for use with SPSS,
SAS, and R. The software supports the correct estimation of population parameters and the method‐
ology for estimating the sampling and imputation variance for most common analyses accounting for
the ICILS survey design. The IDB Analyzer implements the formulas described in this chapter.
Other software supports the analysis of IEA large‐scale assessment data as well. MPlus supports
jackknife replication and the use of multiple plausible values. Stata supports analysis with ICILS using
the svy command for estimation of the sampling variance and the combination of plausible values using
mi. In addition to the IDB Analyzer, there are several other packages available in R, for example: survey,
RALSA, instvy, or EdSurvey (all available in CRAN repository, the latter three are specially designed to
analyze data from large‐scale assessments including IEA studies).
Please note that ICILS’s sampling procedures and analysis specifications can differ from those of other
international large‐scale assessments and even from those of other IEA studies (e.g., PIRLS, TIMSS,
and ICCS). Therefore, it is recommended that you familiarize yourself with the specifics of ICILS doc‐
umentation if you would like to replicate the results in the ICILS 2023 international report.

International study center
The International Study Center (ISC) is located at the International Association for the Evaluation of
Educational Achievement (IEA). Center staff at IEA are responsible for designing and implementing the
study in close cooperation with the National Research Coordinators (NRCs) in ICILS 2023 participating
countries.
IEA is also responsible for coordinating and implementing ICILS. IEA Amsterdam, the Netherlands,
is responsible for membership, translation verification, quality control monitoring, and publication.
IEA Hamburg, Germany is mainly responsible for field operations, sampling procedures, and data‐
processing, scaling, analysis, and reporting.

Appendix B:
National characteristics
This appendix describes, for each education system participating in ICILS 2023, the population cov‐
erage, exclusion categories, stratification variables, and any deviations from the general ICILS 2023
sampling design.
The same sample of schools was selected for the student survey and the teacher survey. However, the
school participation status of a school in the student and teacher survey could differ. It was particularly
common for a school to count as participating in the student survey, but not in the teacher survey;
however, the reverse scenario was also possible.
B.1 Austria
• School‐level exclusions consisted of schools for children with special educational needs, very
small schools (fewer than three students in the target grade) and foreign schools. Within‐school
exclusions consisted of students (in regular or excluded classes) with functional or intellectual
disabilities or non‐native language speakers who who are unable to read or speak the language(s)
of the test.
• Explicit stratification was performed by school type (APS—Allgemeine Pflichtschule, AHS—Allgemeinbildende
Höhere Schule, Miscellaneous School Types), ISF—Individual Student Funding (high, low), and ur‐
banization (rural, urban), resulting in seven explicit strata.
• Implicit stratification was applied by states, giving a total of nine implicit strata.
• The sample was disproportionally allocated to explicit strata, due to the minimum sample size
requirement of very small strata.
• Small schools were selected with equal probabilities.
• The main survey samples for ICILS 2023 and TIMSS 2023 were selected simultaneously, resulting
in a minimum overlap of the sampled schools.

B.2 Azerbaijan
• School‐level exclusions consisted of very small schools (fewer than five students in the target
grade), school with language of instruction other than Azerbaijani, and schools for children with
special educational needs. Within‐school exclusions consisted of students (in regular or excluded
classes) with functional or intellectual disabilities or non‐native language speakers who who are
unable to read or speak the language(s) of the test.
• Explicit stratification was performed by language (Azerbaijani only, Azerbaijani and Russian), and
urbanization (urban in main cities, urban in other regions, rural in main cities, rural in other re‐
gions), resulting in five explicit strata.
• Implicit stratification was applied for Azerbaijani and Russian schools by urbanization (urban in
main cities, urban in other regions, rural in main cities, rural in other regions), giving a total of
four implicit strata.
• Small schools were selected with equal probabilities.
• School sample overlap between ICILS 2023 and TIMSS 2023: The ICILS 2023 sample was se‐
lected with overlap control applying the Chowdhury approach to achieve maximum overlap be‐
tween these studies. Different classes were randomly selected for both studies.

• Explicit stratification was performed by school administration type (public, subsidized schools,

• Small schools were selected with equal probabilities.
• School sample overlap between ICILS 2023 and TIMSS 2023: The ICILS main survey sample

the Chowdhury approach.

B.8 Cyprus
• School‐level exclusions consisted of schools for children with special educational needs, very
small schools (fewer than five students in the target grade), language of instruction other than
Greek or English, and schools in the Turkish area. Within‐school exclusions consisted of students
(in regular or excluded classes) with functional or intellectual disabilities or non‐native language

strata.
• Implicit stratification was applied by urbanization (urban, rural), giving a total of two implicit strata.
• Census of schools and students. All variance estimations were computed using classes as vari‐
ance strata.

B.9 Czech Republic
• School‐level exclusions consisted of schools for children with special educational needs, very
small schools (fewer than four students in the target grade), and Polish language schools. Within‐
school exclusions consisted of students (in regular or excluded classes) with functional or intel‐
lectual disabilities or non‐native language speakers who who are unable to read or speak the
language(s) of the test.
• Explicit stratification was performed by school type (basic school, multi‐year gymnasium), and
district (Praha, Středočeský, Jihočeský, Plzeňský, Karlovarský, Ústecký, Liberecký, Královéhradecký,
Pardubický, Vysočina, Jihomoravský, Olomoucký, Zlínský, Moravskoslezský), resulting in 15 ex‐
plicit strata.
• Implicit stratification by district was applied only for multi‐year gymnasium, giving a total of 14
implicit strata.
• The sample was disproportionally allocated to explicit strata.
• Small schools were selected with equal probabilities.
• The main survey samples for ICILS 2023 and TIMSS 2023 were selected simultaneously, resulting
in a minimum overlap of the sampled schools.

B.15 Hungary
• School‐level exclusions consisted of schools for children with special educational needs and for‐
eign language schools. Within‐school exclusions consisted of students (in regular or excluded
classes) with functional or intellectual disabilities or non‐native language speakers who who are
unable to read or speak the language(s) of the test.
• Explicit stratification was performed by urbanization (capital, county town, town, rural area) and
national assessment mathematics score (high, medium, low or medium, low), resulting in 10
explicit strata.
• Implicit stratification was applied by region, giving a total of seven implicit strata.
• Small schools were selected with equal probabilities.

• School‐level exclusions consisted of schools for children with special educational needs, very

dents (in regular or excluded classes) with functional or intellectual disabilities or non‐native

B.18 Korea, Republic of
• School‐level exclusions consisted of schools for children with special educational needs, very
small schools (fewer than five students in the target grade), alternative schools, and remote
schools. Within‐school exclusions consisted of students (in regular or excluded classes) with
functional or intellectual disabilities or non‐native language speakers who who are unable to
read or speak the language(s) of the test.
• Explicit stratification was performed by urbanization (big city, medium or small city, small town
or village) and school type by gender (girl, boy, mixed), resulting in nine explicit strata.
• Implicit stratification was applied by city, giving a total of 17 implicit strata.
• The sample was disproportionally allocated to explicit strata, due to the minimum sample size
requirement of very small strata.
• Small schools were selected with equal probabilities.
• The main survey samples for ICILS 2023 and TIMSS 2023 were selected simultaneously, resulting
in maximum overlap in the first six strata and minimum overlap in strata seven to nine. In schools
selected for both studies, different classes were randomly selected for both studies.

APPENDIX
317
• No implicit stratification.
• Planned as a census of schools and students, and a sample of teachers. One school offers two
different types of curricula, and its students were therefore listed within two distinct virtual
schools. All variance estimations were computed using classes as variance strata.
• Due to technical issues during the data collection, some classes and students were set to non‐
sampled and considered in the respective base weights. This effectively results in three schools
not participating.
• Class grouping with six levels according to the ability of the students.

B.23 The Netherlands
• School‐level exclusions consisted of schools for children with special educational needs, private
schools, international schools, very small schools (fewer than five students in the target grade),
and schools for refugees. Within‐school exclusions consisted of students (in regular or excluded
classes) with functional or intellectual disabilities or non‐native language speakers who who are
unable to read or speak the language(s) of the test.
• Explicit stratification was performed by educational tracks (Voorbereidend Middelbaar Beroep‐
sonderwijs/Praktijkonderwijs, Hoger Algemeen Voortgezet Onderwijs/ Voorbereidend Weten‐
schappelijk Onderwijs, mixed), resulting in three explicit strata.
• No implicit stratification was applied.
• Small schools were selected with equal probabilities.
• School sample overlap between ICILS 2023 and National Assessment: The ICILS 2023 sample
was selected with overlap control applying the Chowdhury approach to achieve minimum overlap
between these studies.

B.24 Norway
• School‐level exclusions consisted of schools for children with special educational needs, very
small schools (fewer than five students in the target grade), international schools, Samii schools,
and immigrant schools. Within‐school exclusions consisted of students (in regular or excluded
classes) with functional or intellectual disabilities or non‐native language speakers who who are
unable to read or speak the language(s) of the test.
• Explicit stratification was performed by region (Oslo, other), resulting in two explicit strata.
• Implicit stratification was applied by language (two levels) and the national examination school
score (four levels), giving a total of six implicit strata.
• The sample was disproportionally allocated to explicit strata.
• Small schools were selected with equal probabilities.

B.25 Oman
• School‐level exclusions consisted of schools for children with special educational needs, very
small schools (fewer than six students in the target grade), and remote schools. Within‐school
exclusions consisted of students (in regular or excluded classes) with functional or intellectual
disabilities or non‐native language speakers who who are unable to read or speak the language(s)
of the test.
• Explicit stratification was performed by governorate (Muscat, Ash Sharqiyah North, Ash Shar‐

sandam, Dhofar, Al Wusta) and school administration type (private, international), resulting in 13
explicit strata.
• Implicit stratification was applied by school type, giving a total of three implicit strata.
• The sample was disproportionally allocated to explicit strata.
• Small schools were selected with equal probabilities.
• School sample overlap between ICILS 2023 and TIMSS 2023: The ICILS 2023 sample was se‐
lected with overlap control applying the Chowdhury approach to achieve minimum overlap be‐
tween these studies.

B.26 Portugal
• School‐level exclusions consisted of very small schools (fewer than five students in the target
grade) and minority‐language schools. Within‐school exclusions consisted of students (in regular
or excluded classes) with functional or intellectual disabilities or non‐native language speakers
who who are unable to read or speak the language(s) of the test.
• Explicit stratification was performed by grade, school administration type (public, private) and
region (Alentejo, Algarve, Lisboa, Centro, Norte, Madeira, and Açores), resulting in nine explicit
strata.
• Implicit stratification was applied by type of school administration (public, private) and region
(five regions), giving a total of nine implicit strata.
• Small schools were selected with equal probabilities.
• The main survey samples for ICILS 2023 and TIMSS 2023 were selected simultaneously, resulting
in maximum overlap of the schools sampled for both studies. However, different classes were
randomly selected for both studies.

B.28 Serbia
• School‐level exclusions consisted of schools for children with special educational needs, very
small schools (fewer than six students in the target grade), and schools teaching in a language
other than Serbian.
Within‐school exclusions consisted of students (in regular or excluded
classes) with functional or intellectual disabilities or non‐native language speakers who who are

(city, other) and school hierarchy (Main School, Branch Department), resulting in seven explicit
strata.
• Implicit stratification was applied by the national assessment performance group for mathemat‐
ics, giving a total of four implicit strata.
• Small schools were selected with equal probabilities.

• School‐level exclusions consisted of schools for children with special educational needs, very

• The sample was disproportionally allocated to explicit strata.
• Small schools were selected with equal probabilities.

Appendix C:
CIL release module scoring rules
Table C.1 and Table C.2 provide summary information about the human and machine scoring rules
used to score students responses for each task in the CIL release modules Breathing and School trip.
C.1 Breathing
Table C.1: Scoring information for CIL module Breathing
Task
number
Scoring
variable
Scoring rules
1
C1H01ZA
Code 1: The target file is opened by any of the methods listed below.
‐ Method 1 (Double click): Double clicks on the file “Assignment.prs.”
‐ Method 2 (Context menu): Right clicks on the file “Assignment.prs”
and then selects the menu item “[Open file].”
‐ Method 3 (Toolbar menu): Left clicks on the file “Assignment.prs”
then selects the toolbar item “[File]” and menu item “[Open].”
Code 0: The target file is not opened or a non‐target file is opened.
2
C1H02ZA
Code 2: The presentation file is saved as one of the names (exact
match) listed below.
‐ Breathing
‐ Breathing.prs
‐ ‘Breathing’
‐ ‘Breathing.prs’
Code 1: The presentation file is saved as any file name different to
the default file name (“Assignment.prs”).
Code 0: The presentation file is not saved, or the file name is not
changed.
3
C1H03ZA
Code 1: Left clicks on the [WebSearch] button in the taskbar.
Code 0: Other responses.
4
C1H04AC
Code 2: Gives a search phrase that (i) implies or states an explanation
of the process of breathing and (ii) refers to an explanation suitable
for children. E.g., How breathing works (i) explained for children (ii).
Code 1: Gives a search phrase that implies or states an explanation
of the process of breathing. E.g., How lungs work.

ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
Table C.1: Scoring information for CIL module Breathing (Continued)
5
C1H05ZC
Code 1: Response reflects ‘No’ and includes a reason from one of the
categories listed below.
‐ Category 1: The information could be exaggerated/made up to sell
a product.
‐ Category 2: Only one anonymous customer testimonial.
‐ Category 3: Lack of independent research.
‐ Category 4: Lack of cited sources or supporting evidence.
Code 0: Response reflects ‘Yes’ (with any explanation). OR States
that the site serves as promotional material without explicitly
referring to the pills, a ‘product,’ or equivalent. OR Response is vague,
irrelevant, or repeats the stem.
6
C1H06ZC
Code 1: Response includes a reason from one of the categories listed
below.
‐ Category 1: (Yes) The author’s expertise (university professor).
‐ Category 2: (Yes) The information can be edited by the public
(public scrutiny).
‐ Category 3: (No) The information might be false or fake because it
can be edited by the public.
‐ Category 4: (No) There is no verifiable evidence of the author’s
identity or expertise.
Code 0: Includes reasons from any of the categories listed below. OR
Response is vague, irrelevant, or repeats the stem.
‐ Category 1: (Yes) The scientific nature of the information.
‐ Category 2: (Yes) The source of the information is known (without
referring to the author’s expertise).
7
C1H07AL
Presentation title
‐ Code 1: Content of title is appropriate: Title refers to the concept
of “Breathing.”
‐ Code 0: No title or content of title is inappropriate OR other titles
that fail to include the concept of “Breathing.”

Table C.1: Scoring information for CIL module Breathing (Continued)
7
C1H07BL
Text – Information design (distinction between headings and text
body)
Code 2: Clear control of layout
There is clear evidence of control and planning in the layout of the
presentation. Formatting tools have been used successfully to
support readers’ understanding of the role of the different text
elements on all slides that use text (excluding the title slide).
‐ There is a clear distinction between main body text and heading
using bolding, underlining, italicizing or increased font size for the
heading(s) OR placing the heading/sub‐headings on a separate slide.
Code 1: Basic control of layout
‐ There is some evidence of planning in the layout of the
presentation. Formatting tools have been used to some degree to
support readers’ understanding of the role of the different text
elements:
Note: Code 1 may include inconsistent use of formatting. It may
include clear control of some elements with some basic or poor
control of others.
‐ Headings and sub‐headings are mostly distinguishable from the
main body text by formatting, e.g., using color, bolding, underlining,
italicizing, or increased font size OR placing the
heading/sub‐headings on a separate slide.
OR
‐ ONLY (paragraph) spacing has been used to distinguish between
headings and body text.
OR
Text and headings have been copied and pasted from the web
resources, maintaining the formatting that distinguishes between
headings and body text from the original resource.
Code 0: Little or no control of layout
There is little or no evidence of control and planning in the layout of
the text. Formatting tools do not support readers’ understanding of
the role of the different text elements:
‐ The text layout does not distinguish between headings,
sub‐headings, and main body text.

7
C1H07CL
Text position (alignment)
Code 2: Clear control of layout
There is clear evidence of control and planning in the layout of the
presentation.
‐ The position and alignment of text makes it easy to follow the
information visually (such as spacing between paragraphs or use of
bullet points to break up paragraphs).
Code 1: Basic control of layout
There is some evidence of planning in the layout of the presentation.
Note: Code 1 may include inconsistent use of formatting. It may
include clear control of some elements with some basic or poor
control of others.
‐ The text is mostly positioned thoughtfully and mostly well aligned.
Some text may be close together but does not overlap with other
text or the images or inhibit reading flow.
Code 0: Little or no control of layout
There is little or no evidence of control and planning in the layout of
the text.
‐ Text is not well aligned or may overlap (with other text or with the
images), inhibiting reading flow.
7
C1H07DL
Images – Layout
Code 2: Clear control of image layout
The images are well aligned and appropriate in size.
Code 1: Basic control of image layout
There is some evidence of the layout of the image(s). One image may
be too big, too small, or overlap with other images.
Code 0: Little or no control of image layout
There is little or no evidence of planning the layout of the images.
The image(s) are too big, too small, or overlap with other images.

Table C.1: Scoring information for CIL module Breathing (Continued)
7
C1H07EL
Text – Contrast
Code 2: Clear and consistent contrast
Slides show evidence of careful planning regarding the use of color to
support contrast between the slide contents and backgrounds.
‐ There is sufficient contrast to enable all text to be seen and read
easily.
Code 1: Inconsistent contrast
Slides show some evidence of planning regarding the use of color to
support contrast between the slide contents and backgrounds.
‐ The text mostly contrasts sufficiently with the background to be
seen and read easily.
Code 0: Little or no contrast
Slides show no evidence of planning regarding the use of color to
support contrast between the slide contents and backgrounds.
‐ The grey background and text colors have been used throughout.
OR
‐ The colors of the headings, text and backgrounds show poor
contrast throughout the presentation and inhibit reading and viewing
of text.
Continued on next page

340
ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
Table C.1: Scoring information for CIL module Breathing (Continued)
7
C1H07FL
Color – Continuity
Code 2: Careful continuity planning
Slides show evidence of careful planning regarding the use of color to
demonstrate the relationships of elements within and across the
slides.
‐ Colors of headings, body text, or backgrounds across the slides are
consistent. OR
‐ Colors of headings, body text, or backgrounds across the slides
change with clear reason (e.g., to emphasize different processes).
Code 1: Some continuity planning
Slides show some evidence of planning regarding the use of color to
demonstrate the relationships of elements within and across the
slides.
‐ There is some use of color to denote specific purposes of text (such
as to denote headings and text body). This may be evident within but
not across slides, or across slides but with minor inconsistencies.
Code 0: Little or no continuity planning
Slides show no evidence of planning regarding the use of color or the
use of color is jarring/distracting.
‐ The default grey background and text color scheme has been used.
OR
‐ The colors of the headings, text and backgrounds seem to have
been assigned randomly or decoratively without any clear
connection to the purpose of the text within or across the slides. OR
‐ The background color is different for each slide with no other use of
color (this is deemed as insufficient evidence of planning). OR
‐ The presentation consists of a single slide with content (blank
slides—without content—should be ignored).

C1H07GL
Images – Relevance
Code 2: At least two steps are clearly supported by relevant images
only.
Two or three steps of breathing are introduced by headings or
explained with text and each step is supported by the inclusion of a
relevant image only. No irrelevant images have been included.
Code 1: One step and no irrelevant images or more than one step
and any irrelevant images.
One of the three steps of breathing is introduced by a heading or
explained with text and supported by a relevant image and no
irrelevant images have been included.
OR
Two or three steps of breathing are introduced by headings or
explained with text and each step is supported by the inclusion of a
relevant image AND any irrelevant images have been included.
Code 0: One step and any irrelevant images or no steps are clearly
explained by images.
One of the three steps of breathing is introduced by a heading or
explained with text and supported by a relevant image AND any
irrelevant images have been included.
OR
Images included are either irrelevant or not linked to any of the
stages of breathing.
OR
No images have been included.

Table C.1: Scoring information for CIL module Breathing (Continued)
7
C1H07IL
Information – Accuracy
Code 1: Information provided is accurate
The information given about each step in the breathing process that
is presented is accurate. See the marking resource below for what
constitutes an accurate description for a step.
Code 0: Information provided contains inaccuracies
There are errors or omissions in the descriptions of the steps that are
presented about breathing.
7
C1H07JL
Information – Adaptation
Code 1: Some information adapted
The presentation conveys the steps of breathing in a way that is
partly accessible to an 8–9 year old audience:
‐ Some scientific terms have been adapted or defined in plain
language.
‐ There is little explicit detail of any biological and chemical processes.
Code 0: Information not adapted
Makes no attempt to address the needs of an 8–9 year old audience:

APPENDIX
355
D.2 Farm drone
Algorithm construction tasks
In the algorithm construction tasks, students were required to select and sequence block‐based com‐
mands for the farm drone to perform specified actions. These actions included:
• Moving the drone to specified tile locations.
• Dropping water, seeds, or fertilizer on dirt, small crop, and large crop targets.
• Applying conditional logic based on crop size (using an “if‐do” command).
After two warm‐up tasks, students were introduced to the “repeat” command, enabling multiple repe‐
titions of command sequences.
Algorithm debugging tasks
The algorithm debugging tasks required students to modify a pre‐configured sequence of commands to
correct the drone’s behavior according to the specified task objective(s). These tasks typically required
one or two minor changes to the code configuration, though the work space was unconstrained and
students had the option to remove all commands and create an entirely new algorithm.
Task complexity
As students progressed through the module, task complexity increased based on the number of actions
needed to be completed by the drone and the number, type, and arrangement of targets (where water,
seeds, or fertilizer needed to be dropped).
Criteria for scoring
Students’ solutions for both algorithm construction and debugging tasks were evaluated based on two
main criteria: correctness and efficiency. The ICILS research team identified variables and developed
specific scoring logic for each task. The measures for correctness and efficiency were then combined
into a single score for each coding task, which was generated automatically by the test delivery system.
Below, we outline the scoring logic for both criteria and explain how they were combined to form the
overall score for each task.
Scoring correctness
Correctness was determined by how well the drone’s actions aligned with the task instructions. In
simpler tasks, correctness was evaluated based on whether the drone reached a specified location. In
more complex tasks that involved both movement and target actions, correctness was evaluated based
on three sub‐criteria, which were composed of two variables: correct targets and incorrect targets:
1. Correct targets: The number of targets with the correct materials (2 score points per target).
2. Correct targets with incorrect materials: The number of targets with incorrect materials (1 score
point per target).
3. Incorrect targets: Whether any materials were dropped on non‐target tiles (1 score point if no
materials were dropped on incorrect targets; 0 score points if any materials were dropped on
incorrect targets).
Each solution was first scored on these variables (correct targets and incorrect targets). Then, the
frequency of the scores and their combinations for all solutions were analyzed iteratively to establish a
scoring logic for correctness on each task. This logic combined the correct and incorrect target scores
into a single initial combined correctness score.
After the scoring logic was established for each task, a Rasch item response theory (Rasch, 1960) scaling

356
ICILS 2023 TECHNICAL REPORT
analysis (see Appendix 11 for further details) was performed to assess the measurement validity of the
scoring logic. Where necessary, we recoded scoring categories for certain items.
Table D.2 shows an example of how this scoring logic was applied, along with the recoding of categories
following initial scaling.
For each task, the score for a completely correct response remained as the highest unique score cat‐
egory. The recoding of score categories for partially correct responses varied across tasks and was
informed by response frequencies, differences in response quality, and the extent to which these dif‐
ferences were reflected in the student data.

Scoring efficiency
Efficiency was based on the number of commands used in the solution. The most efficient solution for
each task used the fewest commands. For example, if a task required the drone to move forward five
times, this was most commonly achieved by students using either five “move forward” commands in
sequence or using one “repeat” command (configured to 5 repeats) and one “move forward” command.
The latter solution which is the most efficient, uses two commands. In theory other configurations are
also possible, such as to use one “move forward” command and a “repeat” command (configured to 4
repeats) with an additional “move forward” command. This solution would use three commands.
For each task it was possible to identify clusters of the number of commands that most likely corre‐
sponded to different approaches to solving the task. For each task we created an efficiency score hier‐
archy based on the number of commands used in the solution and informed by the task requirements,
the likely characteristics of solutions that could be associated with different numbers of commands,
and the frequencies of the number of commands across the responses.
Table D.3 shows an example of how this scoring logic was applied, together with the recoding of
categories following initial scaling.

Combining correctness and efficiency
To produce a final score for each task, the efficiency score was used to adjust the correctness score.
However, we decided that the efficiency of a solution should only be used to adjust the highest solution
score for each task. Operationally this meant that the efficiency of code was not considered to be
important in evaluating the quality of incorrect or partially correct responses, but that it was considered
to be important in identifying differences in the quality of fully correct responses.
We created a new composite variable (correctness and efficiency) for each coding task. For this variable,
the score for fully correct responses was adjusted such that:
• fully correct responses with the highest efficiency score were adjusted to one score point higher
than the highest correctness score;
• fully correct responses with a partial credit (moderate) efficiency score were not adjusted; and
• fully correct responses with a zero efficiency score were adjusted to one score point lower than
the highest correctness score.
Incorrect or partially correct responses were not adjusted based on efficiency. Table D.4 shows an
example of how correctness and efficiency scores were combined to form the single composite variable.

Appendix E:
Background variables used for conditioning
The table lists all the international student‐level variables (along with their respective scoring) that were
used in the conditioning of plausible values for CIL and CT. To account for between‐school differences,
stratum indicator, the average of weighted likelihood ability (WLE) estimates for all other students in the
same school were introduced as direct conditioning variables. Students’ gender was also included as a
direct conditioning variable. In addition, all available international student questionnaire variables were
included to condition on students’ background information. These categorical variables were dummy‐
coded prior to principal component analysis (PCA). This step extracted a smaller set of uncorrelated
components that captured most of the variance in the original variables. These components were
subsequently used as direct conditioning variables in the model.

Appendix G:
Recoding and scoring rules for variables
included in scaling
This appendix details the recoding and scoring rules that were used to create the scoring variables
included in scaling for the four released modules (two CIL and two CT modules). Scoring variables
contain data about students’ codified responses to tasks. In some cases a single scoring variable con‐
tains data corresponding directly to an evaluation of the quality of responses to a given task. The
score categories in these variables may be used directly in scaling or may need to be recoded before
the variable can be included in scaling. Other scoring variables are derived by combining information
from more than one component variable, each of which captures specific details about how and/or
how well a student executed a task. After the data from component variables have been combined,
the resultant scoring variable may further need to be recoded before it can be included in scaling.
The scoring and recoding rules for the CIL modules, Breathing and School trip, are presented in Ta‐
ble G.1 and Table G.2, respectively. The rules for the CT modules, Automated bus and Farm drone, are
shown in Table G.3 and Table G.4, respectively. Only scoring variables that resulted from combining
variables or recoding are listed.
G.1 Breathing

Errata note
This note lists the incorrect data spotted in the first edition of the ICILS 2023 Technical Report, released
on 26 May 2025.
If you require further information about the errata listed here, please contact icils@iea‐hamburg.de
Chapter
Table/figure
Issue
Correction
2
Table 2.12
A scoring
variable was
omitted.
A scoring variable was inadvertently omitted from
Table 2.12. After its inclusion, the number of
scoring variables for CIL aspect 4.2 increased from
nine to 10. Consequently, the total number of
scoring variables across all CIL aspects was
updated from 108 to 109. The percentages of
scoring variables associated with CIL aspects 3.2
and 4.2 were also revised. Specifically, the
percentage for aspect 3.2 was adjusted from 30
percent to 29 percent, and for aspect 4.2, from
nine percent to eight percent. The reference to
the total number of scoring variables in the
description following Table 2.12 was also updated
from 108 to 109.
2
Table 2.12
Incorrect
description
following
Table 2.12.
The description following Table 2.12 incorrectly
summarized the distribution of score points across
CIL strands. The percentage of score points for
CIL strand 1 was revised from 13 percent to 12
percent, and the percentage for CIL strand 3 was
revised from 48 percent to 49 percent.


The International Computer and Information Literacy Study (ICILS) 2023 investigated 
how well students are prepared for life, learning, and work in an increasingly digitalized 
world. As the third cycle of ICILS, this study has continued to assess international 
differences in students’ computer and information literacy (CIL)—their ability to use 
digital technologies to investigate, create, communicate, and participate effectively 
in personal, educational, occupational, and civic contexts. As initiated in ICILS 2018, 
participating education systems could also choose to assess students’ computational 
thinking (CT), which refers to their capacity to conceptualize real-world problems in 
computational terms and to develop and evaluate algorithmic solutions that can be 
executed by digital systems. 
This technical report complements the international reports that present the results of 
ICILS 2023. It provides comprehensive documentation on the conceptual framework 
and instrument development; translation and verification procedures; sampling design 
and implementation; the use and management of digital assessment platforms; field 
operations including data collection quality control; data processing and construction of 
the international database; and scaling, analysis, and reporting methods. 
The report enables researchers to evaluate published findings based on ICILS 2023 data. 
When used in conjunction with the ICILS 2023 User Guide for the International Database, 
it also serves as a resource for those conducting secondary analyses. 
Since its inception in 2013, ICILS has contributed to the global understanding of digital 
literacy education. The ICILS 2023 database offers rich opportunities for examining 
how education systems equip young people with essential digital competencies and for 
evaluating progress toward national and international educational goals, including those 
articulated in the United Nations’ 2030 Agenda for Sustainable Development, and in 
the European Union’s Resolution on a strategic framework for European cooperation in 
education and training towards the European Education Area and beyond (2021–2030).
